zimrnum

Numerics, statistics and neural-network primitives for zimr, with a CPU implementation and a matching set of GPU kernels. This document covers the library as it exists in src/zimrnum.zig. Sections appear when the code they describe does.

1. What zimrnum is#

A numerical library for zimr: tensors and the operations over them, statistics, and the primitives a neural network is built from. Every operation has a CPU implementation in src/zimrnum.zig, and most have a matching GPU kernel checked against it.

1.1 The dependency contract#

src/zimrnum.zig imports std, zm (src/zimrmath.zig) and kompute (src/kompute.zig), and nothing else. Its whole closure is free of shaders, of the WebGPU bindings and of the engine, so it compiles and tests in seconds, and a module that is itself zm-only can adopt it without acquiring a GPU dependency. The constraint is enforced by build.zig, which creates the zn module with exactly those two imports.

1.2 The CPU implementation and the GPU kernels#

The CPU implementation is the reference. Each GPU kernel is authored in Zig against kompute, compiled to SPIR-V by the stock Zig compiler and translated to WGSL by src/spv2wgsl.zig; every kernel is compared element by element against the corresponding zn function on a real device. §13 covers what that comparison establishes and what it cannot.

Where both sides need the same mathematical function, both call zm. That matters because zm selects its own implementation per backend — zm.atan2 is std.math.atan2 on the host and a polynomial on the GPU — so calling it keeps the choice in one place.

2. What exists#

AreaPresent

Reductions: sum, mean, min, max, along an axisyes

Network pieces: softmax, layer norm, MSE, SGD stepyes
Statistics: variance, deviation, covariance, correlation, scalingyes

Layers, optimisers beyond SGD, a training loopno

FFT, convolution, poolingno
Reinforcement learning, dataframes, serialisationno
Element types other than f16/f32/f64; sparse tensorsno

3. Getting started#

3.1 A complete program#

This fits a line to noisy data and reports the loss before and after. It is the whole library in miniature: a tensor, a layer, a tape, a gradient, an update.

const std = @import("std");
const zn = @import("zn");

// A complete program: fit a line to noisy data, then report the error before and after.
pub fn main() !void {
    var arena: std.heap.ArenaAllocator = .init(std.heap.page_allocator);
    defer arena.deinit();
    const gpa: std.mem.Allocator = arena.allocator();

    // 1. Some data. y = 3x + 2, with noise.
    const rng: zn.Rng = .init(20260913);
    const x: zn.Tensor(f64) = try zn.linspace(f64, gpa, 0, 1, 32, .include);
    const y: zn.Tensor(f64) = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(gpa, &.{ 32, 1 });
    const noise: zn.Tensor(f64) = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(gpa, &.{32});
    rng.fillNormal(f64, noise.data);
    for (0..32) |i| {
        y.data[i] = 3.0 * x.data[i] + 2.0 + 0.1 * noise.data[i];
    }
    const inputs: zn.Tensor(f64) = try x.reshape(&.{ 32, 1 });

    // 2. A layer to fit with.
    const line = try zn.Dense(f64).init(gpa, rng.split(1), 1, 1, .xavier);

    // 3. Train.
    var first: f64 = 0;
    var last: f64 = 0;
    for (0..400) |step| {
        var graph: zn.Graph(f64) = .init(gpa);
        const guess: zn.Dense(f64).Attached = try line.attach(
            &graph,
            try graph.constant(inputs),
        );
        const loss: zn.Var = try graph.mseLoss(guess.out, try graph.constant(y));
        const value: f64 = graph.valueOf(loss).data[0];
        if (step == 0) {
            first = value;
        }
        last = value;
        try graph.backward(loss);
        const dw: zn.Tensor(f64) = try graph.gradOf(guess.weight);
        const db: zn.Tensor(f64) = try graph.gradOf(guess.bias);
        for (line.weight.data, dw.data) |*w, g| {
            w.* -= 0.1 * g;
        }
        for (line.bias.data, db.data) |*b, g| {
            b.* -= 0.1 * g;
        }
    }

    std.debug.print("\n  loss {d:.4} -> {d:.4}\n", .{ first, last });
    std.debug.print(
        "  slope {d:.3} (true 3.0), intercept {d:.3} (true 2.0)\n",
        .{ line.weight.data[0], line.bias.data[0] },
    );
}
Rng
/// A reproducible random source addressed by (seed, index) rather than by mutation.
///
/// WHY COUNTER-BASED, AND NOT A STATEFUL GENERATOR
///
/// A classic PRNG carries state and advances it, so drawing the i-th value means drawing the
/// first i. That is fine on one CPU thread and useless everywhere else this library is going:
///
/// * **A GPU kernel has no shared state.** Thread `i` must be able to compute ITS value without
/// any other thread having run. A counter-based generator is a pure function of the index, so
/// the kernel is `value(seed, thread_id)` and needs no sequencing at all.
/// * **CPU and GPU must agree EXACTLY.** The CPU path is this library's oracle (plan section 5 rung
/// 1); an oracle that produces a different random stream than the thing it checks is not an
/// oracle. Same function, same integers, same answer.
/// * **A failing case is reproducible from two numbers.** "seed 7, index 4096" is the whole
/// state, so a bad draw can be re-created in a unit test without replaying a sequence.
///
/// THE MIXER IS DELIBERATELY MODEST AND THE TESTS SAY SO. `mix32` is the murmur3 finalizer -
/// a bijection with good avalanche, applied to `index` folded with `seed`. It is NOT a
/// cryptographic generator and NOT claimed to pass a full statistical battery; what it is pinned
/// on is the properties this library actually relies on: determinism, range, stream independence,
/// and first/second moments. A stronger generator can replace the body without touching a single
/// call site, precisely because the interface is (seed, index) rather than a stream.
pub const Rng = struct {
    seed: u32,

    /// A generator for a given seed. Two `Rng`s with the same seed are the same function.
    pub fn init(seed: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = seed };
    }

    /// A DERIVED generator, so one seed can feed several independent uses without the caller
    /// inventing seeds. `parent.split(0)` and `parent.split(1)` are unrelated streams.
    ///
    /// Mixing the label rather than adding it: `seed + 1` and `seed + 2` are adjacent inputs to
    /// the mixer, and adjacency is exactly what a hash is asked not to preserve. Passing the
    /// label through `mix32` first means the derived seeds are as far apart as any other pair.
    pub fn split(self: Rng, label: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = mix32(self.seed ^ mix32(label ^ 0x9e3779b9)) };
    }

    /// The number of mantissa bits `T` can hold, including the implicit leading one. This is how
    /// many random bits a draw in `[0, 1)` may use: fewer wastes range, MORE ROUNDS UP TO 1.0 and
    /// breaks the half-open interval.
    fn mantissaWidth(comptime T: type) u32 {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return switch (T) {
            f16 => 11,
            f32 => 24,
            f64 => 53,
            else => unreachable,
        };
    }

    /// One 32-bit word of the generator's output at `index`, from independent stream `stream`.
    ///
    /// EACH KIND OF DRAW OWNS A STREAM, SO KINDS DO NOT INTERFERE
    ///
    /// Without this, every method would start from `bits(index)` and drawing two different KINDS
    /// at one index would return values that determine each other - `intBelow(i, n)` would be a
    /// function of `unitFloat(i)`. A caller has no reason to expect that and every reason to be
    /// hurt by it, so the stream is part of the addressing rather than the caller's problem.
    ///
    /// The stream index is folded into the MIXER, not into the index. Folding it into the index
    /// (`bits(index ^ K)`) makes indices `i` and `i ^ K` draw from the same two words with their
    /// roles swapped, so those pairs are not independent - which is exactly the defect this
    /// replaced.
    ///
    /// `stream == 0` adds nothing, so `bits` is unchanged by the introduction of rounds.
    fn word(self: Rng, index: u32, stream: u32) u32 {
        return mix32(mix32(index *% 0x9e3779b9) ^ self.seed ^ (stream *% 0x85ebca6b));
    }

    /// The raw 32-bit draw at `index`, stream 0. Distinct from every other kind of draw.
    pub fn bits(self: Rng, index: u32) u32 {
        return self.word(index, 0);
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)` from stream `stream` (and `stream + 1` when `T` needs more than 32 bits).
    ///
    /// Exactly `mantissaWidth(T)` random bits, scaled by 2^-width. The largest result is
    /// (2^width - 1) / 2^width, which is the largest `T` strictly below one - so the interval is
    /// half-open for every width, including `f16`, where using 24 bits rounds to 1.0 instead.
    fn unitFloatRound(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32, comptime stream: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const width: u32 = comptime mantissaWidth(T);
        const first: u64 = self.word(index, stream);
        const mantissa: u64 = if (comptime width <= 32)
            first >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(32 - width))
        else
            (first << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width - 32))) |
                (@as(u64, self.word(index, stream + 1)) >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(64 - width)));
        const numerator: T = @floatFromInt(mantissa);
        const denominator: T = @floatFromInt(@as(u64, 1) << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width)));
        return numerator / denominator;
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn unitFloat(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        return self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 1);
    }

    /// A float in `[low, high)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn uniform(
        self: Rng,
        comptime T: type,
        index: u32,
        low: T,
        high: T,
    ) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return low + (high - low) * self.unitFloat(T, index);
    }

    /// A standard normal draw (mean 0, variance 1) at `index`, by the Box-Muller transform.
    ///
    /// ITS TWO UNIFORMS COME FROM ITS OWN STREAMS (3-6), not from the uniform stream, so
    /// `normal(i)` and `unitFloat(i)` are independent and a caller mixing them needs no care.
    ///
    /// Box-Muller rather than a ziggurat because a ziggurat REJECTS: the number of uniforms it
    /// consumes varies per sample, which would make the value at `index` depend on how many
    /// rejections happened. That is not a pure function of the index and would not survive being
    /// evaluated on an independent GPU thread. Box-Muller consumes exactly two, always.
    ///
    /// The radius draw is taken from `(0, 1]` rather than `[0, 1)` because `@log(0)` is -inf.
    pub fn normal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const two_pi: T = 6.283185307179586;
        const radius_draw: T = 1.0 - self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 3);
        const angle_draw: T = self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 5);
        const radius: T = @sqrt(-2.0 * @log(radius_draw));
        return radius * @cos(two_pi * angle_draw);
    }

    /// An integer in `[0, bound)`, drawn at `index` from its own stream. `bound == 0` yields 0.
    ///
    /// Lemire's multiply-shift, not `% bound`: the modulo is biased toward small values whenever
    /// `bound` does not divide 2^32, and the bias stays invisible until something like a replay
    /// buffer over-samples its early entries.
    pub fn intBelow(self: Rng, index: u32, bound: u32) u32 {
        if (bound == 0) {
            return 0;
        }
        const wide: u64 = @as(u64, self.word(index, 7)) * @as(u64, bound);
        return @intCast(wide >> 32);
    }

    /// Fill `out` with uniforms in `[0, 1)`, drawn at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillUniform(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.unitFloat(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }

    /// Fill `out` with standard normal draws, at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillNormal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.normal(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }
};
Tensor
/// A view of `T`-typed storage as a multidimensional array.
///
/// A TENSOR NEVER OWNS ITS STORAGE, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN
///
/// There is no ownership flag and no `deinit`. `data` is a slice the tensor addresses; whoever
/// allocated it frees it, with the allocator that allocated it. Usually that is an arena, in
/// which case nobody frees anything and `arena.deinit()` ends the matter.
///
/// The alternative - an `owns: bool` and a `deinit` that is a no-op for views - is the shape
/// this library was warned about before it had a tensor: both of its failure modes are SILENT.
/// `deinit` on a view does nothing and reads as if it did something; a missing `deinit` on an
/// owner just grows memory. Having no `deinit` at all means a free appears exactly where an
/// allocation appears, and a view has neither.
///
/// Strides are in ELEMENTS and signed, so a reversed or negatively-strided view is expressible
/// without changing this type. `at` computes a signed offset and casts once.
pub fn Tensor(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The storage this tensor addresses. Not owned - see the type's doc comment.
        data: []T,
        /// Extent of each axis. Only the first `rank` entries are meaningful.
        shape: [max_rank]usize,
        /// Step in ELEMENTS between consecutive positions along each axis.
        strides: [max_rank]isize,
        rank: u8,
        /// WHERE THIS VIEW STARTS INSIDE `data`
        ///
        /// Every view until now began at element 0, which is why `slice` could not exist: a
        /// window into the middle of a tensor IS an offset. With this, `slice` and the rest of
        /// znum's `index` namespace become views rather than copies.
        ///
        /// IT ALSO WEAKENS WHAT `data.len` MEANS. `data` is the whole backing store; `base`
        /// and the strides say which of it this view addresses. Everything that walked
        /// `data[0..size()]` assuming those coincide had to be corrected - five sites, all of
        /// them fast paths that skip the index walk, which is exactly where such an assumption
        /// hides.
        base: usize = 0,

        /// A dense row-major tensor over `shape`, allocated from `gpa`.
        ///
        /// The caller frees `result.data` with the same allocator - or allocates from an arena
        /// and frees nothing.
        pub fn alloc(gpa: Allocator, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            const storage: []T = gpa.alloc(T, count) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
            return fromSlice(storage, shape);
        }

        /// A dense row-major view of `data` as `shape`. `data.len` must be exactly the product of
        /// the extents - a slice that is merely LARGE ENOUGH is rejected, because the usual reason
        /// for the mismatch is a shape that is wrong rather than storage that is generous.
        pub fn fromSlice(data: []T, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != data.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            var self: Self = .{
                .data = data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                .base = 0,
            };
            // Row-major: the last axis is contiguous, and each earlier stride is the product of
            // the extents to its right.
            var stride: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = shape.len;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                self.shape[axis] = shape[axis];
                self.strides[axis] = stride;
                stride *= @intCast(shape[axis]);
            }
            return self;
        }

        /// The number of elements this tensor addresses.
        pub fn size(self: Self) usize {
            var count: usize = 1;
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                count *= self.shape[axis];
            }
            return count;
        }

        /// Whether the strides are dense row-major, so `data[0..size()]` is the tensor in order.
        ///
        /// An operation that walks memory linearly must ask this rather than assume it: a view
        /// produced by `transpose` addresses the same storage in a different order, and a linear
        /// walk over it silently reads the wrong elements rather than failing.
        pub fn isContiguous(self: Self) bool {
            var expected: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = self.rank;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                if (self.shape[axis] != 1 and self.strides[axis] != expected) {
                    return false;
                }
                expected *= @intCast(self.shape[axis]);
            }
            return true;
        }

        /// The offset into `data` of the element at `indices`.
        pub fn flatIndex(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!usize {
            if (indices.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                if (i >= self.shape[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// The element at `indices`.
        pub fn at(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!T {
            return self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)];
        }

        /// The flat offset for a coordinate, with no error.
        ///
        /// THE 45 `flatIndex(...) catch unreachable` SITES ARE ALL THIS SHAPE
        ///
        ///     const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        ///     out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = ...
        ///
        /// The slice is `walker[0..rank]`, so `indices.len != rank` **cannot** happen - the
        /// caller built it from the rank. And the coordinate came from a `Walk` over this very
        /// shape, so the bounds cannot fail either. Two impossible checks, one `catch
        /// unreachable`, on the hottest line in the library.
        ///
        /// This computes the offset and returns it. The remaining safety is `data[offset]`,
        /// which is where Zig puts it: a panic with a message in Debug and ReleaseSafe.
        ///
        /// **Use it where the rank is an invariant you have already established** - after a
        /// shape check, or inside a walk over the tensor's own shape. Use `flatIndex` where the
        /// coordinate came from outside and might be wrong.
        pub fn offsetOf(self: Self, indices: []const usize) usize {
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// Read from a rank-2 tensor. **Does not return an error.**
        ///
        /// WHY `at` HAS TO AND THIS DOES NOT
        ///
        /// `at(indices: []const usize)` can fail for two reasons. One is a real bounds check.
        /// The other is `indices.len != rank` - and that check exists ONLY because a slice hides
        /// its arity from the type system. Every call site writes a literal `&.{ i, j }`; the
        /// compiler could know it is two, and does not.
        ///
        /// Naming the arity in the function removes that half entirely. What remains is the
        /// bounds check, and **Zig already has a name for that**: it is what `data[idx]` does -
        /// a panic with a message and a stack trace in Debug and ReleaseSafe, the same UB as any
        /// other slice index in ReleaseFast.
        ///
        /// Nobody writes `arr[i] catch unreachable`. This file had **105 `catch unreachable`**,
        /// most of them on an `at` whose rank the author had checked three lines earlier, and
        /// that noise is the single biggest thing that makes numeric Zig look worse than it is.
        ///
        /// The safety is not weaker - it is Zig's, in the place Zig puts it.
        pub fn at2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-2 tensor. The mirror of `at2`, and error-free for the same reason.
        pub fn setAt2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Read from a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn at1(self: Self, i: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn setAt1(self: Self, i: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Write `value` at `indices`.
        pub fn setAt(self: Self, indices: []const usize, value: T) Error!void {
            self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)] = value;
        }

        /// Set every element to `value`.
        pub fn fill(self: Self, value: T) void {
            if (self.isContiguous()) {
                @memset(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], value);
                return;
            }
            var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var remaining: usize = self.size();
            while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
                self.data[self.offsetOf(walker[0..self.rank])] = value;
                advance(self.shape[0..self.rank], walker[0..self.rank]);
            }
        }

        /// The same storage seen as `shape`. Requires a contiguous tensor and an equal element
        /// count; both are checked rather than assumed.
        pub fn reshape(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (!self.isContiguous()) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != self.size()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // From `base`, not 0: reshaping a slice must keep addressing that slice.
            return fromSlice(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], shape);
        }

        /// Whether two distinct index positions address the same element, which happens exactly
        /// when a stretched axis (stride 0) has extent above 1.
        ///
        /// An operation that WRITES must refuse an aliased destination. Many indices landing on
        /// one element means the last write wins and every earlier one vanishes - a result that is
        /// wrong in a way no bounds check catches. `map` and `zip` check this so a caller who
        /// passes a broadcast view as an output gets an error rather than a plausible answer.
        pub fn isAliased(self: Self) bool {
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                if (self.shape[axis] > 1 and self.strides[axis] == 0) {
                    return true;
                }
            }
            return false;
        }

        /// A read-only view of this tensor stretched to `shape` by the broadcasting rule.
        ///
        /// BROADCASTING IS A STRIDE OF ZERO, NOT A COPY
        ///
        /// An axis whose extent is 1 is stretched by setting its stride to 0, so every position
        /// along it addresses the same element. No storage is allocated and no data moves; a
        /// `(3, 1, 5)` tensor viewed as `(3, 4, 5)` is the same 15 values read four times.
        ///
        /// **WRITING THROUGH A STRETCHED VIEW IS ALMOST CERTAINLY A MISTAKE.** Many indices
        /// alias one element, so the last write wins and the others vanish. This returns a view
        /// because that is what the type system has; treat it as an input.
        ///
        /// `isContiguous` reports false for any stretched axis with extent > 1, which is what
        /// stops a linear walk from reading the underlying 15 values as if they were 60.
        pub fn broadcastTo(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank or shape.len < self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = .{
                .data = self.data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                // Stretching does not move where the view starts.
                .base = self.base,
            };
            // Align at the TRAILING axis, as the broadcast rule does: an axis this tensor does not
            // have behaves as extent 1, and so does an axis it has with extent 1. Both get a
            // stride of 0.
            for (shape, 0..) |extent, axis| {
                const from_end: usize = shape.len - axis;
                view.shape[axis] = extent;
                if (self.rank < from_end) {
                    continue;
                }
                const own_axis: usize = self.rank - from_end;
                const own_extent: usize = self.shape[own_axis];
                if (own_extent == extent) {
                    view.strides[axis] = self.strides[own_axis];
                } else if (own_extent != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A window into this tensor: `len` positions along `axis`, starting at `start`.
        ///
        /// A SLICE IS AN OFFSET, WHICH IS WHY `base` EXISTS
        ///
        /// Nothing is copied: the view keeps every stride and simply starts `start` steps further
        /// into the data along one axis. A batch of 32 rows out of 4096 is a `Tensor` the same
        /// size as any other, addressing 32 rows of the same buffer.
        ///
        /// `start + len` must fit. Clamping instead would hand back a view SMALLER than asked
        /// for, and the caller's next loop would read the shape it requested rather than the one
        /// it got.
        pub fn slice(self: Self, axis: usize, start: usize, len: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (start + len > self.shape[axis]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            const step: isize = self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(start));
            const moved: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) + step;
            if (moved < 0) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            view.base = @intCast(moved);
            view.shape[axis] = len;
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with the axes reordered: `out` axis `i` is this tensor's axis `order[i]`.
        ///
        /// EVERY `manip` OPERATION HERE IS METADATA
        ///
        /// `permute`, `squeeze`, `unsqueeze`, `flatten` and `moveAxis` all return a view: they
        /// permute or insert entries in two fixed-size arrays and copy nothing. That is why they
        /// need no GPU kernel and no sweep row - **there is no arithmetic to disagree about.**
        /// The cost appears later, when a permuted view has to be BOUND to a kernel and must be
        /// densified first, which is the constraint recorded in section 10.3.
        ///
        /// `order` must be a permutation of `0..rank`. A repeated axis would produce a view
        /// where two indices alias, which is `isAliased`'s job to reject at write time - but
        /// catching it here names the actual mistake.
        pub fn permute(self: Self, order: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (order.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var seen: [max_rank]bool = @splat(false);
            for (order) |axis| {
                if (axis >= self.rank or seen[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                seen[axis] = true;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            for (order, 0..) |axis, i| {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[axis];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[axis];
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis removed.
        ///
        /// Removing an axis whose extent is NOT 1 would change how many elements the view
        /// addresses, so it is `ShapeMismatch` rather than a silent reshape.
        pub fn squeeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (self.shape[axis] != 1) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = axis;
            while (i + 1 < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i + 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i + 1];
            }
            view.shape[self.rank - 1] = 1;
            view.strides[self.rank - 1] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank - 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis inserted at `axis`. `axis == rank` appends.
        ///
        /// The new axis gets a stride of 0, which is harmless because its extent is 1 - a
        /// single position cannot step anywhere. `isAliased` only objects when extent exceeds 1.
        pub fn unsqueeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis > self.rank or self.rank + 1 > max_rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = self.rank;
            while (i > axis) : (i -= 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i - 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i - 1];
            }
            view.shape[axis] = 1;
            view.strides[axis] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank + 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A rank-1 view of the whole tensor. Contiguous only, for `reshape`'s reason.
        pub fn flatten(self: Self) Error!Self {
            return self.reshape(&.{self.size()});
        }

        /// A view with one axis moved from `from` to `to`, the others keeping their order.
        pub fn moveAxis(self: Self, from: usize, to: usize) Error!Self {
            if (from >= self.rank or to >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var order: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var k: usize = 0;
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                if (i == from) {
                    continue;
                }
                order[k] = i;
                k += 1;
            }
            // Open a slot at `to` and drop the moved axis into it.
            var j: usize = self.rank - 1;
            while (j > to) : (j -= 1) {
                order[j] = order[j - 1];
            }
            order[to] = from;
            return self.permute(order[0..self.rank]);
        }

        /// A view with axes `a` and `b` exchanged. Shares storage; changes through one are visible
        /// through the other.
        pub fn transpose(self: Self, a: usize, b: usize) Error!Self {
            if (a >= self.rank or b >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            view.shape[a] = self.shape[b];
            view.shape[b] = self.shape[a];
            view.strides[a] = self.strides[b];
            view.strides[b] = self.strides[a];
            return view;
        }
    };
}
linspace
/// `count` evenly spaced values from `start` to `stop`.
///
/// `endpoint` is required for the reason `Ddof` and `CodeOrder` are: both conventions are in
/// common use, and picking the wrong one gives an answer that is off by one element everywhere
/// without being obviously wrong anywhere.
pub fn linspace(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    start: T,
    stop: T,
    count: usize,
    endpoint: EndPoint,
) Error!Tensor(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{count});
    if (count == 0) {
        return out;
    }
    if (count == 1) {
        out.data[0] = start;
        return out;
    }
    const divisor: T = @floatFromInt(if (endpoint == .include) count - 1 else count);
    const step: T = (stop - start) / divisor;
    for (0..count) |i| {
        out.data[i] = start + @as(T, @floatFromInt(i)) * step;
    }
    // THE LAST VALUE IS SET EXACTLY, not computed. `start + (n-1) * step` is `stop` in real
    // arithmetic and a few ulps off in floating point, and a caller who asked for a range ending
    // at 1.0 and got 0.9999999999999999 has a bug report rather than a rounding lesson.
    if (endpoint == .include) {
        out.data[count - 1] = stop;
    }
    return out;
}
Dense
pub fn Dense(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        weight: Tensor(T),
        bias: Tensor(T),

        /// Allocate and initialise. `He` for a layer feeding a rectifier, `Xavier` otherwise.
        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            rng: Rng,
            fan_in: usize,
            fan_out: usize,
            scheme: InitScheme,
        ) Error!Self {
            const weight: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ fan_in, fan_out });
            const bias: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, fan_out });
            switch (scheme) {
                .xavier => try initXavier(T, weight, rng, fan_in, fan_out),
                .he => try initHe(T, weight, rng, fan_in),
            }
            // Biases start at zero. A random bias adds nothing a random weight does not already
            // provide, and a non-zero one shifts every unit's activation before any data is seen.
            bias.fill(0);
            return .{ .weight = weight, .bias = bias };
        }

        /// What `attach` hands back: the output, and handles for the two parameters.
        ///
        /// RETURNING ONLY THE OUTPUT IS NOT ENOUGH, which a first attempt at a training loop
        /// showed immediately: the loop needs the parameter handles to read their gradients, and
        /// with the layer holding no `Var` there is nowhere else to get them. Returning all three
        /// keeps the layer graph-agnostic AND gives the caller what a step requires.
        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            weight: Var,
            bias: Var,
        };

        /// Record `input @ weight + bias` onto `graph`.
        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var) Error!Attached {
            const w: Var = try graph.parameter(self.weight);
            const b: Var = try graph.parameter(self.bias);
            return .{
                .out = try graph.add(try graph.matmul(input, w), b),
                .weight = w,
                .bias = b,
            };
        }
    };
}
Graph
/// Reverse-mode automatic differentiation over a recorded tape.
///
/// EAGER FORWARD, RECORDED BACKWARD
///
/// Each operation computes its result immediately AND appends a node describing how to propagate
/// a gradient back through it. Computing eagerly means every shape is known when it is needed, so
/// the graph allocates outputs as it goes and no separate shape-inference pass exists to disagree
/// with the forward pass.
///
/// THE TAPE IS A LIST, NOT A GRAPH SEARCH. Nodes are appended in execution order, and a value
/// can only depend on values recorded before it, so walking the list backwards visits every
/// consumer of a value before the value itself. That is a topological order by construction -
/// no sort, no visited set, no cycle check.
///
/// Everything is allocated from one allocator, usually an arena. A graph has no
/// `deinit`: closing the scope releases the tape, the values and the gradients together, which is
/// the same ownership rule the rest of the library follows.
pub fn Graph(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// What a node needs to push a gradient from its output to its inputs.
        const Op = enum {
            leaf,
            add,
            sub_op,
            mul_op,
            matmul,
            tanh_op,
            relu_op,
            sigmoid_op,
            mse,
            cross_entropy,
            ppo_clip,
            softmax_op,
            layernorm_op,
            scale_op,
            dropout_op,
            transpose_op,
            exp_op,
            log_op,
            softplus_op,
            elu_op,
            slice_op,
            concat_op,
            conv2d_op,
            embedding_op,
        };

        const Node = struct {
            op: Op,
            /// Up to two inputs. Unused slots hold the node's own index and are ignored.
            lhs: Var = 0,
            rhs: Var = 0,
            /// `mse` records its target here; nothing else uses it.
            target: Var = 0,
            /// `cross_entropy` records its class indices here. A slice rather than a `Var`
            /// because labels are integers the caller already holds, not values the graph
            /// differentiates - putting them on the tape would imply a gradient that does not
            /// exist.
            labels: []const usize = &.{},
            /// `scale_op` keeps its factor here, `layernorm_op` its epsilon, `dropout_op` its
            /// keep-probability's reciprocal. One float, three meanings, each used by one op.
            scalar: T = 0,
            /// `dropout_op` keeps its mask as a value of the graph, so `recompute` reuses it and
            /// `checkGradient` sees the same function it is differentiating.
            mask: Var = 0,
            /// `conv2d_op` keeps `{ stride_y, stride_x, pad_y, pad_x }` here. A dedicated field
            /// rather than borrowing `labels`, which means class indices everywhere else - four
            /// words per node is cheap next to a reader wondering which meaning is in play.
            geometry: [4]usize = @splat(0),
            /// Constants a node needs in its BACKWARD that are not `Var`s.
            ///
            /// `ppo_clip` keeps the old log-probabilities and the advantages here: both are
            /// fixed for the step - the old policy by definition, the advantages because they
            /// were estimated before it - so neither is a tape node, and neither can be
            /// recomputed from the graph. The alternative was to overload `labels`, which is
            /// `[]const usize` and says "class indices"; a slot whose name is a lie costs more
            /// than a field.
            constants: []const T = &.{},
        };

        gpa: Allocator,
        values: std.ArrayList(Tensor(T)),
        grads: std.ArrayList(Tensor(T)),
        /// Whether a value accumulates a gradient. Constants do not, which keeps the backward
        /// pass from allocating and filling buffers nobody reads.
        wants_grad: std.ArrayList(bool),
        nodes: std.ArrayList(Node),

        pub fn init(gpa: Allocator) Self {
            return .{
                .gpa = gpa,
                .values = .empty,
                .grads = .empty,
                .wants_grad = .empty,
                .nodes = .empty,
            };
        }

        fn push(self: *Self, value: Tensor(T), node: Node, wants: bool) Error!Var {
            const shape: []const usize = value.shape[0..value.rank];
            const grad: Tensor(T) = if (wants) try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape) else value;
            if (wants) {
                grad.fill(0);
            }
            try self.values.append(self.gpa, value);
            try self.grads.append(self.gpa, grad);
            try self.wants_grad.append(self.gpa, wants);
            try self.nodes.append(self.gpa, node);
            return @intCast(self.values.items.len - 1);
        }

        /// A value the graph will differentiate with respect to.
        pub fn parameter(self: *Self, value: Tensor(T)) Error!Var {
            return self.push(value, .{ .op = .leaf }, true);
        }

        /// A value that is fixed: inputs, targets, anything not being learned.
        pub fn constant(self: *Self, value: Tensor(T)) Error!Var {
            return self.push(value, .{ .op = .leaf }, false);
        }

        /// The tensor behind a value.
        pub fn valueOf(self: *const Self, v: Var) Tensor(T) {
            return self.values.items[v];
        }

        /// The gradient accumulated into a value by `backward`.
        pub fn gradOf(self: *const Self, v: Var) Error!Tensor(T) {
            if (!self.wants_grad.items[v]) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            return self.grads.items[v];
        }

        /// `out = a + b`, with `b` broadcast to `a`'s shape.
        pub fn add(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            try zimrnum.add(T, out, va, self.values.items[b]);
            const wants: bool = self.wants_grad.items[a] or self.wants_grad.items[b];
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .add, .lhs = a, .rhs = b }, wants);
        }

        /// `out = a @ b`, rank 2.
        pub fn matmul(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const vb: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[b];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, &.{ va.shape[0], vb.shape[1] });
            try zimrnum.matmul(T, out, va, vb);
            const wants: bool = self.wants_grad.items[a] or self.wants_grad.items[b];
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .matmul, .lhs = a, .rhs = b }, wants);
        }

        /// `out = a - b`, elementwise, same shape.
        pub fn sub(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.binary(.sub_op, a, b);
        }

        /// `out = a * b`, elementwise, same shape.
        pub fn mul(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.binary(.mul_op, a, b);
        }

        fn binary(self: *Self, op: Op, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            const vb: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[b];
            switch (op) {
                .sub_op => try zimrnum.sub(T, out, va, vb),
                .mul_op => try zimrnum.mul(T, out, va, vb),
                else => unreachable,
            }
            const wants: bool = self.wants_grad.items[a] or self.wants_grad.items[b];
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = op, .lhs = a, .rhs = b }, wants);
        }

        /// `out = relu(a)`.
        pub fn relu(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.unary(.relu_op, a);
        }

        /// `out = sigmoid(a)`.
        pub fn sigmoid(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.unary(.sigmoid_op, a);
        }

        fn unary(self: *Self, op: Op, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            switch (op) {
                .relu_op => try zimrnum.relu(T, out, va),
                .sigmoid_op => try zimrnum.sigmoid(T, out, va),
                .tanh_op => try zimrnum.tanh(T, out, va),
                else => unreachable,
            }
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// `out = tanh(a)`.
        pub fn tanh(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            try zimrnum.tanh(T, out, va);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .tanh_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// Softmax cross-entropy of rank-2 logits against class indices, as a 1x1 value.
        ///
        /// The backward rule is `softmax(logits) - onehot`, over the row count - the pairing
        /// `exp(x)`, elementwise.
        ///
        /// THE LEVERAGED PAIR - AND WHY `softplus` STILL NEEDS ITS OWN NODE
        ///
        /// `exp` and `log` unlock compositions the tape could not express: `softplus`, `mish`,
        /// `logSumExp` as a differentiable reduction, anything with a ratio of exponentials.
        /// That is the `slice`/`concat` shape again - the primitive is worth more than the
        /// functions it is blocking.
        ///
        /// But `softplus(x) = log(1 + exp(x))` **written that way is wrong**, and the third node
        /// below exists because of it. Measured at f32: `exp(89)` is infinity, so the composition
        /// returns `inf` from x = 89 upward where the true answer is 89 - softplus is very nearly
        /// the identity for large x. **Having the primitives does not mean composing them is
        /// safe**, which is worth knowing before reaching for them.
        pub fn exp(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.exp(T, out, source);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .exp_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// The natural logarithm, elementwise. A nonpositive input gives `-inf` or `nan`, which
        /// is `@log`'s behaviour and is left alone: clamping here would hide the caller's bug.
        pub fn log(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.log(T, out, source);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .log_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// `log(1 + exp(x))`, computed so it neither overflows nor loses its tail.
        ///
        /// ITS OWN NODE, BECAUSE THE COMPOSITION IS NOT SAFE
        ///
        /// `log(1 + exp(x))` overflows at f32 from x = 89 and at f64 from about 710, returning
        /// `inf` where the answer is x. The tensor version uses `max(x, 0) + log1p(exp(-|x|))`,
        /// which is exact at both ends - see its own comment for the identity and for what the
        /// two earlier forms lost.
        ///
        /// **The backward is the reason this is cheap**: `d/dx softplus(x)` is exactly
        /// `sigmoid(x)` - no special case, no branch, and nothing to derive.
        pub fn softplus(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.softplus(T, out, source);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .softplus_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// ELU: `x` above zero, `alpha * (exp(x) - 1)` below.
        ///
        /// A REAL NODE, BECAUSE A BRANCH IS NOT A COMPOSITION
        ///
        /// `silu`, `gelu` and `mish` are all products and sums of nodes that already exist, so
        /// they needed nothing. ELU is not: it switches on the sign, and there is no elementwise
        /// `select` here to build that from. **This is the last activation without a gradient**,
        /// and closing it means one node rather than a fifth composition.
        ///
        /// The backward is `1` above zero and `alpha * exp(x)` below - which is `elu(x) + alpha`,
        /// so it could read the output. It reads the INPUT instead: the two agree mathematically
        /// and only the input says which side of the branch a value was on. At exactly zero both
        /// give `1`, so the kink is continuous in the derivative for `alpha = 1` and the test
        /// probes across it rather than around it.
        pub fn elu(self: *Self, a: Var, alpha: T) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.elu(T, out, source, alpha);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .elu_op, .lhs = a, .scalar = alpha },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// Mish: `x * tanh(softplus(x))`.
        ///
        /// A composition again, now that `softplus` is a node - which is what the pair was for.
        pub fn mish(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.mul(x, try self.tanh(try self.softplus(x)));
        }

        /// SiLU, also called swish: `x * sigmoid(x)`.
        ///
        /// ACTIVATIONS THE TAPE ALREADY CONTAINS, AND THE ONES IT DOES NOT
        ///
        /// `silu`, `gelu`, `softplus` and `elu` all exist at tensor level here and **none of them
        /// had a tape node**, so a network using any of them could be evaluated and not trained.
        /// That is a worse gap than a missing gradient function: the forward works, the loss
        /// falls for the layers that do have nodes, and the activation simply never learns.
        ///
        /// Two of the four are compositions of nodes that already exist, so they cost nothing and
        /// their gradients are the tape's:
        ///
        ///     silu(x) = x * sigmoid(x)
        ///     gelu(x) = 0.5x * (1 + tanh(0.7978845608 * (x + 0.044715 x^3)))
        ///
        /// The other two are not, and the plan records why rather than half-building them:
        /// `softplus` needs `log1p(exp(x))` and the tape has neither log nor exp; `elu` branches
        /// on the sign, which is not an elementwise composition of anything here.
        pub fn silu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.mul(x, try self.sigmoid(x));
        }

        /// GELU, in the tanh form every transformer uses.
        ///
        /// The exact definition is `x * Phi(x)` with `Phi` the normal CDF, which needs `erf`. The
        /// tanh approximation is what PyTorch, JAX and every published transformer actually run -
        /// it agrees to about 1e-3 and is the thing weights were trained against, so matching it
        /// matters more than matching the definition.
        pub fn gelu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            // `x * sigmoid(2u)`, NOT `0.5x * (1 + tanh(u))` - THE SAME FUNCTION, DERIVED
            //
            //     (1 + tanh u) / 2
            //   = (1 + (e^u - e^-u)/(e^u + e^-u)) / 2
            //   = (2 e^u / (e^u + e^-u)) / 2
            //   =  e^u / (e^u + e^-u)
            //   =    1 / (1 + e^-2u)
            //   =  sigmoid(2u)
            //
            // The first form needs a tensor of ones to add - **allocated on every call**, and on
            // a graph rebuilt each training step that is a per-step allocation for a constant.
            // The second needs none, and is two nodes shorter. zimrmath's own `gelu` already used
            // this identity; the tape's did not until it was read.
            //
            // `sqrt(2/pi)` is the 0.7978845608 in the published formula, doubled here because the
            // identity takes `2u`.
            const cubed: Var = try self.mul(x, try self.mul(x, x));
            const inner: Var = try self.add(x, try self.scale(cubed, 0.044715));
            return self.mul(x, try self.sigmoid(try self.scale(inner, 1.5957691216057308)));
        }

        /// A gated linear unit: the first half of the columns, gated by the second.
        ///
        /// NO BACKWARD - AND THAT IS THE POINT
        ///
        /// znum writes `gatedForward` and then a hand-derived `gatedBackward`, walking
        /// coordinates and applying the product rule by hand. Here the whole thing is
        ///
        ///     slice(x, 0, half) * sigmoid(slice(x, half, half))
        ///
        /// and every one of those is already a tape node, so **the gradient is the tape's**.
        /// There is no second derivation to keep in step with the first, and no chance of the
        /// pair disagreeing under maintenance.
        ///
        /// This is the third thing `graph.slice` and `graph.concat` paid for, after multi-head
        /// attention and the batched cartpole twin. The ops were built because their absence had
        /// shaped two designs; they keep shaping ones that come after.
        ///
        /// The last axis must be even - half the columns carry the value and half the gate.
        pub fn glu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.gateWith(x, .sigmoid);
        }

        /// SwiGLU: the same shape, gated by SiLU rather than sigmoid.
        ///
        /// The gate in every recent transformer feed-forward. `silu(v)` is `v * sigmoid(v)`, so
        /// this too is existing nodes and nothing new.
        pub fn swiglu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.gateWith(x, .silu);
        }

        /// Which gate `glu` and `swiglu` apply.
        pub const Gate = enum { sigmoid, silu };

        /// The shared body. Splitting once and gating once, with the gate chosen at comptime.
        fn gateWith(self: *Self, x: Var, comptime gate: Gate) Error!Var {
            const value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[x];
            if (value.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const width: usize = value.shape[1];
            if (width % 2 != 0 or width == 0) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const half: usize = width / 2;
            const carried: Var = try self.slice(x, 1, 0, half);
            const gating: Var = try self.slice(x, 1, half, half);
            const opened: Var = switch (gate) {
                .sigmoid => try self.sigmoid(gating),
                // SiLU is `v * sigmoid(v)` - two nodes, no new op.
                .silu => try self.mul(gating, try self.sigmoid(gating)),
            };
            return self.mul(carried, opened);
        }

        /// PPO's clipped objective, AS A LOSS - the version `ppoClipObjective` could not be.
        ///
        /// WHY THIS EXISTS: THE SCALAR ONE COMPUTES PPO AND CANNOT TRAIN WITH IT
        ///
        /// `ppoClipObjective` returns a `T`. It is correct, it is tested, and **it is not on the
        /// tape** - so it reports what the objective is worth and cannot produce a gradient. The
        /// library had PPO's arithmetic and not PPO's training step, and nothing said so until an
        /// end-to-end test tried to learn with it.
        ///
        /// THE CLIP IS THE WHOLE ALGORITHM, AND IT LIVES IN THE BACKWARD
        ///
        /// The forward takes a minimum, which looks like a detail. The gradient is where it acts:
        /// **when the clip is the smaller branch, the gradient is ZERO** - the update stops
        /// pushing a ratio that has already moved too far. That is what stops a policy step from
        /// destroying the policy, and it is the reason PPO works where plain policy gradient
        /// diverges.
        ///
        /// `logp_old` and `advantages` are constants of the step: the old policy is fixed by
        /// definition and the advantages were estimated before it. Only `logp_new` carries a
        /// gradient, which is why it is the only `Var`.
        ///
        /// Returns the NEGATED mean, so it is a loss to descend rather than an objective to
        /// ascend - every other `*Loss` here descends, and one that did not would be a trap.
        pub fn ppoClipLoss(
            self: *Self,
            logp_new: Var,
            logp_old: []const T,
            advantages: []const T,
            clip: T,
        ) Error!Var {
            const fresh: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[logp_new];
            if (fresh.size() != logp_old.len or logp_old.len != advantages.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            if (clip <= 0 or fresh.size() == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, &.{1});
            out.data[0] = -(try ppoClipObjective(T, fresh.data, logp_old, advantages, clip));
            // `labels` is the node's spare slot for caller data; the two constant slices ride
            // there as one allocation so the backward can recompute the branch.
            const kept: []T = try self.gpa.alloc(T, logp_old.len * 2);
            @memcpy(kept[0..logp_old.len], logp_old);
            @memcpy(kept[logp_old.len..], advantages);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .ppo_clip, .lhs = logp_new, .scalar = clip, .constants = kept },
                self.wants_grad.items[logp_new],
            );
        }

        /// that makes the two functions' derivatives compose into a subtraction.
        pub fn crossEntropy(self: *Self, logits: Var, labels: []const usize) Error!Var {
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, &.{ 1, 1 });
            out.data[0] = try crossEntropyRows(T, self.values.items[logits], labels);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .cross_entropy, .lhs = logits, .labels = labels },
                self.wants_grad.items[logits],
            );
        }

        /// Row-wise softmax of a rank-2 value.
        pub fn softmaxRows(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, va.shape[0..va.rank]);
            try zimrnum.softmaxRows(T, out, va);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .softmax_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// Row-wise layer normalisation of a rank-2 value.
        pub fn layerNormRows(self: *Self, a: Var, epsilon: T) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, va.shape[0..va.rank]);
            try zimrnum.layerNormRows(T, out, va, epsilon);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .layernorm_op, .lhs = a, .scalar = epsilon },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// 2-D convolution of a rank-2 value with a learned kernel.
        ///
        /// THE BACKWARD PASS IS THE FORWARD LOOP WITH THE ACCUMULATION REVERSED
        ///
        /// Forward, each output reads a window: `y[i][j] += x[i*s+a-p][j*s+b-p] * k[a][b]`. Both
        /// gradients fall out of that one line by moving what is read and what is written:
        ///
        /// dx[i*s+a-p][j*s+b-p] += g[i][j] * k[a][b]
        /// dk[a][b]             += g[i][j] * x[i*s+a-p][j*s+b-p]
        ///
        /// So the backward rule is the SAME loop nest with the same bounds and the same
        /// in-range test - which is why it is written as one pass producing both, rather than as
        /// the "full correlation with a flipped kernel" the textbooks give. That form is correct
        /// and is a second thing to get right; this one cannot disagree with the forward pass
        /// about which taps are in range, because it asks the identical question.
        pub fn conv2d(
            self: *Self,
            input: Var,
            kernel: Var,
            stride: [2]usize,
            pad: [2]usize,
        ) Error!Var {
            const x: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[input];
            const k: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[kernel];
            if (x.rank != 2 or k.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            if (stride[0] == 0 or stride[1] == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const out_h: usize = (x.shape[0] + 2 * pad[0] - k.shape[0]) / stride[0] + 1;
            const out_w: usize = (x.shape[1] + 2 * pad[1] - k.shape[1]) / stride[1] + 1;
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, &.{ out_h, out_w });
            try zimrnum.conv2d(T, out, x, k, stride, pad);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{
                    .op = .conv2d_op,
                    .lhs = input,
                    .rhs = kernel,
                    .geometry = .{ stride[0], stride[1], pad[0], pad[1] },
                },
                self.wants_grad.items[input] or self.wants_grad.items[kernel],
            );
        }

        /// A contiguous run along one axis, as a differentiable operation.
        ///
        /// THE VIEW ALREADY EXISTS; WHAT IS NEW IS THE GRADIENT
        ///
        /// `Tensor.slice` gives a strided view for free and always has. What it cannot do is
        /// carry a gradient, so anything that slices a computed value has been out of reach:
        /// multi-head attention splitting a projection, an LSTM slicing four fused gates out of
        /// one matmul. **Both designs were shaped by this absence** - each said "stated rather
        /// than half-built" and moved on. znum has no differentiable slice either.
        ///
        /// The backward is a scatter: the gradient lands in the rows the slice took and every
        /// other row gets zero, because a row nobody read cannot have influenced anything.
        pub fn slice(self: *Self, a: Var, axis: usize, start: usize, len: usize) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            if (axis >= source.rank or start + len > source.shape[axis]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var shape: [max_rank]usize = source.shape;
            shape[axis] = len;
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape[0..source.rank]);
            try materialise(T, out, try source.slice(axis, start, len));
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .slice_op, .lhs = a, .geometry = .{ axis, start, len, 0 } },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// Two values joined along one axis, as a differentiable operation.
        ///
        /// The backward is the slice's, read in the other direction: each input gets back the
        /// part of the gradient that came from its own rows.
        pub fn concat(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var, axis: usize) Error!Var {
            const first: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const second: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[b];
            if (first.rank != second.rank or axis >= first.rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var shape: [max_rank]usize = first.shape;
            shape[axis] = first.shape[axis] + second.shape[axis];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape[0..first.rank]);
            try zimrnum.concat(T, out, first, second, axis);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{
                    .op = .concat_op,
                    .lhs = a,
                    .rhs = b,
                    .geometry = .{ axis, first.shape[axis], 0, 0 },
                },
                self.wants_grad.items[a] or self.wants_grad.items[b],
            );
        }

        /// Gather rows of a table by index: `out[i] = table[rows[i]]`.
        ///
        /// `rows` reuses the node's `labels` field, and here that is not a borrow - an
        /// embedding's indices ARE the same kind of thing as a classifier's labels: positions
        /// into a first axis, with no gradient of their own.
        ///
        /// The gradient is a SCATTER-ADD, and `+=` is the whole point: a token appearing three
        /// times in a batch must accumulate three contributions into one row of the table. A
        /// scatter that assigned would train on the last occurrence and silently discard the
        /// rest.
        pub fn embedding(self: *Self, table: Var, rows: []const usize) Error!Var {
            const t: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[table];
            if (t.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            for (rows) |r| {
                if (r >= t.shape[0]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
            }
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows.len, t.shape[1] });
            try gatherRows(T, out, t, rows);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .embedding_op, .lhs = table, .labels = rows },
                self.wants_grad.items[table],
            );
        }

        /// The transpose of a rank-2 value, MATERIALISED.
        ///
        /// A view would be free on the CPU, but a value of the graph must be a tensor a later
        /// `matmul` can read and a later `recompute` can overwrite in place - and a transposed
        /// view of another value's storage is neither. The copy is the price of the tape owning
        /// every value it records. Its gradient is the transpose of the incoming one.
        pub fn transpose(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            if (va.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, &.{ va.shape[1], va.shape[0] });
            try materialise(T, out, try va.transpose(0, 1));
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .transpose_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// `out = a * factor`.
        pub fn scale(self: *Self, a: Var, factor: T) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, va.shape[0..va.rank]);
            try zimrnum.scale(T, out, va, factor);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .scale_op, .lhs = a, .scalar = factor },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// Inverted dropout: each element is kept with probability `1 - rate` and scaled by
        /// `1 / (1 - rate)`, so the expected value is unchanged.
        ///
        /// THE MASK IS DRAWN ONCE AND STORED
        ///
        /// A dropout that redrew its mask on every `recompute` would be a different function each
        /// time - `checkGradient` would compare a derivative against a finite difference of
        /// something else and report noise. The mask is a value of the graph, fixed until
        /// `resampleDropout` is called, which a training loop does once per step.
        ///
        /// Inverted scaling means nothing changes at inference: dropping the operation from the
        /// graph is exactly equivalent to `rate = 0`, with no `1 - rate` factor to remember.
        pub fn dropout(self: *Self, a: Var, rate: T, rng: Rng) Error!Var {
            if (rate < 0 or rate >= 1) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const mask: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            const keep: T = 1 - rate;
            fillDropoutMask(T, mask, rng, keep);
            const mask_var: Var = try self.constant(mask);
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            try zimrnum.mul(T, out, va, mask);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .dropout_op, .lhs = a, .mask = mask_var, .scalar = keep },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// Draw fresh masks for every dropout node, from a new generator.
        pub fn resampleDropout(self: *Self, rng: Rng) void {
            for (self.nodes.items) |node| {
                if (node.op == .dropout_op) {
                    fillDropoutMask(T, self.values.items[node.mask], rng, node.scalar);
                }
            }
        }

        /// Mean squared error against `target`, as a 1x1 value so it is a `Var` like any other.
        pub fn mseLoss(self: *Self, a: Var, target: Var) Error!Var {
            const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, &.{ 1, 1 });
            out.data[0] = try zimrnum.mseLoss(T, self.values.items[a], self.values.items[target]);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .mse, .lhs = a, .target = target },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// Propagate a gradient of 1 from `loss` back to every parameter.
        ///
        /// THE SEED IS 1, AND THAT IS WHY `loss` MUST BE A SINGLE VALUE. Differentiating a
        /// tensor with respect to parameters gives a Jacobian, not a gradient; reverse mode
        /// answers "how does THIS ONE NUMBER change", so the entry point takes a scalar and
        /// seeds it with its own derivative with respect to itself.
        pub fn backward(self: *Self, loss: Var) Error!void {
            if (self.values.items[loss].size() != 1) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            for (self.grads.items, self.wants_grad.items) |g, wants| {
                if (wants) {
                    g.fill(0);
                }
            }
            self.grads.items[loss].fill(1);

            var i: usize = self.nodes.items.len;
            while (i > 0) {
                i -= 1;
                if (!self.wants_grad.items[i]) {
                    continue;
                }
                const node: Node = self.nodes.items[i];
                const g: Tensor(T) = self.grads.items[i];
                switch (node.op) {
                    .leaf => {},
                    .add => {
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, g);
                    },
                    .sub_op => {
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, g);
                        const shape: []const usize = g.shape[0..g.rank];
                        const negated: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
                        try neg(T, negated, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, negated);
                    },
                    .mul_op => {
                        // Each side's gradient is the OTHER side's value times the incoming one.
                        const shape: []const usize = g.shape[0..g.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, scratch, g, self.values.items[node.rhs]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                        const other: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, other, g, self.values.items[node.lhs]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, other);
                    },
                    .relu_op => {
                        // reluGrad takes the forward INPUT, which is what `lhs` holds.
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const shape: []const usize = in.shape[0..in.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
                        try reluGrad(T, scratch, in, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .sigmoid_op => {
                        // sigmoidGrad takes the forward OUTPUT, which is this node's value.
                        const out: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
                        try sigmoidGrad(T, scratch, out, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .cross_entropy => {
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const shape: []const usize = in.shape[0..in.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
                        try crossEntropyRowsGrad(T, scratch, in, node.labels);
                        try zimrnum.scale(T, scratch, scratch, g.data[g.base]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .ppo_clip => {
                        // THE CLIP ACTS HERE, NOT IN THE FORWARD
                        //
                        // Where the clipped branch is the smaller one, the objective no longer
                        // depends on the ratio - so **the gradient is exactly zero** and the
                        // update stops pushing a ratio that has already moved too far. That is
                        // what keeps a policy step from destroying the policy.
                        //
                        // Elsewhere the objective is `ratio * advantage` and `d ratio/d logp` is
                        // `ratio`, so the derivative is `ratio * advantage`. The mean divides by
                        // the count, and the loss is negated, so both appear here too.
                        const fresh: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const n: usize = fresh.size();
                        const old: []const T = node.constants[0..n];
                        const advantages: []const T = node.constants[n..];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                            self.gpa,
                            fresh.shape[0..fresh.rank],
                        );
                        const low: T = 1 - node.scalar;
                        const high: T = 1 + node.scalar;
                        const seed: T = g.data[g.base] / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
                        for (0..n) |k| {
                            const ratio: T = @exp(fresh.data[k] - old[k]);
                            const unclipped: T = ratio * advantages[k];
                            const clipped: T = scalarClamp(ratio, low, high) * advantages[k];
                            // A zero where the clip won: the objective is flat in `ratio` there.
                            const active: bool = unclipped <= clipped;
                            scratch.data[k] = if (active) -seed * ratio * advantages[k] else 0;
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .softmax_op => {
                        // dx = y * (g - sum(g * y)) per row: the Jacobian of a softmax applied
                        // to g, without forming it.
                        const y: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, y.shape[0..y.rank]);
                        try softmaxBackward(T, scratch, y, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .layernorm_op => {
                        const y: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const x: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, y.shape[0..y.rank]);
                        try layerNormBackward(T, scratch, x, y, g, node.scalar);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .conv2d_op => {
                        const x: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const k: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.rhs];
                        const dx: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, x.shape[0..2]);
                        const dk: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, k.shape[0..2]);
                        dx.fill(0);
                        dk.fill(0);
                        try conv2dBackward(T, dx, dk, x, k, g, node.geometry);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, dx);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, dk);
                    },
                    .embedding_op => {
                        const t: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const dt: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, t.shape[0..2]);
                        dt.fill(0);
                        try scatterAddRows(T, dt, g, node.labels);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, dt);
                    },
                    .transpose_op => {
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, in.shape[0..in.rank]);
                        try materialise(T, scratch, try g.transpose(0, 1));
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .exp_op => {
                        // `d/dx exp(x)` is `exp(x)` - the value already computed, so the
                        // backward reads the forward's output rather than recomputing it.
                        const value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                            self.gpa,
                            value.shape[0..value.rank],
                        );
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, scratch, g, value);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .log_op => {
                        // `d/dx log(x)` is `1/x`, and `x` is the INPUT - the one case here where
                        // the output is not enough.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                            self.gpa,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
