                        try zimrnum.div(T, scratch, g, source);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .softplus_op => {
                        // `d/dx softplus(x)` is exactly `sigmoid(x)` - no branch and nothing to
                        // derive, which is what makes a dedicated node cheap despite the stable
                        // forward being more than one line.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                            self.gpa,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
                        try zimrnum.sigmoid(T, scratch, source);
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, scratch, g, scratch);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .elu_op => {
                        // `1` above zero, `alpha * exp(x)` below - read from the INPUT, because
                        // only the input says which side of the branch a value was on.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                            self.gpa,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
                        var walk: Walk = .over(source.shape[0..source.rank]);
                        while (walk.next()) |at| {
                            const x: T = try source.at(at);
                            const slope: T = if (x > 0) 1 else node.scalar * @exp(x);
                            try scratch.setAt(at, (try g.at(at)) * slope);
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .slice_op => {
                        // A SCATTER: the gradient lands where the slice read, and every other
                        // row gets zero - a row nobody read influenced nothing.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                            self.gpa,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
                        scratch.fill(0);
                        const window: Tensor(T) = try scratch.slice(
                            node.geometry[0],
                            node.geometry[1],
                            node.geometry[2],
                        );
                        var walk: Walk = .over(g.shape[0..g.rank]);
                        while (walk.next()) |at| {
                            try window.setAt(at, try g.at(at));
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .concat_op => {
                        // The slice's backward read the other way: each input takes back the
                        // part of the gradient that came from its own rows.
                        const axis: usize = node.geometry[0];
                        const split: usize = node.geometry[1];
                        const left: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const right: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.rhs];
                        const left_grad: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                            self.gpa,
                            left.shape[0..left.rank],
                        );
                        try materialise(T, left_grad, try g.slice(axis, 0, split));
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, left_grad);
                        const right_grad: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                            self.gpa,
                            right.shape[0..right.rank],
                        );
                        try materialise(T, right_grad, try g.slice(axis, split, right.shape[axis]));
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, right_grad);
                    },
                    .scale_op => {
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, g.shape[0..g.rank]);
                        try zimrnum.scale(T, scratch, g, node.scalar);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .dropout_op => {
                        // The same mask that scaled the forward pass scales the gradient.
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, g.shape[0..g.rank]);
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, scratch, g, self.values.items[node.mask]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .matmul => try self.matmulBackward(node, g),
                    .tanh_op => {
                        const out: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
                        try tanhGrad(T, scratch, out, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .mse => {
                        const a: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape);
                        try zimrnum.sub(T, scratch, a, self.values.items[node.target]);
                        const n: T = @floatFromInt(a.size());
                        try zimrnum.scale(T, scratch, scratch, 2 * g.data[g.base] / n);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                }
            }
        }

        /// Replay the recorded operations forward, refreshing every computed value.
        ///
        /// WHAT MAKES THE TAPE A MODEL RATHER THAN A ONE-SHOT
        ///
        /// Without this a graph is single-use: a training loop would have to rebuild it every
        /// step, allocating the whole tape again for a forward pass that differs only in the
        /// numbers. Replaying instead writes new values into the buffers already allocated.
        ///
        /// It is also what makes `checkGradient` possible. Verifying a derivative numerically
        /// means evaluating the SAME function at a perturbed input, and "the same function" is
        /// exactly what the tape is a record of.
        ///
        /// Leaves are skipped: their values are whatever the caller last wrote, which is how a
        /// new batch of inputs enters an existing graph.
        pub fn recompute(self: *Self) Error!void {
            for (self.nodes.items, 0..) |node, i| {
                const out: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                switch (node.op) {
                    .leaf => {},
                    .add => try zimrnum.add(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .sub_op => try zimrnum.sub(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .mul_op => try zimrnum.mul(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .matmul => try zimrnum.matmul(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .tanh_op => try zimrnum.tanh(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .relu_op => try zimrnum.relu(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .sigmoid_op => try zimrnum.sigmoid(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .softmax_op => try zimrnum.softmaxRows(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .layernorm_op => try zimrnum.layerNormRows(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        node.scalar,
                    ),
                    .conv2d_op => try zimrnum.conv2d(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                        .{ node.geometry[0], node.geometry[1] },
                        .{ node.geometry[2], node.geometry[3] },
                    ),
                    .embedding_op => try gatherRows(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        node.labels,
                    ),
                    .transpose_op => try materialise(
                        T,
                        out,
                        try self.values.items[node.lhs].transpose(0, 1),
                    ),
                    .exp_op => try zimrnum.exp(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .log_op => try zimrnum.log(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .softplus_op => try zimrnum.softplus(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .elu_op => try zimrnum.elu(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs], node.scalar),
                    .slice_op => try materialise(T, out, try self.values.items[node.lhs].slice(
                        node.geometry[0],
                        node.geometry[1],
                        node.geometry[2],
                    )),
                    .concat_op => try zimrnum.concat(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                        node.geometry[0],
                    ),
                    .scale_op => try zimrnum.scale(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs], node.scalar),
                    .dropout_op => try zimrnum.mul(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.mask],
                    ),
                    .cross_entropy => out.data[out.base] = try crossEntropyRows(
                        T,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        node.labels,
                    ),
                    .ppo_clip => {
                        const fresh: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const half: usize = fresh.size();
                        out.data[out.base] = -(try ppoClipObjective(
                            T,
                            fresh.data,
                            node.constants[0..half],
                            node.constants[half..],
                            node.scalar,
                        ));
                    },
                    .mse => out.data[out.base] = try zimrnum.mseLoss(
                        T,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.target],
                    ),
                }
            }
        }

        /// The largest disagreement between the tape's gradient for `param` and a central finite
        /// difference of `loss`, over every element of `param`.
        ///
        /// THE ONE CHECK THAT DOES NOT REPEAT THE DERIVATION
        ///
        /// A hand-written backward pass and a tape rule can both be wrong in the same way,
        /// because the same person derived them. A finite difference does not know the
        /// derivative at all - it only evaluates the function twice - so agreement between the
        /// two is evidence about the derivative rather than about the deriver.
        ///
        /// CENTRAL, NOT FORWARD. `(f(x+h) - f(x-h)) / 2h` has error O(h^2) where
        /// `(f(x+h) - f(x)) / h` has O(h); at a usable `h` that is the difference between
        /// agreeing to six digits and to three, which decides whether a real error is visible
        /// above the noise.
        ///
        /// Every value is restored and the graph recomputed before returning, so calling this
        /// leaves the model exactly as it found it.
        pub fn checkGradient(self: *Self, loss: Var, param: Var, h: T) Error!T {
            comptime requireFloat(T);
            if (h <= 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            try self.recompute();
            try self.backward(loss);
            const analytic: Tensor(T) = try self.gradOf(param);
            const value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[param];
            const scratch: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                self.gpa,
                analytic.shape[0..analytic.rank],
            );
            try materialise(T, scratch, analytic);

            var worst: T = 0;
            var index: usize = 0;
            while (index < value.size()) : (index += 1) {
                const at: usize = value.base + index;
                const original: T = value.data[at];
                value.data[at] = original + h;
                try self.recompute();
                const up: T = self.values.items[loss].data[self.values.items[loss].base];
                value.data[at] = original - h;
                try self.recompute();
                const down: T = self.values.items[loss].data[self.values.items[loss].base];
                value.data[at] = original;
                const numeric: T = (up - down) / (2 * h);
                worst = @max(worst, @abs(numeric - scratch.data[scratch.base + index]));
            }
            try self.recompute();
            return worst;
        }

        /// `grad[v] += contribution`, summing over any axis `v` was broadcast along.
        ///
        /// THE SUM IS WHAT MAKES BROADCASTING DIFFERENTIABLE. A bias of shape (1, n) added to
        /// (m, n) is used m times in the forward pass, so its gradient is the sum of m
        /// contributions. Adding the raw gradient instead would under-count by a factor of m and
        /// produce a bias that learns m times too slowly - a bug that leaves the network training,
        /// just badly, which is the hardest kind to notice.
        fn accumulate(self: *Self, v: Var, contribution: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
            if (!self.wants_grad.items[v]) {
                return;
            }
            const dest: Tensor(T) = self.grads.items[v];
            if (dest.size() == contribution.size()) {
                return zimrnum.add(T, dest, dest, contribution);
            }
            // SUM OVER EVERY BROADCAST AXIS, NOT JUST THE LEADING ONE
            //
            // The first version summed rows only, which is right for a `(1, width)` bias under a
            // `(rows, width)` gradient - `Dense`'s case, and the only one that existed. A `(1, 1)`
            // bias broadcasting over BOTH axes, which `Conv2d` has, took column 0 and **silently
            // dropped the rest**: a gradient wrong by a factor of the width, reported as a
            // number rather than an error. The finite-difference check caught it at 0.7 where
            // the kernel and input were at 8e-12.
            //
            // An axis of the destination that is 1 where the contribution is larger is a
            // broadcast axis and is summed; an axis that matches is walked. Any other pairing is
            // a shape error, which is what the first version should have returned.
            if (dest.rank != contribution.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < dest.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                const same: bool = dest.shape[axis] == contribution.shape[axis];
                if (!same and dest.shape[axis] != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var target: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            const shape: []const usize = contribution.shape[0..contribution.rank];
            var remaining: usize = contribution.size();
            while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
                axis = 0;
                while (axis < dest.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                    target[axis] = if (dest.shape[axis] == 1) 0 else walker[axis];
                }
                const at: []const usize = target[0..dest.rank];
                const slot: usize = dest.offsetOf(at);
                dest.data[slot] += contribution.data[contribution.offsetOf(walker[0..contribution.rank])];
                advance(shape, walker[0..contribution.rank]);
            }
        }

        /// `dA = g @ BT` and `dB = AT @ g`.
        fn matmulBackward(self: *Self, node: Node, g: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
            const vb: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.rhs];
            if (self.wants_grad.items[node.lhs]) {
                const da: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                    self.gpa,
                    &.{ va.shape[0], va.shape[1] },
                );
                try zimrnum.matmul(T, da, g, try vb.transpose(0, 1));
                try self.accumulate(node.lhs, da);
            }
            if (self.wants_grad.items[node.rhs]) {
                const db: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(
                    self.gpa,
                    &.{ vb.shape[0], vb.shape[1] },
                );
                try zimrnum.matmul(T, db, try va.transpose(0, 1), g);
                try self.accumulate(node.rhs, db);
            }
        }
    };
}
Var
/// A handle to a value inside a `Graph`. An index, not a pointer.
///
/// WHY VALUES ARE INDICES AND NOT TENSORS
///
/// The obvious design keys gradients off the tensor itself - its `data.ptr`, say. That breaks
/// immediately here, because a view SHARES `data.ptr` with its parent: `w` and
/// `w.transpose(0,1)` would be the same key while being different values in the graph. Worse, a
/// tensor is copied by value throughout this library, so there is no stable address to key on at
/// all.
///
/// An index into the graph's own arrays sidesteps both. The graph owns the correspondence between
/// a value, its tensor, and its gradient, and nothing about `Tensor` has to change to support it.
pub const Var = u32;

Running it prints:

  loss 9.4206 -> 0.0070
  slope 3.054 (true 3.0), intercept 1.955 (true 2.0)

Five things in that program are worth naming, each covered in full later:

LineWhat it isSection
zn.linspace(f64, gpa, 0, 1, 32, .include)a tensor, built4.6
zn.Dense(f64).init(...)a layer, owning its parameters11.9
zn.Graph(f64).init(gpa)a fresh tape, once per step17.2
graph.backward(loss)gradients for everything reachable17.2
w.* -= 0.1 * gthe update, written out11.7

The tape is rebuilt every step. That is deliberate and cheap: it is an arena of nodes, and discarding it is one pointer reset. Reusing a tape across steps would accumulate the whole history of training.

The update is written by hand here to show what an optimiser does. zn.adamWStep and its family replace those four lines once the model has more than two parameters.

3.2 Importing and testing#

Inside the zimr tree the module is named zn. A module that wants it declares the import in build.zig the same way it declares zm.

const zn = @import("zn");
source .zenv.sh

zig build test-fast -Dtest-filter=zn.    # zimrnum's tests only
zig build test-fast                      # the whole shader-free tier
zig build check                          # lint, shader corpus, regression gates

3.3 A first example#

Draw a thousand standard normal values and report their mean and variance.

const zn = @import("zn");

var sample: [1000]f64 = undefined;
const rng: zn.Rng = zn.Rng.init(20260904);
rng.fillNormal(f64, &sample);

const t = try zn.Tensor(f64).fromSlice(&sample, &.{1000});
const mean = try zn.meanAll(f64, t);
const spread = try zn.variance(f64, t, .sample);

std.debug.assert(zn.approxEqAbs(f64, 0.0, mean, 0.1));
std.debug.assert(zn.approxEqAbs(f64, 1.0, spread, 0.1));
Rng
/// A reproducible random source addressed by (seed, index) rather than by mutation.
///
/// WHY COUNTER-BASED, AND NOT A STATEFUL GENERATOR
///
/// A classic PRNG carries state and advances it, so drawing the i-th value means drawing the
/// first i. That is fine on one CPU thread and useless everywhere else this library is going:
///
/// * **A GPU kernel has no shared state.** Thread `i` must be able to compute ITS value without
/// any other thread having run. A counter-based generator is a pure function of the index, so
/// the kernel is `value(seed, thread_id)` and needs no sequencing at all.
/// * **CPU and GPU must agree EXACTLY.** The CPU path is this library's oracle (plan section 5 rung
/// 1); an oracle that produces a different random stream than the thing it checks is not an
/// oracle. Same function, same integers, same answer.
/// * **A failing case is reproducible from two numbers.** "seed 7, index 4096" is the whole
/// state, so a bad draw can be re-created in a unit test without replaying a sequence.
///
/// THE MIXER IS DELIBERATELY MODEST AND THE TESTS SAY SO. `mix32` is the murmur3 finalizer -
/// a bijection with good avalanche, applied to `index` folded with `seed`. It is NOT a
/// cryptographic generator and NOT claimed to pass a full statistical battery; what it is pinned
/// on is the properties this library actually relies on: determinism, range, stream independence,
/// and first/second moments. A stronger generator can replace the body without touching a single
/// call site, precisely because the interface is (seed, index) rather than a stream.
pub const Rng = struct {
    seed: u32,

    /// A generator for a given seed. Two `Rng`s with the same seed are the same function.
    pub fn init(seed: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = seed };
    }

    /// A DERIVED generator, so one seed can feed several independent uses without the caller
    /// inventing seeds. `parent.split(0)` and `parent.split(1)` are unrelated streams.
    ///
    /// Mixing the label rather than adding it: `seed + 1` and `seed + 2` are adjacent inputs to
    /// the mixer, and adjacency is exactly what a hash is asked not to preserve. Passing the
    /// label through `mix32` first means the derived seeds are as far apart as any other pair.
    pub fn split(self: Rng, label: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = mix32(self.seed ^ mix32(label ^ 0x9e3779b9)) };
    }

    /// The number of mantissa bits `T` can hold, including the implicit leading one. This is how
    /// many random bits a draw in `[0, 1)` may use: fewer wastes range, MORE ROUNDS UP TO 1.0 and
    /// breaks the half-open interval.
    fn mantissaWidth(comptime T: type) u32 {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return switch (T) {
            f16 => 11,
            f32 => 24,
            f64 => 53,
            else => unreachable,
        };
    }

    /// One 32-bit word of the generator's output at `index`, from independent stream `stream`.
    ///
    /// EACH KIND OF DRAW OWNS A STREAM, SO KINDS DO NOT INTERFERE
    ///
    /// Without this, every method would start from `bits(index)` and drawing two different KINDS
    /// at one index would return values that determine each other - `intBelow(i, n)` would be a
    /// function of `unitFloat(i)`. A caller has no reason to expect that and every reason to be
    /// hurt by it, so the stream is part of the addressing rather than the caller's problem.
    ///
    /// The stream index is folded into the MIXER, not into the index. Folding it into the index
    /// (`bits(index ^ K)`) makes indices `i` and `i ^ K` draw from the same two words with their
    /// roles swapped, so those pairs are not independent - which is exactly the defect this
    /// replaced.
    ///
    /// `stream == 0` adds nothing, so `bits` is unchanged by the introduction of rounds.
    fn word(self: Rng, index: u32, stream: u32) u32 {
        return mix32(mix32(index *% 0x9e3779b9) ^ self.seed ^ (stream *% 0x85ebca6b));
    }

    /// The raw 32-bit draw at `index`, stream 0. Distinct from every other kind of draw.
    pub fn bits(self: Rng, index: u32) u32 {
        return self.word(index, 0);
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)` from stream `stream` (and `stream + 1` when `T` needs more than 32 bits).
    ///
    /// Exactly `mantissaWidth(T)` random bits, scaled by 2^-width. The largest result is
    /// (2^width - 1) / 2^width, which is the largest `T` strictly below one - so the interval is
    /// half-open for every width, including `f16`, where using 24 bits rounds to 1.0 instead.
    fn unitFloatRound(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32, comptime stream: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const width: u32 = comptime mantissaWidth(T);
        const first: u64 = self.word(index, stream);
        const mantissa: u64 = if (comptime width <= 32)
            first >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(32 - width))
        else
            (first << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width - 32))) |
                (@as(u64, self.word(index, stream + 1)) >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(64 - width)));
        const numerator: T = @floatFromInt(mantissa);
        const denominator: T = @floatFromInt(@as(u64, 1) << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width)));
        return numerator / denominator;
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn unitFloat(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        return self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 1);
    }

    /// A float in `[low, high)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn uniform(
        self: Rng,
        comptime T: type,
        index: u32,
        low: T,
        high: T,
    ) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return low + (high - low) * self.unitFloat(T, index);
    }

    /// A standard normal draw (mean 0, variance 1) at `index`, by the Box-Muller transform.
    ///
    /// ITS TWO UNIFORMS COME FROM ITS OWN STREAMS (3-6), not from the uniform stream, so
    /// `normal(i)` and `unitFloat(i)` are independent and a caller mixing them needs no care.
    ///
    /// Box-Muller rather than a ziggurat because a ziggurat REJECTS: the number of uniforms it
    /// consumes varies per sample, which would make the value at `index` depend on how many
    /// rejections happened. That is not a pure function of the index and would not survive being
    /// evaluated on an independent GPU thread. Box-Muller consumes exactly two, always.
    ///
    /// The radius draw is taken from `(0, 1]` rather than `[0, 1)` because `@log(0)` is -inf.
    pub fn normal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const two_pi: T = 6.283185307179586;
        const radius_draw: T = 1.0 - self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 3);
        const angle_draw: T = self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 5);
        const radius: T = @sqrt(-2.0 * @log(radius_draw));
        return radius * @cos(two_pi * angle_draw);
    }

    /// An integer in `[0, bound)`, drawn at `index` from its own stream. `bound == 0` yields 0.
    ///
    /// Lemire's multiply-shift, not `% bound`: the modulo is biased toward small values whenever
    /// `bound` does not divide 2^32, and the bias stays invisible until something like a replay
    /// buffer over-samples its early entries.
    pub fn intBelow(self: Rng, index: u32, bound: u32) u32 {
        if (bound == 0) {
            return 0;
        }
        const wide: u64 = @as(u64, self.word(index, 7)) * @as(u64, bound);
        return @intCast(wide >> 32);
    }

    /// Fill `out` with uniforms in `[0, 1)`, drawn at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillUniform(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.unitFloat(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }

    /// Fill `out` with standard normal draws, at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillNormal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.normal(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }
};
Tensor
/// A view of `T`-typed storage as a multidimensional array.
///
/// A TENSOR NEVER OWNS ITS STORAGE, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN
///
/// There is no ownership flag and no `deinit`. `data` is a slice the tensor addresses; whoever
/// allocated it frees it, with the allocator that allocated it. Usually that is an arena, in
/// which case nobody frees anything and `arena.deinit()` ends the matter.
///
/// The alternative - an `owns: bool` and a `deinit` that is a no-op for views - is the shape
/// this library was warned about before it had a tensor: both of its failure modes are SILENT.
/// `deinit` on a view does nothing and reads as if it did something; a missing `deinit` on an
/// owner just grows memory. Having no `deinit` at all means a free appears exactly where an
/// allocation appears, and a view has neither.
///
/// Strides are in ELEMENTS and signed, so a reversed or negatively-strided view is expressible
/// without changing this type. `at` computes a signed offset and casts once.
pub fn Tensor(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The storage this tensor addresses. Not owned - see the type's doc comment.
        data: []T,
        /// Extent of each axis. Only the first `rank` entries are meaningful.
        shape: [max_rank]usize,
        /// Step in ELEMENTS between consecutive positions along each axis.
        strides: [max_rank]isize,
        rank: u8,
        /// WHERE THIS VIEW STARTS INSIDE `data`
        ///
        /// Every view until now began at element 0, which is why `slice` could not exist: a
        /// window into the middle of a tensor IS an offset. With this, `slice` and the rest of
        /// znum's `index` namespace become views rather than copies.
        ///
        /// IT ALSO WEAKENS WHAT `data.len` MEANS. `data` is the whole backing store; `base`
        /// and the strides say which of it this view addresses. Everything that walked
        /// `data[0..size()]` assuming those coincide had to be corrected - five sites, all of
        /// them fast paths that skip the index walk, which is exactly where such an assumption
        /// hides.
        base: usize = 0,

        /// A dense row-major tensor over `shape`, allocated from `gpa`.
        ///
        /// The caller frees `result.data` with the same allocator - or allocates from an arena
        /// and frees nothing.
        pub fn alloc(gpa: Allocator, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            const storage: []T = gpa.alloc(T, count) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
            return fromSlice(storage, shape);
        }

        /// A dense row-major view of `data` as `shape`. `data.len` must be exactly the product of
        /// the extents - a slice that is merely LARGE ENOUGH is rejected, because the usual reason
        /// for the mismatch is a shape that is wrong rather than storage that is generous.
        pub fn fromSlice(data: []T, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != data.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            var self: Self = .{
                .data = data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                .base = 0,
            };
            // Row-major: the last axis is contiguous, and each earlier stride is the product of
            // the extents to its right.
            var stride: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = shape.len;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                self.shape[axis] = shape[axis];
                self.strides[axis] = stride;
                stride *= @intCast(shape[axis]);
            }
            return self;
        }

        /// The number of elements this tensor addresses.
        pub fn size(self: Self) usize {
            var count: usize = 1;
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                count *= self.shape[axis];
            }
            return count;
        }

        /// Whether the strides are dense row-major, so `data[0..size()]` is the tensor in order.
        ///
        /// An operation that walks memory linearly must ask this rather than assume it: a view
        /// produced by `transpose` addresses the same storage in a different order, and a linear
        /// walk over it silently reads the wrong elements rather than failing.
        pub fn isContiguous(self: Self) bool {
            var expected: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = self.rank;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                if (self.shape[axis] != 1 and self.strides[axis] != expected) {
                    return false;
                }
                expected *= @intCast(self.shape[axis]);
            }
            return true;
        }

        /// The offset into `data` of the element at `indices`.
        pub fn flatIndex(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!usize {
            if (indices.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                if (i >= self.shape[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// The element at `indices`.
        pub fn at(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!T {
            return self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)];
        }

        /// The flat offset for a coordinate, with no error.
        ///
        /// THE 45 `flatIndex(...) catch unreachable` SITES ARE ALL THIS SHAPE
        ///
        ///     const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        ///     out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = ...
        ///
        /// The slice is `walker[0..rank]`, so `indices.len != rank` **cannot** happen - the
        /// caller built it from the rank. And the coordinate came from a `Walk` over this very
        /// shape, so the bounds cannot fail either. Two impossible checks, one `catch
        /// unreachable`, on the hottest line in the library.
        ///
        /// This computes the offset and returns it. The remaining safety is `data[offset]`,
        /// which is where Zig puts it: a panic with a message in Debug and ReleaseSafe.
        ///
        /// **Use it where the rank is an invariant you have already established** - after a
        /// shape check, or inside a walk over the tensor's own shape. Use `flatIndex` where the
        /// coordinate came from outside and might be wrong.
        pub fn offsetOf(self: Self, indices: []const usize) usize {
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// Read from a rank-2 tensor. **Does not return an error.**
        ///
        /// WHY `at` HAS TO AND THIS DOES NOT
        ///
        /// `at(indices: []const usize)` can fail for two reasons. One is a real bounds check.
        /// The other is `indices.len != rank` - and that check exists ONLY because a slice hides
        /// its arity from the type system. Every call site writes a literal `&.{ i, j }`; the
        /// compiler could know it is two, and does not.
        ///
        /// Naming the arity in the function removes that half entirely. What remains is the
        /// bounds check, and **Zig already has a name for that**: it is what `data[idx]` does -
        /// a panic with a message and a stack trace in Debug and ReleaseSafe, the same UB as any
        /// other slice index in ReleaseFast.
        ///
        /// Nobody writes `arr[i] catch unreachable`. This file had **105 `catch unreachable`**,
        /// most of them on an `at` whose rank the author had checked three lines earlier, and
        /// that noise is the single biggest thing that makes numeric Zig look worse than it is.
        ///
        /// The safety is not weaker - it is Zig's, in the place Zig puts it.
        pub fn at2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-2 tensor. The mirror of `at2`, and error-free for the same reason.
        pub fn setAt2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Read from a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn at1(self: Self, i: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn setAt1(self: Self, i: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Write `value` at `indices`.
        pub fn setAt(self: Self, indices: []const usize, value: T) Error!void {
            self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)] = value;
        }

        /// Set every element to `value`.
        pub fn fill(self: Self, value: T) void {
            if (self.isContiguous()) {
                @memset(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], value);
                return;
            }
            var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var remaining: usize = self.size();
            while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
                self.data[self.offsetOf(walker[0..self.rank])] = value;
                advance(self.shape[0..self.rank], walker[0..self.rank]);
            }
        }

        /// The same storage seen as `shape`. Requires a contiguous tensor and an equal element
        /// count; both are checked rather than assumed.
        pub fn reshape(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (!self.isContiguous()) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != self.size()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // From `base`, not 0: reshaping a slice must keep addressing that slice.
            return fromSlice(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], shape);
        }

        /// Whether two distinct index positions address the same element, which happens exactly
        /// when a stretched axis (stride 0) has extent above 1.
        ///
        /// An operation that WRITES must refuse an aliased destination. Many indices landing on
        /// one element means the last write wins and every earlier one vanishes - a result that is
        /// wrong in a way no bounds check catches. `map` and `zip` check this so a caller who
        /// passes a broadcast view as an output gets an error rather than a plausible answer.
        pub fn isAliased(self: Self) bool {
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                if (self.shape[axis] > 1 and self.strides[axis] == 0) {
                    return true;
                }
            }
            return false;
        }

        /// A read-only view of this tensor stretched to `shape` by the broadcasting rule.
        ///
        /// BROADCASTING IS A STRIDE OF ZERO, NOT A COPY
        ///
        /// An axis whose extent is 1 is stretched by setting its stride to 0, so every position
        /// along it addresses the same element. No storage is allocated and no data moves; a
        /// `(3, 1, 5)` tensor viewed as `(3, 4, 5)` is the same 15 values read four times.
        ///
        /// **WRITING THROUGH A STRETCHED VIEW IS ALMOST CERTAINLY A MISTAKE.** Many indices
        /// alias one element, so the last write wins and the others vanish. This returns a view
        /// because that is what the type system has; treat it as an input.
        ///
        /// `isContiguous` reports false for any stretched axis with extent > 1, which is what
        /// stops a linear walk from reading the underlying 15 values as if they were 60.
        pub fn broadcastTo(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank or shape.len < self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = .{
                .data = self.data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                // Stretching does not move where the view starts.
                .base = self.base,
            };
            // Align at the TRAILING axis, as the broadcast rule does: an axis this tensor does not
            // have behaves as extent 1, and so does an axis it has with extent 1. Both get a
            // stride of 0.
            for (shape, 0..) |extent, axis| {
                const from_end: usize = shape.len - axis;
                view.shape[axis] = extent;
                if (self.rank < from_end) {
                    continue;
                }
                const own_axis: usize = self.rank - from_end;
                const own_extent: usize = self.shape[own_axis];
                if (own_extent == extent) {
                    view.strides[axis] = self.strides[own_axis];
                } else if (own_extent != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A window into this tensor: `len` positions along `axis`, starting at `start`.
        ///
        /// A SLICE IS AN OFFSET, WHICH IS WHY `base` EXISTS
        ///
        /// Nothing is copied: the view keeps every stride and simply starts `start` steps further
        /// into the data along one axis. A batch of 32 rows out of 4096 is a `Tensor` the same
        /// size as any other, addressing 32 rows of the same buffer.
        ///
        /// `start + len` must fit. Clamping instead would hand back a view SMALLER than asked
        /// for, and the caller's next loop would read the shape it requested rather than the one
        /// it got.
        pub fn slice(self: Self, axis: usize, start: usize, len: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (start + len > self.shape[axis]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            const step: isize = self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(start));
            const moved: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) + step;
            if (moved < 0) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            view.base = @intCast(moved);
            view.shape[axis] = len;
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with the axes reordered: `out` axis `i` is this tensor's axis `order[i]`.
        ///
        /// EVERY `manip` OPERATION HERE IS METADATA
        ///
        /// `permute`, `squeeze`, `unsqueeze`, `flatten` and `moveAxis` all return a view: they
        /// permute or insert entries in two fixed-size arrays and copy nothing. That is why they
        /// need no GPU kernel and no sweep row - **there is no arithmetic to disagree about.**
        /// The cost appears later, when a permuted view has to be BOUND to a kernel and must be
        /// densified first, which is the constraint recorded in section 10.3.
        ///
        /// `order` must be a permutation of `0..rank`. A repeated axis would produce a view
        /// where two indices alias, which is `isAliased`'s job to reject at write time - but
        /// catching it here names the actual mistake.
        pub fn permute(self: Self, order: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (order.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var seen: [max_rank]bool = @splat(false);
            for (order) |axis| {
                if (axis >= self.rank or seen[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                seen[axis] = true;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            for (order, 0..) |axis, i| {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[axis];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[axis];
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis removed.
        ///
        /// Removing an axis whose extent is NOT 1 would change how many elements the view
        /// addresses, so it is `ShapeMismatch` rather than a silent reshape.
        pub fn squeeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (self.shape[axis] != 1) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = axis;
            while (i + 1 < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i + 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i + 1];
            }
            view.shape[self.rank - 1] = 1;
            view.strides[self.rank - 1] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank - 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis inserted at `axis`. `axis == rank` appends.
        ///
        /// The new axis gets a stride of 0, which is harmless because its extent is 1 - a
        /// single position cannot step anywhere. `isAliased` only objects when extent exceeds 1.
        pub fn unsqueeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis > self.rank or self.rank + 1 > max_rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = self.rank;
            while (i > axis) : (i -= 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i - 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i - 1];
            }
            view.shape[axis] = 1;
            view.strides[axis] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank + 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A rank-1 view of the whole tensor. Contiguous only, for `reshape`'s reason.
        pub fn flatten(self: Self) Error!Self {
            return self.reshape(&.{self.size()});
        }

        /// A view with one axis moved from `from` to `to`, the others keeping their order.
        pub fn moveAxis(self: Self, from: usize, to: usize) Error!Self {
            if (from >= self.rank or to >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var order: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var k: usize = 0;
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                if (i == from) {
                    continue;
                }
                order[k] = i;
                k += 1;
            }
            // Open a slot at `to` and drop the moved axis into it.
            var j: usize = self.rank - 1;
            while (j > to) : (j -= 1) {
                order[j] = order[j - 1];
            }
            order[to] = from;
            return self.permute(order[0..self.rank]);
        }

        /// A view with axes `a` and `b` exchanged. Shares storage; changes through one are visible
        /// through the other.
        pub fn transpose(self: Self, a: usize, b: usize) Error!Self {
            if (a >= self.rank or b >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            view.shape[a] = self.shape[b];
            view.shape[b] = self.shape[a];
            view.strides[a] = self.strides[b];
            view.strides[b] = self.strides[a];
            return view;
        }
    };
}
meanAll
/// The arithmetic mean of every element, or `DomainError` for an empty tensor.
///
/// An empty mean is undefined rather than zero or NaN, and saying so is the caller's cue to
/// decide what an empty batch means - which is a question only the caller can answer.
pub fn meanAll(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const divisor: T = @floatFromInt(n);
    return sumAll(T, a) / divisor;
}
variance
/// The variance of every element, with the divisor chosen by `ddof`.
///
/// TWO PASSES, FOR THE REASON `layerNormRows` GIVES
///
/// `E[x^2] - E[x]^2` is one pass and subtracts two large nearly-equal numbers, losing most of its
/// significant digits when the mean is large relative to the spread. Two passes cost one more
/// read of memory that is already in cache and are correct for every input.
///
/// Returns `DomainError` when the divisor is undefined - `n = 0`, or `n = 1` with
/// `.sample` - rather than a NaN that propagates into a result nobody examines. That is what
/// `Ddof.divisor` returning an optional is for.
pub fn variance(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), ddof: Ddof) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    const divisor: f64 = ddof.divisor(n) orelse return Error.DomainError;
    const mean: T = try meanAll(T, a);

    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const d: T = (try a.at(at)) - mean;
        const term: T = d * d;
        total.add(term);
    }
    const divide_by: T = @floatCast(divisor);
    return total.value() / divide_by;
}
approxEqAbs
/// True when `a` and `b` agree to within `tol` in ABSOLUTE terms, with NaN never equal to
/// anything and infinities equal only to themselves.
///
/// Absolute, not relative, and that is a deliberate limitation: a relative tolerance is the
/// right tool near zero and the wrong one for a residual that is SUPPOSED to be near zero. A
/// caller comparing large magnitudes should scale its own tolerance and say so.
pub fn approxEqAbs(comptime T: type, a: T, b: T, tol: T) bool {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    // `x != x` rather than a helper: `zm.isNan` is VECTOR-only (it returns
    // `@Vector(veclen(T), bool)` and `veclen` rejects a scalar float). The inequality is
    // the definition, is GPU-portable, and needs nothing imported.
    if (a != a or b != b) {
        return false;
    }
    if (a == b) {
        return true;
    }
    return @abs(a - b) <= tol;
}

Every code block in this document is either the body of a test in src/zimrnum.zig or built from one.

4. Element types, errors and tolerance#

4.1 Choosing a float type#

Every float-taking entry point is generic over f16, f32 and f64; there is no default. Use f32 to match the engine and anything destined for a GPU buffer. Use f64 where precision determines whether the answer means anything: finite differences, long accumulations, ill-conditioned solves.

isFloat answers the question; requireFloat is the guard the library calls, which fails at your call site with your type named rather than deeper down.

pub fn isFloat(comptime T: type) bool {
    return switch (T) {
        f16, f32, f64 => true,
        else => false,
    };
}

4.2 The error set#

zn.Error is one set for the whole library, so a caller handling a shape mismatch does not need to know which layer noticed.

ErrorRaised when
OutOfMemoryAn allocation failed.
ShapeMismatchOperands disagree about shape and no broadcasting rule reconciles them; an output is sized wrong.
OutOfRangeAn axis, index or window is outside the tensor it addresses.
DomainErrorDefined, but not for these values: an empty mean, a variance with no degrees of freedom, a constant tensor to minMaxScale.
UnsupportedShapeA shape the operation cannot accept: rank other than 2 to matmul, a non-contiguous tensor to reshape, an aliased destination to any writing operation.

4.3 Ddof#

Whether a variance divides by n or n − 1. NumPy spells this ddof=0 and ddof=1; the named form reads at the call site.

pub fn divisor(self: Ddof, n: usize) ?f64 {
    return switch (self) {
        .population => if (n == 0) null else float64(n),
        .sample => if (n < 2) null else float64(n - 1),
    };
}
n.population.sample
0nullnull
11.0null
44.03.0

The optional is the point: a sample of one has a population variance — zero — and no unbiased sample variance. variance turns the null into DomainError rather than dividing by zero.

4.4 approxEqAbs#

zn.approxEqAbs(f64, expected, actual, 1.0e-9)
approxEqAbs
/// True when `a` and `b` agree to within `tol` in ABSOLUTE terms, with NaN never equal to
/// anything and infinities equal only to themselves.
///
/// Absolute, not relative, and that is a deliberate limitation: a relative tolerance is the
/// right tool near zero and the wrong one for a residual that is SUPPOSED to be near zero. A
/// caller comparing large magnitudes should scale its own tolerance and say so.
pub fn approxEqAbs(comptime T: type, a: T, b: T, tol: T) bool {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    // `x != x` rather than a helper: `zm.isNan` is VECTOR-only (it returns
    // `@Vector(veclen(T), bool)` and `veclen` rejects a scalar float). The inequality is
    // the definition, is GPU-portable, and needs nothing imported.
    if (a != a or b != b) {
        return false;
    }
    if (a == b) {
        return true;
    }
    return @abs(a - b) <= tol;
}

Absolute tolerance. NaN is equal to nothing, including itself; infinities are equal only to themselves. The tolerance is absolute and not relative, which suits a residual that should be near zero. If you are comparing large magnitudes, scale the tolerance yourself.

4.5 The zm pairing, and what checks it#

Thirty-five tensor operations here are named after a zimrmath function and are meant to be that function at a higher rank. A comptime gate asserts the name exists on both sides, and fails the build if one is added without the other.

That is a spelling check. Two functions can share a name and disagree, and one did: pow computed exp(y * log(x)), which is NaN for a negative base, while zm.pow(-2, 3) returns −8. The doc comment called that deliberate. Nothing would ever have reported it.

pow(-2, 3)  ->  NaN
zm.pow(-2, 3)  ->  -8

Two tests now compare answers. One sweeps 42 base/power pairs for pow; the other runs all thirty unary names over a probe that deliberately leaves several domains — negatives for sqrt, values above one for atanh, zero for log. NaN and infinity are accepted on both sides rather than skipped, because those are exactly the cases where two implementations drift.

360 comparisons, zero further disagreements. That result is worth as much as the find: the pairing can be relied on, and the sweep is what keeps it that way.

A third test covers the four remaining names — atan2Rad, hypot, clamp, lerp — which are the group pow came from. A function taking two operands has more ways to disagree than a unary one:

  • Argument order. atan2(y, x) takes the vertical component first, and a version passing them the other way is wrong everywhere except the diagonal. The probe is deliberately not symmetric, because a symmetric one would miss it.
  • Degenerate pairs. clamp is swept with an inverted range (lo > hi). Whatever the two do there, they must do the same thing.
  • Outside the usual domain. lerp with t at −3 and 3 is extrapolation, not an error, and both must extrapolate alike.

All thirty-five paired names are now checked by value, not just by name.

Every one of these tests was checked to fail — the unary sweep by perturbing an implementation by 1e−7, the non-unary one by swapping atan2's arguments, which two tests caught. A test that has never been seen to fail is not yet a test.

4.6 Making a tensor, and two endpoint conventions#

zn.zeros(f64, gpa, &.{ 2, 3 })       every element 0
zn.ones(f64, gpa, &.{4})             every element 1
zn.full(i32, gpa, &.{3}, 7)          every element 7
zn.eye(f64, gpa, 3, 3)               identity
zn.arange(i32, gpa, 0, 5, 1)         0 1 2 3 4
zn.linspace(f64, gpa, 0, 1, 5, .include)
zeros
/// A tensor of the given shape, every element zero.
///
/// `alloc` LEAVES GARBAGE, WHICH IS WHY THIS IS NOT THE SAME FUNCTION
///
/// Allocating and filling are separate steps and the allocator does not zero. A caller who
/// writes `alloc` and forgets the fill reads whatever was in that memory - plausible numbers,
/// sometimes, which is the worst kind.
///
/// So the common case gets a name. `alloc` stays for the case where every element is about to be
/// written anyway and zeroing would be wasted work.
pub fn zeros(comptime T: type, gpa: Allocator, shape: []const usize) Error!Tensor(T) {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, shape);
    out.fill(0);
    return out;
}
ones
/// A tensor of the given shape, every element one.
pub fn ones(comptime T: type, gpa: Allocator, shape: []const usize) Error!Tensor(T) {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, shape);
    out.fill(1);
    return out;
}
full
/// A tensor of the given shape, every element `value`.
pub fn full(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    shape: []const usize,
    value: T,
) Error!Tensor(T) {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, shape);
    out.fill(value);
    return out;
}
eye
/// An identity matrix, `rows` by `columns`.
///
/// RECTANGULAR IS ALLOWED, AND IT IS NOT A CURIOSITY
///
/// A non-square `eye` has ones down the main diagonal until one side runs out. That is what you
/// want for a projection between spaces of different sizes, and for the initial state of a
/// decomposition whose factors are not square. numpy allows it; refusing it would send a caller
/// to build one by hand.
pub fn eye(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    rows: usize,
    columns: usize,
) Error!Tensor(T) {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const out: Tensor(T) = try zeros(T, gpa, &.{ rows, columns });
    for (0..@min(rows, columns)) |i| {
        out.setAt2(i, i, 1);
    }
    return out;
}
arange
/// Evenly spaced values from `start`, stepping by `step`, stopping before `stop`.
///
/// THE ENDPOINT IS EXCLUDED, WHICH IS `arange`'S CONVENTION EVERYWHERE
///
/// `arange(0, 5, 1)` is `0 1 2 3 4` - five values, not six. numpy, Python's `range` and Zig's
/// `0..5` all agree, and a version that included the stop would be surprising in three languages
/// at once.
///
/// `linspace` is the other convention and takes a named argument, because there the count is
/// what you asked for and the endpoint is genuinely a choice.
pub fn arange(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    start: T,
    stop: T,
    step: T,
) Error!Tensor(T) {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (step == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    // A range that steps away from its stop has no end, and returning an empty tensor would hide
    // the mistake. Counting first also means one allocation of exactly the right size.
    var count: usize = 0;
    if ((step > 0 and stop > start) or (step < 0 and stop < start)) {
        const span: T = stop - start;
        count = switch (@typeInfo(T)) {
            .int => @intCast(@divTrunc(span + step - (if (step > 0) @as(T, 1) else @as(T, -1)), step)),
            else => @intFromFloat(@ceil(span / step)),
        };
    }
    const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{count});
    for (0..count) |i| {
        // `start + i * step`, NOT a running sum: a running sum accumulates rounding, so the last
        // value of `arange(0, 1, 0.1)` would drift. Multiplying keeps each value independent.
        out.data[i] = start + @as(T, switch (@typeInfo(T)) {
            .int => @intCast(i),
            else => @floatFromInt(i),
        }) * step;
    }
    return out;
}
linspace
/// `count` evenly spaced values from `start` to `stop`.
///
/// `endpoint` is required for the reason `Ddof` and `CodeOrder` are: both conventions are in
/// common use, and picking the wrong one gives an answer that is off by one element everywhere
/// without being obviously wrong anywhere.
pub fn linspace(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    start: T,
    stop: T,
    count: usize,
    endpoint: EndPoint,
) Error!Tensor(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{count});
    if (count == 0) {
        return out;
    }
    if (count == 1) {
        out.data[0] = start;
        return out;
    }
    const divisor: T = @floatFromInt(if (endpoint == .include) count - 1 else count);
    const step: T = (stop - start) / divisor;
    for (0..count) |i| {
        out.data[i] = start + @as(T, @floatFromInt(i)) * step;
    }
    // THE LAST VALUE IS SET EXACTLY, not computed. `start + (n-1) * step` is `stop` in real
    // arithmetic and a few ulps off in floating point, and a caller who asked for a range ending
    // at 1.0 and got 0.9999999999999999 has a bug report rather than a rounding lesson.
    if (endpoint == .include) {
        out.data[count - 1] = stop;
    }
    return out;
}

alloc is still there and still leaves garbage — the allocator does not zero. A caller who allocates and forgets to fill reads whatever was in that memory, which is sometimes plausible numbers. So the common case gets a name, and alloc stays for when every element is about to be overwritten anyway.

Two conventions, and both are right

arange(0, 5, 1)                ->  0 1 2 3 4        five values, stop EXCLUDED
linspace(0, 1, 5, .include)    ->  0 .25 .5 .75 1   five values, stop INCLUDED
linspace(0, 1, 5, .exclude)    ->  0 .2 .4 .6 .8    five values, stop excluded

arange excludes its stop and does not ask. numpy, Python's range and Zig's 0..5 all agree, and a version that included it would be surprising in three languages at once.

linspace asks, because there the count is what you requested and the endpoint is a genuine choice. Same spacing question, different answers — and the argument is required for the reason Ddof and CodeOrder are: the wrong one gives a range that is off by one element everywhere without being obviously wrong anywhere.

The last value is set, not computed

start + (n-1) * step is stop in real arithmetic and a few ulps off in floating point. A caller who asked for a range ending at 1.0 and got 0.9999999999999999 has a bug report rather than a rounding lesson, so .include assigns the final slot directly.

.exclude has no final value to pin, so every entry is start + i * step and 3 * 0.2 comes out 0.6000000000000001. That is correct arithmetic, not a defect — and it approximately where the inclusive case is asserted exactly, which is the distinction in one place.

A rectangular eye is not a curiosity

eye(2, 4) has ones down the main diagonal until one side runs out. That is what a projection between spaces of different sizes needs, and what a decomposition whose factors are not square starts from. numpy allows it; refusing it would send a caller to build one by hand.

4.7 astype, and a case where Zig forced the better answer#

try zn.astype(f64, i32, out, a);   // f64 tensor -> i32 tensor
astype
/// Convert every element to another numeric type.
///
/// WHAT THIS HAS TO DECIDE, AND WHY numpy'S ANSWER DOES NOT TRANSFER
///
/// Four conversions and four different hazards:
///
///     f64 -> f32   1e300 becomes infinity
///     f64 -> i32   3.7 becomes 3 or 4, depending on a rounding rule
///     f64 -> i8    300.0 is OUT OF RANGE
///     i32 -> f32   2^24 + 1 loses its last bit
///
/// numpy truncates toward zero and **wraps out-of-range values silently**, which is how a
/// temperature of 300 becomes 44 in an i8 column and nobody notices for a week.
///
/// Zig cannot follow that even if it wanted to: `@intFromFloat` out of range is **undefined
/// behaviour**, not a wrap. So the choice is forced - check, and this returns `DomainError`.
///
/// TRUNCATION, NAMED
///
/// `3.7` becomes `3` and `-3.7` becomes `-3`: truncation toward zero, which is `@intFromFloat`'s
/// rule, C's, and numpy's. It is not `floor`, and the negative case is where they differ - `floor`
/// would give `-4`. Callers who want a different rounding apply `round` or `floor` FIRST, which
/// makes the choice visible at the call site instead of hidden in a conversion.
///
/// NaN AND INFINITY HAVE NO INTEGER, so they are a `DomainError` too rather than whatever the
/// hardware happens to produce.
pub fn astype(
    comptime From: type,
    comptime To: type,
    out: Tensor(To),
    a: Tensor(From),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(From);
    comptime requireNumeric(To);
    if (out.rank != a.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..a.rank) |d| {
        if (out.shape[d] != a.shape[d]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    const to_int: bool = comptime @typeInfo(To) == .int;
    const from_float: bool = comptime @typeInfo(From) == .float;
    // VALIDATE BEFORE WRITING ANYTHING - the all-or-nothing rule `divideBy` learned the hard way,
    // when a failure left `out` half-converted and the caller could not tell how far it got.
    if (comptime to_int and from_float) {
        const low: From = @floatFromInt(zm.minInt(To));
        const high: From = @floatFromInt(zm.maxInt(To));
        var check: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
        while (check.next()) |at| {
            const x: From = a.data[a.offsetOf(at)];
            if (isNan(x) or !zm.isFinite(x)) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            // `@trunc` first: 2.9 fits in an i2 whose maximum is 1 only after truncation, and
            // comparing the untruncated value would reject a conversion that is exact.
            const whole: From = @trunc(x);
            if (whole < low or whole > high) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
        }
    }
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: From = a.data[a.offsetOf(at)];
        const converted: To = switch (@typeInfo(To)) {
            .int => switch (@typeInfo(From)) {
                .int => @intCast(x),
                // `@trunc` and NOT `@intFromFloat`: the linter's `int-from-float` rule wants
                // the rounding NAMED, because `@intFromFloat` alone leaves a reader to remember
                // which of four roundings it does. `@trunc` says toward zero, and the coercion
                // to `To` carries it the rest of the way.
                else => @trunc(x),
            },
            else => switch (@typeInfo(From)) {
                .int => @floatFromInt(x),
                else => @floatCast(x),
            },
        };
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = converted;
    }
}

Four conversions, four different hazards:

f64 -> f32   1e300 becomes infinity
f64 -> i32   3.7 becomes 3 or 4, depending on a rounding rule
f64 -> i8    300.0 is OUT OF RANGE
i32 -> f32   2^24 + 1 loses its last bit

Truncation toward zero, named

3.7 becomes 3 and -3.7 becomes -3. That is @trunc's rule, C's and numpy's — and the negative case is where it differs from floor, which would give −4.

A caller wanting different rounding applies it first: round then astype gives 4. The choice ends up at the call site instead of hidden inside a conversion.

Out of range is an error, and that is not a style choice

numpy wraps silently. That is how a temperature of 300 becomes 44 in an i8 column and nobody notices for a week.

Zig cannot follow it even if it wanted to: @intFromFloat out of range is undefined behaviour, not a wrap. So the choice was forced — and forcing it produced the better answer. astype validates the whole tensor before writing anything, so a refused conversion leaves the output exactly as it was.

NaN and infinity are refused for the same reason: neither has an integer, and returning whatever the hardware produces would be inventing one.

One case that is accepted: 127.9 into an i8. It does not fit as a real number and does after truncation — which is precisely what truncation means, so rejecting it would refuse a correct conversion.

Narrowing a float stays silent

An f32 cannot hold 1e300 and inf is the IEEE answer, not a mistake to report. The asymmetry is deliberate: an out-of-range integer conversion has no defined result, an out-of-range float conversion has one and it is infinity.

5. Random numbers#

5.1 Seeds and indices#

zn.Rng is not a stream you advance. Every draw is a pure function of a seed and an index, so you address values rather than consume them:

const rng: zn.Rng = zn.Rng.init(7);
const a: u32 = rng.bits(900);   // no draws 0..899 were made
Rng
/// A reproducible random source addressed by (seed, index) rather than by mutation.
///
/// WHY COUNTER-BASED, AND NOT A STATEFUL GENERATOR
///
/// A classic PRNG carries state and advances it, so drawing the i-th value means drawing the
/// first i. That is fine on one CPU thread and useless everywhere else this library is going:
///
/// * **A GPU kernel has no shared state.** Thread `i` must be able to compute ITS value without
/// any other thread having run. A counter-based generator is a pure function of the index, so
/// the kernel is `value(seed, thread_id)` and needs no sequencing at all.
/// * **CPU and GPU must agree EXACTLY.** The CPU path is this library's oracle (plan section 5 rung
/// 1); an oracle that produces a different random stream than the thing it checks is not an
/// oracle. Same function, same integers, same answer.
/// * **A failing case is reproducible from two numbers.** "seed 7, index 4096" is the whole
/// state, so a bad draw can be re-created in a unit test without replaying a sequence.
///
/// THE MIXER IS DELIBERATELY MODEST AND THE TESTS SAY SO. `mix32` is the murmur3 finalizer -
/// a bijection with good avalanche, applied to `index` folded with `seed`. It is NOT a
/// cryptographic generator and NOT claimed to pass a full statistical battery; what it is pinned
/// on is the properties this library actually relies on: determinism, range, stream independence,
/// and first/second moments. A stronger generator can replace the body without touching a single
/// call site, precisely because the interface is (seed, index) rather than a stream.
pub const Rng = struct {
    seed: u32,

    /// A generator for a given seed. Two `Rng`s with the same seed are the same function.
    pub fn init(seed: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = seed };
    }

    /// A DERIVED generator, so one seed can feed several independent uses without the caller
    /// inventing seeds. `parent.split(0)` and `parent.split(1)` are unrelated streams.
    ///
    /// Mixing the label rather than adding it: `seed + 1` and `seed + 2` are adjacent inputs to
    /// the mixer, and adjacency is exactly what a hash is asked not to preserve. Passing the
    /// label through `mix32` first means the derived seeds are as far apart as any other pair.
    pub fn split(self: Rng, label: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = mix32(self.seed ^ mix32(label ^ 0x9e3779b9)) };
    }

    /// The number of mantissa bits `T` can hold, including the implicit leading one. This is how
    /// many random bits a draw in `[0, 1)` may use: fewer wastes range, MORE ROUNDS UP TO 1.0 and
    /// breaks the half-open interval.
    fn mantissaWidth(comptime T: type) u32 {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return switch (T) {
            f16 => 11,
            f32 => 24,
            f64 => 53,
            else => unreachable,
        };
    }

    /// One 32-bit word of the generator's output at `index`, from independent stream `stream`.
    ///
    /// EACH KIND OF DRAW OWNS A STREAM, SO KINDS DO NOT INTERFERE
    ///
    /// Without this, every method would start from `bits(index)` and drawing two different KINDS
    /// at one index would return values that determine each other - `intBelow(i, n)` would be a
    /// function of `unitFloat(i)`. A caller has no reason to expect that and every reason to be
    /// hurt by it, so the stream is part of the addressing rather than the caller's problem.
    ///
    /// The stream index is folded into the MIXER, not into the index. Folding it into the index
    /// (`bits(index ^ K)`) makes indices `i` and `i ^ K` draw from the same two words with their
    /// roles swapped, so those pairs are not independent - which is exactly the defect this
    /// replaced.
    ///
    /// `stream == 0` adds nothing, so `bits` is unchanged by the introduction of rounds.
    fn word(self: Rng, index: u32, stream: u32) u32 {
        return mix32(mix32(index *% 0x9e3779b9) ^ self.seed ^ (stream *% 0x85ebca6b));
    }

    /// The raw 32-bit draw at `index`, stream 0. Distinct from every other kind of draw.
    pub fn bits(self: Rng, index: u32) u32 {
        return self.word(index, 0);
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)` from stream `stream` (and `stream + 1` when `T` needs more than 32 bits).
    ///
    /// Exactly `mantissaWidth(T)` random bits, scaled by 2^-width. The largest result is
    /// (2^width - 1) / 2^width, which is the largest `T` strictly below one - so the interval is
    /// half-open for every width, including `f16`, where using 24 bits rounds to 1.0 instead.
    fn unitFloatRound(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32, comptime stream: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const width: u32 = comptime mantissaWidth(T);
        const first: u64 = self.word(index, stream);
        const mantissa: u64 = if (comptime width <= 32)
            first >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(32 - width))
        else
            (first << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width - 32))) |
                (@as(u64, self.word(index, stream + 1)) >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(64 - width)));
        const numerator: T = @floatFromInt(mantissa);
        const denominator: T = @floatFromInt(@as(u64, 1) << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width)));
        return numerator / denominator;
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn unitFloat(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        return self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 1);
    }

    /// A float in `[low, high)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn uniform(
        self: Rng,
        comptime T: type,
        index: u32,
        low: T,
        high: T,
    ) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return low + (high - low) * self.unitFloat(T, index);
    }

    /// A standard normal draw (mean 0, variance 1) at `index`, by the Box-Muller transform.
    ///
    /// ITS TWO UNIFORMS COME FROM ITS OWN STREAMS (3-6), not from the uniform stream, so
    /// `normal(i)` and `unitFloat(i)` are independent and a caller mixing them needs no care.
    ///
    /// Box-Muller rather than a ziggurat because a ziggurat REJECTS: the number of uniforms it
    /// consumes varies per sample, which would make the value at `index` depend on how many
    /// rejections happened. That is not a pure function of the index and would not survive being
    /// evaluated on an independent GPU thread. Box-Muller consumes exactly two, always.
    ///
    /// The radius draw is taken from `(0, 1]` rather than `[0, 1)` because `@log(0)` is -inf.
    pub fn normal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const two_pi: T = 6.283185307179586;
        const radius_draw: T = 1.0 - self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 3);
        const angle_draw: T = self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 5);
        const radius: T = @sqrt(-2.0 * @log(radius_draw));
        return radius * @cos(two_pi * angle_draw);
    }

    /// An integer in `[0, bound)`, drawn at `index` from its own stream. `bound == 0` yields 0.
    ///
    /// Lemire's multiply-shift, not `% bound`: the modulo is biased toward small values whenever
    /// `bound` does not divide 2^32, and the bias stays invisible until something like a replay
    /// buffer over-samples its early entries.
    pub fn intBelow(self: Rng, index: u32, bound: u32) u32 {
        if (bound == 0) {
            return 0;
        }
        const wide: u64 = @as(u64, self.word(index, 7)) * @as(u64, bound);
        return @intCast(wide >> 32);
    }

    /// Fill `out` with uniforms in `[0, 1)`, drawn at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillUniform(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.unitFloat(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }

    /// Fill `out` with standard normal draws, at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillNormal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.normal(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }
};

Three consequences:

  • Order does not matter. Two pieces of code drawing at the same index get the same value regardless of what else ran. Parallel work needs no coordination: give each unit a disjoint index range, or its own stream.
  • A failing case is two numbers. Seed and index are the whole state, so a bad draw reproduces in a unit test without replaying a sequence.
  • The same values appear on the GPU. The generator is integer arithmetic that lowers to WGSL, so a kernel computing value(seed, thread_id) produces the identical stream.

5.2 How a draw is computed#

One 32-bit word comes from mixing the index, the seed and a stream number:

fn word(self: Rng, index: u32, stream: u32) u32 {
    return mix32(mix32(index *% 0x9e3779b9) ^ self.seed ^ (stream *% 0x85ebca6b));
}

mix32 is the 32-bit MurmurHash3 finaliser. Each kind of draw owns a stream, so draws of different kinds at the same index are independent:

StreamUsed by
0bits
1, 2unitFloat (the second word supplies the extra mantissa bits of f64)
3–6normal, whose two uniforms each take a pair
7intBelow

The stream is folded into the mixer rather than into the index. Folding it into the index would make indices i and i ^ K draw from the same words with their roles swapped.

unitFloat takes exactly as many random bits as T's mantissa holds — 11 for f16, 24 for f32, 53 for f64 — and scales by 2−width. The largest result is the largest T strictly below one, so the interval is half-open at every width.

5.3 Drawing values#

const rng: zn.Rng = zn.Rng.init(20260904);
