const raw: u32 = rng.bits(0);                       // raw 32 bits
const u: f64  = rng.unitFloat(f64, 0);              // [0, 1)
const t: f32  = rng.uniform(f32, 0, -1.0, 1.0);     // [-1, 1)
const z: f64  = rng.normal(f64, 0);                 // standard normal
const k: u32  = rng.intBelow(0, 52);                // [0, 52), unbiased

var buf: [256]f32 = undefined;
rng.fillUniform(f32, &buf);                        // indices 0..255
rng.fillNormal(f32, &buf);                         // indices 0..255
Rng
/// A reproducible random source addressed by (seed, index) rather than by mutation.
///
/// WHY COUNTER-BASED, AND NOT A STATEFUL GENERATOR
///
/// A classic PRNG carries state and advances it, so drawing the i-th value means drawing the
/// first i. That is fine on one CPU thread and useless everywhere else this library is going:
///
/// * **A GPU kernel has no shared state.** Thread `i` must be able to compute ITS value without
/// any other thread having run. A counter-based generator is a pure function of the index, so
/// the kernel is `value(seed, thread_id)` and needs no sequencing at all.
/// * **CPU and GPU must agree EXACTLY.** The CPU path is this library's oracle (plan section 5 rung
/// 1); an oracle that produces a different random stream than the thing it checks is not an
/// oracle. Same function, same integers, same answer.
/// * **A failing case is reproducible from two numbers.** "seed 7, index 4096" is the whole
/// state, so a bad draw can be re-created in a unit test without replaying a sequence.
///
/// THE MIXER IS DELIBERATELY MODEST AND THE TESTS SAY SO. `mix32` is the murmur3 finalizer -
/// a bijection with good avalanche, applied to `index` folded with `seed`. It is NOT a
/// cryptographic generator and NOT claimed to pass a full statistical battery; what it is pinned
/// on is the properties this library actually relies on: determinism, range, stream independence,
/// and first/second moments. A stronger generator can replace the body without touching a single
/// call site, precisely because the interface is (seed, index) rather than a stream.
pub const Rng = struct {
    seed: u32,

    /// A generator for a given seed. Two `Rng`s with the same seed are the same function.
    pub fn init(seed: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = seed };
    }

    /// A DERIVED generator, so one seed can feed several independent uses without the caller
    /// inventing seeds. `parent.split(0)` and `parent.split(1)` are unrelated streams.
    ///
    /// Mixing the label rather than adding it: `seed + 1` and `seed + 2` are adjacent inputs to
    /// the mixer, and adjacency is exactly what a hash is asked not to preserve. Passing the
    /// label through `mix32` first means the derived seeds are as far apart as any other pair.
    pub fn split(self: Rng, label: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = mix32(self.seed ^ mix32(label ^ 0x9e3779b9)) };
    }

    /// The number of mantissa bits `T` can hold, including the implicit leading one. This is how
    /// many random bits a draw in `[0, 1)` may use: fewer wastes range, MORE ROUNDS UP TO 1.0 and
    /// breaks the half-open interval.
    fn mantissaWidth(comptime T: type) u32 {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return switch (T) {
            f16 => 11,
            f32 => 24,
            f64 => 53,
            else => unreachable,
        };
    }

    /// One 32-bit word of the generator's output at `index`, from independent stream `stream`.
    ///
    /// EACH KIND OF DRAW OWNS A STREAM, SO KINDS DO NOT INTERFERE
    ///
    /// Without this, every method would start from `bits(index)` and drawing two different KINDS
    /// at one index would return values that determine each other - `intBelow(i, n)` would be a
    /// function of `unitFloat(i)`. A caller has no reason to expect that and every reason to be
    /// hurt by it, so the stream is part of the addressing rather than the caller's problem.
    ///
    /// The stream index is folded into the MIXER, not into the index. Folding it into the index
    /// (`bits(index ^ K)`) makes indices `i` and `i ^ K` draw from the same two words with their
    /// roles swapped, so those pairs are not independent - which is exactly the defect this
    /// replaced.
    ///
    /// `stream == 0` adds nothing, so `bits` is unchanged by the introduction of rounds.
    fn word(self: Rng, index: u32, stream: u32) u32 {
        return mix32(mix32(index *% 0x9e3779b9) ^ self.seed ^ (stream *% 0x85ebca6b));
    }

    /// The raw 32-bit draw at `index`, stream 0. Distinct from every other kind of draw.
    pub fn bits(self: Rng, index: u32) u32 {
        return self.word(index, 0);
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)` from stream `stream` (and `stream + 1` when `T` needs more than 32 bits).
    ///
    /// Exactly `mantissaWidth(T)` random bits, scaled by 2^-width. The largest result is
    /// (2^width - 1) / 2^width, which is the largest `T` strictly below one - so the interval is
    /// half-open for every width, including `f16`, where using 24 bits rounds to 1.0 instead.
    fn unitFloatRound(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32, comptime stream: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const width: u32 = comptime mantissaWidth(T);
        const first: u64 = self.word(index, stream);
        const mantissa: u64 = if (comptime width <= 32)
            first >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(32 - width))
        else
            (first << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width - 32))) |
                (@as(u64, self.word(index, stream + 1)) >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(64 - width)));
        const numerator: T = @floatFromInt(mantissa);
        const denominator: T = @floatFromInt(@as(u64, 1) << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width)));
        return numerator / denominator;
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn unitFloat(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        return self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 1);
    }

    /// A float in `[low, high)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn uniform(
        self: Rng,
        comptime T: type,
        index: u32,
        low: T,
        high: T,
    ) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return low + (high - low) * self.unitFloat(T, index);
    }

    /// A standard normal draw (mean 0, variance 1) at `index`, by the Box-Muller transform.
    ///
    /// ITS TWO UNIFORMS COME FROM ITS OWN STREAMS (3-6), not from the uniform stream, so
    /// `normal(i)` and `unitFloat(i)` are independent and a caller mixing them needs no care.
    ///
    /// Box-Muller rather than a ziggurat because a ziggurat REJECTS: the number of uniforms it
    /// consumes varies per sample, which would make the value at `index` depend on how many
    /// rejections happened. That is not a pure function of the index and would not survive being
    /// evaluated on an independent GPU thread. Box-Muller consumes exactly two, always.
    ///
    /// The radius draw is taken from `(0, 1]` rather than `[0, 1)` because `@log(0)` is -inf.
    pub fn normal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const two_pi: T = 6.283185307179586;
        const radius_draw: T = 1.0 - self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 3);
        const angle_draw: T = self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 5);
        const radius: T = @sqrt(-2.0 * @log(radius_draw));
        return radius * @cos(two_pi * angle_draw);
    }

    /// An integer in `[0, bound)`, drawn at `index` from its own stream. `bound == 0` yields 0.
    ///
    /// Lemire's multiply-shift, not `% bound`: the modulo is biased toward small values whenever
    /// `bound` does not divide 2^32, and the bias stays invisible until something like a replay
    /// buffer over-samples its early entries.
    pub fn intBelow(self: Rng, index: u32, bound: u32) u32 {
        if (bound == 0) {
            return 0;
        }
        const wide: u64 = @as(u64, self.word(index, 7)) * @as(u64, bound);
        return @intCast(wide >> 32);
    }

    /// Fill `out` with uniforms in `[0, 1)`, drawn at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillUniform(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.unitFloat(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }

    /// Fill `out` with standard normal draws, at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillNormal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.normal(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }
};

The method is unitFloat rather than float because float is reserved in this tree — zm.float is the integer-to-f32 conversion. intBelow uses Lemire's multiply-shift, not a remainder, so it carries no bias toward small values.

The bulk fills write indices 0..out.len and are exactly equivalent to calling the single-value method at each index, so the two forms mix freely.

5.4 Independent streams#

const root: zn.Rng = zn.Rng.init(1);
const weights: zn.Rng = root.split(0);
const dropout: zn.Rng = root.split(1);
Rng
/// A reproducible random source addressed by (seed, index) rather than by mutation.
///
/// WHY COUNTER-BASED, AND NOT A STATEFUL GENERATOR
///
/// A classic PRNG carries state and advances it, so drawing the i-th value means drawing the
/// first i. That is fine on one CPU thread and useless everywhere else this library is going:
///
/// * **A GPU kernel has no shared state.** Thread `i` must be able to compute ITS value without
/// any other thread having run. A counter-based generator is a pure function of the index, so
/// the kernel is `value(seed, thread_id)` and needs no sequencing at all.
/// * **CPU and GPU must agree EXACTLY.** The CPU path is this library's oracle (plan section 5 rung
/// 1); an oracle that produces a different random stream than the thing it checks is not an
/// oracle. Same function, same integers, same answer.
/// * **A failing case is reproducible from two numbers.** "seed 7, index 4096" is the whole
/// state, so a bad draw can be re-created in a unit test without replaying a sequence.
///
/// THE MIXER IS DELIBERATELY MODEST AND THE TESTS SAY SO. `mix32` is the murmur3 finalizer -
/// a bijection with good avalanche, applied to `index` folded with `seed`. It is NOT a
/// cryptographic generator and NOT claimed to pass a full statistical battery; what it is pinned
/// on is the properties this library actually relies on: determinism, range, stream independence,
/// and first/second moments. A stronger generator can replace the body without touching a single
/// call site, precisely because the interface is (seed, index) rather than a stream.
pub const Rng = struct {
    seed: u32,

    /// A generator for a given seed. Two `Rng`s with the same seed are the same function.
    pub fn init(seed: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = seed };
    }

    /// A DERIVED generator, so one seed can feed several independent uses without the caller
    /// inventing seeds. `parent.split(0)` and `parent.split(1)` are unrelated streams.
    ///
    /// Mixing the label rather than adding it: `seed + 1` and `seed + 2` are adjacent inputs to
    /// the mixer, and adjacency is exactly what a hash is asked not to preserve. Passing the
    /// label through `mix32` first means the derived seeds are as far apart as any other pair.
    pub fn split(self: Rng, label: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = mix32(self.seed ^ mix32(label ^ 0x9e3779b9)) };
    }

    /// The number of mantissa bits `T` can hold, including the implicit leading one. This is how
    /// many random bits a draw in `[0, 1)` may use: fewer wastes range, MORE ROUNDS UP TO 1.0 and
    /// breaks the half-open interval.
    fn mantissaWidth(comptime T: type) u32 {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return switch (T) {
            f16 => 11,
            f32 => 24,
            f64 => 53,
            else => unreachable,
        };
    }

    /// One 32-bit word of the generator's output at `index`, from independent stream `stream`.
    ///
    /// EACH KIND OF DRAW OWNS A STREAM, SO KINDS DO NOT INTERFERE
    ///
    /// Without this, every method would start from `bits(index)` and drawing two different KINDS
    /// at one index would return values that determine each other - `intBelow(i, n)` would be a
    /// function of `unitFloat(i)`. A caller has no reason to expect that and every reason to be
    /// hurt by it, so the stream is part of the addressing rather than the caller's problem.
    ///
    /// The stream index is folded into the MIXER, not into the index. Folding it into the index
    /// (`bits(index ^ K)`) makes indices `i` and `i ^ K` draw from the same two words with their
    /// roles swapped, so those pairs are not independent - which is exactly the defect this
    /// replaced.
    ///
    /// `stream == 0` adds nothing, so `bits` is unchanged by the introduction of rounds.
    fn word(self: Rng, index: u32, stream: u32) u32 {
        return mix32(mix32(index *% 0x9e3779b9) ^ self.seed ^ (stream *% 0x85ebca6b));
    }

    /// The raw 32-bit draw at `index`, stream 0. Distinct from every other kind of draw.
    pub fn bits(self: Rng, index: u32) u32 {
        return self.word(index, 0);
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)` from stream `stream` (and `stream + 1` when `T` needs more than 32 bits).
    ///
    /// Exactly `mantissaWidth(T)` random bits, scaled by 2^-width. The largest result is
    /// (2^width - 1) / 2^width, which is the largest `T` strictly below one - so the interval is
    /// half-open for every width, including `f16`, where using 24 bits rounds to 1.0 instead.
    fn unitFloatRound(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32, comptime stream: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const width: u32 = comptime mantissaWidth(T);
        const first: u64 = self.word(index, stream);
        const mantissa: u64 = if (comptime width <= 32)
            first >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(32 - width))
        else
            (first << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width - 32))) |
                (@as(u64, self.word(index, stream + 1)) >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(64 - width)));
        const numerator: T = @floatFromInt(mantissa);
        const denominator: T = @floatFromInt(@as(u64, 1) << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width)));
        return numerator / denominator;
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn unitFloat(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        return self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 1);
    }

    /// A float in `[low, high)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn uniform(
        self: Rng,
        comptime T: type,
        index: u32,
        low: T,
        high: T,
    ) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return low + (high - low) * self.unitFloat(T, index);
    }

    /// A standard normal draw (mean 0, variance 1) at `index`, by the Box-Muller transform.
    ///
    /// ITS TWO UNIFORMS COME FROM ITS OWN STREAMS (3-6), not from the uniform stream, so
    /// `normal(i)` and `unitFloat(i)` are independent and a caller mixing them needs no care.
    ///
    /// Box-Muller rather than a ziggurat because a ziggurat REJECTS: the number of uniforms it
    /// consumes varies per sample, which would make the value at `index` depend on how many
    /// rejections happened. That is not a pure function of the index and would not survive being
    /// evaluated on an independent GPU thread. Box-Muller consumes exactly two, always.
    ///
    /// The radius draw is taken from `(0, 1]` rather than `[0, 1)` because `@log(0)` is -inf.
    pub fn normal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const two_pi: T = 6.283185307179586;
        const radius_draw: T = 1.0 - self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 3);
        const angle_draw: T = self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 5);
        const radius: T = @sqrt(-2.0 * @log(radius_draw));
        return radius * @cos(two_pi * angle_draw);
    }

    /// An integer in `[0, bound)`, drawn at `index` from its own stream. `bound == 0` yields 0.
    ///
    /// Lemire's multiply-shift, not `% bound`: the modulo is biased toward small values whenever
    /// `bound` does not divide 2^32, and the bias stays invisible until something like a replay
    /// buffer over-samples its early entries.
    pub fn intBelow(self: Rng, index: u32, bound: u32) u32 {
        if (bound == 0) {
            return 0;
        }
        const wide: u64 = @as(u64, self.word(index, 7)) * @as(u64, bound);
        return @intCast(wide >> 32);
    }

    /// Fill `out` with uniforms in `[0, 1)`, drawn at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillUniform(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.unitFloat(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }

    /// Fill `out` with standard normal draws, at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillNormal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.normal(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }
};

split derives a generator from a label, so one seed feeds several uses without inventing seeds by hand. Labels are hashed rather than added, so split(0) and split(1) are as unrelated as any other pair.

5.5 What is checked#

GuaranteeChecked by
The same (seed, index) gives the same value, in any orderDrawing one index first from one generator and last from another
unitFloat is in [0, 1) at every float width; uniform in [low, high)50,000 draws each at f16, f32, f64
Draws of different kinds at one index are independent20,000 indices, no coincidences between bits, unitFloat, intBelow and normal
Uniform mean and variance are 1/2 and 1/12100,000 draws, tolerance about four standard errors
Normal mean and variance are 0 and 1100,000 draws, same tolerance
Different seeds and different split labels give different streamsZero identical draws across 10,000 indices
intBelow is unbiasedA bound that does not divide 232, flat to five standard deviations
The mixer is injective and avalanchesNo collisions over 50,000 values; one input bit flips 16 ± 0.5 output bits

The moment tolerances are floors: a healthy generator effectively never trips them and a broken one always does. Passing them says the generator is not obviously broken, not that it is good. It is not cryptographic.

6. Memory#

6.1 Ctx#

const ctx: zn.Ctx = zn.Ctx.init(gpa, 4242);   // { gpa, rng }

const dropout: zn.Ctx = ctx.derive(0);        // same allocator, own stream
const weights: zn.Ctx = ctx.derive(1);
Ctx
/// What a zimrnum call needs from its caller: somewhere to allocate, and a source of randomness.
///
/// WHY THIS IS A PARAMETER AND NOT A GLOBAL
///
/// A numerical library has exactly two ambient resources it is tempted to hide, and hiding either
/// one costs more than it saves. A module-level allocator makes every function's memory behaviour
/// invisible at the call site and untestable under a leak-checking allocator. A module-level
/// random state makes results depend on evaluation order, which is the property this library's
/// generator was specifically designed not to have (see `Rng`).
///
/// zimr forbids the first outright - the `module-var` lint rejects mutable file-scope state - and
/// `Ctx` is how the second stays honest too. A function that takes a `Ctx` says what it uses.
pub const Ctx = struct {
    gpa: Allocator,
    rng: Rng,

    /// A context over `gpa`, with a generator seeded by `seed`.
    pub fn init(gpa: Allocator, seed: u32) Ctx {
        return .{ .gpa = gpa, .rng = Rng.init(seed) };
    }

    /// A context sharing this allocator, with a DERIVED generator. Use it when a sub-computation
    /// needs its own random stream and must not consume this one's - `Rng.split` is what makes
    /// that free of coordination.
    pub fn derive(self: Ctx, label: u32) Ctx {
        return .{ .gpa = self.gpa, .rng = self.rng.split(label) };
    }
};

A Ctx carries the two things a numerical call needs from its caller: somewhere to allocate and a source of randomness. Both are parameters rather than globals, so a function's memory behaviour is visible at its call site and testable under a leak-checking allocator, and its results do not depend on what else has drawn.

6.2 An arena for one unit of work#

var arena: std.heap.ArenaAllocator = .init(gpa);
defer arena.deinit();
const a: Allocator = arena.allocator();

const buffer: []f64 = try a.alloc(f64, 4096);
rng.fillNormal(f64, buffer);
// no free for `buffer`: deinit releases the arena and everything in it

An arena covers one unit of work — a training step, one inference, one solve — released as a whole. Intermediates inside such a unit have exactly the arena's lifetime, so they need no individual defer.

The caller owns it. zimrnum used to wrap this in a Scope holding *std.heap.ArenaAllocator, heap-allocated so the Allocator interface stayed valid when the Scope value moved. It was removed: three lines either way, and one fewer heap allocation.

The hazard the wrapper guarded against is real but narrow, and worth knowing. Measured:

by-value arena moved, interface RE-MINTED    passes
by-value arena moved, interface CACHED       ABORTS

Do not cache an Allocator across a move of the value that made it. Re-mint from the arena where it now lives. No caller in this library ever cached one, which is why the wrapper was paying for a hazard nothing had.

A struct that must own an arena takes an out pointer:

fn initState(gpa: Allocator, s: *State) !void {
    s.arena = .init(gpa);            // written to its FINAL address
    const a = s.arena.allocator();   // minted from there
    ...
}

Nothing moves after the interface exists, so this is safer than a movable wrapper rather than merely simpler. It is also what the Zig standard library expects of you.

6.3 Reusing an arena in a loop#

var arena: std.heap.ArenaAllocator = .init(gpa);
defer arena.deinit();

for (0..epochs) |_| {
    trainOneStep(arena.allocator());
    _ = arena.reset(.retain_capacity);  // frees the step, keeps the memory
}

reset(.retain_capacity) releases the contents and retains the memory. For a loop whose iterations do the same shape of work, the first grows the arena to its high-water mark and every one after reuses it — so a training loop allocates from the parent once.

Note that arena.allocator() is called inside the loop. That costs nothing and sidesteps the caching hazard above entirely.

7. Tensors#

7.1 Creating one#

// From an arena: nothing to free.
var arena: std.heap.ArenaAllocator = .init(gpa);
defer arena.deinit();
const t = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(s.allocator(), &.{ 8, 8 });

// From an allocator you manage:
const u = try zn.Tensor(f32).alloc(gpa, &.{ 2, 3, 4 });
defer gpa.free(u.data);

// Over storage you already have:
var backing: [10]f32 = @splat(0);
const v = try zn.Tensor(f32).fromSlice(&backing, &.{ 2, 5 });
Tensor
/// A view of `T`-typed storage as a multidimensional array.
///
/// A TENSOR NEVER OWNS ITS STORAGE, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN
///
/// There is no ownership flag and no `deinit`. `data` is a slice the tensor addresses; whoever
/// allocated it frees it, with the allocator that allocated it. Usually that is an arena, in
/// which case nobody frees anything and `arena.deinit()` ends the matter.
///
/// The alternative - an `owns: bool` and a `deinit` that is a no-op for views - is the shape
/// this library was warned about before it had a tensor: both of its failure modes are SILENT.
/// `deinit` on a view does nothing and reads as if it did something; a missing `deinit` on an
/// owner just grows memory. Having no `deinit` at all means a free appears exactly where an
/// allocation appears, and a view has neither.
///
/// Strides are in ELEMENTS and signed, so a reversed or negatively-strided view is expressible
/// without changing this type. `at` computes a signed offset and casts once.
pub fn Tensor(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The storage this tensor addresses. Not owned - see the type's doc comment.
        data: []T,
        /// Extent of each axis. Only the first `rank` entries are meaningful.
        shape: [max_rank]usize,
        /// Step in ELEMENTS between consecutive positions along each axis.
        strides: [max_rank]isize,
        rank: u8,
        /// WHERE THIS VIEW STARTS INSIDE `data`
        ///
        /// Every view until now began at element 0, which is why `slice` could not exist: a
        /// window into the middle of a tensor IS an offset. With this, `slice` and the rest of
        /// znum's `index` namespace become views rather than copies.
        ///
        /// IT ALSO WEAKENS WHAT `data.len` MEANS. `data` is the whole backing store; `base`
        /// and the strides say which of it this view addresses. Everything that walked
        /// `data[0..size()]` assuming those coincide had to be corrected - five sites, all of
        /// them fast paths that skip the index walk, which is exactly where such an assumption
        /// hides.
        base: usize = 0,

        /// A dense row-major tensor over `shape`, allocated from `gpa`.
        ///
        /// The caller frees `result.data` with the same allocator - or allocates from an arena
        /// and frees nothing.
        pub fn alloc(gpa: Allocator, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            const storage: []T = gpa.alloc(T, count) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
            return fromSlice(storage, shape);
        }

        /// A dense row-major view of `data` as `shape`. `data.len` must be exactly the product of
        /// the extents - a slice that is merely LARGE ENOUGH is rejected, because the usual reason
        /// for the mismatch is a shape that is wrong rather than storage that is generous.
        pub fn fromSlice(data: []T, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != data.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            var self: Self = .{
                .data = data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                .base = 0,
            };
            // Row-major: the last axis is contiguous, and each earlier stride is the product of
            // the extents to its right.
            var stride: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = shape.len;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                self.shape[axis] = shape[axis];
                self.strides[axis] = stride;
                stride *= @intCast(shape[axis]);
            }
            return self;
        }

        /// The number of elements this tensor addresses.
        pub fn size(self: Self) usize {
            var count: usize = 1;
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                count *= self.shape[axis];
            }
            return count;
        }

        /// Whether the strides are dense row-major, so `data[0..size()]` is the tensor in order.
        ///
        /// An operation that walks memory linearly must ask this rather than assume it: a view
        /// produced by `transpose` addresses the same storage in a different order, and a linear
        /// walk over it silently reads the wrong elements rather than failing.
        pub fn isContiguous(self: Self) bool {
            var expected: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = self.rank;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                if (self.shape[axis] != 1 and self.strides[axis] != expected) {
                    return false;
                }
                expected *= @intCast(self.shape[axis]);
            }
            return true;
        }

        /// The offset into `data` of the element at `indices`.
        pub fn flatIndex(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!usize {
            if (indices.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                if (i >= self.shape[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// The element at `indices`.
        pub fn at(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!T {
            return self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)];
        }

        /// The flat offset for a coordinate, with no error.
        ///
        /// THE 45 `flatIndex(...) catch unreachable` SITES ARE ALL THIS SHAPE
        ///
        ///     const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        ///     out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = ...
        ///
        /// The slice is `walker[0..rank]`, so `indices.len != rank` **cannot** happen - the
        /// caller built it from the rank. And the coordinate came from a `Walk` over this very
        /// shape, so the bounds cannot fail either. Two impossible checks, one `catch
        /// unreachable`, on the hottest line in the library.
        ///
        /// This computes the offset and returns it. The remaining safety is `data[offset]`,
        /// which is where Zig puts it: a panic with a message in Debug and ReleaseSafe.
        ///
        /// **Use it where the rank is an invariant you have already established** - after a
        /// shape check, or inside a walk over the tensor's own shape. Use `flatIndex` where the
        /// coordinate came from outside and might be wrong.
        pub fn offsetOf(self: Self, indices: []const usize) usize {
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// Read from a rank-2 tensor. **Does not return an error.**
        ///
        /// WHY `at` HAS TO AND THIS DOES NOT
        ///
        /// `at(indices: []const usize)` can fail for two reasons. One is a real bounds check.
        /// The other is `indices.len != rank` - and that check exists ONLY because a slice hides
        /// its arity from the type system. Every call site writes a literal `&.{ i, j }`; the
        /// compiler could know it is two, and does not.
        ///
        /// Naming the arity in the function removes that half entirely. What remains is the
        /// bounds check, and **Zig already has a name for that**: it is what `data[idx]` does -
        /// a panic with a message and a stack trace in Debug and ReleaseSafe, the same UB as any
        /// other slice index in ReleaseFast.
        ///
        /// Nobody writes `arr[i] catch unreachable`. This file had **105 `catch unreachable`**,
        /// most of them on an `at` whose rank the author had checked three lines earlier, and
        /// that noise is the single biggest thing that makes numeric Zig look worse than it is.
        ///
        /// The safety is not weaker - it is Zig's, in the place Zig puts it.
        pub fn at2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-2 tensor. The mirror of `at2`, and error-free for the same reason.
        pub fn setAt2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Read from a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn at1(self: Self, i: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn setAt1(self: Self, i: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Write `value` at `indices`.
        pub fn setAt(self: Self, indices: []const usize, value: T) Error!void {
            self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)] = value;
        }

        /// Set every element to `value`.
        pub fn fill(self: Self, value: T) void {
            if (self.isContiguous()) {
                @memset(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], value);
                return;
            }
            var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var remaining: usize = self.size();
            while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
                self.data[self.offsetOf(walker[0..self.rank])] = value;
                advance(self.shape[0..self.rank], walker[0..self.rank]);
            }
        }

        /// The same storage seen as `shape`. Requires a contiguous tensor and an equal element
        /// count; both are checked rather than assumed.
        pub fn reshape(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (!self.isContiguous()) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != self.size()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // From `base`, not 0: reshaping a slice must keep addressing that slice.
            return fromSlice(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], shape);
        }

        /// Whether two distinct index positions address the same element, which happens exactly
        /// when a stretched axis (stride 0) has extent above 1.
        ///
        /// An operation that WRITES must refuse an aliased destination. Many indices landing on
        /// one element means the last write wins and every earlier one vanishes - a result that is
        /// wrong in a way no bounds check catches. `map` and `zip` check this so a caller who
        /// passes a broadcast view as an output gets an error rather than a plausible answer.
        pub fn isAliased(self: Self) bool {
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                if (self.shape[axis] > 1 and self.strides[axis] == 0) {
                    return true;
                }
            }
            return false;
        }

        /// A read-only view of this tensor stretched to `shape` by the broadcasting rule.
        ///
        /// BROADCASTING IS A STRIDE OF ZERO, NOT A COPY
        ///
        /// An axis whose extent is 1 is stretched by setting its stride to 0, so every position
        /// along it addresses the same element. No storage is allocated and no data moves; a
        /// `(3, 1, 5)` tensor viewed as `(3, 4, 5)` is the same 15 values read four times.
        ///
        /// **WRITING THROUGH A STRETCHED VIEW IS ALMOST CERTAINLY A MISTAKE.** Many indices
        /// alias one element, so the last write wins and the others vanish. This returns a view
        /// because that is what the type system has; treat it as an input.
        ///
        /// `isContiguous` reports false for any stretched axis with extent > 1, which is what
        /// stops a linear walk from reading the underlying 15 values as if they were 60.
        pub fn broadcastTo(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank or shape.len < self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = .{
                .data = self.data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                // Stretching does not move where the view starts.
                .base = self.base,
            };
            // Align at the TRAILING axis, as the broadcast rule does: an axis this tensor does not
            // have behaves as extent 1, and so does an axis it has with extent 1. Both get a
            // stride of 0.
            for (shape, 0..) |extent, axis| {
                const from_end: usize = shape.len - axis;
                view.shape[axis] = extent;
                if (self.rank < from_end) {
                    continue;
                }
                const own_axis: usize = self.rank - from_end;
                const own_extent: usize = self.shape[own_axis];
                if (own_extent == extent) {
                    view.strides[axis] = self.strides[own_axis];
                } else if (own_extent != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A window into this tensor: `len` positions along `axis`, starting at `start`.
        ///
        /// A SLICE IS AN OFFSET, WHICH IS WHY `base` EXISTS
        ///
        /// Nothing is copied: the view keeps every stride and simply starts `start` steps further
        /// into the data along one axis. A batch of 32 rows out of 4096 is a `Tensor` the same
        /// size as any other, addressing 32 rows of the same buffer.
        ///
        /// `start + len` must fit. Clamping instead would hand back a view SMALLER than asked
        /// for, and the caller's next loop would read the shape it requested rather than the one
        /// it got.
        pub fn slice(self: Self, axis: usize, start: usize, len: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (start + len > self.shape[axis]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            const step: isize = self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(start));
            const moved: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) + step;
            if (moved < 0) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            view.base = @intCast(moved);
            view.shape[axis] = len;
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with the axes reordered: `out` axis `i` is this tensor's axis `order[i]`.
        ///
        /// EVERY `manip` OPERATION HERE IS METADATA
        ///
        /// `permute`, `squeeze`, `unsqueeze`, `flatten` and `moveAxis` all return a view: they
        /// permute or insert entries in two fixed-size arrays and copy nothing. That is why they
        /// need no GPU kernel and no sweep row - **there is no arithmetic to disagree about.**
        /// The cost appears later, when a permuted view has to be BOUND to a kernel and must be
        /// densified first, which is the constraint recorded in section 10.3.
        ///
        /// `order` must be a permutation of `0..rank`. A repeated axis would produce a view
        /// where two indices alias, which is `isAliased`'s job to reject at write time - but
        /// catching it here names the actual mistake.
        pub fn permute(self: Self, order: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (order.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var seen: [max_rank]bool = @splat(false);
            for (order) |axis| {
                if (axis >= self.rank or seen[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                seen[axis] = true;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            for (order, 0..) |axis, i| {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[axis];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[axis];
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis removed.
        ///
        /// Removing an axis whose extent is NOT 1 would change how many elements the view
        /// addresses, so it is `ShapeMismatch` rather than a silent reshape.
        pub fn squeeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (self.shape[axis] != 1) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = axis;
            while (i + 1 < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i + 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i + 1];
            }
            view.shape[self.rank - 1] = 1;
            view.strides[self.rank - 1] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank - 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis inserted at `axis`. `axis == rank` appends.
        ///
        /// The new axis gets a stride of 0, which is harmless because its extent is 1 - a
        /// single position cannot step anywhere. `isAliased` only objects when extent exceeds 1.
        pub fn unsqueeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis > self.rank or self.rank + 1 > max_rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = self.rank;
            while (i > axis) : (i -= 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i - 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i - 1];
            }
            view.shape[axis] = 1;
            view.strides[axis] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank + 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A rank-1 view of the whole tensor. Contiguous only, for `reshape`'s reason.
        pub fn flatten(self: Self) Error!Self {
            return self.reshape(&.{self.size()});
        }

        /// A view with one axis moved from `from` to `to`, the others keeping their order.
        pub fn moveAxis(self: Self, from: usize, to: usize) Error!Self {
            if (from >= self.rank or to >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var order: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var k: usize = 0;
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                if (i == from) {
                    continue;
                }
                order[k] = i;
                k += 1;
            }
            // Open a slot at `to` and drop the moved axis into it.
            var j: usize = self.rank - 1;
            while (j > to) : (j -= 1) {
                order[j] = order[j - 1];
            }
            order[to] = from;
            return self.permute(order[0..self.rank]);
        }

        /// A view with axes `a` and `b` exchanged. Shares storage; changes through one are visible
        /// through the other.
        pub fn transpose(self: Self, a: usize, b: usize) Error!Self {
            if (a >= self.rank or b >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            view.shape[a] = self.shape[b];
            view.shape[b] = self.shape[a];
            view.strides[a] = self.strides[b];
            view.strides[b] = self.strides[a];
            return view;
        }
    };
}

A tensor never owns its storage. There is no ownership flag and no deinit. t.data is a slice the tensor addresses; whoever allocated it frees it, and if that was a Scope, nobody frees anything. A free therefore appears exactly where an allocation appears, and a view has neither.

fromSlice requires the extents to multiply to exactly data.len. A slice that is merely large enough is rejected.

7.2 Shape, strides, base: how indexing works#

A tensor is four public fields and no allocation of its own:

data: []T,                    // the backing store, not owned
shape: [max_rank]usize,       // extent of each axis
strides: [max_rank]isize,     // step in ELEMENTS along each axis
rank: u8,
base: usize,                  // where this view starts inside `data`

Every read and write goes through one function:

pub fn flatIndex(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!usize {
    if (indices.len != self.rank) return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
    for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
        if (i >= self.shape[axis]) return Error.OutOfRange;
        offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
    }
    return @intCast(offset);
}

That is the whole addressing model, and it explains every view operation in this section. alloc and fromSlice produce row-major strides — the last axis steps by 1, each earlier stride is the product of the extents to its right — with base zero. Everything else permutes those numbers.

Strides are signed and in elements. max_rank is 6, so shape and strides are fixed-size arrays and copying a Tensor is free.

7.3 Reading and writing elements#

try t.setAt(&.{ 1, 2 }, 3.5);
const x: f64 = try t.at(&.{ 1, 2 });
t.fill(0.0);

const n: usize = t.size();                         // total elements
const flat: usize = try t.flatIndex(&.{ 1, 2 });   // offset into t.data

at and setAt check the index count and each bound, returning ShapeMismatch or OutOfRange. They are the readable path, not the fast one: for a hot loop, take t.data and the strides and walk them yourself.

7.4 Views that cost nothing#

These return a new Tensor sharing the same data. They permute or insert entries in the shape and stride arrays and copy nothing.

const tr   = try t.transpose(0, 1);        // exchange two axes
const p    = try t.permute(&.{ 2, 0, 1 }); // any permutation
const m    = try t.moveAxis(0, 2);         // move one axis, others keep order
const up   = try t.unsqueeze(1);           // insert a size-1 axis
const down = try up.squeeze(1);            // remove one
const flat = try t.flatten();              // rank-1 view; contiguous only
const r    = try t.reshape(&.{ 4, 6 });    // contiguous only, same element count

Writing through one is visible through the others: they are the same memory. permute requires a genuine permutation of 0..rank; a repeated axis is OutOfRange. squeeze refuses an axis whose extent is not 1, because removing it would change how many elements the view addresses.

7.5 Windows into a tensor#

const batch = try data.slice(0, 128, 32);   // 32 rows starting at row 128

slice keeps every stride and moves base forward by start × strides[axis], then shortens that axis. Nothing is copied, and slices compose: a slice of a slice adds the offsets.

const mid = try t.slice(0, 1, 2);      // rows 1 and 2
try mid.setAt(&.{ 0, 0 }, -1.0);       // visible as t[1][0]

start + len must fit; a window that would run past the end is OutOfRange rather than a shorter view than requested.

7.6 Broadcasting is a stride of zero#

var shape: [zn.max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
const rank = try zn.broadcastShape(&.{ 3, 1, 5 }, &.{ 4, 5 }, &shape);
// rank == 3, shape[0..3] == { 3, 4, 5 }

const wide = try row.broadcastTo(&.{ 4, 3 });   // row is (1, 3)
// wide.strides[0] == 0 — all four rows read the same three values
max_rank
/// The most axes a tensor may have. Shape and strides are fixed-size arrays of this length, so a
/// `Tensor` is a small value with no allocation of its own and copying one is free.
///
/// Six because it covers what this library will actually meet - batch, channel, depth, height,
/// width, and one spare - and because the arrays are indexed in inner loops, where a bound the
/// compiler knows is worth more than generality nobody uses.
pub const max_rank: usize = 6;
broadcastShape
/// The broadcast shape of two operands, or `ShapeMismatch` when they do not broadcast.
///
/// The NumPy rule: align the shapes at their TRAILING axis, and for each pair the extents must be
/// equal or one of them must be 1, in which case the other wins. A missing leading axis behaves
/// as 1, so `(3, 1, 5)` and `(4, 5)` broadcast to `(3, 4, 5)`.
///
/// Returns the shape rather than a bool, because every caller that asks whether two shapes
/// broadcast immediately needs the result, and computing it twice invites the two answers to
/// disagree.
pub fn broadcastShape(
    a: []const usize,
    b: []const usize,
    out_shape: *[max_rank]usize,
) Error!u8 {
    const rank: usize = @max(a.len, b.len);
    if (rank > max_rank) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    var axis: usize = 0;
    while (axis < rank) : (axis += 1) {
        // Count from the trailing axis; an axis the shorter operand does not have behaves as 1.
        const from_end: usize = rank - axis;
        const ea: usize = if (a.len >= from_end) a[a.len - from_end] else 1;
        const eb: usize = if (b.len >= from_end) b[b.len - from_end] else 1;
        if (ea != eb and ea != 1 and eb != 1) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        out_shape[axis] = @max(ea, eb);
    }
    return @intCast(rank);
}

The NumPy rule: align the shapes at their trailing axis; for each pair the extents must be equal or one must be 1, in which case the other wins. A missing leading axis behaves as 1. Shapes that do not broadcast return ShapeMismatch — (3) against (4) is an error, not a guess.

Stretching sets the stride of a size-1 axis to zero, so every position along it addresses the same element. Nothing is allocated and no data moves.

A stretched view cannot be a destination. Many indices alias one element, so the last write would win and the rest vanish. map, zip and everything built on them return UnsupportedShape when out.isAliased().

7.7 Contiguity and aliasing#

Two predicates decide what may be done with a view.

PredicateFalse whenConsequences
isContiguous()The strides are not dense row-major — after transpose, permute, slice or a stretchreshape and flatten refuse; anything walking data linearly must not; a GPU kernel cannot bind it
isAliased()Never — it is true when a stretched axis has extent above 1Every writing operation refuses such a destination
pub fn isAliased(self: Self) bool {
    var axis: usize = 0;
    while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
        if (self.shape[axis] > 1 and self.strides[axis] == 0) return true;
    }
    return false;
}

7.8 Views that do copy#

try zn.materialise(f32, dense, view);              // any view -> dense
try zn.concat(f32, out, a, b, 0);              // join along an axis
try zn.tile(f32, out, a, 0);                   // repeat along an axis
try zn.take(f32, out, a, &.{ 3, 0, 3 }, 0);    // gather named positions
materialise
/// Copy `a` into `out`, element for element. `out` must have `a`'s shape.
///
/// THIS IS `contiguous` IN znum's SENSE, AND IT IS THE DENSIFY STEP
///
/// The view operations above copy nothing, which is what makes them free - and also what makes
/// them unbindable: a kernel takes a dense buffer and indexes from its start. `materialise` is
/// how a permuted, transposed or stretched view becomes something a GPU can read, and every
/// dispatch that meets such a view must call it first.
///
/// It reads through `at`, so ANY view works as the source: strided, permuted, stretched. The
/// destination must not be aliased, for `zip`'s reason.
pub fn materialise(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return x;
        }
    }.apply);
}
concat
/// Join `a` and `b` along `axis` into `out`.
///
/// Every axis except `axis` must match, and `out`'s `axis` extent must be the SUM. Checked
/// before anything is written, so a partially-filled output is not a state this can produce.
pub fn concat(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
    axis: usize,
) Error!void {
    if (a.rank != b.rank or out.rank != a.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (axis >= a.rank) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < a.rank) : (i += 1) {
        const want: usize = if (i == axis) a.shape[i] + b.shape[i] else a.shape[i];
        if (out.shape[i] != want) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        if (i != axis and a.shape[i] != b.shape[i]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }

    // Walk the OUTPUT and pick the source per position: `axis` below the split comes from `a`,
    // at or above it from `b` with the offset removed. Walking the output means every element is
    // written exactly once by construction.
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var src: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    var remaining: usize = out.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        @memcpy(src[0..out.rank], at);
        const from_a: bool = at[axis] < a.shape[axis];
        if (!from_a) {
            src[axis] = at[axis] - a.shape[axis];
        }
        const source: Tensor(T) = if (from_a) a else b;
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] =
            source.data[source.offsetOf(src[0..out.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..out.rank]);
    }
}
tile
/// Repeat `a` along `axis` until `out` is filled. `out`'s `axis` extent must be a whole multiple
/// of `a`'s.
///
/// A whole multiple, not "at least": a partial final tile is a different operation, and
/// silently producing one would make `tile` and `pad` indistinguishable at the call site.
pub fn tile(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    if (out.rank != a.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (axis >= a.rank or a.shape[axis] == 0) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < a.rank) : (i += 1) {
        if (i == axis) {
            if (out.shape[i] % a.shape[i] != 0) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
        } else if (out.shape[i] != a.shape[i]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }

    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var src: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    var remaining: usize = out.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        @memcpy(src[0..out.rank], at);
        src[axis] = at[axis] % a.shape[axis];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] =
            a.data[a.offsetOf(src[0..out.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..out.rank]);
    }
}
take
/// Gather rows of `a` named by `indices` along `axis` into `out`.
///
/// `indices` are `usize` in host memory rather than a `Tensor`, because on the CPU that is what
/// a caller has - a slice of positions. The GPU form will take an index BUFFER, and that
/// difference is real: this signature will not be the kernel's.
///
/// An out-of-range index is `OutOfRange`, checked BEFORE any element is written. Silently
/// clamping - which is what a GPU kernel is tempted to do, having nowhere to report - would fill
/// the output with a plausible wrong row.
pub fn take(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    indices: []const usize,
    axis: usize,
) Error!void {
    if (out.rank != a.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (axis >= a.rank) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    if (out.shape[axis] != indices.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < a.rank) : (i += 1) {
        if (i != axis and out.shape[i] != a.shape[i]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    for (indices) |idx| {
        if (idx >= a.shape[axis]) {
            return Error.OutOfRange;
        }
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }

    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var src: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    var remaining: usize = out.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        @memcpy(src[0..out.rank], at);
        src[axis] = indices[at[axis]];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] =
            a.data[a.offsetOf(src[0..out.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..out.rank]);
    }
}

materialise is the step that turns a strided or permuted view into something dense — needed before a view can be handed to a GPU kernel, which indexes from the start of a buffer. It reads through at, so any view works as the source.

concat requires every axis except the join axis to match and the output's join extent to be the sum. tile requires a whole multiple. take checks every index before writing anything; repeats are allowed and the output order follows the index list.

7.9 Named dimensions#

const Activations = zn.Named(f64, &.{ "batch", "width" });
const a = try Activations.of(raw);

try zn.sumAxis(f64, out, a.tensor, comptime Activations.axis("width"));
const flipped = try a.transposed("batch", "width");   // names follow the data
try zn.namedMatmul(f64, &.{ "batch", "width" }, &.{ "width", "classes" }, logits, a, w);
Named
/// A tensor whose axes carry names, checked at compile time.
///
/// THE MAGIC NUMBER IS THE BUG THIS REMOVES
///
/// `sumAxis(out, activations, 1)` is a sentence with a hole in it. Which axis is 1? On a
/// `(batch, width)` tensor it is the width; on a `(width, batch)` one it is the batch, and the
/// two produce different numbers of the same type with no complaint from anything. Every axis
/// argument in this file is that hole, and the only defence has been care.
///
/// `activations.axis("width")` closes it. The names live in the TYPE, so the lookup happens at
/// compile time and costs nothing at runtime - and a name that is not there is a compile error
/// naming it, rather than an index that happens to be in range.
///
/// WHAT THIS IS NOT: it is not a new tensor. `Named` wraps a `Tensor(T)` and hands it back
/// through `.tensor`, so every function in this file keeps working unchanged and a caller adopts
/// names where they help rather than everywhere at once. znum's `dimnames` is 3 302 lines
/// building a parallel API; this is the part that carries the weight.
pub fn Named(comptime T: type, comptime names: []const []const u8) type {
    comptime {
        if (names.len > max_rank) {
            @compileError("Named: too many axes");
        }
        for (names, 0..) |name, i| {
            if (name.len == 0) {
                @compileError("Named: an axis name may not be empty");
            }
            for (names[0..i]) |earlier| {
                if (std.mem.eql(u8, earlier, name)) {
                    @compileError("Named: axis name `" ++ name ++ "` is repeated");
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The tensor itself. Every zimrnum function takes this directly.
        tensor: Tensor(T),

        /// The axis names, in order.
        pub const axis_names: []const []const u8 = names;

        /// Attach names to a tensor. The rank must match, which is the one thing that cannot be
        /// checked at compile time because rank is a runtime field.
        pub fn of(tensor: Tensor(T)) Error!Self {
            if (tensor.rank != names.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            return .{ .tensor = tensor };
        }

        /// The index of the axis called `name`. A COMPILE ERROR if there is no such axis, which
        /// is the whole point: a typo cannot become an in-range index.
        pub fn axis(comptime name: []const u8) usize {
            comptime {
                for (names, 0..) |candidate, i| {
                    if (std.mem.eql(u8, candidate, name)) {
                        return i;
                    }
                }
                // The message lists what IS there, because "no axis called `wdith`" next to
                // "has: batch width" is a typo a reader fixes in a second.
                var known: []const u8 = "";
                for (names) |candidate| {
                    known = known ++ candidate ++ " ";
                }
                @compileError("Named: no axis called `" ++ name ++ "`; has: " ++ known);
            }
        }

        /// The extent of the axis called `name`.
        pub fn extent(self: Self, comptime name: []const u8) usize {
            return self.tensor.shape[comptime axis(name)];
        }

        /// Sum along a NAMED axis into `out`, whose names are this one's with that axis removed.
        ///
        /// The reason names exist. `sumAxis(out, a, 1)` and `a.sumAxis(out, "width")` compile to
        /// the same loop, and only one of them says which axis it meant.
        pub fn sumAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.sumAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// Mean along a named axis.
        pub fn meanAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.meanAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// Largest value along a named axis.
        pub fn maxAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.maxAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// A window of `len` positions along a named axis, keeping every name.
        ///
        /// Batching is exactly this: `activations.window("batch", 0, 32)` is a `Named` of the
        /// same type, so nothing downstream changes and nothing has to be told which axis was
        /// cut.
        pub fn window(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            start: usize,
            len: usize,
        ) Error!Self {
            return Self.of(try self.tensor.slice(comptime axis(name), start, len));
        }

        /// This tensor with two axes exchanged, and the names exchanged to match.
        ///
        /// The names follow the data, which is the property that makes the whole thing worth
        /// having: a transposed `(batch, width)` is a `(width, batch)`, and every later
        /// `axis("width")` on it returns the new index without the caller adjusting anything.
        pub fn transposed(
            self: Self,
            comptime first: []const u8,
            comptime second: []const u8,
        ) Error!Named(T, swapNames(names, first, second)) {
            const i: usize = comptime axis(first);
            const j: usize = comptime axis(second);
            return Named(T, swapNames(names, first, second))
                .of(try self.tensor.transpose(i, j));
        }
    };
}
sumAxis
/// Sum `a` along `axis` into `out`, which must have `a`'s shape with that axis removed.
///
/// The output's rank is one less, not one with a size-1 axis. Keeping the axis would be the
/// `keepdims` option, and options that change the RESULT SHAPE are better as separate calls than
/// as a flag a reader has to look up - a caller who wants the axis back has `reshape`.
pub fn sumAxis(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (axis >= a.rank) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    if (out.rank + 1 != a.rank or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    // The surviving axes must match, in order.
    var src: usize = 0;
    var dst: usize = 0;
    while (src < a.rank) : (src += 1) {
        if (src == axis) {
            continue;
        }
        if (a.shape[src] != out.shape[dst]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        dst += 1;
    }

    out.fill(0);
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var reduced: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    var remaining: usize = a.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        // Project the source position onto the output by dropping the reduced axis.
        var k: usize = 0;
        var i: usize = 0;
        while (i < a.rank) : (i += 1) {
            if (i == axis) {
                continue;
            }
            reduced[k] = walker[i];
            k += 1;
        }
        const at: []const usize = reduced[0..out.rank];
        const slot: usize = out.offsetOf(at);
        out.data[slot] += a.data[a.offsetOf(walker[0..a.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
}
namedMatmul
/// `out = a @ b`, with the contracted axes required to have THE SAME NAME.
///
/// THE CHECK THAT SHAPES CANNOT MAKE
///
/// `matmul` verifies that `a`'s columns equal `b`'s rows - a check on NUMBERS. Two tensors can
/// agree on that and still be the wrong pair: a `(batch, width)` times a `(width, classes)` is a
/// layer, and a `(batch, width)` times a `(batch, classes)` is nonsense that happens to typecheck
/// whenever batch and width are equal. Nothing in this file could tell them apart before.
///
/// Requiring the contracted axes to share a name is a check on MEANING, and it happens at compile
/// time. The result is named for the two surviving axes.
pub fn namedMatmul(
    comptime T: type,
    comptime a_names: []const []const u8,
    comptime b_names: []const []const u8,
    out: Named(T, &.{ a_names[0], b_names[1] }),
    a: Named(T, a_names),
    b: Named(T, b_names),
) Error!void {
    comptime {
        if (a_names.len != 2 or b_names.len != 2) {
            @compileError("namedMatmul: both operands must be rank 2");
        }
        if (!std.mem.eql(u8, a_names[1], b_names[0])) {
            @compileError("namedMatmul: contracting `" ++ a_names[1] ++ "` against `" ++
                b_names[0] ++ "` - the axes being summed over must be the same axis");
        }
    }
    return matmul(T, out.tensor, a.tensor, b.tensor);
}

sumAxis(out, activations, 1) is a sentence with a hole in it. Which axis is 1? On a (batch, width) tensor it is the width; on a (width, batch) one it is the batch, and the two produce different numbers of the same type with no complaint from anything. Every axis argument in this library is that hole, and the only defence has been care.

The names live in the type, so axis("width") resolves at compile time and costs nothing at runtime — and a name that is not there is a compile error naming it:

WrittenResult
A.axis("wdith")Named: no axis called `wdith`; has: batch width
Named(f64, &.{"batch","batch"})Named: axis name `batch` is repeated
contracting width against batchnamedMatmul: the axes being summed over must be the same axis

matmul checks that a's columns equal b's rows — a check on numbers. Two tensors can agree on that and still be the wrong pair: a (batch, width) times a (width, classes) is a layer, and a (batch, width) times a (batch, classes) is nonsense that typechecks whenever batch and width happen to be equal. namedMatmul checks meaning instead, at compile time.

The names follow the data. A transposed (batch, width) is a (width, batch), and every later axis("width") on it returns the new index without the caller adjusting anything. That is the property that makes names worth having rather than merely pleasant.

The reductions take a name instead of an index, and the result's type has that axis removed:

const Logits = zn.Named(f64, &.{ "batch", "classes" });
const PerClass = zn.Named(f64, &.{"classes"});

try logits.meanAxis("batch", per_class);      // averages over the batch
try logits.maxAxis("classes", per_sample);    // one number per sample
const mini = try inputs.window("batch", 2, 3);
Named
/// A tensor whose axes carry names, checked at compile time.
///
/// THE MAGIC NUMBER IS THE BUG THIS REMOVES
///
/// `sumAxis(out, activations, 1)` is a sentence with a hole in it. Which axis is 1? On a
/// `(batch, width)` tensor it is the width; on a `(width, batch)` one it is the batch, and the
/// two produce different numbers of the same type with no complaint from anything. Every axis
/// argument in this file is that hole, and the only defence has been care.
///
/// `activations.axis("width")` closes it. The names live in the TYPE, so the lookup happens at
/// compile time and costs nothing at runtime - and a name that is not there is a compile error
/// naming it, rather than an index that happens to be in range.
///
/// WHAT THIS IS NOT: it is not a new tensor. `Named` wraps a `Tensor(T)` and hands it back
/// through `.tensor`, so every function in this file keeps working unchanged and a caller adopts
/// names where they help rather than everywhere at once. znum's `dimnames` is 3 302 lines
/// building a parallel API; this is the part that carries the weight.
pub fn Named(comptime T: type, comptime names: []const []const u8) type {
    comptime {
        if (names.len > max_rank) {
            @compileError("Named: too many axes");
        }
        for (names, 0..) |name, i| {
            if (name.len == 0) {
                @compileError("Named: an axis name may not be empty");
            }
            for (names[0..i]) |earlier| {
                if (std.mem.eql(u8, earlier, name)) {
                    @compileError("Named: axis name `" ++ name ++ "` is repeated");
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The tensor itself. Every zimrnum function takes this directly.
        tensor: Tensor(T),

        /// The axis names, in order.
        pub const axis_names: []const []const u8 = names;

        /// Attach names to a tensor. The rank must match, which is the one thing that cannot be
        /// checked at compile time because rank is a runtime field.
        pub fn of(tensor: Tensor(T)) Error!Self {
            if (tensor.rank != names.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            return .{ .tensor = tensor };
        }

        /// The index of the axis called `name`. A COMPILE ERROR if there is no such axis, which
        /// is the whole point: a typo cannot become an in-range index.
        pub fn axis(comptime name: []const u8) usize {
            comptime {
                for (names, 0..) |candidate, i| {
                    if (std.mem.eql(u8, candidate, name)) {
                        return i;
                    }
                }
                // The message lists what IS there, because "no axis called `wdith`" next to
                // "has: batch width" is a typo a reader fixes in a second.
                var known: []const u8 = "";
                for (names) |candidate| {
                    known = known ++ candidate ++ " ";
                }
                @compileError("Named: no axis called `" ++ name ++ "`; has: " ++ known);
            }
        }

        /// The extent of the axis called `name`.
        pub fn extent(self: Self, comptime name: []const u8) usize {
            return self.tensor.shape[comptime axis(name)];
        }

        /// Sum along a NAMED axis into `out`, whose names are this one's with that axis removed.
        ///
        /// The reason names exist. `sumAxis(out, a, 1)` and `a.sumAxis(out, "width")` compile to
        /// the same loop, and only one of them says which axis it meant.
        pub fn sumAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.sumAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// Mean along a named axis.
        pub fn meanAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.meanAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// Largest value along a named axis.
        pub fn maxAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.maxAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// A window of `len` positions along a named axis, keeping every name.
        ///
        /// Batching is exactly this: `activations.window("batch", 0, 32)` is a `Named` of the
        /// same type, so nothing downstream changes and nothing has to be told which axis was
        /// cut.
        pub fn window(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            start: usize,
            len: usize,
        ) Error!Self {
            return Self.of(try self.tensor.slice(comptime axis(name), start, len));
        }

        /// This tensor with two axes exchanged, and the names exchanged to match.
        ///
        /// The names follow the data, which is the property that makes the whole thing worth
        /// having: a transposed `(batch, width)` is a `(width, batch)`, and every later
        /// `axis("width")` on it returns the new index without the caller adjusting anything.
        pub fn transposed(
            self: Self,
            comptime first: []const u8,
            comptime second: []const u8,
        ) Error!Named(T, swapNames(names, first, second)) {
            const i: usize = comptime axis(first);
            const j: usize = comptime axis(second);
            return Named(T, swapNames(names, first, second))
                .of(try self.tensor.transpose(i, j));
        }
    };
}

Naming the result is what makes this more than sugar. meanAxis("batch", x) where x is still named (batch, classes) does not compile — the axis is gone from the type, so a result of the wrong shape cannot be passed on. And window returns the same type, which is what a mini-batch is: fewer rows, same meaning, nothing downstream needing to be told.

A two-layer network reads as a sentence, and every transition in it is checked at compile time:

const Inputs = zn.Named(f64, &.{ "batch", "features" });
const W1     = zn.Named(f64, &.{ "features", "hidden" });
const Acts   = zn.Named(f64, &.{ "batch", "hidden" });
const W2     = zn.Named(f64, &.{ "hidden", "classes" });
const Logits = zn.Named(f64, &.{ "batch", "classes" });

try zn.namedMatmul(f64, Inputs.axis_names, W1.axis_names, acts, inputs, w1);
try zn.relu(f64, acts.tensor, acts.tensor);
try zn.namedMatmul(f64, Acts.axis_names, W2.axis_names, logits, acts, w2);
Named

///
/// THE MAGIC NUMBER IS THE BUG THIS REMOVES
///
/// `sumAxis(out, activations, 1)` is a sentence with a hole in it. Which axis is 1? On a

        }

        /// The index of the axis called `name`. A COMPILE ERROR if there is no such axis, which
        /// is the whole point: a typo cannot become an in-range index.

            comptime {
                for (names, 0..) |candidate, i| {
                    if (std.mem.eql(u8, candidate, name)) {
                        return i;
                    }
                }
                // The message lists what IS there, because "no axis called `wdith`" next to
                // "has: batch width" is a typo a reader fixes in a second.

                    known = known ++ candidate ++ " ";

                @compileError("Named: no axis called `" ++ name ++ "`; has: " ++ known);

        }

        /// The extent of the axis called `name`.
        pub fn extent(self: Self, comptime name: []const u8) usize {
            return self.tensor.shape[comptime axis(name)];
        }

        /// Sum along a NAMED axis into `out`, whose names are this one's with that axis removed.
        ///
        /// The reason names exist. `sumAxis(out, a, 1)` and `a.sumAxis(out, "width")` compile to
        /// the same loop, and only one of them says which axis it meant.
        pub fn sumAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.sumAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// Mean along a named axis.
        pub fn meanAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.meanAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// Largest value along a named axis.
        pub fn maxAxis(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            out: Named(T, dropName(names, name)),
        ) Error!void {
            return zimrnum.maxAxis(T, out.tensor, self.tensor, comptime axis(name));
        }

        /// A window of `len` positions along a named axis, keeping every name.
        ///
        /// Batching is exactly this: `activations.window("batch", 0, 32)` is a `Named` of the
        /// same type, so nothing downstream changes and nothing has to be told which axis was
        /// cut.
        pub fn window(
            self: Self,
            comptime name: []const u8,
            start: usize,
            len: usize,
        ) Error!Self {
            return Self.of(try self.tensor.slice(comptime axis(name), start, len));
        }

        /// This tensor with two axes exchanged, and the names exchanged to match.
        ///
        /// The names follow the data, which is the property that makes the whole thing worth
        /// having: a transposed `(batch, width)` is a `(width, batch)`, and every later
        /// `axis("width")` on it returns the new index without the caller adjusting anything.
        pub fn transposed(
            self: Self,
            comptime first: []const u8,
            comptime second: []const u8,
        ) Error!Named(T, swapNames(names, first, second)) {
            const i: usize = comptime axis(first);
            const j: usize = comptime axis(second);
            return Named(T, swapNames(names, first, second))
                .of(try self.tensor.transpose(i, j));
        }
    };
}
namedMatmul
/// `out = a @ b`, with the contracted axes required to have THE SAME NAME.
///
/// THE CHECK THAT SHAPES CANNOT MAKE
///
/// `matmul` verifies that `a`'s columns equal `b`'s rows - a check on NUMBERS. Two tensors can
/// agree on that and still be the wrong pair: a `(batch, width)` times a `(width, classes)` is a
/// layer, and a `(batch, width)` times a `(batch, classes)` is nonsense that happens to typecheck
/// whenever batch and width are equal. Nothing in this file could tell them apart before.
///
/// Requiring the contracted axes to share a name is a check on MEANING, and it happens at compile
/// time. The result is named for the two surviving axes.
pub fn namedMatmul(
    comptime T: type,
    comptime a_names: []const []const u8,
    comptime b_names: []const []const u8,
    out: Named(T, &.{ a_names[0], b_names[1] }),
    a: Named(T, a_names),
    b: Named(T, b_names),
) Error!void {
    comptime {
        if (a_names.len != 2 or b_names.len != 2) {
            @compileError("namedMatmul: both operands must be rank 2");
        }
        if (!std.mem.eql(u8, a_names[1], b_names[0])) {
