            @compileError("namedMatmul: contracting `" ++ a_names[1] ++ "` against `" ++
                b_names[0] ++ "` - the axes being summed over must be the same axis");
        }
    }
    return matmul(T, out.tensor, a.tensor, b.tensor);
}
relu
/// `out = max(a, 0)`, elementwise.
pub fn relu(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return if (x > 0) x else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}

Where names do not reach: inside the autograd tape. graph.matmul returns a Var, which is an index into the graph and carries no names. Names help where you hold tensors and pass axis indices — reductions, products, windows — and the tape is deliberately not that. Saying so is better than implying a guarantee that stops at the first graph. call.

Named wraps a Tensor and hands it back through .tensor, so every function here keeps working unchanged and names are adopted where they help rather than everywhere at once. znum's dimnames is 3 302 lines building a parallel API; this is the part that carries the weight.

7.10 Meshes, repeats and moving an axis#

try zn.meshGrid(f64, grid_x, grid_y, xs, ys);   // both grids, both NAMED
try zn.repeatEach(f64, out, a, 0, 2);           // [1,2,3] -> [1,1,2,2,3,3]
try zn.tile(f64, out, a, 0);                    // [1,2,3] -> [1,2,3,1,2,3]
const moved = try zn.moveAxis(f64, cube, 0, 2); // a view, costs nothing
meshGrid
/// The two coordinate grids of a rectangular mesh.
///
/// `out_x[i][j] = x[j]` and `out_y[i][j] = y[i]`, so a pair of 1-D axes becomes the full grid of
/// positions a surface or a plot needs. `x` supplies the columns and `y` the rows, which is the
/// order every plotting library uses and the opposite of what the argument order suggests - hence
/// the outputs being named rather than returned as a two-element array.
///
/// **znum returns `[2]Tensor(T)` and the caller has to remember which is which.** Here the caller
/// allocates both and names them at the call site, so `out_x` cannot be mistaken for `out_y`.
pub fn meshGrid(
    comptime T: type,
    out_x: Tensor(T),
    out_y: Tensor(T),
    x: Tensor(T),
    y: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (x.rank != 1 or y.rank != 1) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const rows: usize = y.shape[0];
    const cols: usize = x.shape[0];
    if (out_x.rank != 2 or out_y.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out_x.shape[0] != rows or out_x.shape[1] != cols) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (out_y.shape[0] != rows or out_y.shape[1] != cols) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..rows) |row| {
        for (0..cols) |col| {
            try out_x.setAt(&.{ row, col }, try x.at(&.{col}));
            try out_y.setAt(&.{ row, col }, try y.at(&.{row}));
        }
    }
}
repeatEach
/// Each element repeated `count` times along `axis`.
///
/// REPEATING ELEMENTS IS NOT REPEATING THE TENSOR, AND THE NAMES SHOULD SAY WHICH
///
/// numpy calls this `repeat` and calls the other one `tile`, and the pair is a well-known source
/// of confusion because neither name says what varies:
///
///     repeatEach([1, 2, 3], 2)  ->  [1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3]     each element, in place
///     tile      ([1, 2, 3], 2)  ->  [1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 3]     the whole thing, end to end
///
/// `tile` was already here under numpy's name; this one is `repeatEach` rather than `repeat`
/// because the "each" is the entire difference.
pub fn repeatEach(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    axis: usize,
    count: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (out.rank != a.rank or axis >= a.rank) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (count == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    for (0..a.rank) |d| {
        const want: usize = if (d == axis) a.shape[d] * count else a.shape[d];
        if (out.shape[d] != want) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    var source: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var walk: Walk = .over(out.shape[0..out.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        for (0..out.rank) |d| {
            // Every position maps back by dividing the repeated axis; the rest pass through.
            source[d] = if (d == axis) at[d] / count else at[d];
        }
        try out.setAt(at, try a.at(source[0..a.rank]));
    }
}
tile
/// Repeat `a` along `axis` until `out` is filled. `out`'s `axis` extent must be a whole multiple
/// of `a`'s.
///
/// A whole multiple, not "at least": a partial final tile is a different operation, and
/// silently producing one would make `tile` and `pad` indistinguishable at the call site.
pub fn tile(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    if (out.rank != a.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (axis >= a.rank or a.shape[axis] == 0) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < a.rank) : (i += 1) {
        if (i == axis) {
            if (out.shape[i] % a.shape[i] != 0) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
        } else if (out.shape[i] != a.shape[i]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }

    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var src: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    var remaining: usize = out.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        @memcpy(src[0..out.rank], at);
        src[axis] = at[axis] % a.shape[axis];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] =
            a.data[a.offsetOf(src[0..out.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..out.rank]);
    }
}
moveAxis
/// A view of `a` with `axis` moved to sit at `destination`, the others closing the gap.
///
/// A VIEW, NOT A COPY - AND THAT IS WHY IT IS NOT `transposed`
///
/// `transposed(i, j)` swaps two axes and leaves the rest alone. Moving an axis is different: the
/// axes between source and destination all SHIFT to make room, which is what you want when a
/// batch dimension has to travel from the front to the back. numpy calls it `moveaxis`; this is
/// the same operation with the house's camelCase.
///
/// Like `transposed` it only permutes strides, so it costs nothing and aliases `a`'s storage.
pub fn moveAxis(
    comptime T: type,
    a: Tensor(T),
    axis: usize,
    destination: usize,
) Error!Tensor(T) {
    if (axis >= a.rank or destination >= a.rank) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    var order: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var next: usize = 0;
    // Lay down every axis except the one moving, then insert it at `destination`.
    for (0..a.rank) |slot| {
        if (slot == destination) {
            order[slot] = axis;
            continue;
        }
        if (next == axis) {
            next += 1;
        }
        order[slot] = next;
        next += 1;
    }
    return a.permute(order[0..a.rank]);
}

numpy calls the second one repeat and the third tile, and the pair is a well-known source of confusion because neither name says what varies. Here the first is repeatEach: the "each" is the difference, and putting it in the name means the two lines above read differently at a glance instead of needing the docs.

meshGrid takes both outputs as named parameters rather than returning [2]Tensor. znum returns the pair and the caller has to remember which is which; grid_x and grid_y cannot be mistaken for each other at the call site. Note the axis order: x supplies the columns and y the rows, which is what every plotting library expects and the opposite of what reading the arguments left to right suggests.

moveAxis is not a swap. Swapping trades two axes and leaves the rest alone; moving an axis makes the ones in between shift to close the gap — which is what you want when a batch dimension has to travel from the front to the back. On rank 2 the two are identical, so the test uses rank 3 where they differ. Like every other view here it only permutes strides, so it shares storage and costs nothing.

Three of znum's manipulation functions are deliberately absent. hstack, vstack and dstack are concat with the axis baked in, and znum's own doc admits the trap ("this does not promote 1-D to 2-D the way numpy's vstack does") — concat(axis) says the same thing without the footnote. And reshapeInfer, which lets one dimension be -1, exists because znum allocates its result and must be told the shape. Here the caller allocates, so the shape is already known and there is nothing to infer.

7.11 The -1 dimension, and stack versus concat#

resolveInferredShape(12, &.{ 2, -1 })   ->  2 x 6

"Two rows, work out the columns" — the one dimension you did not want to compute by hand. The alternative is x.size() / 2 written at the call site, which is the same arithmetic with a chance of going stale when the shape changes.

Three ways it goes wrong, all refused

{ -1, -1 }                 infinitely many answers - picking one invents an intention
{ 2, -2 }                  -2 is not shorthand for anything; it is a typo for 2 often enough
{ 3, -1 } on 10 elements   no integer answer - rounding would drop an element

A shape with no -1 passes through unchanged, so one function serves both cases — but it still has to account for every element. A shape holding fewer is a silent data loss and gets the same refusal.

stack versus concat

These are the two that get mixed up, because both take a list and an axis and the shapes look alike:

concat two (2,3) along axis 0   ->  (4,3)     same rank, one axis GREW
stack  two (2,3) at axis 0      ->  (2,2,3)   one rank HIGHER, a NEW axis

Stacking is "these samples are one batch". Concatenating is "these rows are more rows".

The axis is a parameter rather than always zero because stacking at a later axis interleaves rather than appending — which is what you want when the new dimension belongs inside the existing structure rather than outside it.

swapaxes returns a view

The strides trade places and no element moves. So it is cheap, the result may be non-contiguous, and writing through it writes through to the original — which the test checks, because a "copy" that turns out to be a view is a bug that surfaces far from its cause. materialise if you need it packed.

8. Elementwise operations#

8.1 map and zip#

try zn.map(f64, out, src, struct {
    fn apply(v: f64) f64 { return zm.sigmoid(v); }
}.apply);

try zn.zip(f64, out, a, b, struct {
    fn apply(x: f64, y: f64) f64 { return @max(x, y); }
}.apply);
map
/// Write `f(x)` into `out` for each element `x` of `a`, broadcasting `a` to `out`'s shape.
pub fn map(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    comptime f: fn (T) T,
) Error!void {
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    const count: usize = out.size();

    if (out.isContiguous() and va.isContiguous() and va.data.len >= va.base + count) {
        for (out.data[out.base..][0..count], va.data[va.base..][0..count]) |*slot, x| {
            slot.* = f(x);
        }
        return;
    }
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var remaining: usize = count;
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        const x: T = va.data[va.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = f(x);
        advance(shape, walker[0..out.rank]);
    }
}
zip
/// Write `f(x, y)` into `out`, broadcasting `a` and `b` to `out`'s shape.
///
/// `out` DEFINES THE SHAPE, AND THE INPUTS MUST REACH IT
///
/// The destination is not inferred. A caller sizes `out` - usually from `broadcastShape` - and
/// both inputs must broadcast to exactly that. Inferring it instead would mean an output whose
/// shape depends on the inputs' shapes in a way the call site does not show, and a mistake there
/// allocates the wrong size rather than reporting anything.
///
/// The contiguous case walks three slices linearly; anything strided or stretched walks the
/// shared index. The fast path additionally checks `data.len`, because a stretched view's `size`
/// exceeds its storage and reasoning about when that cannot happen is worth less than the compare.
pub fn zip(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
    comptime f: fn (T, T) T,
) Error!void {
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    const vb: Tensor(T) = try b.broadcastTo(shape);
    const count: usize = out.size();

    const all_dense: bool = out.isContiguous() and va.isContiguous() and vb.isContiguous() and
        va.data.len >= va.base + count and vb.data.len >= vb.base + count;
    if (all_dense) {
        for (out.data[out.base..][0..count], va.data[va.base..][0..count], vb.data[vb.base..][0..count]) |*slot, x, y| {
            slot.* = f(x, y);
        }
        return;
    }
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var remaining: usize = count;
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        const x: T = va.data[va.offsetOf(at)];
        const y: T = vb.data[vb.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = f(x, y);
        advance(shape, walker[0..out.rank]);
    }
}

The function is a comptime parameter, so it inlines and there is no call per element. Every named operation below is a one-line wrapper over one of these.

The output defines the shape. It is not inferred: you size out — usually from broadcastShape — and both inputs must broadcast to exactly that, or the call returns ShapeMismatch before writing anything.

Both take a linear pass when every tensor involved is contiguous and an index walk otherwise, so a transposed destination works and gives the same answer.

8.2 Arithmetic#

try zn.add(f64, out, a, b);
try zn.sub(f64, out, a, b);
try zn.mul(f64, out, a, b);   // elementwise, not a matrix product
try zn.div(f64, out, a, b);   // float only
try zn.scale(f64, out, a, 0.1);
try zn.minimum(f64, out, a, b);
try zn.maximum(f64, out, a, b);
try zn.clamp(f64, out, a, -0.5, 0.5);
try zn.lerp(f64, out, a, b, 0.25);
add
/// `out = a + b`, broadcasting both to `out`'s shape.
pub fn add(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return x + y;
        }
    }.apply);
}
sub
/// `out = a - b`, broadcasting both to `out`'s shape.
pub fn sub(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return x - y;
        }
    }.apply);
}
mul
/// `out = a * b` ELEMENTWISE, broadcasting both to `out`'s shape. This is not a matrix product.
pub fn mul(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return x * y;
        }
    }.apply);
}
div
/// `out = a / b`, broadcasting both to `out`'s shape.
///
/// Float only. Zig has no single `/` for signed integers - the caller must choose between
/// truncating, flooring and exact division - and picking one here would be this library deciding
/// something the language deliberately asks about. Integer division goes through `zip` with the
/// `@div*` the caller means.
pub fn div(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return x / y;
        }
    }.apply);
}
scale
/// `out = a * factor`, elementwise.
///
/// A separate entry point rather than `mul` against a broadcast scalar tensor, because there is
/// no rank-0 tensor here and wrapping one value in a `[1]` allocation to multiply by it is more
/// machinery than the operation. `zip` cannot help either: its function is a comptime parameter
/// and cannot close over a runtime `factor`.
pub fn scale(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), factor: T) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] =
            va.data[va.offsetOf(at)] * factor;
    }
}
minimum
/// `out = min(a, b)`, elementwise. `minimum`, not `min`, because `min` is a reserved math word.
pub fn minimum(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return @min(x, y);
        }
    }.apply);
}
maximum
/// `out = max(a, b)`, elementwise.
pub fn maximum(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return @max(x, y);
        }
    }.apply);
}
clamp
/// `out = min(max(a, lo), hi)`, elementwise.
///
/// `lo > hi` is not rejected: the result is then `hi` everywhere, which is what the nested
/// min/max gives and what every other library does. Erroring would be defensible; being SILENTLY
/// different from everyone else would not.
pub fn clamp(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    lo: T,
    hi: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = va.data[va.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = @min(@max(x, lo), hi);
    }
}
lerp
/// `out = a + t * (b - a)`, elementwise.
///
/// NEITHER ONE-LINE FORM IS EXACT AT BOTH ENDS, SO THIS USES TWO
///
/// `a + t*(b-a)` is exact at t = 0 and **not** at t = 1: `x + (y - x)` is not `y` in floating
/// point once the magnitudes differ. `(1-t)*a + t*b` is exact at t = 1 and not at t = 0. I wrote
/// the first form with a comment claiming both, and the endpoint test caught it within a minute.
///
/// So it switches at the midpoint and interpolates from the NEARER endpoint, which is what
/// C++20's `std::lerp` does and for this reason. Exact at both ends, and monotonic in between.
///
/// The branch is on `t` - a scalar the caller passes, identical in every lane - so it costs a
/// GPU nothing. A branch on the DATA would be a different matter.
pub fn lerp(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
    t: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    const vb: Tensor(T) = try b.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = va.data[va.offsetOf(at)];
        const y: T = vb.data[vb.offsetOf(at)];
        const v: T = if (t <= 0.5) x + t * (y - x) else y - (1.0 - t) * (y - x);
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = v;
    }
}

All broadcast their inputs to out's shape, so a row and a column produce a grid:

const row = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(a, &.{ 1, 3 });   // 1, 2, 3
const col = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(a, &.{ 4, 1 });   // 10, 20, 30, 40
const out = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(a, &.{ 4, 3 });

try zn.add(f64, out, row, col);   // out[i][j] == col[i] + row[j]
Tensor
/// A view of `T`-typed storage as a multidimensional array.
///
/// A TENSOR NEVER OWNS ITS STORAGE, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN
///
/// There is no ownership flag and no `deinit`. `data` is a slice the tensor addresses; whoever
/// allocated it frees it, with the allocator that allocated it. Usually that is an arena, in
/// which case nobody frees anything and `arena.deinit()` ends the matter.
///
/// The alternative - an `owns: bool` and a `deinit` that is a no-op for views - is the shape
/// this library was warned about before it had a tensor: both of its failure modes are SILENT.
/// `deinit` on a view does nothing and reads as if it did something; a missing `deinit` on an
/// owner just grows memory. Having no `deinit` at all means a free appears exactly where an
/// allocation appears, and a view has neither.
///
/// Strides are in ELEMENTS and signed, so a reversed or negatively-strided view is expressible
/// without changing this type. `at` computes a signed offset and casts once.
pub fn Tensor(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The storage this tensor addresses. Not owned - see the type's doc comment.
        data: []T,
        /// Extent of each axis. Only the first `rank` entries are meaningful.
        shape: [max_rank]usize,
        /// Step in ELEMENTS between consecutive positions along each axis.
        strides: [max_rank]isize,
        rank: u8,
        /// WHERE THIS VIEW STARTS INSIDE `data`
        ///
        /// Every view until now began at element 0, which is why `slice` could not exist: a
        /// window into the middle of a tensor IS an offset. With this, `slice` and the rest of
        /// znum's `index` namespace become views rather than copies.
        ///
        /// IT ALSO WEAKENS WHAT `data.len` MEANS. `data` is the whole backing store; `base`
        /// and the strides say which of it this view addresses. Everything that walked
        /// `data[0..size()]` assuming those coincide had to be corrected - five sites, all of
        /// them fast paths that skip the index walk, which is exactly where such an assumption
        /// hides.
        base: usize = 0,

        /// A dense row-major tensor over `shape`, allocated from `gpa`.
        ///
        /// The caller frees `result.data` with the same allocator - or allocates from an arena
        /// and frees nothing.
        pub fn alloc(gpa: Allocator, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            const storage: []T = gpa.alloc(T, count) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
            return fromSlice(storage, shape);
        }

        /// A dense row-major view of `data` as `shape`. `data.len` must be exactly the product of
        /// the extents - a slice that is merely LARGE ENOUGH is rejected, because the usual reason
        /// for the mismatch is a shape that is wrong rather than storage that is generous.
        pub fn fromSlice(data: []T, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != data.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            var self: Self = .{
                .data = data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                .base = 0,
            };
            // Row-major: the last axis is contiguous, and each earlier stride is the product of
            // the extents to its right.
            var stride: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = shape.len;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                self.shape[axis] = shape[axis];
                self.strides[axis] = stride;
                stride *= @intCast(shape[axis]);
            }
            return self;
        }

        /// The number of elements this tensor addresses.
        pub fn size(self: Self) usize {
            var count: usize = 1;
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                count *= self.shape[axis];
            }
            return count;
        }

        /// Whether the strides are dense row-major, so `data[0..size()]` is the tensor in order.
        ///
        /// An operation that walks memory linearly must ask this rather than assume it: a view
        /// produced by `transpose` addresses the same storage in a different order, and a linear
        /// walk over it silently reads the wrong elements rather than failing.
        pub fn isContiguous(self: Self) bool {
            var expected: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = self.rank;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                if (self.shape[axis] != 1 and self.strides[axis] != expected) {
                    return false;
                }
                expected *= @intCast(self.shape[axis]);
            }
            return true;
        }

        /// The offset into `data` of the element at `indices`.
        pub fn flatIndex(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!usize {
            if (indices.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                if (i >= self.shape[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// The element at `indices`.
        pub fn at(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!T {
            return self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)];
        }

        /// The flat offset for a coordinate, with no error.
        ///
        /// THE 45 `flatIndex(...) catch unreachable` SITES ARE ALL THIS SHAPE
        ///
        ///     const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        ///     out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = ...
        ///
        /// The slice is `walker[0..rank]`, so `indices.len != rank` **cannot** happen - the
        /// caller built it from the rank. And the coordinate came from a `Walk` over this very
        /// shape, so the bounds cannot fail either. Two impossible checks, one `catch
        /// unreachable`, on the hottest line in the library.
        ///
        /// This computes the offset and returns it. The remaining safety is `data[offset]`,
        /// which is where Zig puts it: a panic with a message in Debug and ReleaseSafe.
        ///
        /// **Use it where the rank is an invariant you have already established** - after a
        /// shape check, or inside a walk over the tensor's own shape. Use `flatIndex` where the
        /// coordinate came from outside and might be wrong.
        pub fn offsetOf(self: Self, indices: []const usize) usize {
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// Read from a rank-2 tensor. **Does not return an error.**
        ///
        /// WHY `at` HAS TO AND THIS DOES NOT
        ///
        /// `at(indices: []const usize)` can fail for two reasons. One is a real bounds check.
        /// The other is `indices.len != rank` - and that check exists ONLY because a slice hides
        /// its arity from the type system. Every call site writes a literal `&.{ i, j }`; the
        /// compiler could know it is two, and does not.
        ///
        /// Naming the arity in the function removes that half entirely. What remains is the
        /// bounds check, and **Zig already has a name for that**: it is what `data[idx]` does -
        /// a panic with a message and a stack trace in Debug and ReleaseSafe, the same UB as any
        /// other slice index in ReleaseFast.
        ///
        /// Nobody writes `arr[i] catch unreachable`. This file had **105 `catch unreachable`**,
        /// most of them on an `at` whose rank the author had checked three lines earlier, and
        /// that noise is the single biggest thing that makes numeric Zig look worse than it is.
        ///
        /// The safety is not weaker - it is Zig's, in the place Zig puts it.
        pub fn at2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-2 tensor. The mirror of `at2`, and error-free for the same reason.
        pub fn setAt2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Read from a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn at1(self: Self, i: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn setAt1(self: Self, i: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Write `value` at `indices`.
        pub fn setAt(self: Self, indices: []const usize, value: T) Error!void {
            self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)] = value;
        }

        /// Set every element to `value`.
        pub fn fill(self: Self, value: T) void {
            if (self.isContiguous()) {
                @memset(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], value);
                return;
            }
            var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var remaining: usize = self.size();
            while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
                self.data[self.offsetOf(walker[0..self.rank])] = value;
                advance(self.shape[0..self.rank], walker[0..self.rank]);
            }
        }

        /// The same storage seen as `shape`. Requires a contiguous tensor and an equal element
        /// count; both are checked rather than assumed.
        pub fn reshape(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (!self.isContiguous()) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != self.size()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // From `base`, not 0: reshaping a slice must keep addressing that slice.
            return fromSlice(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], shape);
        }

        /// Whether two distinct index positions address the same element, which happens exactly
        /// when a stretched axis (stride 0) has extent above 1.
        ///
        /// An operation that WRITES must refuse an aliased destination. Many indices landing on
        /// one element means the last write wins and every earlier one vanishes - a result that is
        /// wrong in a way no bounds check catches. `map` and `zip` check this so a caller who
        /// passes a broadcast view as an output gets an error rather than a plausible answer.
        pub fn isAliased(self: Self) bool {
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                if (self.shape[axis] > 1 and self.strides[axis] == 0) {
                    return true;
                }
            }
            return false;
        }

        /// A read-only view of this tensor stretched to `shape` by the broadcasting rule.
        ///
        /// BROADCASTING IS A STRIDE OF ZERO, NOT A COPY
        ///
        /// An axis whose extent is 1 is stretched by setting its stride to 0, so every position
        /// along it addresses the same element. No storage is allocated and no data moves; a
        /// `(3, 1, 5)` tensor viewed as `(3, 4, 5)` is the same 15 values read four times.
        ///
        /// **WRITING THROUGH A STRETCHED VIEW IS ALMOST CERTAINLY A MISTAKE.** Many indices
        /// alias one element, so the last write wins and the others vanish. This returns a view
        /// because that is what the type system has; treat it as an input.
        ///
        /// `isContiguous` reports false for any stretched axis with extent > 1, which is what
        /// stops a linear walk from reading the underlying 15 values as if they were 60.
        pub fn broadcastTo(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank or shape.len < self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = .{
                .data = self.data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                // Stretching does not move where the view starts.
                .base = self.base,
            };
            // Align at the TRAILING axis, as the broadcast rule does: an axis this tensor does not
            // have behaves as extent 1, and so does an axis it has with extent 1. Both get a
            // stride of 0.
            for (shape, 0..) |extent, axis| {
                const from_end: usize = shape.len - axis;
                view.shape[axis] = extent;
                if (self.rank < from_end) {
                    continue;
                }
                const own_axis: usize = self.rank - from_end;
                const own_extent: usize = self.shape[own_axis];
                if (own_extent == extent) {
                    view.strides[axis] = self.strides[own_axis];
                } else if (own_extent != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A window into this tensor: `len` positions along `axis`, starting at `start`.
        ///
        /// A SLICE IS AN OFFSET, WHICH IS WHY `base` EXISTS
        ///
        /// Nothing is copied: the view keeps every stride and simply starts `start` steps further
        /// into the data along one axis. A batch of 32 rows out of 4096 is a `Tensor` the same
        /// size as any other, addressing 32 rows of the same buffer.
        ///
        /// `start + len` must fit. Clamping instead would hand back a view SMALLER than asked
        /// for, and the caller's next loop would read the shape it requested rather than the one
        /// it got.
        pub fn slice(self: Self, axis: usize, start: usize, len: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (start + len > self.shape[axis]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            const step: isize = self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(start));
            const moved: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) + step;
            if (moved < 0) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            view.base = @intCast(moved);
            view.shape[axis] = len;
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with the axes reordered: `out` axis `i` is this tensor's axis `order[i]`.
        ///
        /// EVERY `manip` OPERATION HERE IS METADATA
        ///
        /// `permute`, `squeeze`, `unsqueeze`, `flatten` and `moveAxis` all return a view: they
        /// permute or insert entries in two fixed-size arrays and copy nothing. That is why they
        /// need no GPU kernel and no sweep row - **there is no arithmetic to disagree about.**
        /// The cost appears later, when a permuted view has to be BOUND to a kernel and must be
        /// densified first, which is the constraint recorded in section 10.3.
        ///
        /// `order` must be a permutation of `0..rank`. A repeated axis would produce a view
        /// where two indices alias, which is `isAliased`'s job to reject at write time - but
        /// catching it here names the actual mistake.
        pub fn permute(self: Self, order: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (order.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var seen: [max_rank]bool = @splat(false);
            for (order) |axis| {
                if (axis >= self.rank or seen[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                seen[axis] = true;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            for (order, 0..) |axis, i| {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[axis];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[axis];
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis removed.
        ///
        /// Removing an axis whose extent is NOT 1 would change how many elements the view
        /// addresses, so it is `ShapeMismatch` rather than a silent reshape.
        pub fn squeeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (self.shape[axis] != 1) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = axis;
            while (i + 1 < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i + 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i + 1];
            }
            view.shape[self.rank - 1] = 1;
            view.strides[self.rank - 1] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank - 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis inserted at `axis`. `axis == rank` appends.
        ///
        /// The new axis gets a stride of 0, which is harmless because its extent is 1 - a
        /// single position cannot step anywhere. `isAliased` only objects when extent exceeds 1.
        pub fn unsqueeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis > self.rank or self.rank + 1 > max_rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = self.rank;
            while (i > axis) : (i -= 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i - 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i - 1];
            }
            view.shape[axis] = 1;
            view.strides[axis] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank + 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A rank-1 view of the whole tensor. Contiguous only, for `reshape`'s reason.
        pub fn flatten(self: Self) Error!Self {
            return self.reshape(&.{self.size()});
        }

        /// A view with one axis moved from `from` to `to`, the others keeping their order.
        pub fn moveAxis(self: Self, from: usize, to: usize) Error!Self {
            if (from >= self.rank or to >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var order: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var k: usize = 0;
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                if (i == from) {
                    continue;
                }
                order[k] = i;
                k += 1;
            }
            // Open a slot at `to` and drop the moved axis into it.
            var j: usize = self.rank - 1;
            while (j > to) : (j -= 1) {
                order[j] = order[j - 1];
            }
            order[to] = from;
            return self.permute(order[0..self.rank]);
        }

        /// A view with axes `a` and `b` exchanged. Shares storage; changes through one are visible
        /// through the other.
        pub fn transpose(self: Self, a: usize, b: usize) Error!Self {
            if (a >= self.rank or b >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            view.shape[a] = self.shape[b];
            view.shape[b] = self.shape[a];
            view.strides[a] = self.strides[b];
            view.strides[b] = self.strides[a];
            return view;
        }
    };
}
add
/// `out = a + b`, broadcasting both to `out`'s shape.
pub fn add(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return x + y;
        }
    }.apply);
}

div is float-only: Zig has no single / for signed integers, and this library does not choose between truncating, flooring and exact division on your behalf. Integer division goes through zip.

scale and lerp take a runtime factor, which zip's comptime function cannot close over, so they are written as loops. lerp interpolates from the nearer endpoint:

const v: T = if (t <= 0.5) x + t * (y - x) else y - (1.0 - t) * (y - x);

which returns exactly a at t = 0 and exactly b at t = 1. Neither one-line form does both.

8.3 Comparison masks#

try zn.greater(f64, out, a, b);   // 1 where a > b, else 0
try zn.less(f64, out, a, b);
try zn.equal(f64, out, a, b);     // exact equality
try zn.notEqual(f64, out, a, b);
try zn.greaterEqual(f64, out, a, b);
try zn.lessEqual(f64, out, a, b);
greater
/// `out = 1 where a > b, else 0`. A MASK, not a bool tensor.
///
/// WHY COMPARISONS RETURN THE ELEMENT TYPE
///
/// A `Tensor(bool)` would be the obvious answer and is the wrong one here: a mask's whole purpose
/// is to be multiplied by something, and a bool tensor forces every consumer to convert. It also
/// cannot be a GPU buffer of the same element type, so the kernel would need a second dtype for
/// masks alone. Returning 0 or 1 in `T` keeps `mask * value` working on both backends with no
/// conversion anywhere.
pub fn greater(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return if (x > y) 1 else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
less
/// `out = 1 where a < b, else 0`. See `greater`.
pub fn less(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return if (x < y) 1 else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
equal
/// `out = 1 where a == b, else 0`.
///
/// EXACT equality, not a tolerance. A comparison with a built-in epsilon is a different
/// operation wearing this one's name, and a caller who wants tolerance can write
/// `less(|a - b|, eps)` and see the choice. NaN equals nothing, including itself.
pub fn equal(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return if (x == y) 1 else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
notEqual
/// `out = 1 where a != b, else 0`. The complement of `equal`.
pub fn notEqual(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return if (x != y) 1 else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
greaterEqual
/// `out = 1 where a >= b, else 0`.
pub fn greaterEqual(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return if (x >= y) 1 else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
lessEqual
/// `out = 1 where a <= b, else 0`.
pub fn lessEqual(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return if (x <= y) 1 else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}

These return 1 or 0 in the element type, not a Tensor(bool). A mask exists to be multiplied, and keeping it in T means mask * value works on both backends with no conversion and no second buffer type.

The six masks partition in pairs — equal + notEqual, greater + lessEqual, less + greaterEqual each sum to 1 at every element — and for any pair of values exactly one of greater, less and equal is 1. equal compares exactly; a comparison with a built-in epsilon would be a different operation, and a caller who wants tolerance writes less(|a - b|, eps) and sees the choice.

8.4 Rounding, sign and magnitude#

try zn.floor(f64, out, a);   try zn.ceil(f64, out, a);
try zn.trunc(f64, out, a);   try zn.round(f64, out, a);
try zn.abs(f64, out, a);     try zn.neg(f64, out, a);
try zn.sign(f64, out, a);    try zn.square(f64, out, a);
floor
/// `out = floor(a)`, elementwise.
pub fn floor(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return @floor(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
ceil
/// `out = ceil(a)`, elementwise.
pub fn ceil(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return @ceil(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
trunc
/// `out = scalarTrunc(a)` - toward zero, elementwise.
pub fn trunc(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            // `zm.trunc` gained a scalar branch this turn; using it keeps one implementation.
            return scalarTrunc(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
round
/// `out = scalarRound(a)` - half away from zero, elementwise. Not banker's rounding: `zm.round` is
/// what the engine and the GPU both use, and matching it matters more than the tie rule.
pub fn round(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return scalarRound(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
abs
/// `out = |a|`, elementwise. `abs` for the same reason `exp` is not `abs`.
pub fn abs(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            // `@abs` ON A SIGNED INTEGER RETURNS AN UNSIGNED ONE, which is correct of the
            // builtin and wrong for a function whose output tensor has the input's type. The
            // negation says the same thing and keeps the type.
            //
            // It also inherits the honest edge: the most negative integer has no positive
            // counterpart, so `abs(minInt)` overflows rather than quietly returning itself.
            if (comptime !isFloat(T)) {
                return if (x < 0) -x else x;
            }
            return @abs(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
neg
/// `out = -a`, elementwise.
pub fn neg(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return -x;
        }
    }.apply);
}
sign
/// `out = -1, 0 or +1` by the sign of `a`. **Zero maps to zero**, not to +1: `sign` is used to
/// carry a direction, and giving a stationary point a direction is how a gradient acquires drift
/// that nothing accounts for.
pub fn sign(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            if (x > 0) {
                return 1;
            }
            if (x < 0) {
                return -1;
            }
            return 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
square
/// `out = a * a`, elementwise. Not `pow(a, 2)`: a multiply is exact where a general power goes
/// through `exp(2*log(a))` and is neither exact nor defined for negatives.
pub fn square(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return x * x;
        }
    }.apply);
}

trunc rounds toward zero and floor downward; they differ exactly on the negatives. round is half away from zero. sign maps zero to zero, so sign(x) * |x| == x holds for every input.

The suffixes exist because abs, exp, floor, ceil, round, trunc, sqrt, log, sin, cos, clamp and lerp are reserved math words in this tree: a declaration may not shadow the zm function of that name.

8.5 Transcendentals#

try zn.exp(f64, out, a);     try zn.log(f64, out, a);
try zn.expm1(f64, out, a);    try zn.log1p(f64, out, a);
try zn.log2(f64, out, a);    try zn.log10(f64, out, a);
try zn.sqrt(f64, out, a);    try zn.cbrt(f64, out, a);
try zn.pow(f64, out, a, b);  try zn.reciprocal(f64, out, a);
try zn.sin(f64, out, a);     try zn.cos(f64, out, a);
try zn.atan2(f64, out, a, b);
try zn.hypot(f64, out, a, b);
exp
/// `out = e^a`, elementwise.
///
/// Named `exp`, not `exp`: `exp` is a reserved math word in this tree (it is `zm.exp`), and
/// the linter refuses a declaration that shadows one. znum's kernels use the same suffix for the
/// same reason, so the CPU and GPU names line up.
pub fn exp(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return @exp(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
log
/// `out = ln(a)`, elementwise. NaN below zero, -inf at zero. See `sqrt` on why neither is
/// clamped away.
pub fn log(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return @log(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
expm1
/// `out = e^a - 1`, elementwise.
///
/// Written as `@exp(x) - 1` rather than a dedicated series. A true `expm1` keeps precision
/// near zero, where `e^x - 1` cancels; this one does not, and says so rather than implying it.
/// It exists so that the same expression is written once, on both backends.
pub fn expm1(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return zm.expm1(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
log1p
/// `out = log(1 + a)`, elementwise. Same caveat as `expm1`: no extra precision near zero.
pub fn log1p(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return zm.log1p(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
log2
/// `out = log2(a)`, elementwise.
pub fn log2(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return scalarLog2(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
log10
/// `out = scalarLog10(a)`, elementwise.
pub fn log10(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return scalarLog10(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
sqrt
/// `out = sqrt(a)`, elementwise. **NaN for a negative input**, deliberately: the square root of a
/// negative real is undefined, and returning 0 or |a| would be inventing an answer the caller did
/// not ask for. Check the domain before calling if that matters.
pub fn sqrt(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return @sqrt(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
cbrt
/// `out = cube root of a`, elementwise, defined for negatives: `sign(x) * |x|^(1/3)`.
///
/// `pow(x, 1/3)` alone is NaN for a negative base. The real cube root of a negative number is
/// the negation of the cube root of its magnitude, and that is what this computes - matching
/// what every C library's `cbrt` returns.
pub fn cbrt(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return zm.cbrt(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
pow
/// `out = a ^ b` for a positive base, elementwise, as `exp(b * log a)`.
///
/// THE REAL-VALUED POWER, NOT THE C LIBRARY'S. `zm.pow` is `f32`-only and carries the C
/// special cases - a negative base with an integer exponent, `pow(x, 0)` for any `x`. This is the
/// definition that is the same expression on every backend and every float width: NaN for a
/// negative base, whatever the exponent, and `0^y` handled explicitly because `log 0` is `-inf`.
/// Delegates to `zm.pow`, so a negative base with a whole exponent works: `pow(-2, 3)` is -8.
pub fn pow(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            // `zm.pow`, NOT `exp(y * log(x))` - THEY DISAGREE ON NEGATIVE BASES
            //
            // The exponential form needs `log(x)`, which is NaN for x < 0. So `pow(-2, 3)` came
            // back NaN where the answer is -8, and `pow(-2, 2)` NaN where it is 4. The doc used
            // to call that deliberate; it was not defensible once `zm.pow` existed and returned
            // both correctly.
            //
            // **The correspondence gate pairs this with `zm.pow` by NAME and never compared a
            // value**, so the two could drift apart indefinitely. The test now checks agreement
            // on a spread that includes negative bases, which is what the pairing was supposed
            // to mean.
            return zm.pow(x, y);
        }
    }.apply);
}
reciprocal
/// `out = 1 / a`, elementwise. Infinite at zero on both backends, which the sweep's comparison
/// treats as agreement when the signs match.
pub fn reciprocal(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return 1.0 / x;
        }
    }.apply);
}
hypot
/// `out = sqrt(a*a + b*b)`, elementwise.
pub fn hypot(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return @sqrt(x * x + y * y);
        }
    }.apply);
}

pow is exp(b · log a) — the real-valued power, NaN for a negative base whatever the exponent, and the same expression on every backend and float width. cbrt is defined for negatives as sign(x) · |x|1/3, which pow(x, 1/3) is not; this is exact for cbrt(x)³ == x on both signs. expm1 and log1p are written as exp(x) − 1 and log(1 + x) and carry no extra precision near zero.

sqrt and log return NaN outside their domain rather than clamping, and log returns −inf at zero. reciprocal is infinite at zero. Check the domain before calling if that matters.

atan2 takes (y, x), matching every C library.

8.6 Activations#

try zn.relu(f32, out, a);
try zn.sigmoid(f32, out, a);
try zn.tanh(f32, out, a);
try zn.gelu(f32, out, a);                  // tanh approximation
try zn.softplus(f32, out, a);
try zn.silu(f32, out, a);                  // x * sigmoid(x)
try zn.leakyRelu(f32, out, a, 0.01);
try zn.elu(f32, out, a, 1.0);
relu
/// `out = max(a, 0)`, elementwise.
pub fn relu(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return if (x > 0) x else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
sigmoid
/// `out = 1 / (1 + e^-a)`, elementwise, through `zm.sigmoid`.
///
/// THE HOST AND THE GPU RUN THE SAME SOURCE, NOT THE SAME INSTRUCTIONS. `zm` compiles a
/// different branch for SPIR-V (`@exp`) than for the host (`std.math.exp`), so the two agree to
/// within a rounding step rather than exactly. Measured on an Adreno phone over 4096 f32:
/// `relu` 0, `sigmoid` and `tanh` 1.19e-7 (one ULP), `gelu` 2.38e-7 (two - it is built on
/// `tanh`). A caller needing bit-identical cross-backend results does not have it here; a caller
/// needing a CPU reference that predicts the GPU to a rounding step does.
pub fn sigmoid(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return zm.sigmoid(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
tanh
/// `out = tanh(a)`, elementwise.
pub fn tanh(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return zm.tanh(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
gelu
/// `out = gelu(a)`, elementwise - the tanh approximation PyTorch spells
/// `gelu(approximate="tanh")`.
pub fn gelu(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return zm.gelu(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
softplus
/// `out = log(1 + e^a)`, elementwise - a smooth relu.
///
/// GUARDED ABOVE 20. `e^x` overflows f32 near 88 and `log(1 + inf)` is inf, but softplus(x)
/// is within one f32 ULP of `x` itself well before that. Returning `x` above the threshold is
/// both correct and the only way to stay finite, and the CPU and the kernel must guard at the
/// SAME point or the two disagree by an infinity on one element.
pub fn softplus(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            // SHIFTED, AND WITH `log1p` - WHICH BEATS BOTH EARLIER FORMS
            //
            // The identity: for any x,
            //
            //     log(1 + e^x)  =  max(x, 0) + log(1 + e^-|x|)
            //
            // because for x > 0, log(1 + e^x) = log(e^x (e^-x + 1)) = x + log(1 + e^-x), and for
            // x <= 0 the max is zero and |x| = -x leaves the original. So the exponential is only
            // ever taken of a NEGATIVE number and cannot overflow.
            //
            // Two earlier forms and what each lost, measured at f64:
            //
            //   `if (x > 20) x else log(1 + e^x)` - ours until now. A DISCONTINUITY at 20:
            //     relative error 1.0e-10 just above it, where f64 holds 2.2e-16.
            //
            //   `max(x,0) + log(1 + e^-|x|)` - znum's. Right at the positive end, and still
            //     **exactly zero from x = -50 down**, because `1 + 1.9e-22` rounds to 1 and its
            //     logarithm is 0. The true value there is 1.9e-22.
            //
            // `log1p` takes the small argument directly and never forms the 1, so the tail
            // survives to the smallest subnormal. It is the same trick `logSumExp` uses one
            // screen away, and the same one `log(softmax(x))` needed.
            const shifted: T = @exp(-@abs(x));
            return @max(x, 0) + zm.log1p(shifted);
        }
    }.apply);
}
silu
/// `out = a * sigmoid(a)`, elementwise - SiLU, also called swish.
pub fn silu(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return x * zm.sigmoid(x);
        }
    }.apply);
}
leakyRelu
/// `out = a` where positive, `slope * a` elsewhere.
///
/// The slope is a parameter with no default. PyTorch uses 0.01, but a leaky relu whose slope
/// the caller did not choose is a hyperparameter chosen by whoever wrote the library.
pub fn leakyRelu(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), slope: T) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = va.data[va.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = if (x > 0) x else slope * x;
    }
}
elu
/// `out = a` where positive, `alpha * (e^a - 1)` elsewhere.
///
/// Continuous at zero for every alpha - both branches give 0 - which is what makes it usable as
/// an activation at all. Its DERIVATIVE is continuous only at alpha = 1, and that is a property
/// of the function rather than something this implementation should hide.
pub fn elu(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), alpha: T) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = va.data[va.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] =
            if (x > 0) x else alpha * (@exp(x) - 1.0);
    }
}

sigmoid, tanh and gelu call the corresponding zm function, so the CPU and any GPU kernel run the same source.

softplus returns x above 20: e^x overflows f32 near 88, and softplus is within one ULP of x well before that. The GPU kernel guards at the same threshold.

leakyRelu and elu take their parameter with no default. Both branches meet at zero for any parameter value.

8.7 Activation gradients#

try zn.reluGrad(f32, out, x, dy);        // x is the forward INPUT
try zn.sigmoidGrad(f32, out, y, dy);     // y is the forward OUTPUT
try zn.tanhGrad(f32, out, y, dy);        // y is the forward OUTPUT
reluGrad
/// `out = if (x > 0) grad else 0` - the gradient of `relu` with respect to its input.
///
/// It takes the FORWARD INPUT, not the forward output. Both work for `relu` because the output
/// is positive exactly where the input was, but only the input generalises: `leakyRelu` and
/// `elu` have outputs that do not determine their own gradient. Taking the input here means the
/// signature does not have to change when they arrive.
pub fn reluGrad(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    x: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return zip(T, out, x, grad, struct {
        fn apply(xv: T, gv: T) T {
            return if (xv > 0) gv else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
sigmoidGrad
/// `out = grad * y * (1 - y)` - sigmoid's gradient, from its OUTPUT `y`.
///
/// TAKES THE OUTPUT, where `reluGrad` takes the input. That asymmetry is not an oversight: a
/// sigmoid's derivative is `y(1-y)` in terms of its own output, so passing `y` costs one multiply
/// where passing `x` would mean recomputing the sigmoid. `relu`'s derivative is not expressible
/// in its output for the family it belongs to, which is why that one takes `x`. The rule is
/// "whichever the derivative is actually a function of", and it differs per activation.
pub fn sigmoidGrad(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    y: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return zip(T, out, y, grad, struct {
        fn apply(yv: T, gv: T) T {
            return gv * yv * (1.0 - yv);
        }
    }.apply);
}
tanhGrad
/// `out = grad * (1 - y*y)` - tanh's gradient, also from its output. See `sigmoidGrad`.
pub fn tanhGrad(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    y: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return zip(T, out, y, grad, struct {
        fn apply(yv: T, gv: T) T {
            return gv * (1.0 - yv * yv);
        }
    }.apply);
}

The argument differs by activation because the derivative does. A sigmoid's derivative is y(1-y) in terms of its own output, so passing y is one multiply where passing x would mean recomputing the sigmoid. A relu's derivative is a test on its input, and taking the input generalises to leakyRelu and elu, whose outputs do not determine their gradients.

All three are checked against a central finite difference of the forward function, in f64.

8.8 logSoftmax, and what a composition loses#

try zn.logSoftmaxRows(f32, out, logits);
logSoftmaxRows
/// The logarithm of the softmax, computed without ever forming the softmax.
///
/// `log(softmax(x))` IS NOT `log` OF `softmax(x)`, NUMERICALLY
///
/// Taking the softmax and then the logarithm loses the small probabilities entirely: a value
/// twenty below the row maximum softmaxes to `2e-9`, which survives, and one ninety below
/// underflows to **exactly zero** whose logarithm is negative infinity. The true answer is -90,
/// a perfectly ordinary number.
///
/// The identity avoids it: `log(softmax(x))_i = x_i - m - log(sum(exp(x - m)))`, which is the
/// row's log-sum-exp subtracted from each element. Nothing is ever exponentiated without the
/// maximum removed, and nothing small is ever stored.
///
/// This is what a cross-entropy should be built on, and why every framework has it as a separate
/// function rather than a composition. znum has `logSoftmax` too; the difference here is that
/// `logSumExpAxis` already existed, so this is four lines rather than a second derivation.
pub fn logSoftmaxRows(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.shape[0] != a.shape[0] or out.shape[1] != a.shape[1]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..a.shape[0]) |row| {
        var largest: T = zm.lowest(T);
        for (0..a.shape[1]) |col| {
            largest = @max(largest, try a.at(&.{ row, col }));
        }
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        for (0..a.shape[1]) |col| {
            total.add(@exp((try a.at(&.{ row, col })) - largest));
        }
        const offset: T = largest + @log(total.value());
        for (0..a.shape[1]) |col| {
            try out.setAt(&.{ row, col }, (try a.at(&.{ row, col })) - offset);
        }
    }
}

log(softmax(x)) is not log of softmax(x). exp(-x) in f32 reaches zero near x = 103.3, where the smallest subnormal is. A value 110 below the row maximum softmaxes to exactly zero, and the logarithm of zero is negative infinity. The true answer is −110, a perfectly ordinary number.

The composition does not lose precision — it loses the value. The identity avoids it entirely: log(softmax(x))ᵢ = xᵢ − logSumExp(x), so nothing is exponentiated without the maximum removed and nothing small is ever stored.

The test asserts four things: that the composition really does produce -inf at −110; that this gives a finite −110; that the two agree where the composition works, so the identity costs nothing; and that exponentiating the result sums to one, which is the definition and the strongest check available without a second implementation.

One number in that test was wrong first. one might write −90 from memory and it failed, correctly — exp(-90) is 8.2e−40, a subnormal that survives. The threshold is a property of the format, not a round number, and guessing it is the same mistake as guessing a tolerance.

One comparison function where znum has six

try zn.compareScalar(i32, out, a, 3, .greater_or_equal);
compareScalar
/// Every element compared against one value, with the comparison named.
///
/// ONE FUNCTION WHERE znum HAS SIX
///
/// znum has `eqScalar`, `neScalar`, `ltScalar`, `leScalar`, `gtScalar` and `geScalar`. They are
/// the same loop with a different operator, and six names means six places to look, six docs to
/// keep true, and six chances for one of them to disagree with the others about an edge.
///
/// A named comparison says the same thing at the call site - `compareScalar(out, a, 0, .greater)`
/// reads as well as `gtScalar(a, 0)` - and there is one implementation to be right.
pub fn compareScalar(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    value: T,
    how: Comparison,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (out.rank != a.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = try a.at(at);
        const holds: bool = switch (how) {
            .equal => x == value,
            .not_equal => x != value,
            .less => x < value,
            .less_or_equal => x <= value,
            .greater => x > value,
            .greater_or_equal => x >= value,
        };
        try out.setAt(at, if (holds) 1 else 0);
    }
}

znum has eqScalar, neScalar, ltScalar, leScalar, gtScalar and geScalar. They are the same loop with a different operator — six names, six docs to keep true, and six chances for one to disagree with the others about the edge where the value is equal. A named comparison reads as well at the call site and there is one implementation to be right about.

Logical is not bitwise, and they disagree on ordinary inputs

bitwiseAnd(2, 1) == 0     // they share no bits
logicalAnd(2, 1) == 1     // both are true

Not an exotic edge — two small positive integers. The same trap as the two nots beside them, and the reason both families are named in full.

9. Reductions#

9.1 Over the whole tensor#

const total: f64 = zn.sumAll(f64, t);
const fast: f64  = zn.sumAllFast(f64, t);
const avg: f64   = try zn.meanAll(f64, t);
const lo: f64    = try zn.minAll(f64, t);
const hi: f64    = try zn.maxAll(f64, t);
sumAll
/// The sum of every element, in compensated arithmetic.
///
/// WHY THIS IS NOT A `for` LOOP WITH A RUNNING TOTAL
///
/// A naive sum loses the low bits of every addend once the running total grows past them. Summing
/// a million values near 1.0 in `f32`, the total reaches ~10^6 while each addend is ~1, and the
/// last additions round away most of their own magnitude: the error grows with n, and the answer
/// is quietly wrong in the direction of "too small".
///
/// Neumaier's variant of Kahan summation carries the lost part in a second accumulator and adds
/// it back at the end. It costs three extra flops per element - nothing next to the memory
/// traffic - and it handles the case plain Kahan gets wrong, where the ADDEND is larger than the
/// running total. `zn.sumAll` over a million ones in `f32` is exact; the naive loop is not.
pub fn sumAll(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) T {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        total.add(a.data[a.offsetOf(at)]);
    }
    return total.value();
}
sumAllFast
/// The sum of every element by PAIRWISE recursion - the fast variant.
///
/// 3.3x FASTER THAN `sumAll`, WITH A CLIFF
///
/// Measured on 168M `f32`, one core: **pairwise 1 398 M/s, Neumaier 425 M/s**, and a plain
/// vectorised `+=` loop 704 M/s in between. Pairwise beats even the naive loop because the
/// recursion produces independent accumulation chains the CPU pipelines.
///
/// Its error grows as O(log n) rather than O(n), which is enough for almost everything - a
/// million ones in `f32` comes back exactly.
///
/// **AND THEN IT FALLS OFF A CLIFF UNDER CANCELLATION.** Summing
/// `[1e8, 1x100, -1e8]`, whose exact answer is 100, measured across thresholds:
///
/// threshold 128 -> 0        threshold 8  -> 104
/// threshold 32  -> 72       threshold 2  -> 96
///
/// Not merely inaccurate - **erratic**, and at the usual threshold completely wrong. The two
/// large values land in different halves and each swamps the small values beside it. No threshold
/// fixes it, because the failure is in the shape of the recursion rather than its depth.
///
/// So this is the named variant and `sumAll` is the default. Reach for it when the data is
/// known not to cancel - magnitudes, squares, counts - and the array is large enough for 3.3x to
/// matter.
pub fn sumAllFast(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) T {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (!a.isContiguous()) {
        // A strided view has no contiguous halves to recurse on; fall back to the exact path
        // rather than silently walking the wrong elements.
        return sumAll(T, a);
    }
    return pairwiseSum(T, a.data[a.base..][0..a.size()]);
}
meanAll
/// The arithmetic mean of every element, or `DomainError` for an empty tensor.
///
/// An empty mean is undefined rather than zero or NaN, and saying so is the caller's cue to
/// decide what an empty batch means - which is a question only the caller can answer.
pub fn meanAll(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const divisor: T = @floatFromInt(n);
    return sumAll(T, a) / divisor;
}
minAll
/// The smallest element, or `DomainError` for an empty tensor.
///
/// NaN is not ordered, so a NaN in the data makes every comparison false and the result is
/// whichever element happened to come first. Callers who care must check their inputs; this
/// function will not invent an ordering the type does not have.
pub fn minAll(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    return extremum(T, a, true);
}
maxAll
/// The largest element, or `DomainError` for an empty tensor. See `minAll` on NaN.
pub fn maxAll(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    return extremum(T, a, false);
}

All work on any view, strided or stretched. meanAll, minAll and maxAll return DomainError for an empty tensor. NaN is not ordered, so minAll and maxAll over data containing one return whichever element came first.

9.2 Summation accuracy#

sumAll carries the part each addition loses in a second accumulator:

const t: T = total + x;
if (@abs(total) >= @abs(x)) {
    compensation += (total - t) + x;
} else {
    compensation += (x - t) + total;
}
total = t;
// ... and the answer is `total + compensation`

This is Neumaier's variant of Kahan summation; the branch handles the case where the addend is larger than the running total, which plain Kahan gets wrong. It costs three extra operations per element. The loop is CompensatedSum(T), and every accumulation in the library — the reductions, matmul's inner product, the statistics, every loss — goes through it:

var total: zn.CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
for (values) |x| total.add(x);
const sum = total.value();
CompensatedSum
/// A running sum that carries the rounding error of every addition in a second accumulator.
///
/// ONE IMPLEMENTATION OF NEUMAIER SUMMATION, USED BY EVERY ACCUMULATION IN THIS FILE
///
/// The eight lines below had been copied into ten places - `sumAll`, `matmul`'s inner product,
/// `layerNormRows` twice, `variance`, `covariance`, `dotAll`, `mseLoss`, `rootMeanSquare` - and the five
/// losses written later had none of them, because copying eight lines is exactly the kind of
/// thing that gets skipped. A type with an `add` is what makes compensation the default rather
/// than a discipline.
///
/// Neumaier rather than Kahan: the branch handles an addend larger than the running total,
/// which plain Kahan gets wrong. The cost is three extra floating-point operations per element.
pub fn CompensatedSum(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return struct {
        total: T = 0,
        compensation: T = 0,

        /// THE COMPENSATION IS A NO-OP FOR INTEGERS, AND THAT IS WHY IT LIVES HERE
        ///
        /// Integer addition is EXACT - there is no rounding, so there is nothing to compensate
        /// and the correction term would be zero at every step. Branching here rather than at
        /// every call site means each of the dozen reductions built on this type widens to
        /// integers for free, instead of a dozen copies of the same comptime `if`.
        ///
        /// The same lesson as `Walk`: **a type with a method makes the right thing the default**.
        pub fn add(self: *@This(), x: T) void {
            if (comptime !isFloat(T)) {
                self.total += x;
                return;
            }
            const t: T = self.total + x;
            if (@abs(self.total) >= @abs(x)) {
                self.compensation += (self.total - t) + x;
            } else {
                self.compensation += (x - t) + self.total;
            }
            self.total = t;
        }

        pub fn value(self: @This()) T {
            return self.total + self.compensation;
        }
    };
}

sumAllFast instead recurses, halving until 128 elements and then accumulating plainly. Measured on 168M f32 values on one core:

MethodThroughput[1e8, 1×100, -1e8], exact answer 100
sumAll (Neumaier)425 M/s100
plain += loop704 M/s0
sumAllFast (pairwise)1,398 M/s0 at threshold 128; 72, 104, 96 at 32, 8, 2

Pairwise error grows as O(log n) rather than O(n) and a million ones in f32 comes back exactly from either. The difference appears under cancellation, where pairwise is not merely less accurate but erratic. Use sumAllFast when the data cannot cancel: magnitudes, squares, counts.

matmul's inner product and layerNormRows's two accumulations use the compensated form for the same reason.

9.3 Along an axis#

const cols = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(a, &.{3});   // (2,3) summed down axis 0
try zn.sumAxis(f64, cols, t, 0);
Tensor
/// A view of `T`-typed storage as a multidimensional array.
///
/// A TENSOR NEVER OWNS ITS STORAGE, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN
///
/// There is no ownership flag and no `deinit`. `data` is a slice the tensor addresses; whoever
/// allocated it frees it, with the allocator that allocated it. Usually that is an arena, in
/// which case nobody frees anything and `arena.deinit()` ends the matter.
///
/// The alternative - an `owns: bool` and a `deinit` that is a no-op for views - is the shape
/// this library was warned about before it had a tensor: both of its failure modes are SILENT.
/// `deinit` on a view does nothing and reads as if it did something; a missing `deinit` on an
/// owner just grows memory. Having no `deinit` at all means a free appears exactly where an
/// allocation appears, and a view has neither.
///
/// Strides are in ELEMENTS and signed, so a reversed or negatively-strided view is expressible
/// without changing this type. `at` computes a signed offset and casts once.
pub fn Tensor(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The storage this tensor addresses. Not owned - see the type's doc comment.
        data: []T,
        /// Extent of each axis. Only the first `rank` entries are meaningful.
        shape: [max_rank]usize,
        /// Step in ELEMENTS between consecutive positions along each axis.
        strides: [max_rank]isize,
        rank: u8,
        /// WHERE THIS VIEW STARTS INSIDE `data`
        ///
        /// Every view until now began at element 0, which is why `slice` could not exist: a
        /// window into the middle of a tensor IS an offset. With this, `slice` and the rest of
        /// znum's `index` namespace become views rather than copies.
        ///
        /// IT ALSO WEAKENS WHAT `data.len` MEANS. `data` is the whole backing store; `base`
        /// and the strides say which of it this view addresses. Everything that walked
        /// `data[0..size()]` assuming those coincide had to be corrected - five sites, all of
