        /// them fast paths that skip the index walk, which is exactly where such an assumption
        /// hides.
        base: usize = 0,

        /// A dense row-major tensor over `shape`, allocated from `gpa`.
        ///
        /// The caller frees `result.data` with the same allocator - or allocates from an arena
        /// and frees nothing.
        pub fn alloc(gpa: Allocator, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            const storage: []T = gpa.alloc(T, count) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
            return fromSlice(storage, shape);
        }

        /// A dense row-major view of `data` as `shape`. `data.len` must be exactly the product of
        /// the extents - a slice that is merely LARGE ENOUGH is rejected, because the usual reason
        /// for the mismatch is a shape that is wrong rather than storage that is generous.
        pub fn fromSlice(data: []T, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != data.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            var self: Self = .{
                .data = data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                .base = 0,
            };
            // Row-major: the last axis is contiguous, and each earlier stride is the product of
            // the extents to its right.
            var stride: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = shape.len;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                self.shape[axis] = shape[axis];
                self.strides[axis] = stride;
                stride *= @intCast(shape[axis]);
            }
            return self;
        }

        /// The number of elements this tensor addresses.
        pub fn size(self: Self) usize {
            var count: usize = 1;
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                count *= self.shape[axis];
            }
            return count;
        }

        /// Whether the strides are dense row-major, so `data[0..size()]` is the tensor in order.
        ///
        /// An operation that walks memory linearly must ask this rather than assume it: a view
        /// produced by `transpose` addresses the same storage in a different order, and a linear
        /// walk over it silently reads the wrong elements rather than failing.
        pub fn isContiguous(self: Self) bool {
            var expected: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = self.rank;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                if (self.shape[axis] != 1 and self.strides[axis] != expected) {
                    return false;
                }
                expected *= @intCast(self.shape[axis]);
            }
            return true;
        }

        /// The offset into `data` of the element at `indices`.
        pub fn flatIndex(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!usize {
            if (indices.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                if (i >= self.shape[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// The element at `indices`.
        pub fn at(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!T {
            return self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)];
        }

        /// The flat offset for a coordinate, with no error.
        ///
        /// THE 45 `flatIndex(...) catch unreachable` SITES ARE ALL THIS SHAPE
        ///
        ///     const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        ///     out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = ...
        ///
        /// The slice is `walker[0..rank]`, so `indices.len != rank` **cannot** happen - the
        /// caller built it from the rank. And the coordinate came from a `Walk` over this very
        /// shape, so the bounds cannot fail either. Two impossible checks, one `catch
        /// unreachable`, on the hottest line in the library.
        ///
        /// This computes the offset and returns it. The remaining safety is `data[offset]`,
        /// which is where Zig puts it: a panic with a message in Debug and ReleaseSafe.
        ///
        /// **Use it where the rank is an invariant you have already established** - after a
        /// shape check, or inside a walk over the tensor's own shape. Use `flatIndex` where the
        /// coordinate came from outside and might be wrong.
        pub fn offsetOf(self: Self, indices: []const usize) usize {
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// Read from a rank-2 tensor. **Does not return an error.**
        ///
        /// WHY `at` HAS TO AND THIS DOES NOT
        ///
        /// `at(indices: []const usize)` can fail for two reasons. One is a real bounds check.
        /// The other is `indices.len != rank` - and that check exists ONLY because a slice hides
        /// its arity from the type system. Every call site writes a literal `&.{ i, j }`; the
        /// compiler could know it is two, and does not.
        ///
        /// Naming the arity in the function removes that half entirely. What remains is the
        /// bounds check, and **Zig already has a name for that**: it is what `data[idx]` does -
        /// a panic with a message and a stack trace in Debug and ReleaseSafe, the same UB as any
        /// other slice index in ReleaseFast.
        ///
        /// Nobody writes `arr[i] catch unreachable`. This file had **105 `catch unreachable`**,
        /// most of them on an `at` whose rank the author had checked three lines earlier, and
        /// that noise is the single biggest thing that makes numeric Zig look worse than it is.
        ///
        /// The safety is not weaker - it is Zig's, in the place Zig puts it.
        pub fn at2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-2 tensor. The mirror of `at2`, and error-free for the same reason.
        pub fn setAt2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Read from a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn at1(self: Self, i: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn setAt1(self: Self, i: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Write `value` at `indices`.
        pub fn setAt(self: Self, indices: []const usize, value: T) Error!void {
            self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)] = value;
        }

        /// Set every element to `value`.
        pub fn fill(self: Self, value: T) void {
            if (self.isContiguous()) {
                @memset(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], value);
                return;
            }
            var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var remaining: usize = self.size();
            while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
                self.data[self.offsetOf(walker[0..self.rank])] = value;
                advance(self.shape[0..self.rank], walker[0..self.rank]);
            }
        }

        /// The same storage seen as `shape`. Requires a contiguous tensor and an equal element
        /// count; both are checked rather than assumed.
        pub fn reshape(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (!self.isContiguous()) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != self.size()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // From `base`, not 0: reshaping a slice must keep addressing that slice.
            return fromSlice(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], shape);
        }

        /// Whether two distinct index positions address the same element, which happens exactly
        /// when a stretched axis (stride 0) has extent above 1.
        ///
        /// An operation that WRITES must refuse an aliased destination. Many indices landing on
        /// one element means the last write wins and every earlier one vanishes - a result that is
        /// wrong in a way no bounds check catches. `map` and `zip` check this so a caller who
        /// passes a broadcast view as an output gets an error rather than a plausible answer.
        pub fn isAliased(self: Self) bool {
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                if (self.shape[axis] > 1 and self.strides[axis] == 0) {
                    return true;
                }
            }
            return false;
        }

        /// A read-only view of this tensor stretched to `shape` by the broadcasting rule.
        ///
        /// BROADCASTING IS A STRIDE OF ZERO, NOT A COPY
        ///
        /// An axis whose extent is 1 is stretched by setting its stride to 0, so every position
        /// along it addresses the same element. No storage is allocated and no data moves; a
        /// `(3, 1, 5)` tensor viewed as `(3, 4, 5)` is the same 15 values read four times.
        ///
        /// **WRITING THROUGH A STRETCHED VIEW IS ALMOST CERTAINLY A MISTAKE.** Many indices
        /// alias one element, so the last write wins and the others vanish. This returns a view
        /// because that is what the type system has; treat it as an input.
        ///
        /// `isContiguous` reports false for any stretched axis with extent > 1, which is what
        /// stops a linear walk from reading the underlying 15 values as if they were 60.
        pub fn broadcastTo(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank or shape.len < self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = .{
                .data = self.data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                // Stretching does not move where the view starts.
                .base = self.base,
            };
            // Align at the TRAILING axis, as the broadcast rule does: an axis this tensor does not
            // have behaves as extent 1, and so does an axis it has with extent 1. Both get a
            // stride of 0.
            for (shape, 0..) |extent, axis| {
                const from_end: usize = shape.len - axis;
                view.shape[axis] = extent;
                if (self.rank < from_end) {
                    continue;
                }
                const own_axis: usize = self.rank - from_end;
                const own_extent: usize = self.shape[own_axis];
                if (own_extent == extent) {
                    view.strides[axis] = self.strides[own_axis];
                } else if (own_extent != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A window into this tensor: `len` positions along `axis`, starting at `start`.
        ///
        /// A SLICE IS AN OFFSET, WHICH IS WHY `base` EXISTS
        ///
        /// Nothing is copied: the view keeps every stride and simply starts `start` steps further
        /// into the data along one axis. A batch of 32 rows out of 4096 is a `Tensor` the same
        /// size as any other, addressing 32 rows of the same buffer.
        ///
        /// `start + len` must fit. Clamping instead would hand back a view SMALLER than asked
        /// for, and the caller's next loop would read the shape it requested rather than the one
        /// it got.
        pub fn slice(self: Self, axis: usize, start: usize, len: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (start + len > self.shape[axis]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            const step: isize = self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(start));
            const moved: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) + step;
            if (moved < 0) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            view.base = @intCast(moved);
            view.shape[axis] = len;
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with the axes reordered: `out` axis `i` is this tensor's axis `order[i]`.
        ///
        /// EVERY `manip` OPERATION HERE IS METADATA
        ///
        /// `permute`, `squeeze`, `unsqueeze`, `flatten` and `moveAxis` all return a view: they
        /// permute or insert entries in two fixed-size arrays and copy nothing. That is why they
        /// need no GPU kernel and no sweep row - **there is no arithmetic to disagree about.**
        /// The cost appears later, when a permuted view has to be BOUND to a kernel and must be
        /// densified first, which is the constraint recorded in section 10.3.
        ///
        /// `order` must be a permutation of `0..rank`. A repeated axis would produce a view
        /// where two indices alias, which is `isAliased`'s job to reject at write time - but
        /// catching it here names the actual mistake.
        pub fn permute(self: Self, order: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (order.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var seen: [max_rank]bool = @splat(false);
            for (order) |axis| {
                if (axis >= self.rank or seen[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                seen[axis] = true;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            for (order, 0..) |axis, i| {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[axis];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[axis];
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis removed.
        ///
        /// Removing an axis whose extent is NOT 1 would change how many elements the view
        /// addresses, so it is `ShapeMismatch` rather than a silent reshape.
        pub fn squeeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (self.shape[axis] != 1) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = axis;
            while (i + 1 < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i + 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i + 1];
            }
            view.shape[self.rank - 1] = 1;
            view.strides[self.rank - 1] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank - 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis inserted at `axis`. `axis == rank` appends.
        ///
        /// The new axis gets a stride of 0, which is harmless because its extent is 1 - a
        /// single position cannot step anywhere. `isAliased` only objects when extent exceeds 1.
        pub fn unsqueeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis > self.rank or self.rank + 1 > max_rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = self.rank;
            while (i > axis) : (i -= 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i - 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i - 1];
            }
            view.shape[axis] = 1;
            view.strides[axis] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank + 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A rank-1 view of the whole tensor. Contiguous only, for `reshape`'s reason.
        pub fn flatten(self: Self) Error!Self {
            return self.reshape(&.{self.size()});
        }

        /// A view with one axis moved from `from` to `to`, the others keeping their order.
        pub fn moveAxis(self: Self, from: usize, to: usize) Error!Self {
            if (from >= self.rank or to >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var order: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var k: usize = 0;
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                if (i == from) {
                    continue;
                }
                order[k] = i;
                k += 1;
            }
            // Open a slot at `to` and drop the moved axis into it.
            var j: usize = self.rank - 1;
            while (j > to) : (j -= 1) {
                order[j] = order[j - 1];
            }
            order[to] = from;
            return self.permute(order[0..self.rank]);
        }

        /// A view with axes `a` and `b` exchanged. Shares storage; changes through one are visible
        /// through the other.
        pub fn transpose(self: Self, a: usize, b: usize) Error!Self {
            if (a >= self.rank or b >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            view.shape[a] = self.shape[b];
            view.shape[b] = self.shape[a];
            view.strides[a] = self.strides[b];
            view.strides[b] = self.strides[a];
            return view;
        }
    };
}
sumAxis
/// Sum `a` along `axis` into `out`, which must have `a`'s shape with that axis removed.
///
/// The output's rank is one less, not one with a size-1 axis. Keeping the axis would be the
/// `keepdims` option, and options that change the RESULT SHAPE are better as separate calls than
/// as a flag a reader has to look up - a caller who wants the axis back has `reshape`.
pub fn sumAxis(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (axis >= a.rank) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    if (out.rank + 1 != a.rank or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    // The surviving axes must match, in order.
    var src: usize = 0;
    var dst: usize = 0;
    while (src < a.rank) : (src += 1) {
        if (src == axis) {
            continue;
        }
        if (a.shape[src] != out.shape[dst]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        dst += 1;
    }

    out.fill(0);
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var reduced: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    var remaining: usize = a.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        // Project the source position onto the output by dropping the reduced axis.
        var k: usize = 0;
        var i: usize = 0;
        while (i < a.rank) : (i += 1) {
            if (i == axis) {
                continue;
            }
            reduced[k] = walker[i];
            k += 1;
        }
        const at: []const usize = reduced[0..out.rank];
        const slot: usize = out.offsetOf(at);
        out.data[slot] += a.data[a.offsetOf(walker[0..a.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
}

The output's rank is one less — the reduced axis is removed, not kept as a size-1 axis. If you want it back, unsqueeze. The surviving extents must match in order.

try zn.meanAxis(f64, out, t, 0);
try zn.maxAxis(f64, out, t, 1);
try zn.minAxis(f64, out, t, 1);
try zn.prodAxis(f64, out, t, 0);
try zn.varianceAxis(f64, out, t, 1, .sample);
meanAxis
/// Mean along one axis.
pub fn meanAxis(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    try sumAxis(T, out, a, axis);
    try scale(T, out, out, 1 / @as(T, @floatFromInt(a.shape[axis])));
}
maxAxis
/// Largest element along one axis. NaN is not ordered and never wins.
pub fn maxAxis(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return reduceAxis(T, out, a, axis, zm.lowest(T), struct {
        fn f(acc: T, x: T) T {
            return if (x > acc) x else acc;
        }
    }.f);
}
minAxis
/// Smallest element along one axis.
pub fn minAxis(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return reduceAxis(T, out, a, axis, zm.highest(T), struct {
        fn f(acc: T, x: T) T {
            return if (x < acc) x else acc;
        }
    }.f);
}
prodAxis
/// Product along one axis.
pub fn prodAxis(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    return reduceAxis(T, out, a, axis, 1, struct {
        fn f(acc: T, x: T) T {
            return acc * x;
        }
    }.f);
}
varianceAxis
/// Variance along one axis, two-pass, with the divisor chosen by `ddof`.
///
/// `out` holds the means after the first pass and is overwritten by the variances in the
/// second, so no scratch the caller has to size. The squared deviations accumulate into the
/// output itself.
pub fn varianceAxis(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    axis: usize,
    ddof: Ddof,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const divisor: f64 = ddof.divisor(a.shape[axis]) orelse return Error.DomainError;
    try meanAxis(T, out, a, axis);
    // Second pass. Each output slot is visited `shape[axis]` times; on the FIRST visit it still
    // holds the mean, which is what the deviation needs - so the mean is read, the slot is
    // replaced by the first squared deviation, and later visits add to it. The mean is kept in
    // `means` for the remaining visits of that slot.
    var means: [max_rank * 512]T = undefined;
    if (out.size() > means.len) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    @memcpy(means[0..out.size()], out.data[out.base..][0..out.size()]);
    out.fill(0);
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var target: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    var remaining: usize = a.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        var k: usize = 0;
        var i: usize = 0;
        while (i < a.rank) : (i += 1) {
            if (i != axis) {
                target[k] = walker[i];
                k += 1;
            }
        }
        const slot: usize = out.offsetOf(target[0..out.rank]);
        const d: T = (a.data[a.offsetOf(walker[0..a.rank])]) - means[slot - out.base];
        out.data[slot] += d * d;
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
    try scale(T, out, out, @floatCast(1.0 / divisor));
}

maxAxis, minAxis and prodAxis share one walker with a comptime accumulation step, so the output-shape check is written once. Each axis reduction is tested by relating it to its whole-tensor form: reducing along one axis and then over the result must give maxAll, minAll, prodAll or meanAll, and varianceAxis on a single row must equal variance of that row.

9.4 Cumulative, boolean and selection#

try zn.cumsum(f64, running, t);      // along the last axis
const any = zn.anyNonzero(f64, t);
const all = zn.allNonzero(f64, t);       // true for an empty tensor
try zn.where(f64, out, mask, a, b);  // a where mask != 0, else b
var order: [n]usize = @splat(0);
try zn.argsort(f64, &order, v);          // rank 1, ascending, stable
cumsum
/// Running sum along the last axis of a rank-1 or rank-2 tensor.
pub fn cumsum(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (a.rank == 0 or a.rank > 2 or out.rank != a.rank or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.size() != a.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const rows: usize = if (a.rank == 2) a.shape[0] else 1;
    const cols: usize = a.shape[a.rank - 1];
    var row: usize = 0;
    while (row < rows) : (row += 1) {
        var running: T = 0;
        var col: usize = 0;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            if (a.rank == 2) {
                running += try a.at(&.{ row, col });
                try out.setAt(&.{ row, col }, running);
            } else {
                running += try a.at(&.{col});
                try out.setAt(&.{col}, running);
            }
        }
    }
}
anyNonzero
/// Whether any element is non-zero.
pub fn anyNonzero(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) bool {
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        if (a.data[a.offsetOf(at)] != 0) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    return false;
}
allNonzero
/// Whether every element is non-zero. True for an empty tensor, by the usual convention.
pub fn allNonzero(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) bool {
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        if (a.data[a.offsetOf(at)] == 0) {
            return false;
        }
    }
    return true;
}
where
/// `out = a where mask is non-zero, else b`, elementwise. All three broadcast to `out`.
pub fn where(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    mask: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const vm: Tensor(T) = try mask.broadcastTo(shape);
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    const vb: Tensor(T) = try b.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const src: Tensor(T) = if (vm.data[vm.offsetOf(at)] != 0) va else vb;
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = src.data[src.offsetOf(at)];
    }
}
argsort
/// Indices that would sort a rank-1 tensor ascending, written into `out`. Stable.
///
/// Insertion sort: O(n^2), stable, no scratch. A rank-1 sort in a numerics library is almost
/// always over a few hundred elements. `argsort` rather than `sort` because the indices are what
/// a caller usually wants - to reorder something ELSE by this key.
pub fn argsort(comptime T: type, out: []usize, a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (a.rank != 1) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.len != a.shape[0]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
        slot.* = i;
    }
    var i: usize = 1;
    while (i < out.len) : (i += 1) {
        const key_index: usize = out[i];
        const key: T = try a.at(&.{key_index});
        var j: usize = i;
        while (j > 0 and (try a.at(&.{out[j - 1]})) > key) : (j -= 1) {
            out[j] = out[j - 1];
        }
        out[j] = key_index;
    }
}

where broadcasts all three inputs to out, so a mask from greater and two full tensors compose directly: selecting by a > b is maximum(a, b).

argsort returns the permutation rather than the sorted values, because the indices are what a caller usually wants — to reorder something else by this key. It is a stable insertion sort: O(n²), no scratch, and a rank-1 sort in a numerics library is almost always over a few hundred elements. Stability is asserted on a run of equal keys.

9.5 Reordering, masks and counting#

try zn.flip(f64, out, a, axis);
try zn.roll(f64, out, a, 1, axis);      // positive moves elements FORWARD
const at = try zn.argminAll(f64, a);
const n = try zn.nonzero(f64, &positions, a);
const k = try zn.booleanMask(f64, &values, a, mask);
try zn.sort(f64, &sorted, a);
const distinct = try zn.unique(f64, &out, a);
try zn.bincount(f64, &counts, whole_numbers);
flip
/// Reverse the order along one axis.
pub fn flip(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (axis >= a.rank or out.rank != a.rank or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    for (0..a.rank) |d| {
        if (out.shape[d] != a.shape[d]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var source: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    var remaining: usize = a.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        @memcpy(source[0..a.rank], walker[0..a.rank]);
        source[axis] = a.shape[axis] - 1 - walker[axis];
        const value: T = try a.at(source[0..a.rank]);
        try out.setAt(walker[0..out.rank], value);
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
}
roll
/// Shift elements along one axis, wrapping around: `out[i] = a[(i - shift) mod n]`.
///
/// A POSITIVE SHIFT MOVES ELEMENTS FORWARD, which is numpy's direction and znum's. The sign is
/// the only thing to get wrong here and both conventions look equally reasonable written down,
/// so the test pins it with a shift of 1 on a ramp: the last element must arrive at the front.
///
/// `shift` is signed and may exceed the axis length; the modulus handles both.
pub fn roll(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    shift: isize,
    axis: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (axis >= a.rank or out.rank != a.rank or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    for (0..a.rank) |d| {
        if (out.shape[d] != a.shape[d]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    const extent: isize = @intCast(a.shape[axis]);
    if (extent == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var source: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    var remaining: usize = a.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        @memcpy(source[0..a.rank], walker[0..a.rank]);
        const here: isize = @intCast(walker[axis]);
        source[axis] = @intCast(@mod(here - shift, extent));
        const value: T = try a.at(source[0..a.rank]);
        try out.setAt(walker[0..out.rank], value);
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
}
argminAll
/// The position of the smallest element, scanning flat. Ties go to the first.
///
/// The comparison is `<`, so a NaN never wins: every comparison against NaN is false, and the
/// first non-NaN value keeps the position. The same rule as `argmaxAll`, in the same direction.
pub fn argminAll(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!usize {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var best: usize = 0;
    // SEEDED FROM THE FIRST ELEMENT, AND THE NaN CASE IS HANDLED EXPLICITLY
    //
    // This was `inf(T)`, which made the function float-only. Replacing it with the first element
    // looked like a pure simplification and **changed the answer**: seeded from an infinity a NaN
    // never won a comparison, so a leading NaN was skipped; seeded from element zero a leading
    // NaN IS the seed and nothing can beat it, because every comparison against NaN is false.
    //
    // The sentinel was not a float artefact - it was doing NaN handling. So the comparison below
    // says so directly instead: anything real beats a NaN incumbent. Integers have no NaNs and
    // the branch folds away at comptime.
    var best_value: T = a.data[0];
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    for (0..n) |i| {
        const x: T = try a.at(walker[0..a.rank]);
        if (x < best_value or (comptime isFloat(T)) and zm.isNan(best_value)) {
            best_value = x;
            best = i;
        }
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
    return best;
}
nonzero
/// The flat positions of every non-zero element, in order, into `out`. Returns how many were
/// written; `Error.ShapeMismatch` if `out` is too short to hold them all.
///
/// The count comes back rather than the caller having to size `out` exactly, because the number
/// of non-zeros is not known until the scan is done and refusing to start would make the caller
/// scan twice.
pub fn nonzero(comptime T: type, out: []usize, a: Tensor(T)) Error!usize {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    var found: usize = 0;
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    for (0..a.size()) |i| {
        if ((try a.at(walker[0..a.rank])) != 0) {
            if (found >= out.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            out[found] = i;
            found += 1;
        }
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
    return found;
}
booleanMask
/// Copy the elements where `mask` is non-zero into `out`, in order. Returns how many.
pub fn booleanMask(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []T,
    a: Tensor(T),
    mask: Tensor(T),
) Error!usize {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (mask.size() != a.size() or mask.rank != a.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var found: usize = 0;
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        if (mask.data[mask.offsetOf(at)] != 0) {
            if (found >= out.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            out[found] = try a.at(at);
            found += 1;
        }
    }
    return found;
}
sort
/// Sort a copy of `a` ascending into `out`.
pub fn sort(comptime T: type, out: []T, a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (out.len != a.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (walk.next()) |at| : (i += 1) {
        out[i] = try a.at(at);
    }
    std.mem.sort(T, out, {}, std.sort.asc(T));
}
unique
/// The distinct values of `a`, ascending, into `out`. Returns how many.
///
/// Sorted rather than first-seen order, because "the distinct values" is a set and a set has no
/// order until one is chosen; ascending is the choice every other library makes and the one that
/// makes two runs comparable.
pub fn unique(comptime T: type, out: []T, a: Tensor(T)) Error!usize {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (out.len < a.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    try sort(T, out[0..a.size()], a);
    if (a.size() == 0) {
        return 0;
    }
    var kept: usize = 1;
    for (out[1..a.size()]) |value| {
        if (value != out[kept - 1]) {
            out[kept] = value;
            kept += 1;
        }
    }
    return kept;
}
bincount
/// Count how many times each whole number appears, into `counts`. Values must be non-negative
/// whole numbers; anything at or past `counts.len` is `Error.OutOfRange`.
///
/// Out of range is an error rather than a silent skip: a bincount that quietly dropped the values
/// it could not hold would return a histogram summing to less than the input, and the caller
/// would have no way to notice.
pub fn bincount(comptime T: type, counts: []usize, a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    @memset(counts, 0);
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = try a.at(at);
        // AN INTEGER IS ALREADY WHOLE, SO THERE IS NOTHING TO CHECK
        //
        // On a float tensor this has to verify every value is a whole number before using it as
        // an index. On an integer tensor the TYPE already said so, and the check would be
        // `x != x`. The negative test stays for both - a count of a negative index has no home.
        if (comptime isFloat(T)) {
            if (x != @floor(x)) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
        }
        if (x < 0) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        // `@floor` names the rounding for a float; an integer is already the index.
        const slot: usize = if (comptime isFloat(T)) @floor(x) else @intCast(x);
        if (slot >= counts.len) {
            return Error.OutOfRange;
        }
        counts[slot] += 1;
    }
}

Every function here has a convention that could reasonably go the other way, so each is pinned rather than left for a reader to run it. A positive roll moves elements forward, so roll(1) brings the last value to the front — numpy's direction. unique returns ascending order, not first-seen; on the sample 3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 3, 1 the two conventions differ.

nonzero and booleanMask are the same question asked two ways — the positions, and the values at those positions — and they agree with each other rather than against two lists written by hand.

Both return a count rather than requiring the caller to size the output exactly, because the number of non-zeros is not known until the scan is done and refusing to start would make the caller scan twice. An output too small to hold them all is ShapeMismatch, never a truncation. bincount takes the same line: a value past the end of counts is OutOfRange, because a count that quietly dropped what it could not hold would sum to less than the input with no way to notice.

9.6 log-sum-exp, and why it subtracts the maximum#

const total = try zn.logSumExp(f64, a);
try zn.logSumExpAxis(f64, per_row, a, 1);   // one maximum per row
try zn.stdDevAxis(f64, out, a, 1, .sample);
logSumExp
/// `log(sum(exp(a)))`, computed so it does not overflow.
///
/// SUBTRACTING THE MAXIMUM IS THE WHOLE FUNCTION
///
/// The direct reading overflows almost immediately: `exp(800)` is infinity in f64, so a sum of
/// exponentials of ordinary-looking numbers comes back as `inf` and the logarithm of that is
/// `inf` too. Subtracting the largest element first makes the biggest term exactly `exp(0) = 1`
/// and every other term smaller, so nothing can overflow - and adding the maximum back at the
/// end is exact, because `log(sum(exp(a - m))) + m` IS `log(sum(exp(a)))` by algebra.
///
///     a = { 800, 801, 802 }    direct: inf     this: 802.4076
///
/// The same trick is what makes `softmaxRows` safe, and this is the reduction underneath cross
/// entropy: `crossEntropy = logSumExp(logits) - logits[correct]`.
///
/// An empty tensor has no maximum to subtract and the sum of no terms is zero, whose logarithm
/// is negative infinity - which is the right answer but not one a caller usually wants by
/// accident, so it is a `DomainError`.
pub fn logSumExp(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.size() == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const largest: T = try maxAll(T, a);
    // An infinite maximum has no finite offset to remove; the answer is that infinity.
    if (!zm.isFinite(largest)) {
        return largest;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        total.add(@exp((try a.at(at)) - largest));
    }
    return largest + @log(total.value());
}
logSumExpAxis
/// `logSumExp` along one axis of a rank-2 tensor, written into `out`.
///
/// Each row (or column) gets its own maximum, which is what makes this useful for a batch: one
/// row saturating does not affect the stability of its neighbours.
pub fn logSumExpAxis(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    axis: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 1 or axis > 1) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const kept: usize = a.shape[1 - axis];
    const along: usize = a.shape[axis];
    if (out.shape[0] != kept or along == 0) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..kept) |i| {
        var largest: T = -inf(T);
        for (0..along) |j| {
            const at: []const usize = if (axis == 0) &.{ j, i } else &.{ i, j };
            largest = @max(largest, try a.at(at));
        }
        if (!zm.isFinite(largest)) {
            try out.setAt(&.{i}, largest);
            continue;
        }
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        for (0..along) |j| {
            const at: []const usize = if (axis == 0) &.{ j, i } else &.{ i, j };
            total.add(@exp((try a.at(at)) - largest));
        }
        try out.setAt(&.{i}, largest + @log(total.value()));
    }
}
stdDevAxis
/// Standard deviation along one axis: the square root of `varianceAxis`.
///
/// Thin on purpose. It exists because `@sqrt(varianceAxis(...))` needs a second buffer and a
/// second pass at every call site, and because a reader looking for a standard deviation should
/// find one rather than have to know it is a square root away.
pub fn stdDevAxis(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    axis: usize,
    ddof: Ddof,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    try varianceAxis(T, out, a, axis, ddof);
    for (out.data) |*slot| {
        slot.* = @sqrt(slot.*);
    }
}

The direct reading of log(sum(exp(a))) overflows almost immediately — exp(800) is infinity in f64, so a sum of exponentials of ordinary-looking numbers comes back inf and its logarithm is inf too:

a = { 800, 801, 802 }    direct: inf     logSumExp: 802.4076

Subtracting the largest element first is the whole function. It makes the biggest term exactly exp(0) = 1 and every other one smaller, so nothing can overflow — and adding the maximum back at the end is exact, because log(sum(exp(a - m))) + m is log(sum(exp(a))) by algebra, not by approximation.

The test asserts three things rather than one: that the direct form really does produce inf (so the function has a reason to exist), that this one agrees with the direct form to 1e-14 where the direct form works (so the trick costs nothing), and that shifting every element by 500 shifts the answer by exactly 500 (the identity the subtraction relies on — break the subtraction and this breaks).

logSumExpAxis takes a maximum per row, so one saturating row does not destabilise its neighbours. There is a GPU twin, log_sum_exp_rows, one thread per row — and it subtracts the maximum for the same reason softmax_rows beside it does. A shader has no more headroom than a host: exp(800) is infinity in f32 long before f64 gives up. One trick, two kernels, and if either drops it the failure is inf rather than a wrong digit. This is the reduction underneath cross entropy: crossEntropy = logSumExp(logits) - logits[correct].

9.7 determinant, and an error that becomes an answer#

const d = try zn.determinant(f64, a, &pivots);   // OVERWRITES `a`
determinant
/// The determinant, by LU factorisation.
///
/// THE PIVOT SIGN IS WHY THIS IS FIVE LINES AND NOT FIFTY
///
/// `lu` already does the hard part and already returns the sign the row swaps accumulated, so
/// the determinant is that sign times the product of the diagonal. Writing a separate
/// cofactor expansion would be a second implementation of the same arithmetic, `O(n!)` instead
/// of `O(n^3)`, and a second place for a bug.
///
/// **`a` is overwritten** - `lu` factorises in place, which is what makes it cheap. A caller who
/// still needs the matrix passes a copy, and the doc says so rather than the caller discovering
/// it.
///
/// A SINGULAR MATRIX IS ZERO HERE AND AN ERROR IN `lu`, AND BOTH ARE RIGHT
///
/// `lu` returns `DomainError` when it cannot pivot, because a factorisation of a singular matrix
/// is not usable for what factorisations are for - solving. A determinant is different: **the
/// determinant of a singular matrix is exactly zero**, which is a complete and useful answer, and
/// is precisely how a caller tests for singularity in the first place.
///
/// So the error is caught here and turned into the zero it means. The same underlying condition
/// is a failure for one caller and an answer for another, and the function each of them calls
/// should say which.
pub fn determinant(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), pivots: []usize) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const pivot_sign: T = lu(T, a, pivots) catch |failure| switch (failure) {
        Error.DomainError => return 0,
        else => return failure,
    };
    var product: T = pivot_sign;
    for (0..a.shape[0]) |i| {
        product *= try a.at(&.{ i, i });
    }
    return product;
}

lu already does the hard part and already returns the sign the row swaps accumulated, so the determinant is that sign times the diagonal — five lines rather than a cofactor expansion, which would be a second implementation of the same arithmetic at O(n!) instead of O(n³).

The test swaps two rows and checks the answer negates. A version that lost the pivot sign would return −306 both times and pass a single-matrix test looking perfectly correct.

A singular matrix is zero here and an error in lu, and both are right. lu returns DomainError when it cannot pivot, because a factorisation of a singular matrix is not usable for what factorisations are for. A determinant is different: the determinant of a singular matrix is exactly zero, which is a complete answer and is precisely how a caller tests for singularity. The same underlying condition is a failure for one caller and an answer for another, and each function says which.

9.8 all and any, and why empty is not a coin flip#

zn.all(i32, t)            every element nonzero
zn.any(i32, t)            some element nonzero
zn.countNonzero(i32, t)   how many
all
/// True when every element is nonzero. An empty tensor is TRUE.
///
/// THE EMPTY CASE IS NOT ARBITRARY
///
/// `all` over nothing is true and `any` over nothing is false, and that is not a coin flip - it
/// is what makes them compose. `all(a ++ b)` must equal `all(a) and all(b)` for every pair
/// including an empty one, which forces `all(empty) == true`; the same argument on `or` forces
/// `any(empty) == false`.
///
/// numpy, Python's builtins and SQL all agree, so a reader can check an answer against any of
/// them. Getting it backwards produces a function that looks right on every non-empty test.
pub fn all(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) bool {
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        if (a.data[a.offsetOf(at)] == 0) {
            return false;
        }
    }
    return true;
}
any
/// True when any element is nonzero. An empty tensor is FALSE - see `all`.
pub fn any(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) bool {
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        if (a.data[a.offsetOf(at)] != 0) {
            return true;
        }
    }
    return false;
}
countNonzero
/// How many elements are nonzero.
///
/// Returns a `usize`, not a `T`. A count is not a measurement in the tensor's units, and at f32
/// it would stop being exact at 2^24 - the same ceiling the index-tensor work ran into.
pub fn countNonzero(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) usize {
    var total: usize = 0;
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        if (a.data[a.offsetOf(at)] != 0) {
            total += 1;
        }
    }
    return total;
}

The empty case is forced

all over nothing is true. any over nothing is false. That looks arbitrary and is not:

all(a ++ b) == all(a) and all(b)      must hold for every pair
take b empty  ->  all(a) == all(a) and all(empty)
                  so all(empty) must be true

The same argument on or forces any(empty) == false. numpy, Python's builtins and SQL all agree, so an answer can be checked against any of them.

Getting it backwards produces a function that looks right on every non-empty test, the composition directly rather than just the two values.

nanSum is a name, not a flag

values    1, NaN, 3
sumAll    -> NaN      correct: it tells you one arrived
nanSum    -> 4        skipped

sumAll propagating a NaN is useful. Skipping silently by default would hide it, so the skipping version is a different name and reaching for it is a decision the caller makes rather than a default they inherit.

For a column with real gaps, Series.sumValid is better still: it skips what is absent rather than what is NaN — and for an integer column there is no NaN to skip.

One small consistency: nanMean of an all-NaN tensor returns NaN, where Series.meanValid returns null. The difference is deliberate — here the caller already has NaNs in their data; there the absence was recorded rather than inferred from a value.

9.9 argExtreme, and when a reduction row gets a zero bar#

zn.argExtreme(f64, .largest, t)    which element wins, ANYWHERE  -> one number
zn.argmaxAxis(f64, i32, out, t, 1)      which column wins in each row -> a tensor
argExtreme
/// The FLAT index of the largest or smallest element in the whole tensor.
///
/// FLAT, WHERE `argmaxAxis` IS PER-AXIS - AND THAT IS THE WHOLE DIFFERENCE
///
/// `argmaxAxis` answers "which column wins in each row". This answers "which element wins,
/// anywhere". Both are useful and they are not variants of each other: one returns a tensor of
/// indices, the other a single number.
///
/// The number is a FLAT index into the walk order, not a coordinate. A coordinate would need a
/// buffer to return it in, and the caller who wants one has `unravel` arithmetic that depends on
/// a shape they already have.
///
/// TIES GO TO THE FIRST, which is numpy's rule and the only one that makes the answer independent
/// of how the tensor is traversed. A later-wins rule would give a different index for a tensor and
/// its own transpose.
///
/// `which` is comptime, so the comparison is one branch resolved at compile time rather than a
/// test in the inner loop.
pub fn argExtreme(comptime T: type, comptime which: Extreme, a: Tensor(T)) Error!usize {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (a.size() == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var best: T = if (which == .largest) zm.lowest(T) else zm.highest(T);
    var best_at: usize = 0;
    var position: usize = 0;
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| : (position += 1) {
        const x: T = a.data[a.offsetOf(at)];
        const better: bool = if (which == .largest) x > best else x < best;
        if (better) {
            best = x;
            best_at = position;
        }
    }
    return best_at;
}
argmaxAxis
/// The index of the largest element along `axis`, written into `out` as a float.
///
/// A float, because every tensor here holds one type and an index tensor would need a second.
/// Every index a rank-2 tensor can address is exact in f32 up to 2^24, and in f64 up to 2^53 -
/// far past any axis this library can hold. Stated rather than left to be discovered.
pub fn argmaxAxis(
    comptime T: type,
    comptime Index: type,
    out: Tensor(Index),
    a: Tensor(T),
    axis: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    comptime requireNumeric(Index);
    return argExtremumAxis(T, Index, out, a, axis, true);
}

These are not variants of each other. One returns a single index, the other a tensor of them, and reaching for the wrong one is a shape error rather than a wrong answer — which is at least loud.

The index is flat, a position in the walk, not an offset into the buffer. That distinction is what makes the answer usable on a strided column: the middle column of a matrix has its largest element at walk position 0 while sitting at buffer index 1.

Ties go to the first. numpy's rule, and the only one that makes the answer independent of traversal order — a later-wins rule would give a different index for a tensor and its own transpose, which is the kind of inconsistency nobody thinks to look for.

When a reduction row gets a zero bar

Most reduction rows in the GPU sweep carry a ULP allowance, because a sum accumulates differently depending on the order the threads finish in. Two of the new ones do not:

count nonzero   bar 0    a count INCREMENTS; integer addition is exact in any order
diff            bar 0    one subtraction, one rounding, on both sides

That is worth knowing when reading the sweep: a nonzero difference in those rows is a real bug, not rounding. Choosing a generous bar for every reduction because most of them need one would have thrown that away.

9.10 Reductions on the GPU, and which bars can be zero#

Every operation in this library has a GPU kernel checked against its CPU twin on the same input. Each row carries a tolerance, and choosing it is the interesting part.

all nonzero     tol 0        exact
any nonzero     tol 0        exact
count nonzero   tol 0        exact
diff            tol 0        exact
prod all        tol 1e-3     NOT exact
sum all         tol 6e-4     NOT exact

Why some are exactly zero

all and any combine with and and or, which are associative and idempotent. No order of combination can change the answer, so any difference at all is a real bug rather than accumulated rounding.

count increments an integer. diff subtracts neighbouring pairs, each rounding once and independently. Same story: the arithmetic has no room to drift.

A zero bar is a stronger claim than a small one, and using one where it holds is how the sweep stays informative. A tolerance everywhere would pass a kernel that was subtly wrong.

Why a product cannot have one

Multiplication is associative in real arithmetic and is not in floating point: (a*b)*c and a*(b*c) can differ in the last bits. A product accumulates that over the whole buffer, where a sum accumulates only absolute error — and it reaches infinity far sooner.

So the tolerance beside two zeros is not laziness about the product. It is the measurement, and the contrast is what makes all three bars mean something.

One thing the row proves that a unit test does not

all_nonzero exits early on the first zero. A kernel that exited on the first nonzero instead would pass any test that only fed it an all-zero buffer. The sweep runs the real field — a spread of values in an order nobody chose — against the host function, which is how an early-exit mistake surfaces.

9.11 Integrating a sampled function, and labelling a spectrum#

const area = try zn.trapz(f64, y, spacing);       evenly spaced
const area = try zn.trapzCoords(f64, y, x);       positions given
trapz
/// The integral of a sampled function by the trapezoid rule, evenly spaced.
///
/// WHY TRAPEZOIDS AND NOT RECTANGLES
///
/// A rectangle takes one endpoint of each interval and ignores the other, so it is systematically
/// wrong on any sloped function - too low on a rise, too high on a fall. A trapezoid averages the
/// two, which makes it **exact for any straight line** and therefore exact for the two cases
/// people check first.
///
/// The weights fall out of that: the interior samples each belong to two intervals and count
/// once, while the two ENDPOINTS belong to one each and count half. Getting that backwards gives
/// an answer wrong by roughly one sample's worth, which on a long series looks like a small
/// calibration error rather than a bug.
pub fn trapz(comptime T: type, y: Tensor(T), spacing: T) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = y.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (n == 1) {
        // One sample spans no interval, so the integral is zero rather than the sample's value.
        return 0;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    for (0..n) |i| {
        const weight: T = if (i == 0 or i == n - 1) 0.5 else 1.0;
        total.add(weight * y.data[y.offsetOf(&.{i})]);
    }
    return total.value() * spacing;
}
trapzCoords
/// The same, with the sample positions given rather than assumed even.
///
/// Each interval carries its own width, so this handles data logged whenever something happened
/// rather than on a clock. `x` must be increasing: a repeated or decreasing coordinate would
/// contribute a zero or negative width, and silently subtracting area is worse than refusing.
pub fn trapzCoords(comptime T: type, y: Tensor(T), x: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = y.size();
    if (n != x.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (n == 1) {
        return 0;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    for (0..n - 1) |i| {
        const left: T = x.data[x.offsetOf(&.{i})];
        const right: T = x.data[x.offsetOf(&.{i + 1})];
        if (!(right > left)) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        const ly: T = y.data[y.offsetOf(&.{i})];
        const ry: T = y.data[y.offsetOf(&.{i + 1})];
        total.add(0.5 * (ly + ry) * (right - left));
    }
    return total.value();
}

Trapezoids, because they are exact on a line

A rectangle rule takes one endpoint of each interval and ignores the other, so it is systematically wrong on any slope — too low on a rise, too high on a fall. A trapezoid averages the two, which makes it exact for any straight line.

y = x over [0,4], spacing 1     true 8
  trapezoid    8      exact
  left rect    6      too low
  right rect  10      too high

The endpoints count half

This is the classic error. Five samples at unit spacing span four intervals:

y = 1, five samples    ->  integral 4, not 5

The interior samples each belong to two intervals and count once; the two endpoints belong to one each and count half. Weighting everything equally gives 5 — wrong by exactly one sample's worth, which on a long series reads like a small calibration error rather than a bug.

A consequence worth knowing: one sample integrates to zero, not to its own value. It spans no interval.

On a curve the answer is approximate and the error has a known sign: trapezoids over-estimate a convex function, because the chord sits above the curve. The test asserts that direction, not just the magnitude.

Uneven spacing refuses a backward step

trapzCoords handles data logged whenever something happened rather than on a clock. A repeated coordinate contributes zero width and a decreasing one would subtract area — silently. Both are refused.

And the two agree on the case they share: even coordinates through trapzCoords give exactly what trapz gives and so the implementations cannot drift.

Without rfftfreq a spectrum is unlabelled numbers

rfft returns n/2 + 1 bins and says nothing about what frequency each one is. The mapping is k / (n * spacing):

8 samples, 1 ms apart  ->  0, 125, 250, 375, 500 Hz

Bin 0 is DC and the last is Nyquist — the highest frequency a sample rate can represent. Working it out by hand goes right until n is odd, and the symptom is a plot whose peak sits at the wrong pitch.

10. Linear algebra#

10.1 Matrix multiplication#

try zn.matmul(f64, out, a, b);                       // (m,k) @ (k,n) -> (m,n)
try zn.matmul(f64, out, a, try b.transpose(0, 1));   // a @ bT, no copy
matmul
/// `out = a @ b` - the matrix product, for rank-2 tensors only.
///
/// THE ONE OPERATION THAT IS NOT ELEMENTWISE
///
/// `(m, k) @ (k, n) -> (m, n)`. Every shape is checked; a mismatch is `ShapeMismatch` before any
/// element is written, so a partially-filled output is not a state this can produce.
///
/// The inner sum is compensated, for the reason in `sumAll`: a dot product over a long
/// contraction axis is exactly the accumulation that loses its low bits, and in a neural network
/// that axis is the layer width. Getting this wrong does not crash - it makes gradients slightly
/// wrong, everywhere, in a way that reads as "training is a bit unstable".
///
/// This is the naive triple loop, deliberately. It is the SPECIFICATION every faster
/// implementation is checked against, and it stays in the file when a blocked or tiled version
/// arrives. Strided and stretched inputs are handled by going through `at`, so a transposed view
/// multiplies correctly without being copied first.
pub fn matmul(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or b.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const m: usize = a.shape[0];
    const k: usize = a.shape[1];
    const n: usize = b.shape[1];
    if (b.shape[0] != k or out.shape[0] != m or out.shape[1] != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }

    var row: usize = 0;
    while (row < m) : (row += 1) {
        var col: usize = 0;
        while (col < n) : (col += 1) {
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < k) : (i += 1) {
                const product: T = a.at2(row, i) *
                    b.at2(i, col);
                total.add(product);
            }
            out.setAt2(row, col, total.value());
        }
    }
}

Rank 2 only. Every shape is checked before any element is written, so a partially-filled output is not a state this can produce. The inner sum is compensated.

Strided inputs work without being copied, because the implementation reads through at. A transposed operand is therefore free on the CPU — a stride swap — which is not true of the GPU kernels, where the transpose has to live in the index arithmetic or the operand has to be materialised first.

This is the naive triple loop. It is the specification a faster implementation is checked against.

10.2 Dot, trace and norm#

const d  = try zn.dotAll(f64, u, v);     // sizes must match; shapes need not
const tr = try zn.trace(f64, a);      // square, rank 2
const nm = try zn.norm(f64, a);       // Frobenius, over every element
dotAll
/// The dot product of two tensors with the same number of elements.
///
/// Shapes need not match, only sizes: a row and a column of the same length have the same dot
/// product, and requiring identical shapes would force a reshape at every call site.
pub fn dotAll(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n != b.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var pair: Walk2 = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank], b.shape[0..b.rank]);
    while (pair.next()) |at| {
        const term: T = (try a.at(at.first)) *
            (try b.at(at.second));
        total.add(term);
    }
    return total.value();
}
trace
/// The sum of the diagonal of a square rank-2 tensor.
pub fn trace(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or a.shape[0] != a.shape[1]) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    var total: T = 0;
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < a.shape[0]) : (i += 1) {
        total += a.at2(i, i);
    }
    return total;
}
norm
/// The Euclidean (Frobenius) norm: the square root of the sum of squares.
///
/// SCALED BY THE LARGEST MAGNITUDE BEFORE SQUARING. A vector whose elements are near
/// `sqrt(maxFloat)` overflows a direct sum of squares even though its norm is representable, and
/// one near `sqrt(minFloat)` underflows to zero. Factoring the largest element out costs one extra
/// pass and removes both failures.
pub fn norm(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    var largest: T = 0;
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        largest = @max(largest, @abs(try a.at(at)));
    }
    if (largest == 0) {
        return 0;
    }
    // A SECOND pass over the same shape: a fresh `Walk` rather than resetting the first, which
    // is what the old code did and is one more thing to forget.
    var second: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    var total: T = 0;
    while (second.next()) |at| {
        const scaled: T = (try a.at(at)) / largest;
        total += scaled * scaled;
    }
    return largest * @sqrt(total);
}

dotAll requires equal element counts rather than equal shapes, so a row and a column of the same length pair without a reshape. Its accumulation is compensated.

norm divides by the largest magnitude before squaring and multiplies back afterwards:

if (largest == 0) return 0;
// ... total += (x / largest) * (x / largest) ...
return largest * @sqrt(total);

A direct sum of squares overflows for a vector whose elements approach √maxFloat and underflows to zero near √minFloat, in both cases for inputs whose norm is perfectly representable. The extra pass removes both. A vector of two 1e200 values returns 1.414e200 rather than infinity.

10.3 LU, solve and determinant#

var pivots: [3]usize = @splat(0);

const sign = try zn.lu(f64, a, &pivots);            // a is overwritten
try zn.solve(f64, a, b, &pivots);                   // a overwritten, b becomes x
const det = try zn.determinantLu(f64, a, &pivots);    // a overwritten
lu
/// An LU factorisation with partial pivoting, computed in place.
///
/// WHAT `lu` LEAVES BEHIND
///
/// `a` is overwritten: the strict lower triangle holds L's off-diagonal entries (L's diagonal is
/// all ones and is not stored), and the upper triangle including the diagonal holds U.
/// `pivots[i]` is the row swapped into position `i`. The return value is the SIGN of the
/// permutation, +1 or -1, which `determinant` needs and which would otherwise have to be
/// recomputed by counting the swaps.
///
/// PARTIAL PIVOTING IS NOT OPTIONAL. Without it a matrix whose leading entry is small but
/// non-zero divides by that small number and loses most of its precision, and a leading zero
/// fails outright on a matrix that is perfectly well conditioned. Choosing the largest available
/// pivot in the column bounds the multipliers by 1.
///
/// A column with no non-zero pivot means the matrix is singular: `DomainError`, rather than an
/// infinity that reaches the caller as a plausible-looking result.
pub fn lu(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), pivots: []usize) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or a.shape[0] != a.shape[1]) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const n: usize = a.shape[0];
    if (pivots.len != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var pivot_sign: T = 1;
    var col: usize = 0;
    while (col < n) : (col += 1) {
        // Largest magnitude at or below the diagonal becomes the pivot.
        var best: usize = col;
        var best_value: T = @abs(a.at2(col, col));
        var row: usize = col + 1;
        while (row < n) : (row += 1) {
            const candidate: T = @abs(a.at2(row, col));
            if (candidate > best_value) {
                best_value = candidate;
                best = row;
            }
        }
        if (best_value == 0) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        pivots[col] = best;
        if (best != col) {
            pivot_sign = -pivot_sign;
            var j: usize = 0;
            while (j < n) : (j += 1) {
                const top: T = a.at2(col, j);
                a.setAt2(col, j, a.at2(best, j));
                a.setAt2(best, j, top);
            }
        }

        const pivot: T = a.at2(col, col);
        row = col + 1;
        while (row < n) : (row += 1) {
            const factor: T = a.at2(row, col) / pivot;
            a.setAt2(row, col, factor);
            var j: usize = col + 1;
            while (j < n) : (j += 1) {
                const updated: T = a.at2(row, j) -
                    factor * a.at2(col, j);
                a.setAt2(row, j, updated);
            }
        }
    }
    return pivot_sign;
}
solve
/// Solve `A x = b` for a square `A`, overwriting `a` with its factorisation and `b` with `x`.
///
/// `b` may have several columns, each an independent right-hand side. Solving for many at once
/// reuses the one factorisation, which is the expensive part.
pub fn solve(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T), pivots: []usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (b.rank != 2 or b.shape[0] != a.shape[0]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    _ = try lu(T, a, pivots);
    const n: usize = a.shape[0];
    const rhs: usize = b.shape[1];

    // Apply the same row swaps to b.
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < n) : (i += 1) {
        if (pivots[i] != i) {
            var j: usize = 0;
            while (j < rhs) : (j += 1) {
                const top: T = b.at2(i, j);
                b.setAt2(i, j, b.at2(pivots[i], j));
                b.setAt2(pivots[i], j, top);
            }
        }
    }
    // Forward substitution through L, whose diagonal is implicitly 1.
    i = 0;
    while (i < n) : (i += 1) {
        var j: usize = 0;
        while (j < rhs) : (j += 1) {
            var acc: T = b.at2(i, j);
            var k: usize = 0;
            while (k < i) : (k += 1) {
                acc -= a.at2(i, k) * b.at2(k, j);
            }
            b.setAt2(i, j, acc);
        }
    }
    // Back substitution through U.
    i = n;
    while (i > 0) {
        i -= 1;
        var j: usize = 0;
        while (j < rhs) : (j += 1) {
            var acc: T = b.at2(i, j);
            var k: usize = i + 1;
            while (k < n) : (k += 1) {
                acc -= a.at2(i, k) * b.at2(k, j);
            }
            b.setAt2(i, j, acc / a.at2(i, i));
        }
    }
}
determinantLu
/// The determinant of a square matrix. `a` is overwritten by its factorisation.
///
/// The product of U's diagonal times the permutation sign. Returns 0 for a singular matrix
/// rather than propagating `lu`'s `DomainError`: a determinant of zero is the correct answer to
/// the question, not a failure to answer it.
pub fn determinantLu(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), pivots: []usize) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const permutation_sign: T = lu(T, a, pivots) catch |err| switch (err) {
        Error.DomainError => return 0,
        else => return err,
    };
    var product: T = permutation_sign;
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < a.shape[0]) : (i += 1) {
        product *= a.at2(i, i);
    }
    return product;
}

lu factorises in place with partial pivoting. Afterwards the strict lower triangle of a holds L's off-diagonal entries — L's diagonal is all ones and is not stored — and the upper triangle including the diagonal holds U. pivots[i] is the row swapped into position i. The return value is the sign of the permutation, +1 or −1, which determinantLu needs.

Pivoting selects the largest available magnitude in the column, which bounds every multiplier by 1. Without it, a small leading entry divides by that small number and loses most of the available precision, and a zero leading entry fails on a matrix that is otherwise well conditioned.

A column with no non-zero pivot means the matrix is singular. lu and solve return DomainError; determinantLu returns 0, because a determinant of zero is the answer to the question rather than a failure to answer it.

solve factorises, applies the same row swaps to b, then substitutes forward through L and back through U. b may have several columns, each an independent right-hand side; solving for many at once reuses the single factorisation, which is the expensive part.

All three overwrite a. Keep a copy if you need the original — checking a solution's residual requires one.

10.4 QR, by Householder#

try zn.qr(f64, q, r, a);   // a is (m, n) with m >= n; q is (m, m), r is (m, n)
qr
/// QR by Householder reflections: `a = q * r`, with `q` orthogonal and `r` upper triangular.
///
/// `a` is `(m, n)` with `m >= n`; `q` is `(m, m)` and `r` is `(m, n)`. Both are overwritten.
///
/// HOUSEHOLDER, NOT GRAM-SCHMIDT, AND THE DIFFERENCE IS MEASURED
///
/// Gram-Schmidt builds `q` a column at a time by subtracting projections. Even the modified form
/// loses orthogonality in proportion to the matrix's condition number: on an ill-conditioned
/// input the columns it returns are **visibly not orthogonal**, and every later use of `q`
/// inherits that error. Householder builds `q` from reflections, each orthogonal to working
/// precision by construction, so their product is too however badly conditioned the input.
///
/// The test runs both on the same matrix and asserts the gap - this is not a preference
/// between algorithms, it is one of them being wrong.
///
/// Each reflection zeroes one column below the diagonal. `alpha` takes the sign OPPOSITE the
/// pivot so `v = x - alpha*e1` never subtracts two nearby numbers; the same sign would cancel
/// catastrophically exactly when the pivot dominates its column, which is the common case.
pub fn qr(comptime T: type, q: Tensor(T), r: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or q.rank != 2 or r.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const m: usize = a.shape[0];
    const n: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (m < n) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (q.shape[0] != m or q.shape[1] != m or r.shape[0] != m or r.shape[1] != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var reflector: [max_rank * 512]T = undefined;
    if (m > reflector.len) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }

    try materialise(T, r, a);
    q.fill(0);
    var d: usize = 0;
    while (d < m) : (d += 1) {
        try q.setAt(&.{ d, d }, 1);
    }

    var k: usize = 0;
    while (k < n) : (k += 1) {
        var norm_squared: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        var i: usize = k;
        while (i < m) : (i += 1) {
            const x: T = try r.at(&.{ i, k });
            norm_squared.add(x * x);
        }
        const column_norm: T = @sqrt(norm_squared.value());
        if (column_norm == 0) {
            continue;
        }
        const pivot: T = try r.at(&.{ k, k });
        const alpha: T = if (pivot > 0) -column_norm else column_norm;

        reflector[k] = pivot - alpha;
        i = k + 1;
        while (i < m) : (i += 1) {
            reflector[i] = try r.at(&.{ i, k });
        }
        var v_squared: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        i = k;
        while (i < m) : (i += 1) {
            v_squared.add(reflector[i] * reflector[i]);
        }
        const v_norm_squared: T = v_squared.value();
        if (v_norm_squared == 0) {
            continue;
        }

        var j: usize = k;
        while (j < n) : (j += 1) {
            var projection: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            i = k;
            while (i < m) : (i += 1) {
                projection.add(reflector[i] * (try r.at(&.{ i, j })));
            }
            const factor: T = 2 * projection.value() / v_norm_squared;
            i = k;
            while (i < m) : (i += 1) {
                try r.setAt(&.{ i, j }, (try r.at(&.{ i, j })) - factor * reflector[i]);
            }
        }

        var row: usize = 0;
        while (row < m) : (row += 1) {
            var projection: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            i = k;
            while (i < m) : (i += 1) {
                projection.add(reflector[i] * (try q.at(&.{ row, i })));
            }
            const factor: T = 2 * projection.value() / v_norm_squared;
            i = k;
            while (i < m) : (i += 1) {
                try q.setAt(&.{ row, i }, (try q.at(&.{ row, i })) - factor * reflector[i]);
            }
        }
    }

    // The reflections leave arithmetic dust below the diagonal; it is structurally zero.
    var row: usize = 1;
    while (row < m) : (row += 1) {
        var col: usize = 0;
        while (col < @min(row, n)) : (col += 1) {
            try r.setAt(&.{ row, col }, 0);
        }
    }
}

Gram–Schmidt builds q a column at a time by subtracting projections, and even the modified form loses orthogonality in proportion to the matrix's condition number. Householder builds q from reflections, each orthogonal to working precision by construction, so their product is too however badly conditioned the input. Measured on Hilbert matrices — ill-conditioned by construction, one line to build:

SizeHouseholderGram–Schmidt
6×64.4e-161.8e-10
8×83.3e-164.4e-7
10×104.4e-161.8e-4

Householder does not degrade with size at all. A 1.8e-4 error is not a rounding difference — it is q not being orthogonal, and every later use of it inherits that. The test asserts Gram–Schmidt is visibly worse, so if that ever stopped being true the reason for the extra work would be gone.

Each reflection takes the sign opposite the pivot when forming v = x − αe₁, so it never subtracts two nearby numbers; the same sign cancels catastrophically exactly when the pivot dominates its column, which is the common case. r is upper triangular exactly — the dust below the diagonal is zeroed, not merely small.

10.5 Symmetric eigenvalues#

try zn.eigh(f64, &values, vectors, a);   // a is overwritten; vectors' COLUMNS are eigenvectors
eigh
/// Eigenvalues and eigenvectors of a symmetric matrix, by cyclic Jacobi rotations.
///
/// `a` is `(n, n)` and symmetric, and is OVERWRITTEN - it becomes the diagonal matrix. `values`
/// receives the eigenvalues, `vectors` the eigenvectors as its COLUMNS, so `a*vectors[:,k] =
/// values[k]*vectors[:,k]`.
///
/// THE CONVERGENCE TEST IS RELATIVE TO THE MATRIX, NOT AN ABSOLUTE CONSTANT
///
/// Each rotation zeroes one off-diagonal pair and the sweep repeats until what remains off the
/// diagonal is negligible. **Negligible compared to what** is the whole question. A fixed
/// threshold - znum uses `1e-30` on the sum of squares - declares any sufficiently small matrix
/// already diagonal: scale a matrix by `1e-16` and its off-diagonal sum of squares falls below
/// `1e-30` before a single rotation, so the routine returns the untouched diagonal as the
/// eigenvalues. They are not the eigenvalues, and nothing reports a problem.
///
/// Measuring against the matrix's own Frobenius norm makes the test scale-free: the same
/// matrix times `1e-20` gives the same eigenvalues times `1e-20`, which the test asserts.
///
/// `DomainError` if the sweeps run out. Cyclic Jacobi converges quadratically and reaches
/// working precision in well under ten sweeps for any matrix this will see; returning an
/// unconverged answer silently is the failure this avoids.
pub fn eigh(comptime T: type, values: []T, vectors: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or vectors.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const n: usize = a.shape[0];
    if (a.shape[1] != n or vectors.shape[0] != n or vectors.shape[1] != n or values.len != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }

    // The scale every later comparison is relative to, taken once from the input.
    var frobenius: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    for (0..n) |i| {
        for (0..n) |j| {
            const x: T = try a.at(&.{ i, j });
            frobenius.add(x * x);
        }
    }
    const frobenius_norm: T = @sqrt(frobenius.value());
    // An all-zero matrix is already diagonal, and its eigenvalues are zero.
    const threshold: T = frobenius_norm * @as(T, @floatCast(1.0e-15));

    vectors.fill(0);
    for (0..n) |i| {
        try vectors.setAt(&.{ i, i }, 1);
    }

    const max_sweeps: usize = 60;
    var sweep: usize = 0;
