    while (sweep < max_sweeps) : (sweep += 1) {
        var off: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        for (0..n) |p| {
            for (p + 1..n) |q| {
                const x: T = try a.at(&.{ p, q });
                off.add(x * x);
            }
        }
        if (@sqrt(off.value()) <= threshold) {
            for (0..n) |i| {
                values[i] = try a.at(&.{ i, i });
            }
            return;
        }

        for (0..n) |p| {
            for (p + 1..n) |q| {
                const apq: T = try a.at(&.{ p, q });
                if (apq == 0) {
                    continue;
                }
                // The rotation that zeroes a[p][q]. `t` is the SMALLER root, taken in the form
                // that avoids subtracting nearby numbers when theta is large.
                const theta: T = ((try a.at(&.{ q, q })) - (try a.at(&.{ p, p }))) / (2 * apq);
                const magnitude: T = @abs(theta) + @sqrt(theta * theta + 1);
                const t: T = if (theta >= 0) 1 / magnitude else -1 / magnitude;
                const c: T = 1 / @sqrt(t * t + 1);
                const sn: T = t * c;

                // Columns p and q of `a`, then rows p and q, then the accumulated rotations.
                for (0..n) |k| {
                    const kp: T = try a.at(&.{ k, p });
                    const kq: T = try a.at(&.{ k, q });
                    try a.setAt(&.{ k, p }, c * kp - sn * kq);
                    try a.setAt(&.{ k, q }, sn * kp + c * kq);
                }
                for (0..n) |k| {
                    const pk: T = try a.at(&.{ p, k });
                    const qk: T = try a.at(&.{ q, k });
                    try a.setAt(&.{ p, k }, c * pk - sn * qk);
                    try a.setAt(&.{ q, k }, sn * pk + c * qk);
                }
                for (0..n) |k| {
                    const kp: T = try vectors.at(&.{ k, p });
                    const kq: T = try vectors.at(&.{ k, q });
                    try vectors.setAt(&.{ k, p }, c * kp - sn * kq);
                    try vectors.setAt(&.{ k, q }, sn * kp + c * kq);
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return Error.DomainError;
}

Cyclic Jacobi: each rotation zeroes one off-diagonal pair, and the sweep repeats until what remains off the diagonal is negligible. Negligible compared to what is the whole question. A fixed threshold declares any sufficiently small matrix already diagonal — scale a matrix by 1e-16 and its off-diagonal sum of squares falls below 1e-30 before a single rotation, so the routine returns the untouched diagonal as the eigenvalues. They are not the eigenvalues, and nothing reports a problem. Measuring against the matrix's own Frobenius norm makes the check scale-free:

ScaleResidual max |Av − λv|λ₀
11.6e-156.7328e-1
1e-101.3e-256.7328e-11
1e-202.9e-356.7328e-21

The test checks A·v = λv for every eigenpair — checking eigenvalues alone would pass on correct values paired with the wrong vectors — plus the trace identity as an independent check that none was lost, and orthonormality of the vectors. Running out of sweeps is a DomainError: Jacobi converges quadratically and reaches working precision in well under ten sweeps, so returning an unconverged answer silently is the failure worth avoiding.

10.6 Least squares#

try zn.matmulNT(f64, out, a, b);   // a · bᵀ
try zn.matmulTN(f64, out, a, b);   // aᵀ · b
try zn.solveTriangular(f64, x, r, b, .upper);
try zn.lstsq(f64, x, q, r, a, b);  // q and r are scratch and come back as the factorisation
matmulNT
/// `out = a * bT`. The right-hand matrix is read transposed rather than materialised.
///
/// THIS IS AN INDEXING CHOICE, NOT AN ALGORITHM. `matmul(out, a, transpose(b))` gives the same
/// answer, but a transposed VIEW makes the inner loop stride across memory; naming the variant
/// lets the loop read both operands the way they are stored. The test asserts it against the
/// materialised form, which is what makes the name safe to reach for.
pub fn matmulNT(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or b.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (a.shape[1] != b.shape[1] or out.shape[0] != a.shape[0] or out.shape[1] != b.shape[0]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    for (0..a.shape[0]) |i| {
        for (0..b.shape[0]) |j| {
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            for (0..a.shape[1]) |k| {
                total.add((try a.at(&.{ i, k })) * (try b.at(&.{ j, k })));
            }
            try out.setAt(&.{ i, j }, total.value());
        }
    }
}
matmulTN
/// `out = aT * b`.
pub fn matmulTN(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or b.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (a.shape[0] != b.shape[0] or out.shape[0] != a.shape[1] or out.shape[1] != b.shape[1]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    for (0..a.shape[1]) |i| {
        for (0..b.shape[1]) |j| {
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            for (0..a.shape[0]) |k| {
                total.add((try a.at(&.{ k, i })) * (try b.at(&.{ k, j })));
            }
            try out.setAt(&.{ i, j }, total.value());
        }
    }
}
solveTriangular
/// Solve `t * x = b` where `t` is triangular, by substitution. `t` is `(n, n)`, `b` and `x` are
/// `(n, k)`, so several right-hand sides are solved at once.
///
/// `DomainError` on a zero diagonal entry - a triangular matrix with a zero on the diagonal is
/// singular, and substitution would divide by it. Reporting that is more useful than an infinity
/// propagating through the rest of the solve.
pub fn solveTriangular(
    comptime T: type,
    x: Tensor(T),
    t: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
    side: Triangle,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (t.rank != 2 or b.rank != 2 or x.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const n: usize = t.shape[0];
    if (t.shape[1] != n or b.shape[0] != n or x.shape[0] != n or x.shape[1] != b.shape[1]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const columns: usize = b.shape[1];
    for (0..columns) |c| {
        var step: usize = 0;
        while (step < n) : (step += 1) {
            // Upper triangles are solved from the bottom row up, lower ones from the top down.
            const i: usize = if (side == .upper) n - 1 - step else step;
            const pivot: T = try t.at(&.{ i, i });
            if (pivot == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            total.add(try b.at(&.{ i, c }));
            var j: usize = if (side == .upper) i + 1 else 0;
            while (if (side == .upper) j < n else j < i) : (j += 1) {
                total.add(-(try t.at(&.{ i, j })) * (try x.at(&.{ j, c })));
            }
            try x.setAt(&.{ i, c }, total.value() / pivot);
        }
    }
}
lstsq
/// Least squares: the `x` minimising `||a*x - b||`, by QR.
///
/// `a` is `(m, n)` with `m >= n`, `b` is `(m, k)`, `x` is `(n, k)`. `q` `(m, m)` and `r` `(m, n)`
/// are scratch that come back holding the factorisation, and `b` is OVERWRITTEN with `qT*b`.
///
/// QR, NOT THE NORMAL EQUATIONS
///
/// The textbook shortcut solves `aTa * x = aTb`, which is one line and squares the condition
/// number: a system solvable to eight digits becomes solvable to four. Factorising `a = q*r`
/// instead turns the problem into `r*x = qTb` - triangular, and no worse conditioned than `a`.
/// znum does this too and is right to.
///
/// What differs is underneath: this `qr` is Householder where znum's is Gram-Schmidt, so on an
/// ill-conditioned system the `q` here is orthogonal and znum's is not. The test measures the
/// residual on a Hilbert system.
///
/// Nothing is allocated. A caller with several right-hand sides can factor once and call
/// `solveTriangular` directly for each.
pub fn lstsq(
    comptime T: type,
    x: Tensor(T),
    q: Tensor(T),
    r: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or b.rank != 2 or x.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const m: usize = a.shape[0];
    const n: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (b.shape[0] != m or x.shape[0] != n or x.shape[1] != b.shape[1]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    try qr(T, q, r, a);

    // b <- qT*b, one column at a time so no second buffer is needed.
    var scratch: [max_rank * 512]T = undefined;
    if (m > scratch.len) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    for (0..b.shape[1]) |c| {
        for (0..m) |i| {
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            for (0..m) |k| {
                total.add((try q.at(&.{ k, i })) * (try b.at(&.{ k, c })));
            }
            scratch[i] = total.value();
        }
        for (0..m) |i| {
            try b.setAt(&.{ i, c }, scratch[i]);
        }
    }

    // The leading n rows are the square triangular system; the rest is the residual.
    const top_r: Tensor(T) = try r.slice(0, 0, n);
    const top_b: Tensor(T) = try b.slice(0, 0, n);
    return solveTriangular(T, x, top_r, top_b, .upper);
}

The textbook shortcut for min ‖Ax − b‖ solves AᵀA·x = Aᵀb. It is one line and it squares the condition number: a system solvable to eight digits becomes solvable to four. Factorising A = QR turns the problem into R·x = Qᵀb — triangular, and no worse conditioned than A. Measured on a Vandermonde design matrix with a known exact solution:

nQRNormal equations
55.9e-158.2e-13
87.8e-131.6e-7
101.3e-111.6e-4

Both are correct algorithms; one of them stops being usable first. The test asserts the normal equations are visibly worse on the same system, which is what makes the extra factorisation worth its cost rather than a claim in a comment. It also checks the defining property of a least-squares fit — that the residual is orthogonal to every column of the design matrix — on a system with no exact solution.

lstsq allocates nothing: q and r are caller-supplied and come back holding the factorisation, so a caller with several right-hand sides can factor once and call solveTriangular for each. b is overwritten with qᵀb.

matmulNT and matmulTN are an indexing choice rather than an algorithm: matmul(out, a, transpose(b)) gives the same answer, but a transposed view makes the inner loop stride across memory. The test asserts both against the materialised form, which is what makes the names safe to reach for.

10.7 Singular values#

try zn.svd(f64, u, &values, v, a);   // a = u · diag(values) · vᵀ, values descending
svd
/// Singular value decomposition by one-sided Jacobi: `a = u * diag(values) * vT`.
///
/// `a` is `(m, n)` with `m >= n`. `u` is `(m, n)` and receives the left singular vectors as its
/// columns - it is also the working copy, so `a` is not modified. `values` is `n`, descending.
/// `v` is `(n, n)` with the right singular vectors as its columns.
///
/// ONE-SIDED JACOBI, NOT THE EIGENDECOMPOSITION OF `aTa`
///
/// The short route is `eigh(aTa)` and `sigma = sqrtlambda`, which znum takes. It squares the condition number
/// in exactly the way the normal equations do, and here the consequence is sharper than lost
/// digits: a matrix with singular values 1 and **1e-9** has eigenvalues 1 and **1e-18**, which is
/// below f64's epsilon. The small singular value is not computed inaccurately - **it is gone**,
/// recovered from whatever noise sits in that entry of the Gram matrix.
///
/// One-sided Jacobi rotates PAIRS OF COLUMNS of `a` itself until they are orthogonal. It never
/// forms `aTa`, so every singular value comes back to full relative accuracy however small.
///
/// The test builds a matrix with a known 1e-9 singular value and measures both routes.
///
/// The convergence test is relative, for the same reason as `eigh`: a fixed threshold on the
/// column inner products would call a scaled-down matrix already diagonal.
pub fn svd(
    comptime T: type,
    u: Tensor(T),
    values: []T,
    v: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or u.rank != 2 or v.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const m: usize = a.shape[0];
    const n: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (m < n) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (u.shape[0] != m or u.shape[1] != n or v.shape[0] != n or v.shape[1] != n or
        values.len != n)
    {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }

    try materialise(T, u, a);
    v.fill(0);
    for (0..n) |i| {
        try v.setAt(&.{ i, i }, 1);
    }

    const max_sweeps: usize = 60;
    var sweep: usize = 0;
    var rotations: usize = 1;
    while (sweep < max_sweeps and rotations > 0) : (sweep += 1) {
        rotations = 0;
        for (0..n) |p| {
            for (p + 1..n) |q| {
                // The two column lengths and their inner product.
                var alpha: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
                var beta: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
                var gamma: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
                for (0..m) |row| {
                    const ap: T = try u.at(&.{ row, p });
                    const aq: T = try u.at(&.{ row, q });
                    alpha.add(ap * ap);
                    beta.add(aq * aq);
                    gamma.add(ap * aq);
                }
                const dot_pq: T = gamma.value();
                // Relative: the columns are orthogonal enough when their inner product is
                // negligible against their own lengths, at any scale.
                const lengths: T = @sqrt(alpha.value() * beta.value());
                if (lengths == 0 or @abs(dot_pq) <= lengths * @as(T, @floatCast(1.0e-15))) {
                    continue;
                }
                rotations += 1;

                const zeta: T = (beta.value() - alpha.value()) / (2 * dot_pq);
                const magnitude: T = @abs(zeta) + @sqrt(zeta * zeta + 1);
                const t: T = if (zeta >= 0) 1 / magnitude else -1 / magnitude;
                const c: T = 1 / @sqrt(t * t + 1);
                const sn: T = c * t;

                for (0..m) |row| {
                    const ap: T = try u.at(&.{ row, p });
                    const aq: T = try u.at(&.{ row, q });
                    try u.setAt(&.{ row, p }, c * ap - sn * aq);
                    try u.setAt(&.{ row, q }, sn * ap + c * aq);
                }
                for (0..n) |row| {
                    const vp: T = try v.at(&.{ row, p });
                    const vq: T = try v.at(&.{ row, q });
                    try v.setAt(&.{ row, p }, c * vp - sn * vq);
                    try v.setAt(&.{ row, q }, sn * vp + c * vq);
                }
            }
        }
    }
    if (rotations > 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }

    // Each column's length IS its singular value; normalising leaves the left vectors.
    for (0..n) |j| {
        var length: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        for (0..m) |row| {
            const x: T = try u.at(&.{ row, j });
            length.add(x * x);
        }
        values[j] = @sqrt(length.value());
        if (values[j] > 0) {
            for (0..m) |row| {
                try u.setAt(&.{ row, j }, (try u.at(&.{ row, j })) / values[j]);
            }
        }
    }

    // Descending, by selection - n is small and this keeps the permutation obvious.
    for (0..n) |i| {
        var best: usize = i;
        for (i + 1..n) |j| {
            if (values[j] > values[best]) {
                best = j;
            }
        }
        if (best == i) {
            continue;
        }
        std.mem.swap(T, &values[i], &values[best]);
        for (0..m) |row| {
            const keep: T = try u.at(&.{ row, i });
            try u.setAt(&.{ row, i }, try u.at(&.{ row, best }));
            try u.setAt(&.{ row, best }, keep);
        }
        for (0..n) |row| {
            const keep: T = try v.at(&.{ row, i });
            try v.setAt(&.{ row, i }, try v.at(&.{ row, best }));
            try v.setAt(&.{ row, best }, keep);
        }
    }
}

The short route is eigh(aᵀa) with σ = √λ. It squares the condition number, and here the consequence is sharper than lost digits: a matrix with singular values 1 and 1e-9 has eigenvalues 1 and 1e-18, which is below f64's epsilon. The small singular value is not computed inaccurately — it is gone, recovered from whatever noise sits in that entry of the Gram matrix.

One-sided Jacobi rotates pairs of columns of a itself until they are orthogonal, never forming aᵀa. Relative error in the smallest singular value:

True σminOne-sided JacobiThrough aᵀa
1e-31.5e-141.7e-11
1e-64.6e-121.1e-5
1e-92.0e-85.7 — 570% wrong

At 1e-9 the Gram route returns 6.7e-9 for a value of 1e-9. That is not a precision difference; it is a different number. The test asserts the gap only where the squaring actually bites — at 1e-3 both routes are fine, and claiming otherwise would overstate the case.

Reconstruction is checked too: u · diag(σ) · vᵀ must be a again, because checking the singular values alone would pass on correct values paired with the wrong vectors. Each column's length is its singular value, so normalising the working copy at the end leaves the left vectors in place with no extra pass.

10.8 Pseudo-inverse and inverse#

try zn.pinv(f64, out, u, &values, v, a, 1.0e-15);   // u, values, v are scratch
try zn.inverse(f64, out, a, pivots);                  // a is overwritten
pinv
/// The Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse, via `svd`: `out = v * diag(1/sigma) * uT`, with the singular
/// values below `rcond * sigma_max` treated as zero.
///
/// `a` is `(m, n)` with `m >= n`; `out` is `(n, m)`. `u` `(m, n)`, `v` `(n, n)` and `values` `n`
/// are scratch and come back holding the decomposition.
///
/// THE RANK CUTOFF IS RELATIVE TO THE LARGEST SINGULAR VALUE
///
/// Deciding which singular values are "zero" is the whole content of a pseudo-inverse - it is
/// what makes it defined for a singular matrix at all. An ABSOLUTE cutoff, which znum uses at
/// `1e-12`, gets this wrong in both directions: **a matrix scaled down by 1e-6 has every
/// singular value discarded and the pseudo-inverse comes back as zeros**, while one scaled up by
/// 1e12 keeps directions that are numerically noise and inverts them into enormous numbers.
///
/// `rcond * sigma_max` is the standard, and it is scale-free by construction: multiplying `a` by
/// any constant multiplies `sigma_max` by the same one, so exactly the same directions are kept.
/// The test asserts that - `pinv(k*a) = pinv(a)/k` across twelve orders of magnitude.
///
/// The default `rcond` is `1e-15`, near f64's epsilon: a direction whose singular value is
/// that far below the largest carries no information the input's own precision can support.
pub fn pinv(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    u: Tensor(T),
    values: []T,
    v: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    rcond: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const m: usize = a.shape[0];
    const n: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (out.shape[0] != n or out.shape[1] != m) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (rcond < 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    try svd(T, u, values, v, a);

    // `values` is descending, so the first is the largest.
    const cutoff: T = values[0] * rcond;
    for (0..n) |i| {
        for (0..m) |j| {
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            for (0..n) |k| {
                if (values[k] <= cutoff) {
                    continue;
                }
                total.add((try v.at(&.{ i, k })) * (try u.at(&.{ j, k })) / values[k]);
            }
            try out.setAt(&.{ i, j }, total.value());
        }
    }
}
inverse
/// The inverse of a square matrix, by solving `a * x = I`.
///
/// `a` and `work` are `(n, n)`; both are overwritten. `pivots` needs `n` entries.
///
/// Solving against the identity rather than computing a closed form: `solve` already does the
/// factorisation with partial pivoting, and an inverse is `n` right-hand sides of the same
/// system.  **Wanting an inverse is usually a sign of wanting a solve** - `solve(a, b)` is more
/// accurate and cheaper than `matmul(inverse(a), b)`, and this exists for the cases where the
/// matrix itself is the answer.
pub fn inverse(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    pivots: []usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const n: usize = a.shape[0];
    if (a.shape[1] != n or out.shape[0] != n or out.shape[1] != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    out.fill(0);
    for (0..n) |i| {
        try out.setAt(&.{ i, i }, 1);
    }
    return solve(T, a, out, pivots);
}

Deciding which singular values count as zero is the content of a pseudo-inverse — it is what makes one defined for a singular matrix at all. An absolute cutoff gets this wrong in both directions. A matrix with singular values 1, 1e-3, 1e-7, scaled:

ScaleSingular valuesAbsolute cutoff at 1e-12
11e0, 1e-3, 1e-7keeps 3 of 3
1e-61e-6, 1e-9, 1e-13keeps 2 of 3 — a real direction, dropped

Nothing about the matrix changed except its units. rcond · σmax is scale-free by construction: multiplying a by any constant multiplies σmax by the same one, so exactly the same directions are kept. The test asserts pinv(k·a) = pinv(a)/k to nine digits across six orders of magnitude, and checks the defining Penrose condition a · a⁺ · a = a — which holds for a rank-deficient matrix too, and is the reason the pseudo-inverse is worth having.

inverse solves against the identity rather than computing a closed form. Wanting an inverse is usually a sign of wanting a solve: solve(a, b) is both more accurate and cheaper than matmul(inverse(a), b), and this exists for the cases where the matrix itself is the answer.

10.9 Cholesky#

try zn.cholesky(f64, lower, a);   // lower * lowerT == a
cholesky
/// The Cholesky factor of a symmetric positive-definite matrix: lower-triangular `L` with
/// `L LT = a`. Written into `out`, which is zeroed above the diagonal.
///
/// A NON-POSITIVE PIVOT IS THE DEFINITION OF NOT POSITIVE-DEFINITE, so `DomainError` here is
/// informative rather than a limitation: attempting the factorisation IS the standard test for
/// the property, and it is cheaper than computing eigenvalues.
pub fn cholesky(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or a.shape[0] != a.shape[1]) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.rank != 2 or out.shape[0] != a.shape[0] or out.shape[1] != a.shape[1]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const n: usize = a.shape[0];
    out.fill(0);
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < n) : (i += 1) {
        var j: usize = 0;
        while (j <= i) : (j += 1) {
            var acc: T = a.at2(i, j);
            var k: usize = 0;
            while (k < j) : (k += 1) {
                acc -= out.at2(i, k) * out.at2(j, k);
            }
            if (i == j) {
                if (acc <= 0) {
                    return Error.DomainError;
                }
                out.setAt2(i, j, @sqrt(acc));
            } else {
                const diag: T = out.at2(j, j);
                out.setAt2(i, j, acc / diag);
            }
        }
    }
}

For a symmetric positive-definite a, produces lower-triangular L with L Lᵀ = a. Unlike the LU routines this writes to a separate output, which it zeroes above the diagonal.

A non-positive value on the diagonal during the factorisation means the matrix is not positive-definite, and the call returns DomainError. Attempting the factorisation is the standard test for the property and is cheaper than computing eigenvalues, so the error is informative rather than a limitation.

10.10 The general product, and the number behind everything#

try zn.tensordot(f64, out, a, b, axis_a, axis_b);   // the general contraction
try zn.bmm(f64, out, batched_a, batched_b);
try zn.outer(f64, out, v1, v2);
try zn.diagonal(f64, diag, m);
try zn.triangle(f64, out, m, .upper, true);
const cond = try zn.conditionNumber(f64, u, &sigma, v, a);
tensordot
/// Contract `a` and `b` over one axis each: sum over `a`'s `axis_a` and `b`'s `axis_b`, which
/// must have the same extent. The output's axes are `a`'s remaining ones followed by `b`'s.
///
/// THE GENERAL CASE OF EVERY PRODUCT IN THIS FILE. `matmul` is `tensordot` over `a`'s last axis
/// and `b`'s first; `dotAll` on rank-1 inputs is `tensordot` with nothing left over; an outer
/// product is `tensordot` over no axes at all. Having the general one written once means the
/// special ones can be checked against it, which the test does.
///
/// Slower than `matmul` for the rank-2 case, by roughly the cost of the index arithmetic. The
/// specialised versions stay for that reason and are what a hot loop should call.
pub fn tensordot(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
    axis_a: usize,
    axis_b: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (axis_a >= a.rank or axis_b >= b.rank) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    const contracted: usize = a.shape[axis_a];
    if (b.shape[axis_b] != contracted) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (out.rank != a.rank + b.rank - 2 or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    // The output's axes are a's surviving ones then b's, in order.
    var free_a: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var free_b: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var count_a: usize = 0;
    var count_b: usize = 0;
    for (0..a.rank) |i| {
        if (i != axis_a) {
            free_a[count_a] = i;
            count_a += 1;
        }
    }
    for (0..b.rank) |i| {
        if (i != axis_b) {
            free_b[count_b] = i;
            count_b += 1;
        }
    }
    for (0..count_a) |i| {
        if (out.shape[i] != a.shape[free_a[i]]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    for (0..count_b) |i| {
        if (out.shape[count_a + i] != b.shape[free_b[i]]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }

    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var index_a: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var index_b: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var remaining: usize = out.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        for (0..count_a) |i| {
            index_a[free_a[i]] = walker[i];
        }
        for (0..count_b) |i| {
            index_b[free_b[i]] = walker[count_a + i];
        }
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        for (0..contracted) |k| {
            index_a[axis_a] = k;
            index_b[axis_b] = k;
            total.add((try a.at(index_a[0..a.rank])) * (try b.at(index_b[0..b.rank])));
        }
        try out.setAt(walker[0..out.rank], total.value());
        advance(out.shape[0..out.rank], walker[0..out.rank]);
    }
}
bmm
/// Batched matrix product: `out[b] = a[b] @ b_operand[b]` for every index of the leading axis.
///
/// Rank 3 throughout, `(batch, m, k) @ (batch, k, n) -> (batch, m, n)`. A loop over `matmul` on
/// slices, which is what it is - the value is that the shape check happens once and covers every
/// batch, rather than the caller writing the loop and checking nothing.
pub fn bmm(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 3 or b.rank != 3 or out.rank != 3) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const batch: usize = a.shape[0];
    if (b.shape[0] != batch or out.shape[0] != batch) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (a.shape[2] != b.shape[1] or out.shape[1] != a.shape[1] or out.shape[2] != b.shape[2]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..batch) |i| {
        const left: Tensor(T) = try (try a.slice(0, i, 1)).squeeze(0);
        const right: Tensor(T) = try (try b.slice(0, i, 1)).squeeze(0);
        const result: Tensor(T) = try (try out.slice(0, i, 1)).squeeze(0);
        try matmul(T, result, left, right);
    }
}
outer
/// The outer product `out[i][j] = a[i] * b[j]` of two rank-1 tensors.
pub fn outer(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (a.rank != 1 or b.rank != 1 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.shape[0] != a.shape[0] or out.shape[1] != b.shape[0]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..a.shape[0]) |i| {
        const x: T = try a.at(&.{i});
        for (0..b.shape[0]) |j| {
            try out.setAt(&.{ i, j }, x * (try b.at(&.{j})));
        }
    }
}
diagonal
/// The diagonal of a rank-2 tensor, into a rank-1 `out`. Not square-only: the diagonal of a
/// rectangular matrix is its `min(rows, cols)` leading entries.
pub fn diagonal(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 1) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const n: usize = @min(a.shape[0], a.shape[1]);
    if (out.shape[0] != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..n) |i| {
        try out.setAt(&.{i}, try a.at(&.{ i, i }));
    }
}
triangle
/// Copy one triangle of `a` into `out` and zero the other. `keep_diagonal` decides whether the
/// diagonal itself belongs to the kept side.
pub fn triangle(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    side: Triangle,
    keep_diagonal: bool,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.shape[0] != a.shape[0] or out.shape[1] != a.shape[1] or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..a.shape[0]) |i| {
        for (0..a.shape[1]) |j| {
            const on_diagonal: bool = i == j;
            const above: bool = j > i;
            const keep: bool = if (on_diagonal)
                keep_diagonal
            else if (side == .upper)
                above
            else
                !above;
            try out.setAt(&.{ i, j }, if (keep) try a.at(&.{ i, j }) else 0);
        }
    }
}
conditionNumber
/// The 2-norm condition number: `sigma_max / sigma_min`, via `svd`.
///
/// THE NUMBER THAT EXPLAINS THE REST OF THIS FILE
///
/// It says how much a relative error in the input can be multiplied on its way to the output. A
/// system with condition 1e8 solved in f64 - sixteen digits - keeps about eight. That single
/// quantity is behind every algorithm choice recorded here: why `qr` is Householder, why `lstsq`
/// avoids the normal equations (which SQUARE this number), why `svd` does not go through the
/// Gram matrix, and why `pinv`'s cutoff is relative.
///
/// Infinite for a singular matrix, which is the honest answer rather than a large finite one:
/// the smallest singular value is zero and the ratio is unbounded.
///
/// `u`, `values` and `v` are scratch, sized as `svd` requires, and come back holding the
/// decomposition. `a` is overwritten.
pub fn conditionNumber(
    comptime T: type,
    u: Tensor(T),
    values: []T,
    v: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    try svd(T, u, values, v, a);
    const smallest: T = values[values.len - 1];
    if (smallest == 0) {
        return inf(T);
    }
    return values[0] / smallest;
}

tensordot is the general case of every product in this file. matmul is a contraction over a's last axis and b's first; matmulNT is over both last axes; a rank-1 contraction with nothing left over is the dot product; an outer product is a contraction over nothing. So this is exact for each specialisation against the general form rather than against hand-written numbers — it is the specialisations that are likely to be wrong and the general form that is easy to reason about.

conditionNumber is σmax / σmin, and it is the number that explains the rest of this file. It says how much a relative error in the input can be multiplied on the way to the output: a system with condition 1e8 solved in f64 — sixteen digits — keeps about eight. That single quantity is behind every algorithm choice recorded here — why qr is Householder, why lstsq avoids the normal equations (which square it), why svd does not go through the Gram matrix, why pinv's cutoff is relative.

It is infinite for a singular matrix, which is the honest answer rather than a large finite one. A 5×5 Hilbert matrix at roughly 4.8e5, which is why that matrix is the one every algorithm here is tested on.

10.11 Einstein summation, checked at compile time#

try zn.einsum(f64, "ij,jk->ik", out, .{ a, b });      // matrix product
try zn.einsum(f64, "ij->ji", out, .{a});               // transpose
try zn.einsum(f64, "ii->", scalar, .{m});              // trace
try zn.einsum(f64, "ii->i", diag, .{m});               // diagonal
try zn.einsum(f64, "i,j->ij", out, .{ v1, v2 });       // outer product
try zn.einsum(f64, "bij,bjk->bik", out, .{ ba, bb });  // batched
einsum
/// Einstein summation: `einsum(f64, "ij,jk->ik", out, .{ a, b })` is a matrix product.
///
/// Every label names an axis. A label in the output is a free axis; a label appearing only in the
/// inputs is summed over. That one rule covers matrix products, transposes, traces, outer
/// products, batched contractions and diagonal extraction, which is why the notation exists.
///
/// The spec is comptime - see `parseEinsum` for what that buys. `operands` is a tuple, so a
/// one-operand form (`"ii->"` for a trace, `"ij->ji"` for a transpose) is written the same way as
/// a two-operand one.
///
/// A REPEATED LABEL WITHIN ONE OPERAND SELECTS ITS DIAGONAL, which is how `"ii->"` is a trace.
/// The index assignment sets both axes to the same value, so the walk visits only the diagonal
/// without anything special being written for it.
pub fn einsum(
    comptime T: type,
    comptime spec: []const u8,
    out: Tensor(T),
    operands: anytype,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const parsed: EinsumSpec = comptime parseEinsum(spec, operands.len);
    if (out.rank != parsed.output.len or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }

    // Each label's extent, taken from wherever it first appears and checked everywhere else.
    var extent: [256]usize = @splat(0);
    var known: [256]bool = @splat(false);
    inline for (0..parsed.input_count) |k| {
        const operand: Tensor(T) = operands[k];
        if (operand.rank != parsed.inputs[k].len) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        for (parsed.inputs[k], 0..) |label, axis| {
            if (known[label]) {
                if (extent[label] != operand.shape[axis]) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            } else {
                extent[label] = operand.shape[axis];
                known[label] = true;
            }
        }
    }
    for (parsed.output, 0..) |label, axis| {
        if (out.shape[axis] != extent[label]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }

    // The contracted axes, as a shape to walk.
    var sum_shape: [max_labels]usize = @splat(1);
    for (parsed.summed, 0..) |label, i| {
        sum_shape[i] = extent[label];
    }
    var sum_total: usize = 1;
    for (0..parsed.summed.len) |i| {
        sum_total *= sum_shape[i];
    }

    var position: [256]usize = @splat(0);
    var out_walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var index: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var remaining_out: usize = out.size();
    while (remaining_out > 0) : (remaining_out -= 1) {
        for (parsed.output, 0..) |label, axis| {
            position[label] = out_walker[axis];
        }
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        var sum_walker: [max_labels]usize = @splat(0);
        var remaining_sum: usize = sum_total;
        while (remaining_sum > 0) : (remaining_sum -= 1) {
            for (parsed.summed, 0..) |label, i| {
                position[label] = sum_walker[i];
            }
            var term: T = 1;
            inline for (0..parsed.input_count) |k| {
                const operand: Tensor(T) = operands[k];
                for (parsed.inputs[k], 0..) |label, axis| {
                    index[axis] = position[label];
                }
                term *= try operand.at(index[0..parsed.inputs[k].len]);
            }
            total.add(term);
            advance(sum_shape[0..parsed.summed.len], sum_walker[0..parsed.summed.len]);
        }
        try out.setAt(out_walker[0..out.rank], total.value());
        advance(out.shape[0..out.rank], out_walker[0..out.rank]);
    }
}

Every label names an axis. A label in the output is a free axis; a label appearing only in the inputs is summed over. That one rule covers all six lines above — and a repeated label within one operand selects its diagonal, which is how "ii->" is a trace with nothing written for the diagonal case at all.

The spec is comptime, and that is the divergence. znum takes spec: []const u8 and parses it at runtime, so a typo, an unknown output label or too many operands all come back as an Error the caller must handle — at the point of use, on data. But every one of those is a property of the spec, which is written in the source and cannot change while the program runs.

WrittenResult
"ij,jk"compile error: spec needs an explicit ->
"ij,jk->iq"compile error: output label q appears in no input
"ij,jk->ii"compile error: output label i is repeated
"ij->ij" with two operandscompile error: spec names 1 operand, 2 given

What is left for runtime is what genuinely depends on the data: a rank that does not match its subscript, and two operands disagreeing about a label's extent. Those stay ShapeMismatch.

The test checks each form against the function it duplicates — matmul, trace, diagonal, outer, dotAll, sumAxis, bmm — rather than against a table of expected numbers. A table would test einsum against whoever wrote the table.

11. Neural-network pieces#

11.1 softmaxRows#

try zn.softmaxRows(f32, probs, logits);   // rank 2; each ROW becomes a distribution
softmaxRows
/// Softmax along the last axis of a rank-2 tensor: each ROW becomes a probability distribution.
///
/// THE ROW MAXIMUM IS SUBTRACTED, AND IT IS NOT AN OPTIMISATION
///
/// `e^x` overflows `f32` above about 88, and logits reach that routinely in an untrained network.
/// Subtracting the row's maximum first leaves the result identical - softmax is invariant under
/// adding a constant to a whole row, which is asserted in the tests - while bounding the largest
/// exponent at `e^0 = 1`. Without it the first forward pass of a badly initialised layer returns
/// NaN, and the failure looks like a bad initialisation rather than a bad softmax.
///
/// Rank 2 only, and the rows are the last axis. A caller with a different layout transposes,
/// which is a view and costs nothing.
pub fn softmaxRows(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.shape[0] != a.shape[0] or out.shape[1] != a.shape[1] or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const rows: usize = a.shape[0];
    const cols: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (cols == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }

    var row: usize = 0;
    while (row < rows) : (row += 1) {
        var largest: T = a.at2(row, 0);
        var col: usize = 1;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            const x: T = a.at2(row, col);
            if (x > largest) {
                largest = x;
            }
        }
        // Compensated, like every other accumulation here: a wide row is exactly the sum that
        // loses its low bits, and the result is a denominator every element is divided by.
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        col = 0;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            const e: T = @exp(a.at2(row, col) - largest);
            out.setAt2(row, col, e);
            total.add(e);
        }
        const denominator: T = total.value();
        col = 0;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            const e: T = out.at2(row, col);
            out.setAt2(row, col, e / denominator);
        }
    }
}

The row maximum is subtracted before exponentiating. e^x overflows f32 above about 88 and logits reach that in an untrained network; subtracting the maximum leaves the result identical, because softmax is invariant under adding a constant to a whole row, while bounding the largest exponent at e0. The row sum is compensated, since every element of the row is divided by it.

11.2 layerNormRows#

try zn.layerNormRows(f32, out, a, 1.0e-5);   // epsilon is yours to pass
layerNormRows
/// Normalise each row of a rank-2 tensor to zero mean and unit variance.
///
/// `out[r][c] = (a[r][c] - mean_r) / sqrt(var_r + epsilon)`, with the POPULATION variance.
///
/// WHY EPSILON IS A PARAMETER AND NOT A CONSTANT
///
/// A row of identical values has zero variance, and dividing by its square root is an infinity
/// that propagates through the rest of the network. Every implementation adds a small epsilon
/// inside the root; the VALUE differs between frameworks (PyTorch defaults to 1e-5, JAX to 1e-6)
/// and it changes results measurably in `f32`. Baking one in would make this library silently
/// disagree with whichever the caller expected, so it is theirs to pass.
///
/// Population variance, not sample: normalisation is a statement about the row in hand, not an
/// estimate of a population it was drawn from. `Ddof` exists for the case where that distinction
/// matters and this is not it.
pub fn layerNormRows(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    epsilon: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.shape[0] != a.shape[0] or out.shape[1] != a.shape[1] or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const rows: usize = a.shape[0];
    const cols: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (cols == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const width: T = @floatFromInt(cols);

    var row: usize = 0;
    while (row < rows) : (row += 1) {
        // Both accumulations are compensated, for the reason recorded with `sumAll`: a wide row
        // is exactly the sum that loses its low bits, and here it feeds a DIVISOR.
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        var col: usize = 0;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            const x: T = a.at2(row, col);
            total.add(x);
        }
        const mean: T = total.value() / width;

        var sq: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        col = 0;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            const d: T = a.at2(row, col) - mean;
            const term: T = d * d;
            sq.add(term);
        }
        const inv: T = 1.0 / @sqrt(sq.value() / width + epsilon);

        col = 0;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            const x: T = a.at2(row, col);
            out.setAt2(row, col, (x - mean) * inv);
        }
    }
}

Each row comes out with mean 0 and population variance 1. Three passes — mean, variance, write. The single-pass identity E[x²] − E[x]² is not used: it subtracts two large nearly-equal numbers and loses most of its significant digits when the mean is large relative to the spread, which is the normal case for an unnormalised activation.

Epsilon is a parameter because frameworks disagree about its value — PyTorch defaults to 1e-5, JAX to 1e-6 — and the difference is visible in f32. A row of identical values has zero variance, and epsilon is what keeps its result finite.

11.3 Loss and the update step#

const loss: f32 = try zn.mseLoss(f32, prediction, target);
try zn.sgdStep(f32, weights, weights, gradient, 0.1);   // in place
mseLoss
/// Mean squared error between two tensors of the same shape.
///
/// Returns a scalar rather than writing into a tensor, because a loss IS a scalar and handing
/// back a rank-0 tensor would make every caller unwrap it. `DomainError` for empty input, since
/// the mean of nothing is a question for the caller.
pub fn mseLoss(comptime T: type, prediction: Tensor(T), target: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (prediction.rank != target.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var axis: usize = 0;
    while (axis < prediction.rank) : (axis += 1) {
        if (prediction.shape[axis] != target.shape[axis]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    const n: usize = prediction.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }

    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(prediction.shape[0..prediction.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const delta: T = (try prediction.at(at)) - (try target.at(at));
        const squared: T = delta * delta;
        total.add(squared);
    }
    const divisor: T = @floatFromInt(n);
    return total.value() / divisor;
}
sgdStep
/// `out = weight - rate * grad` - one step of plain gradient descent.
///
/// NOT IN PLACE, and the caller may still pass `weight` as `out` to get in-place behaviour.
/// Returning a fresh result by default means the operation composes and can be tested against a
/// reference without the reference having been consumed; an in-place-only signature forces every
/// caller to copy first, including the tests.
///
/// Written as a loop rather than through `zip`, because `zip`'s function is a COMPTIME
/// parameter and cannot close over a runtime `rate`. A `zipScalar` seam would fix that and is
/// not worth inventing for one caller.
pub fn sgdStep(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    weight: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
    rate: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const vw: Tensor(T) = try weight.broadcastTo(shape);
    const vg: Tensor(T) = try grad.broadcastTo(shape);

    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const w: T = vw.data[vw.offsetOf(at)];
        const g: T = vg.data[vg.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = w - rate * g;
    }
}

mseLoss returns a scalar rather than a rank-0 tensor, and does not broadcast: shapes must match exactly. sgdStep computes weight - rate * grad into a separate destination by default; passing weight as out gives the in-place form, and the two agree.

11.4 The loss family#

const mse   = try zn.mseLoss(f32, prediction, target);
const mae   = try zn.maeLoss(f32, prediction, target);
const huber = try zn.huberLoss(f32, prediction, target, 1.0);
const bce   = try zn.binaryCrossEntropyFromLogits(f32, logits, target);
const kl    = try zn.klDivergenceRows(f32, p, q);
mseLoss
/// Mean squared error between two tensors of the same shape.
///
/// Returns a scalar rather than writing into a tensor, because a loss IS a scalar and handing
/// back a rank-0 tensor would make every caller unwrap it. `DomainError` for empty input, since
/// the mean of nothing is a question for the caller.
pub fn mseLoss(comptime T: type, prediction: Tensor(T), target: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (prediction.rank != target.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var axis: usize = 0;
    while (axis < prediction.rank) : (axis += 1) {
        if (prediction.shape[axis] != target.shape[axis]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    const n: usize = prediction.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }

    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(prediction.shape[0..prediction.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const delta: T = (try prediction.at(at)) - (try target.at(at));
        const squared: T = delta * delta;
        total.add(squared);
    }
    const divisor: T = @floatFromInt(n);
    return total.value() / divisor;
}
maeLoss
/// Mean absolute error between two tensors of the same shape.
///
/// Not differentiable at zero, which is the whole trade against `mseLoss`: it does not square,
/// so an outlier contributes its distance rather than its distance squared, and a single bad
/// sample cannot dominate the batch.
pub fn maeLoss(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n != b.size() or a.rank != b.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var pair: Walk2 = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank], b.shape[0..b.rank]);
    while (pair.next()) |at| {
        total.add(@abs((try a.at(at.first)) - (try b.at(at.second))));
    }
    return total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
}
huberLoss
/// Huber loss: squared below `delta`, linear above it.
///
/// THE TWO BRANCHES MEET, AND SO DO THEIR SLOPES
///
/// At `|d| = delta` the quadratic gives `delta^2/2` and the linear branch gives
/// `delta*delta - delta^2/2 = delta^2/2`. Their derivatives are `delta` on both sides. That
/// smoothness is the point: Huber is MSE where the error is small and MAE where it is large, and
/// a join with a kink would put a discontinuity in the gradient exactly at the threshold most
/// samples sit near.
///
/// `delta` must be positive; zero would make the loss identically linear and the parameter
/// meaningless.
pub fn huberLoss(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T), delta: T) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (delta <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n != b.size() or a.rank != b.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var pair: Walk2 = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank], b.shape[0..b.rank]);
    while (pair.next()) |at| {
        const d: T = @abs((try a.at(at.first)) - (try b.at(at.second)));
        total.add(if (d <= delta) 0.5 * d * d else delta * (d - 0.5 * delta));
    }
    return total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
}
binaryCrossEntropyFromLogits
/// Binary cross-entropy from LOGITS, averaged over elements. `targets` are in [0, 1].
///
/// COMPUTED AS `max(x,0) - x*t + log(1 + e^-|x|)`
///
/// The definition applies a sigmoid and then a logarithm, and both ends break: a logit of -800
/// gives a sigmoid of exactly 0 and a logarithm of `-inf`, and a logit of +800 gives 1 and the
/// same failure on the other side. The identity above is algebraically equal for every real `x`
/// and contains only `e^-|x|`, whose argument is never positive, so it cannot overflow.
///
/// Taking logits rather than probabilities is not a convenience. A caller who has already
/// applied a sigmoid has lost the information this needs, and no implementation can recover it.
pub fn binaryCrossEntropyFromLogits(
    comptime T: type,
    logits: Tensor(T),
    targets: Tensor(T),
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = logits.size();
    if (n != targets.size() or logits.rank != targets.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var pair: Walk2 = .over(logits.shape[0..logits.rank], targets.shape[0..targets.rank]);
    while (pair.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = try logits.at(at.first);
        const t: T = try targets.at(at.second);
        total.add(@max(x, 0) - x * t + @log(1 + @exp(-@abs(x))));
    }
    return total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
}
klDivergenceRows
/// Kullback-Leibler divergence from `q` to `p`, row by row, averaged over rows. Both are rank-2
/// and each row must be a distribution.
///
/// NOT SYMMETRIC, and the argument order says which way round it goes: this is
/// `sum p*log(p/q)`, the divergence OF `q` FROM `p`, which is the one a loss wants - `p` is the
/// target and the penalty grows without bound where `q` assigns no probability to something `p`
/// does.
///
/// Terms where `p` is zero contribute nothing, by the limit of `p*log p`. A zero in `q` where
/// `p` is not zero is genuinely infinite, and `DomainError` says so rather than returning `inf`.
pub fn klDivergenceRows(comptime T: type, p: Tensor(T), q: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (p.rank != 2 or q.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (p.shape[0] != q.shape[0] or p.shape[1] != q.shape[1]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const rows: usize = p.shape[0];
    if (rows == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var row: usize = 0;
    while (row < rows) : (row += 1) {
        var col: usize = 0;
        while (col < p.shape[1]) : (col += 1) {
            const pv: T = try p.at(&.{ row, col });
            const qv: T = try q.at(&.{ row, col });
            if (pv < 0 or qv < 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            if (pv == 0) {
                continue;
            }
            if (qv == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            total.add(pv * @log(pv / qv));
        }
    }
    return total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(rows));
}

All return a scalar and require matching sizes; none broadcasts.

maeLoss does not square, so an outlier contributes its distance rather than its distance squared and cannot dominate a batch. It is not differentiable at zero.

huberLoss is squared below delta and linear above it:

total += if (d <= delta) 0.5 * d * d else delta * (d - 0.5 * delta);

At |d| = delta both branches give delta²/2 and both derivatives give delta. That smoothness is the point: a kink at the join would put a discontinuity in the gradient exactly at the threshold most samples sit near. delta must be positive.

binaryCrossEntropyFromLogits takes logits, for cross-entropy's reason. The definition applies a sigmoid then a logarithm, and both ends break: a logit of −800 gives a sigmoid of exactly 0 and a logarithm of −inf, and +800 fails the same way on the other side. The form used is max(x, 0) − x·t + log(1 + e−|x|), algebraically equal for every real x and containing only a non-positive exponent, so it cannot overflow. On logits of ±800 it returns exactly 0 for a correct target and exactly 800 for a wrong one.

klDivergenceRows is ∑ p·log(p/q) per row, averaged — the divergence of q from p, which is the direction a loss wants, with p the target. It is not symmetric, so the argument order matters. Terms where p is zero contribute nothing; a zero in q where p is not is genuinely infinite and returns DomainError rather than an infinity.

11.5 Convolution and pooling#

try zn.conv2d(f64, out, image, kernel, .{ 1, 1 }, .{ 0, 0 });   // stride, pad per axis
try zn.conv2d(f64, out, image, kernel, .{ 1, 1 }, .{ 1, 1 });   // 3x3 kernel: "same" size
try zn.conv2d(f64, out, image, kernel, .{ 2, 2 }, .{ 0, 0 });   // half-size output
try zn.maxPool2d(f64, out, image, 2);                            // (h/2, w/2)
try zn.avgPool2d(f64, out, image, 2);
conv2d
/// 2-D cross-correlation of a single-channel image with a kernel, with stride and zero padding.
/// The output is `((h + 2*pad - kh) / stride + 1, (w + 2*pad - kw) / stride + 1)`.
///
/// CROSS-CORRELATION, NOT CONVOLUTION, AND THE NAME SAYS CONVOLUTION ANYWAY
///
/// Every deep-learning framework's "conv2d" slides the kernel WITHOUT flipping it, which is
/// cross-correlation. A true convolution flips the kernel first. Since the kernel is learned, the
/// distinction is invisible to training - a flipped kernel is just a different kernel - and the
/// unflipped form is one indexing operation cheaper. The name follows every framework rather
/// than the mathematics, because that is what a reader will look for.
///
/// `stride` and `pad` are separate per axis, matching znum's signature exactly. Padding is
/// implicit: a tap that lands outside the image contributes zero, and no padded copy is made.
/// `pad = kh / 2` with `stride = 1` gives an output the size of the input.
///
/// Rank 2, one channel. The batched multi-channel form is a loop over this one.
pub fn conv2d(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    image: Tensor(T),
    kernel: Tensor(T),
    stride: [2]usize,
    pad: [2]usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (image.rank != 2 or kernel.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (stride[0] == 0 or stride[1] == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const h: usize = image.shape[0];
    const w: usize = image.shape[1];
    const kh: usize = kernel.shape[0];
    const kw: usize = kernel.shape[1];
    if (kh == 0 or kw == 0 or kh > h + 2 * pad[0] or kw > w + 2 * pad[1]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const oh: usize = (h + 2 * pad[0] - kh) / stride[0] + 1;
    const ow: usize = (w + 2 * pad[1] - kw) / stride[1] + 1;
    if (out.shape[0] != oh or out.shape[1] != ow or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var y: usize = 0;
    while (y < oh) : (y += 1) {
        var x: usize = 0;
        while (x < ow) : (x += 1) {
            var acc: T = 0;
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < kh) : (i += 1) {
                // The tap's row in the unpadded image, or out of range: signed, because the
                // padding makes the top-left taps land above and left of (0, 0).
                const iy: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(y * stride[0] + i)) - @as(isize, @intCast(pad[0]));
                if (iy < 0 or iy >= @as(isize, @intCast(h))) {
                    continue;
                }
                var j: usize = 0;
                while (j < kw) : (j += 1) {
                    const ix: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(x * stride[1] + j)) - @as(isize, @intCast(pad[1]));
                    if (ix < 0 or ix >= @as(isize, @intCast(w))) {
                        continue;
                    }
                    acc += (try image.at(&.{ @intCast(iy), @intCast(ix) })) * (try kernel.at(&.{ i, j }));
                }
            }
            try out.setAt(&.{ y, x }, acc);
        }
    }
}
maxPool2d
/// Max pooling over non-overlapping `size x size` windows. The output is `(h / size, w / size)`;
/// a trailing partial window is dropped.
///
/// Non-overlapping and square, which is what nearly every network uses. Dropping the partial
/// window rather than padding it means every output element saw exactly `size^2` inputs, so the
/// operation's meaning does not change at the edge.
pub fn maxPool2d(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    image: Tensor(T),
    size: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return pool2d(T, out, image, size, .max);
}
avgPool2d
/// Average pooling over non-overlapping `size x size` windows.
pub fn avgPool2d(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    image: Tensor(T),
    size: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return pool2d(T, out, image, size, .mean);
}

The output is ((h + 2·pad − kh) / stride + 1, …). Padding is implicit — a tap landing outside the image contributes zero and no padded copy is made — and pad = kh/2 with stride 1 gives an output the size of the input.

conv2d slides the kernel without flipping it, which is cross-correlation. Every framework's "conv2d" does the same: the kernel is learned, so a flipped kernel is just a different kernel, and the unflipped form is one indexing operation cheaper. The test checks the direction with an asymmetric kernel — [1, 0] must reproduce the left neighbour and [0, 1] the right, which a true convolution would swap.

Rank 2, one channel. Pooling is over non-overlapping square windows and drops a trailing partial window, so every output element saw exactly size² inputs and the operation's meaning does not change at the edge.

11.6 Classification#

var picked: [2]usize = @splat(0);
try zn.argmaxRows(f32, &picked, logits);        // one class per row
const flat = try zn.argmaxAll(f32, logits);      // flat index of the largest

try zn.oneHotRows(f32, hot, &.{ 2, 0 });

const loss = try zn.crossEntropyRows(f32, logits, &.{ 2, 0 });
try zn.crossEntropyRowsGrad(f32, grad, logits, &.{ 2, 0 });
const acc  = try zn.accuracy(f32, logits, &.{ 2, 0 });
argmaxRows
/// The column of the largest element in each row of a rank-2 tensor, written into `out`.
pub fn argmaxRows(comptime T: type, out: []usize, a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.len != a.shape[0]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const cols: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (cols == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    for (out, 0..) |*slot, row| {
        var best: T = try a.at(&.{ row, 0 });
        var best_at: usize = 0;
        var col: usize = 1;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            const x: T = a.at2(row, col);
            if (x > best or (comptime isFloat(T)) and zm.isNan(best)) {
                best = x;
                best_at = col;
            }
        }
        slot.* = best_at;
    }
}
argmaxAll
/// The flat index of the largest element. `DomainError` for an empty tensor.
///
/// Ties go to the FIRST occurrence, and NaN never wins: the comparison is `>`, so a NaN
/// candidate fails it and the running best survives. A `>=` would return the last of a run of
/// equals and would let a trailing NaN take the answer.
pub fn argmaxAll(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!usize {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    var best: T = try a.at(walker[0..a.rank]);
    var best_at: usize = 0;
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < n) : (i += 1) {
        const x: T = a.data[a.offsetOf(walker[0..a.rank])];
        if (x > best or (comptime isFloat(T)) and zm.isNan(best)) {
            best = x;
            best_at = i;
        }
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
    return best_at;
}
oneHotRows
/// One-hot encode class indices into the rows of `out`, which is zeroed first.
pub fn oneHotRows(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), indices: []const usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (out.rank != 2 or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.shape[0] != indices.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (indices) |idx| {
        if (idx >= out.shape[1]) {
            return Error.OutOfRange;
        }
    }
    out.fill(0);
    for (indices, 0..) |idx, row| {
        try out.setAt(&.{ row, idx }, 1);
    }
}
crossEntropyRows
/// Softmax cross-entropy of rank-2 `logits` against integer class `targets`, averaged over rows.
///
/// THE SOFTMAX IS NOT COMPUTED, AND THAT IS THE POINT
///
/// The definition is `-log(softmax(logits)[target])`, and computing it that way exponentiates,
/// normalises, then takes a logarithm - three chances to lose the answer. A logit of 800 gives an
/// infinite exponential and a loss of NaN; a correct probability of 1e-40 underflows to zero and
/// the logarithm is `-inf`.
///
/// The identity `-log(softmax(x)[t]) = logsumexp(x) - x[t]` avoids both: `logsumexp` is evaluated
/// by subtracting the row maximum, so the largest exponent is `e^0`, and the subtraction happens
/// in log space where the numbers are small. This form is finite for every finite input.
///
/// Takes LOGITS, not probabilities. A version taking probabilities would have to undo a softmax
/// the caller had already applied, which is where the precision is lost.
pub fn crossEntropyRows(
    comptime T: type,
    logits: Tensor(T),
    targets: []const usize,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (logits.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const rows: usize = logits.shape[0];
    const cols: usize = logits.shape[1];
    if (targets.len != rows) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (rows == 0 or cols == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    for (targets) |t| {
        if (t >= cols) {
            return Error.OutOfRange;
        }
    }

    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var row: usize = 0;
    while (row < rows) : (row += 1) {
        var largest: T = logits.at2(row, 0);
        var col: usize = 1;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            largest = @max(largest, logits.at2(row, col));
        }
        var sum_exp: T = 0;
        col = 0;
        while (col < cols) : (col += 1) {
            sum_exp += @exp(logits.at2(row, col) - largest);
        }
        const log_sum_exp: T = largest + @log(sum_exp);
        total.add(log_sum_exp - logits.at2(row, targets[row]));
    }
    return total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(rows));
}
crossEntropyRowsGrad
/// The gradient of `crossEntropyRows` with respect to the logits: `softmax(logits) - onehot`,
/// divided by the number of rows.
///
/// THIS IS WHY CROSS-ENTROPY IS PAIRED WITH SOFTMAX. The two functions' derivatives compose
/// into a subtraction - no division, no `exp'`, nothing that can overflow - so the backward pass
/// of the pair is cheaper and better conditioned than either alone.
pub fn crossEntropyRowsGrad(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    logits: Tensor(T),
    targets: []const usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.rank != 2 or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.shape[0] != logits.shape[0] or out.shape[1] != logits.shape[1]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (targets.len != logits.shape[0]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    try softmaxRows(T, out, logits);
    const rows: T = @floatFromInt(out.shape[0]);
    for (targets, 0..) |t, row| {
        if (t >= out.shape[1]) {
            return Error.OutOfRange;
        }
        const p: T = try out.at(&.{ row, t });
        try out.setAt(&.{ row, t }, p - 1);
    }
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < out.size()) : (i += 1) {
        out.data[out.base + i] /= rows;
    }
}
accuracy
/// The fraction of rows whose largest logit is at the target class.
pub fn accuracy(
    comptime T: type,
    logits: Tensor(T),
    targets: []const usize,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (logits.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (targets.len != logits.shape[0]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (targets.len == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var correct: usize = 0;
    for (targets, 0..) |t, row| {
        var best: T = try logits.at(&.{ row, 0 });
        var best_at: usize = 0;
        var col: usize = 1;
        while (col < logits.shape[1]) : (col += 1) {
            const x: T = logits.at2(row, col);
            if (x > best or (comptime isFloat(T)) and zm.isNan(best)) {
                best = x;
                best_at = col;
            }
        }
        if (best_at == t) {
            correct += 1;
        }
    }
    return @as(T, @floatFromInt(correct)) / @as(T, @floatFromInt(targets.len));
}

Class indices are []usize rather than a tensor, because that is what a caller has: a list of labels. Targets are checked against the class count before anything is written.

argmaxAll and argmaxRows compare with >, so a tie goes to the first occurrence and a NaN never wins — a NaN candidate fails the comparison and the running best survives.

crossEntropyRows takes logits and never computes the softmax. The definition is −log(softmax(x)[t]), which exponentiates, normalises, then takes a logarithm — three chances to lose the answer. A logit of 800 gives an infinite exponential and a loss of NaN; a correct probability of 1e-40 underflows to zero and its logarithm is −inf.

The identity −log(softmax(x)[t]) = logsumexp(x) − x[t] avoids both. logsumexp subtracts the row maximum, so the largest exponent is e0, and the subtraction happens in log space where the numbers are small. On the row { 800, 0, −800 } this returns 0 and 800 for the two classes, both exact, where the definition returns NaN.

crossEntropyRowsGrad is softmax(logits) − onehot, divided by the row count. Softmax and cross-entropy are paired because their derivatives compose into a subtraction: no division, no exponential in the backward pass, and each row of the result sums to zero, so a step can shift probability between classes but cannot create any.

11.7 Optimisers#

try zn.sgdStep(f32, w, w, grad, 0.1);
try zn.sgdMomentum(f32, w, w, grad, velocity, 0.1, 0.9);
try zn.adamStep(f32, w, w, grad, moment, velocity, .{}, step);
sgdStep
/// `out = weight - rate * grad` - one step of plain gradient descent.
///
/// NOT IN PLACE, and the caller may still pass `weight` as `out` to get in-place behaviour.
/// Returning a fresh result by default means the operation composes and can be tested against a
/// reference without the reference having been consumed; an in-place-only signature forces every
/// caller to copy first, including the tests.
///
/// Written as a loop rather than through `zip`, because `zip`'s function is a COMPTIME
/// parameter and cannot close over a runtime `rate`. A `zipScalar` seam would fix that and is
/// not worth inventing for one caller.
pub fn sgdStep(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    weight: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
    rate: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const vw: Tensor(T) = try weight.broadcastTo(shape);
    const vg: Tensor(T) = try grad.broadcastTo(shape);

    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const w: T = vw.data[vw.offsetOf(at)];
        const g: T = vg.data[vg.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = w - rate * g;
    }
}
sgdMomentum
/// One SGD step with momentum. `velocity` is read and updated in place.
///
/// `velocity = momentum * velocity + grad`, then `out = weight - rate * velocity`.
///
/// THE VELOCITY CARRIES THE GRADIENT, NOT THE STEP
///
/// The other common form is `velocity = momentum * velocity + rate * grad`, which folds the
/// learning rate into the accumulator. The two differ the moment `rate` changes: with the rate
/// inside, a schedule's old rate persists in the velocity for several steps afterwards. Keeping
/// it out means a rate change takes effect immediately, which is what a schedule is for.
///
/// `momentum = 0` reduces exactly to `sgdStep`, and the test asserts it: an optimiser whose
/// degenerate case does not match the simpler one has an error in the accumulation.
pub fn sgdMomentum(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    weight: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
    velocity: Tensor(T),
    rate: T,
    momentum: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.isAliased() or velocity.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const vw: Tensor(T) = try weight.broadcastTo(shape);
    const vg: Tensor(T) = try grad.broadcastTo(shape);
    if (velocity.size() != out.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const v: T = velocity.data[velocity.offsetOf(at)];
        const g: T = vg.data[vg.offsetOf(at)];
        const updated: T = momentum * v + g;
        velocity.data[velocity.offsetOf(at)] = updated;
        const w: T = vw.data[vw.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = w - rate * updated;
    }
}
adamStep
/// One Adam step. `m` and `v` are the first and second moment buffers, read and updated in place.
/// `step` is the 1-based iteration number, which the bias correction needs.
///
/// THE BIAS CORRECTION IS WHY `step` IS A PARAMETER
///
/// `m` and `v` start at zero, so the first few updates are biased toward zero by exactly the
/// factor `1 - beta^step`. Dividing it out makes the FIRST step the same size as any other; an
/// implementation without the correction takes an opening step smaller by a factor of about
/// `1 - beta2`, which for the usual 0.999 means a thousandth of the intended distance.
///
/// THE STEP SIZE IS INDEPENDENT OF THE GRADIENT'S SCALE. With `m` and `v` both proportional to
/// the gradient, the ratio `m / sqrt(v)` is not, so multiplying every gradient by a thousand
/// leaves the update almost unchanged. That property is what the test checks, because it is the
/// reason to use Adam at all.
pub fn adamStep(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    weight: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
    moment: Tensor(T),
    velocity: Tensor(T),
    hyper: Adam,
    step: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.isAliased() or moment.isAliased() or velocity.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (step == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const vw: Tensor(T) = try weight.broadcastTo(shape);
    const vg: Tensor(T) = try grad.broadcastTo(shape);
    if (moment.size() != out.size() or velocity.size() != out.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }

    const beta1: T = @floatCast(hyper.beta1);
    const beta2: T = @floatCast(hyper.beta2);
    const eps: T = @floatCast(hyper.epsilon);
    const rate: T = @floatCast(hyper.rate);
    // EXPONENTIATION BY SQUARING, NOT REPEATED MULTIPLICATION
    //
    // The first version of this multiplied `step` times to avoid "a rounding `pow` would
    // introduce". That traded an unmeasured rounding for **O(step) work on every call** - 50
    // million multiplications over a 10 000-step run, 5 billion over 100 000, and growing
    // quadratically with the length of training. znum uses `pow` and is right to.
    //
    // Squaring is O(log step) - 17 multiplications at 100 000 steps rather than 100 000 - and
    // stays exact in the sense that matters: it is the same sequence of floating-point
    // multiplications, just associated differently.
    const correct1: T = 1 - powInt(T, beta1, step);
    const correct2: T = 1 - powInt(T, beta2, step);

    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const g: T = vg.data[try vg.flatIndex(at)];
        const m_index: usize = try moment.flatIndex(at);
        const v_index: usize = try velocity.flatIndex(at);
        const m_new: T = beta1 * moment.data[m_index] + (1 - beta1) * g;
        const v_new: T = beta2 * velocity.data[v_index] + (1 - beta2) * g * g;
        moment.data[m_index] = m_new;
        velocity.data[v_index] = v_new;

        const m_hat: T = m_new / correct1;
        const v_hat: T = v_new / correct2;
        const w: T = vw.data[vw.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] =
            w - rate * m_hat / (@sqrt(v_hat) + eps);
    }
}

Each writes into a destination that may be the weight itself. The state buffers — velocity for momentum, moment and velocity for Adam — are read and updated in place, are the caller's to allocate, and must start at zero.

sgdMomentum accumulates the gradient, not the step:

velocity = momentum * velocity + grad
out      = weight - rate * velocity

The alternative folds rate into the accumulator. The two differ as soon as the rate changes: with the rate inside, an old rate persists in the velocity for several steps afterwards. With momentum = 0 this reduces exactly to sgdStep.

adamStep takes its hyperparameters as a struct with the paper's defaults, and a 1-based step number:

pub const Adam = struct {
    rate: f64 = 0.001,
    beta1: f64 = 0.9,
    beta2: f64 = 0.999,
    epsilon: f64 = 1.0e-8,
};

step is a parameter because of the bias correction. moment and velocity start at zero, so early updates are biased toward zero by exactly 1 − betastep; dividing that out makes the first step the same size as any other. Without it the opening step is smaller by roughly 1 − beta2, which at the default 0.999 is a thousandth of the intended distance. A step of 0 is DomainError.

The reason to use Adam is that the step size does not depend on the gradient's scale. Both moments are proportional to the gradient, so their ratio is not: multiplying every gradient by a thousand leaves the update essentially unchanged.

11.8 Gradient clipping#

const before = try zn.clipByNorm(f32, grad, grad, 1.0);
clipByNorm
/// Scale `a` into `out` so its Euclidean norm is at most `limit`. Returns the norm BEFORE
/// clipping, which is the number worth logging.
///
/// THE WHOLE TENSOR IS SCALED BY ONE FACTOR, not clipped elementwise. Clipping each element
/// separately changes the gradient's DIRECTION, which is the one property a gradient has that
/// must survive: a step in a different direction is not a smaller version of the step that was
/// wanted.
pub fn clipByNorm(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), limit: T) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (limit <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const total: T = try norm(T, a);
    if (total <= limit) {
        try materialise(T, out, a);
        return total;
    }
    try scale(T, out, a, limit / total);
    return total;
}

Scales the whole tensor by a single factor so its Euclidean norm is at most the limit, and returns the norm before clipping — the number worth logging. Below the limit the data is copied unchanged.

One factor for the whole tensor, rather than clipping each element, because elementwise clipping changes the gradient's direction. A step in a different direction is not a smaller version of the step that was wanted.

11.9 A dense layer, forward and backward#

Every operation a single dense layer needs is present. With x the input, w the weights and bias a row vector:

// forward: y = relu(x @ w + bias)
try zn.matmul(f32, pre, x, w);
try zn.add(f32, pre, pre, try bias.broadcastTo(pre.shape[0..pre.rank]));
try zn.relu(f32, y, pre);
const loss = try zn.mseLoss(f32, y, target);

// backward
try zn.sub(f32, dy, y, target);                          // d(loss)/dy, up to a constant
try zn.reluGrad(f32, dpre, pre, dy);                     // through the activation
try zn.matmul(f32, dw, try x.transpose(0, 1), dpre);     // dW = xT @ dpre
try zn.sumAxis(f32, dbias, dpre, 0);                     // bias gradient
try zn.matmul(f32, dx, dpre, try w.transpose(0, 1));     // dx = dpre @ wT

// update
try zn.sgdStep(f32, w, w, dw, rate);
matmul
/// `out = a @ b` - the matrix product, for rank-2 tensors only.
///
/// THE ONE OPERATION THAT IS NOT ELEMENTWISE
///
/// `(m, k) @ (k, n) -> (m, n)`. Every shape is checked; a mismatch is `ShapeMismatch` before any
/// element is written, so a partially-filled output is not a state this can produce.
///
/// The inner sum is compensated, for the reason in `sumAll`: a dot product over a long
/// contraction axis is exactly the accumulation that loses its low bits, and in a neural network
/// that axis is the layer width. Getting this wrong does not crash - it makes gradients slightly
/// wrong, everywhere, in a way that reads as "training is a bit unstable".
///
/// This is the naive triple loop, deliberately. It is the SPECIFICATION every faster
