/// implementation is checked against, and it stays in the file when a blocked or tiled version
/// arrives. Strided and stretched inputs are handled by going through `at`, so a transposed view
/// multiplies correctly without being copied first.
pub fn matmul(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or b.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const m: usize = a.shape[0];
    const k: usize = a.shape[1];
    const n: usize = b.shape[1];
    if (b.shape[0] != k or out.shape[0] != m or out.shape[1] != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }

    var row: usize = 0;
    while (row < m) : (row += 1) {
        var col: usize = 0;
        while (col < n) : (col += 1) {
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < k) : (i += 1) {
                const product: T = a.at2(row, i) *
                    b.at2(i, col);
                total.add(product);
            }
            out.setAt2(row, col, total.value());
        }
    }
}
add
/// `out = a + b`, broadcasting both to `out`'s shape.
pub fn add(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return x + y;
        }
    }.apply);
}
relu
/// `out = max(a, 0)`, elementwise.
pub fn relu(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return map(T, out, a, struct {
        fn apply(x: T) T {
            return if (x > 0) x else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
mseLoss
/// Mean squared error between two tensors of the same shape.
///
/// Returns a scalar rather than writing into a tensor, because a loss IS a scalar and handing
/// back a rank-0 tensor would make every caller unwrap it. `DomainError` for empty input, since
/// the mean of nothing is a question for the caller.
pub fn mseLoss(comptime T: type, prediction: Tensor(T), target: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (prediction.rank != target.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var axis: usize = 0;
    while (axis < prediction.rank) : (axis += 1) {
        if (prediction.shape[axis] != target.shape[axis]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
    }
    const n: usize = prediction.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }

    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(prediction.shape[0..prediction.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const delta: T = (try prediction.at(at)) - (try target.at(at));
        const squared: T = delta * delta;
        total.add(squared);
    }
    const divisor: T = @floatFromInt(n);
    return total.value() / divisor;
}
sub
/// `out = a - b`, broadcasting both to `out`'s shape.
pub fn sub(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    return zip(T, out, a, b, struct {
        fn apply(x: T, y: T) T {
            return x - y;
        }
    }.apply);
}
reluGrad
/// `out = if (x > 0) grad else 0` - the gradient of `relu` with respect to its input.
///
/// It takes the FORWARD INPUT, not the forward output. Both work for `relu` because the output
/// is positive exactly where the input was, but only the input generalises: `leakyRelu` and
/// `elu` have outputs that do not determine their own gradient. Taking the input here means the
/// signature does not have to change when they arrive.
pub fn reluGrad(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    x: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return zip(T, out, x, grad, struct {
        fn apply(xv: T, gv: T) T {
            return if (xv > 0) gv else 0;
        }
    }.apply);
}
sumAxis
/// Sum `a` along `axis` into `out`, which must have `a`'s shape with that axis removed.
///
/// The output's rank is one less, not one with a size-1 axis. Keeping the axis would be the
/// `keepdims` option, and options that change the RESULT SHAPE are better as separate calls than
/// as a flag a reader has to look up - a caller who wants the axis back has `reshape`.
pub fn sumAxis(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), axis: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (axis >= a.rank) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    if (out.rank + 1 != a.rank or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    // The surviving axes must match, in order.
    var src: usize = 0;
    var dst: usize = 0;
    while (src < a.rank) : (src += 1) {
        if (src == axis) {
            continue;
        }
        if (a.shape[src] != out.shape[dst]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        dst += 1;
    }

    out.fill(0);
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    var reduced: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    var remaining: usize = a.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        // Project the source position onto the output by dropping the reduced axis.
        var k: usize = 0;
        var i: usize = 0;
        while (i < a.rank) : (i += 1) {
            if (i == axis) {
                continue;
            }
            reduced[k] = walker[i];
            k += 1;
        }
        const at: []const usize = reduced[0..out.rank];
        const slot: usize = out.offsetOf(at);
        out.data[slot] += a.data[a.offsetOf(walker[0..a.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
}
sgdStep
/// `out = weight - rate * grad` - one step of plain gradient descent.
///
/// NOT IN PLACE, and the caller may still pass `weight` as `out` to get in-place behaviour.
/// Returning a fresh result by default means the operation composes and can be tested against a
/// reference without the reference having been consumed; an in-place-only signature forces every
/// caller to copy first, including the tests.
///
/// Written as a loop rather than through `zip`, because `zip`'s function is a COMPTIME
/// parameter and cannot close over a runtime `rate`. A `zipScalar` seam would fix that and is
/// not worth inventing for one caller.
pub fn sgdStep(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    weight: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
    rate: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const vw: Tensor(T) = try weight.broadcastTo(shape);
    const vg: Tensor(T) = try grad.broadcastTo(shape);

    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const w: T = vw.data[vw.offsetOf(at)];
        const g: T = vg.data[vg.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = w - rate * g;
    }
}

There is no autograd; the chain above is written by hand.

11.10 More optimisers, and the schedules#

try zn.adamWStep(f64, out, w, g, m, v, hyper, decay, step);
try zn.rmspropStep(f64, out, w, g, mean_square, rate, rho, eps);
try zn.adagradStep(f64, out, w, g, acc, rate, eps);
try zn.clipByValue(f64, out, g, limit);
const lr = zn.cosineLearningRate(f64, step, total, base, lowest);
const wu = zn.warmupLearningRate(f64, step, warmup, base);
adamWStep
/// One AdamW step: Adam, plus weight decay applied DIRECTLY TO THE WEIGHT.
///
/// WHAT MAKES THIS AdamW AND NOT ADAM WITH L2
///
/// The old way of shrinking weights is to add `decay * weight` to the gradient. With plain SGD
/// the two are the same thing. With Adam they are not: the gradient goes through the moment
/// estimates, so the decay is divided by `sqrt(v_hat)` along with everything else - **a
/// parameter with a large historical gradient gets decayed LESS than one with a small one**,
/// which is the opposite of what a regulariser is for. The decay ends up depending on the
/// gradient history of the weight it is supposed to shrink uniformly.
///
/// Decoupling means subtracting `rate * decay * weight` from the weight after the Adam update,
/// where no moment can touch it. znum does this and documents why; this follows it.
///
/// `decay = 0` reduces exactly to `adamStep`, which the test asserts - an implementation whose
/// degenerate case disagrees with the simpler one has the coupling in the wrong place.
pub fn adamWStep(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    weight: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
    moment: Tensor(T),
    velocity: Tensor(T),
    hyper: Adam,
    decay: T,
    step: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (decay < 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    try adamStep(T, out, weight, grad, moment, velocity, hyper, step);
    if (decay == 0) {
        return;
    }
    const rate: T = @floatCast(hyper.rate);
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const weight_view: Tensor(T) = try weight.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const w_value: T = try weight_view.at(at);
        const slot: usize = try out.flatIndex(at);
        out.data[slot] -= rate * decay * w_value;
    }
}
rmspropStep
/// One RMSProp step: divide the gradient by the root of its own running mean square.
///
/// `mean_square = rho * mean_square + (1 - rho) * grad^2`, then
/// `out = weight - rate * grad / (sqrt(mean_square) + epsilon)`.
///
/// Adam without the first moment, and without bias correction. The missing correction is why
/// RMSProp's opening steps are small where Adam's are not - `mean_square` starts at zero, so the
/// first division is by something too small and the step is too large, then it settles. Stated
/// rather than corrected, because correcting it would make this a different algorithm wearing
/// RMSProp's name.
pub fn rmspropStep(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    weight: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
    mean_square: Tensor(T),
    rate: T,
    rho: T,
    epsilon: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (rho < 0 or rho >= 1 or epsilon <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (out.isAliased() or mean_square.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (mean_square.size() != out.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const weight_view: Tensor(T) = try weight.broadcastTo(shape);
    const grad_view: Tensor(T) = try grad.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const g_value: T = try grad_view.at(at);
        const index: usize = try mean_square.flatIndex(at);
        const updated: T = rho * mean_square.data[index] + (1 - rho) * g_value * g_value;
        mean_square.data[index] = updated;
        const w_value: T = try weight_view.at(at);
        out.data[try out.flatIndex(at)] = w_value - rate * g_value / (@sqrt(updated) + epsilon);
    }
}
adagradStep
/// One Adagrad step: divide by the root of the ACCUMULATED sum of squared gradients.
///
/// The accumulator only grows, so the effective learning rate only shrinks - monotonically, to
/// zero. That is the algorithm's strength on sparse problems, where a rarely-seen feature keeps
/// a large step long after common ones have decayed, and its weakness on dense ones, where
/// learning stops before the model has converged. RMSProp's `rho` exists precisely to forget.
pub fn adagradStep(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    weight: Tensor(T),
    grad: Tensor(T),
    accumulator: Tensor(T),
    rate: T,
    epsilon: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (epsilon <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (out.isAliased() or accumulator.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (accumulator.size() != out.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const weight_view: Tensor(T) = try weight.broadcastTo(shape);
    const grad_view: Tensor(T) = try grad.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const g_value: T = try grad_view.at(at);
        const index: usize = try accumulator.flatIndex(at);
        const total: T = accumulator.data[index] + g_value * g_value;
        accumulator.data[index] = total;
        const w_value: T = try weight_view.at(at);
        out.data[try out.flatIndex(at)] = w_value - rate * g_value / (@sqrt(total) + epsilon);
    }
}
clipByValue
/// Clip every element of `a` into `[-limit, limit]`, into `out`.
///
/// ELEMENTWISE, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE CHOICE FROM `clipByNorm` AND DELIBERATELY SO. This changes
/// the gradient's direction, because it shortens some components and not others. That is
/// sometimes what you want - a single exploding coordinate is contained without touching the
/// rest - and it is never what you want when the direction matters. `clipByNorm` is the one that
/// preserves direction; having both under names that say which is which is the point.
pub fn clipByValue(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T), limit: T) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    if (limit <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    return clamp(T, out, a, -limit, limit);
}
cosineLearningRate
/// The learning rate at `step` under a cosine schedule from `base` down to `lowest`.
///
/// Follows znum exactly: progress is `step / total`, and the rate is
/// `lowest + (base - lowest) * (1 + cos(pi * progress)) / 2`. At step 0 that is `base`, at
/// `total` it is `lowest`, and the descent is steepest in the middle - which is the shape the schedule is
/// chosen for. At or past `total` it returns `lowest` rather than continuing the cosine into its
/// rising half, which would take the rate back up.
pub fn cosineLearningRate(
    comptime T: type,
    step: usize,
    total: usize,
    base: T,
    lowest: T,
) T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    // `lowest`, not `floor`: this file exports a tensor `floor`, and a parameter of that name
    // would shadow it. The same shadowing the rename found four times elsewhere.
    if (total == 0 or step >= total) {
        return lowest;
    }
    const progress: T = @as(T, @floatFromInt(step)) / @as(T, @floatFromInt(total));
    const half_turn: T = @floatCast(3.141592653589793);
    return lowest + (base - lowest) * (1 + scalarCos(half_turn * progress)) / 2;
}
warmupLearningRate
/// A linear ramp from zero to `base` over the first `warmup` steps, then `base`.
///
/// WHY A WARMUP EXISTS AT ALL. Adam's second moment starts at zero and its first few estimates
/// are made from one or two gradients, so the early updates are both large and badly informed.
/// Ramping the rate in means the moments have seen enough data to be worth dividing by before
/// the steps get big. It is the difference between a transformer that trains and one that
/// diverges in the first hundred steps.
///
/// Step 0 gives exactly 0, and step `warmup` gives exactly `base`. `warmup = 0` is the constant
/// schedule rather than a division by zero.
pub fn warmupLearningRate(comptime T: type, step: usize, warmup: usize, base: T) T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (warmup == 0 or step >= warmup) {
        return base;
    }
    return base * @as(T, @floatFromInt(step)) / @as(T, @floatFromInt(warmup));
}

The old way of shrinking weights adds decay · weight to the gradient. With plain SGD that is the same thing as decaying the weight. With Adam it is not: the gradient goes through the moment estimates, so the decay is divided by sqrt(v̂) along with everything else — a parameter with a large historical gradient gets decayed less than one with a small one, which is the opposite of what a regulariser is for.

So there is no claim that the weights got smaller, which any wrong version would also pass. It checks that the difference from plain Adam is exactly rate · decay · weight — a quantity depending on the weight alone. Had the decay gone through the moments, the difference would depend on the gradient too, and that equality would fail on the elements whose gradients differ most.

rmspropStep and adagradStep differ in one thing: whether they forget. Fed the same gradient forty times, Adagrad's step shrinks from 1.00e-1 to 1.58e-2 and never stops shrinking, while RMSProp's settles at 1.01e-1. The measurement also shows what RMSProp's missing bias correction costs: its first step is 3.16e-1, three times its settled one, because mean_square starts at zero. Adam corrects for that; RMSProp does not, and that is a property of the algorithm rather than of this implementation.

clipByValue is elementwise and therefore changes the gradient's direction; clipByNorm scales the whole tensor and preserves it. Both are wanted at different times and the distinction directly — every ratio to the original is equal after norm clipping, and visibly unequal after value clipping.

11.11 Metrics, and their undefined cases#

try zn.confusionMatrix(f64, out, predicted, truth);   // true DOWN, predicted ACROSS
const s = try zn.classScore(f64, confusion, k);
const macro = try zn.macroF1(f64, confusion);
const r2 = try zn.r2Score(f64, prediction, truth);     // can be NEGATIVE
const cos = try zn.cosineSimilarity(f64, a, b);
const hinge = try zn.hingeLoss(f64, scores, labels);
confusionMatrix
/// The confusion matrix of predicted against true classes: `out[t][p]` counts the samples whose
/// true class is `t` and predicted class is `p`.
///
/// TRUE DOWN, PREDICTED ACROSS, which is scikit-learn's orientation and znum's. The transpose is
/// equally common elsewhere and reading a confusion matrix the wrong way round swaps precision
/// with recall, so the convention is stated rather than left to the reader's habit.
pub fn confusionMatrix(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    predicted: []const usize,
    truth: []const usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.rank != 2 or out.shape[0] != out.shape[1] or out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (predicted.len != truth.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const classes: usize = out.shape[0];
    for (predicted, truth) |p, t| {
        if (p >= classes or t >= classes) {
            return Error.OutOfRange;
        }
    }
    out.fill(0);
    for (predicted, truth) |p, t| {
        try out.setAt(&.{ t, p }, (try out.at(&.{ t, p })) + 1);
    }
}
classScore
/// The scores for class `k` of a confusion matrix laid out true-down, predicted-across.
pub fn classScore(comptime T: type, confusion: Tensor(T), k: usize) Error!ClassScore {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (confusion.rank != 2 or confusion.shape[0] != confusion.shape[1]) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const classes: usize = confusion.shape[0];
    if (k >= classes) {
        return Error.OutOfRange;
    }
    const hit: f64 = @floatCast(try confusion.at(&.{ k, k }));
    var predicted_total: f64 = 0;
    var true_total: f64 = 0;
    for (0..classes) |i| {
        predicted_total += @floatCast(try confusion.at(&.{ i, k })); // column: predicted as k
        true_total += @floatCast(try confusion.at(&.{ k, i })); // row: truly k
    }
    const precision: f64 = if (predicted_total > 0) hit / predicted_total else 0;
    const recall: f64 = if (true_total > 0) hit / true_total else 0;
    const denominator: f64 = precision + recall;
    return .{
        .precision = precision,
        .recall = recall,
        .f1 = if (denominator > 0) 2 * precision * recall / denominator else 0,
        .precision_undefined = predicted_total == 0,
        .recall_undefined = true_total == 0,
    };
}
macroF1
/// The unweighted mean of every class's F1 - the macro average.
///
/// Unweighted, so a class with three samples counts as much as one with three thousand. That is
/// the point of a macro average and also its trap: on an imbalanced problem it is dominated by
/// the rare classes, which is either exactly what you want or exactly what misleads you. The
/// weighted alternative is `accuracy`, which is already here.
pub fn macroF1(comptime T: type, confusion: Tensor(T)) Error!f64 {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const classes: usize = confusion.shape[0];
    if (classes == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: f64 = 0;
    for (0..classes) |k| {
        total += (try classScore(T, confusion, k)).f1;
    }
    const class_count: f64 = @floatFromInt(classes);
    return total / class_count;
}
r2Score
/// The coefficient of determination: `1 - residual_sum_of_squares / total_sum_of_squares`.
///
/// IT CAN BE NEGATIVE, AND THAT IS INFORMATION
///
/// The name says "R squared" and squares are not negative, which misleads almost everyone who
/// meets it. `1 - ss_res/ss_tot` is negative whenever the model does worse than predicting the
/// mean of the truth - which happens, and is exactly what you want to be told. Clamping it at
/// zero would turn "worse than a constant" into "explains nothing", and those are different.
///
/// `DomainError` when the truth is constant: `ss_tot` is then zero, and the question "what
/// fraction of the variation is explained" has no answer when there is no variation.
pub fn r2Score(comptime T: type, prediction: Tensor(T), truth: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = truth.size();
    if (n != prediction.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const mean: T = try meanAll(T, truth);
    var residual: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var pair: Walk2 = .over(prediction.shape[0..prediction.rank], truth.shape[0..truth.rank]);
    while (pair.next()) |at| {
        const predicted: T = try prediction.at(at.first);
        const observed: T = try truth.at(at.second);
        residual.add((observed - predicted) * (observed - predicted));
        total.add((observed - mean) * (observed - mean));
    }
    const denominator: T = total.value();
    if (denominator == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    return 1 - residual.value() / denominator;
}
cosineSimilarity
/// The cosine of the angle between two tensors read as vectors.
///
/// `DomainError` for a zero vector, which has no direction to compare. Returning 0 would mean
/// "perpendicular", and a zero vector is not perpendicular to anything - it is undefined, and
/// silently calling it orthogonal is how a degenerate embedding becomes a plausible-looking
/// similarity score.
pub fn cosineSimilarity(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.size() != b.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const norm_a: T = try norm(T, a);
    const norm_b: T = try norm(T, b);
    if (norm_a == 0 or norm_b == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    return (try dotAll(T, a, b)) / (norm_a * norm_b);
}
hingeLoss
/// Hinge loss for binary labels in `{-1, +1}`: `mean(max(0, 1 - label * score))`.
///
/// The margin is 1, not 0: a sample classified correctly but only just still contributes, which
/// is what pushes the boundary away from the data rather than merely onto the right side of it.
/// That margin is the whole difference between a hinge loss and a step function.
pub fn hingeLoss(comptime T: type, scores: Tensor(T), labels: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = scores.size();
    if (n != labels.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var pair: Walk2 = .over(scores.shape[0..scores.rank], labels.shape[0..labels.rank]);
    while (pair.next()) |at| {
        const score: T = try scores.at(at.first);
        const label: T = try labels.at(at.second);
        total.add(@max(0, 1 - label * score));
    }
    return total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
}

Precision is "of everything called class k, how much was". If the model never predicted k at all, that denominator is zero and the quantity is genuinely undefined — there is no fraction of predictions that were right when there were no predictions. znum returns 0 and so does this, because a macro average has to average something. But 0 is a lie in a specific way: it says every prediction of this class was wrong, when the truth is that there were none.

So ClassScore carries precision_undefined and recall_undefined alongside the numbers. The average uses the plain zeros; a caller who cares can tell the two kinds of zero apart. The test is built around a class that occurs once in the truth and is never predicted, so recall is a real zero — the model missed it — while precision's zero describes nothing.

r2Score can be negative, and that is information. The name says "R squared" and squares are not negative, which misleads nearly everyone who meets it. 1 − ss_res/ss_tot goes below zero whenever the model does worse than predicting the mean, which happens and is exactly what you want to be told. Clamping at zero would turn "worse than a constant" into "explains nothing", and those are different. A constant truth is DomainError: there is no variation to explain.

cosineSimilarity refuses a zero vector rather than returning 0. Zero would mean "perpendicular", and a zero vector is not perpendicular to anything — silently calling it orthogonal is how a degenerate embedding becomes a plausible-looking similarity score.

11.12 Chain, and the quietest bug in the file#

const Net = zn.Chain(f64, struct {
    first:  zn.Dense(f64),
    second: zn.Dense(f64),
});
const run = try net.attach(&graph, x);
var opt = try zn.Optimizer(f64).init(gpa, &graph, &run.parameters, .{ .adam = ... });
Chain
/// Layers run in order, with their parameters collected for you.
///
/// COLLECTING THE PARAMETERS IS THE POINT; RUNNING THEM IN ORDER IS THE EASY HALF
///
/// Writing the forward pass by hand is fine - three calls, three named intermediates, and the
/// names help when a shape is wrong. What is NOT fine is the line after it:
///
///     &.{ a1.weight, a1.bias, a2.weight, a2.bias }
///
/// That list is written by hand today, and **a parameter left out of it silently never trains**.
/// The loss still falls, because the other parameters compensate; the network is simply worse
/// than it should be, and nothing anywhere says so. It is the quietest bug in the file.
///
/// A `Chain` holds its layers in a tuple and hands back every parameter it attached, in order,
/// so the list cannot disagree with the network. znum's version iterates a struct's field types
/// and carries a fused-activation tag table; this one carries a tuple and a count, because the
/// tape already fuses nothing and the graph already composes.
pub fn Chain(comptime T: type, comptime Layers: type) type {
    return struct {
        layers: Layers,

        const Self = @This();
        const info = @typeInfo(Layers).@"struct";

        /// Every parameter this chain attached, in the order the layers were listed.
        ///
        /// A fixed array rather than a slice: the size is known at comptime from the layer list,
        /// so there is nothing to allocate and nothing to free.
        pub const Parameters = [parameterCount()]Var;

        /// What `attach` hands back: the last layer's output, and every parameter on the way.
        pub const Run = struct {
            out: Var,
            parameters: Parameters,
        };

        /// How many `Var`s `attach` will produce, counted at comptime from the layer types.
        ///
        /// Every layer declares `pub const Attached`, whose fields are `out` plus one per
        /// parameter - so the count is the field count less one, and it comes from the layer
        /// ITSELF rather than from a table here that could fall out of step with it.
        fn parameterCount() usize {
            var total: usize = 0;
            for (info.field_types) |Layer| {
                total += @typeInfo(Layer.Attached).@"struct".field_names.len - 1;
            }
            return total;
        }

        /// Run every layer in order and collect what they attached.
        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var) Error!Run {
            var current: Var = input;
            var collected: Parameters = undefined;
            comptime var next: usize = 0;
            inline for (info.field_names) |layer_name| {
                const attached = try @field(self.layers, layer_name).attach(graph, current);
                current = attached.out;
                inline for (@typeInfo(@TypeOf(attached)).@"struct".field_names) |slot| {
                    if (comptime !std.mem.eql(u8, slot, "out")) {
                        collected[next] = @field(attached, slot);
                        next += 1;
                    }
                }
            }
            return .{ .out = current, .parameters = collected };
        }
    };
}
Dense
pub fn Dense(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        weight: Tensor(T),
        bias: Tensor(T),

        /// Allocate and initialise. `He` for a layer feeding a rectifier, `Xavier` otherwise.
        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            rng: Rng,
            fan_in: usize,
            fan_out: usize,
            scheme: InitScheme,
        ) Error!Self {
            const weight: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ fan_in, fan_out });
            const bias: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, fan_out });
            switch (scheme) {
                .xavier => try initXavier(T, weight, rng, fan_in, fan_out),
                .he => try initHe(T, weight, rng, fan_in),
            }
            // Biases start at zero. A random bias adds nothing a random weight does not already
            // provide, and a non-zero one shifts every unit's activation before any data is seen.
            bias.fill(0);
            return .{ .weight = weight, .bias = bias };
        }

        /// What `attach` hands back: the output, and handles for the two parameters.
        ///
        /// RETURNING ONLY THE OUTPUT IS NOT ENOUGH, which a first attempt at a training loop
        /// showed immediately: the loop needs the parameter handles to read their gradients, and
        /// with the layer holding no `Var` there is nowhere else to get them. Returning all three
        /// keeps the layer graph-agnostic AND gives the caller what a step requires.
        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            weight: Var,
            bias: Var,
        };

        /// Record `input @ weight + bias` onto `graph`.
        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var) Error!Attached {
            const w: Var = try graph.parameter(self.weight);
            const b: Var = try graph.parameter(self.bias);
            return .{
                .out = try graph.add(try graph.matmul(input, w), b),
                .weight = w,
                .bias = b,
            };
        }
    };
}
Optimizer
/// An optimiser bound to a set of a graph's parameters, owning whatever state the algorithm
/// needs per parameter.
///
/// THE STATE LIVES HERE, NOT WITH THE CALLER
///
/// `sgdMomentum` needs a velocity per parameter and `adamStep` needs two moments; without this,
/// a caller training four parameters with Adam allocates and threads eight extra tensors by
/// hand, and a mistake - one moment buffer shared between two parameters - trains silently
/// wrong. Binding the state to the parameter list at construction makes that mistake
/// unrepresentable.
///
/// ONE TYPE, THREE ALGORITHMS. The choice is a field, not a type parameter, so a training loop
/// can be written once and the algorithm changed at one call site. The state buffers for an
/// algorithm that does not use them are never allocated.
///
/// `step` reads gradients through the graph and writes updates into the graph's parameter
/// tensors, so the next `recompute` sees them with no further bookkeeping.
pub fn Optimizer(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        pub const Kind = union(enum) {
            sgd: struct { rate: T },
            momentum: struct { rate: T, momentum: T },
            adam: Adam,
        };

        kind: Kind,
        params: []const Var,
        /// Per parameter: one buffer for momentum, two for Adam, none for SGD.
        state: []Tensor(T),
        /// Adam's 1-based step counter.
        steps: usize,

        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            graph: *const Graph(T),
            params: []const Var,
            kind: Kind,
        ) Error!Self {
            const per: usize = switch (kind) {
                .sgd => 0,
                .momentum => 1,
                .adam => 2,
            };
            // THE PARAMETER LIST IS COPIED, NOT RETAINED
            //
            // The first version stored the caller's slice. Every test passed, because a test
            // writes `&.{ a, b, c }` in the same frame that calls `step`. The GPU training example
            // built its optimiser in `init` and stepped it from `update`, and the array literal
            // was gone - an out-of-bounds read inside `valueOf`, on the first frame. A type that
            // outlives the call that made it owns what it needs.
            const owned: []Var = try gpa.alloc(Var, params.len);
            @memcpy(owned, params);
            const state: []Tensor(T) = try gpa.alloc(Tensor(T), params.len * per);
            for (params, 0..) |v, i| {
                const shape: []const usize = graph.valueOf(v).shape[0..graph.valueOf(v).rank];
                var k: usize = 0;
                while (k < per) : (k += 1) {
                    state[i * per + k] = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, shape);
                    state[i * per + k].fill(0);
                }
            }
            return .{ .kind = kind, .params = owned, .state = state, .steps = 0 };
        }

        /// Apply one update to every bound parameter, from the gradients `backward` left.
        pub fn step(self: *Self, graph: *const Graph(T)) Error!void {
            self.steps += 1;
            for (self.params, 0..) |v, i| {
                const weight: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(v);
                const grad: Tensor(T) = try graph.gradOf(v);
                switch (self.kind) {
                    .sgd => |h| try sgdStep(T, weight, weight, grad, h.rate),
                    .momentum => |h| try sgdMomentum(
                        T,
                        weight,
                        weight,
                        grad,
                        self.state[i],
                        h.rate,
                        h.momentum,
                    ),
                    .adam => |h| try adamStep(
                        T,
                        weight,
                        weight,
                        grad,
                        self.state[i * 2],
                        self.state[i * 2 + 1],
                        h,
                        self.steps,
                    ),
                }
            }
        }
    };
}

Writing the forward pass by hand is fine — three calls, three named intermediates, and the names help when a shape is wrong. The line after it is not:

&.{ a1.weight, a1.bias, a2.weight, a2.bias }

That list is written by hand, and a parameter missing from it silently never trains. The loss still falls — the other parameters compensate — so the network is simply worse than it should be and nothing anywhere says so. It is the quietest bug in this library, and it is the reason Chain exists. Running the layers in order is the easy half.

The count comes from each layer's own pub const Attached, whose fields are out plus one per parameter. So adding a layer changes the count without anyone editing a table, and a layer that gained a parameter would be counted correctly the day it did.

Parameters is a fixed array, not a slice: the size is known at comptime from the layer list, so there is nothing to allocate and nothing to free.

The test checks two things beyond the count. Every collected Var must be distinct — a chain handing back the same one twice would train one parameter twice and another never, which is the same bug arriving by a different route. And the collected list is compared against attaching the same layers by hand on a fresh graph, so "in order" is asserted rather than assumed.

znum's Chain iterates a struct's field types and carries a fused-activation tag table. This one carries a tuple and a count, because the tape fuses nothing and the graph already composes — there is no second thing for the chain to know.

11.13 BatchNorm, and a mode you cannot forget#

const h = try bn.attach(&graph, x, .training);   // batch stats
const h = try bn.attach(&graph, x, .inference);  // running stats
try bn.observe(batch);                           // move the running stats

znum stores mode: Mode on the struct and defaults it to .training, which is exactly what PyTorch does with model.train() / model.eval(). It is also one of the most common bugs in either: forget to flip it before evaluating and the network silently normalises by whatever batch it is looking at.

On a large evaluation batch the numbers look plausible. On a batch of one they are exactly zero for every feature, because a single sample has no variance — the output carries no information at all. The model "works in training and breaks in production", and nothing anywhere reported a mistake. The test makes that visible rather than describing it: it pushes one row through .training and asserts every output is erased, then pushes the same row through .inference and asserts it survives.

Here use is a required argument. There is no state to forget, no default to inherit, and the call site says which statistics it wants. A caller cannot reach the single-row disaster by omission — only by asking for it.

observe is separate from attach for the same reason. attach only reads; znum folds the running-statistics update into the forward pass, so building the graph mutates the layer, and a caller who recomputes a graph twice ages the statistics twice. The test asserts exactly that: two attaches leave the running mean at zero, and only observe moves it.

Batch norm is layer norm transposed

Layer norm normalises each row across its features; batch norm normalises each column across the batch. That is the same arithmetic on the transpose — verified to 2.2e-16 against a direct per-column implementation — so the forward pass and its gradient both come from the pair that already exists. znum writes both out separately; here attach transposes, calls layerNormRows, and transposes back. Both transposes are views, so the cost is strided access rather than a copy.

11.14 LstmCell, and a one-line change worth 44x#

const cell = try zn.LstmCell(f64).init(gpa, rng, input_size, hidden_size);
const s = try cell.step(&graph, x, hidden, cell_state);
// s.hidden, s.cell, s.parameters
LstmCell
/// One LSTM timestep: four gates deciding what to keep, what to add, and what to show.
///
/// THE FORGET BIAS STARTS AT ONE, AND THAT IS THE DIFFERENCE THAT MATTERS
///
/// znum zeroes every bias, and so does PyTorch. A zero forget bias means `sigmoid(0) = 0.5` on
/// the first step, so **the cell halves its memory every timestep before it has learned
/// anything**: after ten steps 0.1% of the original signal survives, and the gradient that would
/// teach it to remember has to travel back through all that decay.
///
/// Starting the forget bias at one makes `sigmoid(1) = 0.73` instead. Measured over ten steps
/// with no input at all, the cell retains **4.3% against 0.1% - forty times more** - which is the
/// difference between a gradient that arrives and one that does not. The result is Gers's, from
/// 1999, and it is still absent from most implementations.
///
/// Every other bias starts at zero, for the usual reason: a random or nonzero bias adds nothing a
/// weight does not already provide.
///
/// FOUR WEIGHT PAIRS, NOT ONE FUSED MATRIX
///
/// PyTorch fuses the four gates into one `(4*hidden, input)` matrix and slices the result, which
/// is one matmul instead of four. That needs a graph-level SLICE with a backward, and this tape
/// has none - so fusing would mean building tape machinery to save three matmuls on a cell whose
/// cost is dominated by the sequence loop around it. **Four pairs, named for their gates**, and
/// the fusion is a note rather than a half-built abstraction.
pub fn LstmCell(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        /// How much of the new candidate to let in.
        input_gate: Gate,
        /// How much of the existing cell to keep. Its bias starts at ONE - see above.
        forget_gate: Gate,
        /// The new value proposed for the cell, before the input gate scales it.
        candidate: Gate,
        /// How much of the cell to expose as the hidden state.
        output_gate: Gate,

        const Self = @This();

        /// One gate's two weight matrices and its bias.
        ///
        /// Every gate reads BOTH the current input and the previous hidden state, which is the
        /// whole idea: `from_input` is what the world says, `from_hidden` is what the cell
        /// remembers, and the gate is their sum.
        pub const Gate = struct {
            from_input: Tensor(T),
            from_hidden: Tensor(T),
            bias: Tensor(T),
        };

        /// What one step produces. **Two states, not one** - `hidden` is what the next layer
        /// sees, `cell` is the memory that carries forward, and confusing them is the classic
        /// LSTM mistake.
        pub const Stepped = struct {
            hidden: Var,
            cell: Var,
            parameters: [12]Var,
        };

        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            rng: Rng,
            input_size: usize,
            hidden_size: usize,
        ) Error!Self {
            var made: Self = undefined;
            const gates = [_][]const u8{ "input_gate", "forget_gate", "candidate", "output_gate" };
            inline for (gates, 0..) |name, i| {
                const from_input: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ input_size, hidden_size });
                const from_hidden: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ hidden_size, hidden_size });
                const bias: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, hidden_size });
                try initXavier(T, from_input, rng.split(@intCast(i * 2)), input_size, hidden_size);
                try initXavier(T, from_hidden, rng.split(@intCast(i * 2 + 1)), hidden_size, hidden_size);
                // The forget gate is index 1, and it is the one that starts at one.
                bias.fill(if (i == 1) 1 else 0);
                @field(made, name) = .{
                    .from_input = from_input,
                    .from_hidden = from_hidden,
                    .bias = bias,
                };
            }
            return made;
        }

        /// Advance one timestep. Built entirely from tape operations, so the gradient is the
        /// tape's and nothing here needs a backward of its own.
        pub fn step(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            input: Var,
            hidden: Var,
            cell: Var,
        ) Error!Stepped {
            var collected: [12]Var = undefined;
            var next: usize = 0;
            const gates = [_][]const u8{ "input_gate", "forget_gate", "candidate", "output_gate" };
            var summed: [4]Var = undefined;
            inline for (gates, 0..) |name, i| {
                const gate: Gate = @field(self, name);
                const from_input: Var = try graph.parameter(gate.from_input);
                const from_hidden: Var = try graph.parameter(gate.from_hidden);
                const bias: Var = try graph.parameter(gate.bias);
                collected[next] = from_input;
                collected[next + 1] = from_hidden;
                collected[next + 2] = bias;
                next += 3;
                const world: Var = try graph.matmul(input, from_input);
                const memory: Var = try graph.matmul(hidden, from_hidden);
                summed[i] = try graph.add(try graph.add(world, memory), bias);
            }
            const keep: Var = try graph.sigmoid(summed[1]);
            const admit: Var = try graph.sigmoid(summed[0]);
            const proposed: Var = try graph.tanh(summed[2]);
            const show: Var = try graph.sigmoid(summed[3]);
            // The cell is what survives plus what is admitted; the hidden state is the cell seen
            // through the output gate. Keeping these two lines apart is the point of the type.
            const next_cell: Var = try graph.add(
                try graph.mul(keep, cell),
                try graph.mul(admit, proposed),
            );
            const next_hidden: Var = try graph.mul(show, try graph.tanh(next_cell));
            return .{ .hidden = next_hidden, .cell = next_cell, .parameters = collected };
        }
    };
}

The forget bias starts at one. znum zeroes every bias, and so does PyTorch — and a zero forget bias means sigmoid(0) = 0.5, so the cell halves its memory every timestep before it has learned anything. Ten steps in, 0.1% of the original signal survives, and the gradient that would teach it to remember has to travel back through all that decay.

Starting at one gives sigmoid(1) = 0.73. with every weight zeroed so only the biases decide and the input gate shut so nothing but decay happens:

forget bias 0    0.000977   (0.5^10,  as it must be)
forget bias 1    0.043604   (0.73^10)

Forty-four times the signal, and with it forty-four times the gradient. The result is Gers's, from 1999, and it is still absent from most implementations. The test asserts the exact powers rather than just the ratio, so it measures the gate and not the arithmetic around it — and it separately asserts that init produces this default, because a default nobody checks is a default that drifts.

Two states, and confusing them is the classic mistake

step returns hidden and cell. The hidden state is what the next layer sees; the cell is the memory that carries forward. A type returning one value could not stop you mixing them, so this one returns both, and they are different vars.

Four weight pairs, not one fused matrix

PyTorch fuses the four gates into one (4·hidden, input) matrix and slices the result — one matmul instead of four. That needs a graph-level slice with a backward, and this tape has none. Fusing would mean building tape machinery to save three matmuls on a cell whose cost is dominated by the sequence loop around it, so the fusion is a note here rather than a half-built abstraction.

znum keeps twelve separately named tensors (w_ii, w_hi, b_i, and so on). Here they are grouped into four Gates with from_input and from_hidden, because that grouping is the idea: every gate reads both what the world says and what the cell remembers.

Every operation in step is already on the tape — matmul, sigmoid, tanh, add, mul — so the gradient is the tape's and nothing here needs a backward of its own.

11.15 Attention, and a mask you cannot forget#

try attention.attach(&graph, x, .everywhere);     // encoder: all positions
try attention.attach(&graph, x, .backward_only);  // decoder: no peeking ahead

Same shape as BatchNorm.Use, and the consequence is worse. A language model trained without a causal mask can see the answer it is predicting: the loss drops to near zero, the training curve looks superb, and the model generates nonsense the moment it has to predict something it has not been shown. Nothing fails, nothing warns, and the metric that would tell you is the one that looks best.

So look is required at every call. There is no default to inherit and no field to forget.

The test perturbs the future and watches the past

Inspecting the mask would only confirm the mask is the one intended. Instead, replacing the last position with a large value and measures how much every earlier output moves:

.backward_only   largest shift  EXACTLY 0
.everywhere      largest shift  > 0.1

Not "small" — zero. A causal mask that let through a millionth would be a causal mask with a bug, and a tolerance would hide it. The unmasked run is what proves the zero came from the mask rather than from the input being ignored.

Two smaller choices

The scale is derived, not passed. Dot products grow with the width they are summed over, so without 1/sqrt(width) the softmax saturates and the gradient vanishes. Passing the wrong dimension is a quiet, common mistake; here it comes from the projection's own shape and cannot disagree with the tensors it scales.

The mask adds a large negative, not -inf. Negative infinity is exactly right in algebra and produces inf - inf = NaN the moment a row is entirely masked — which the first row of a causal mask never is, but a padded batch would be. A number the exponential flushes to zero says the same thing and survives.

Multi-head, and what the name hides

try zn.Attention(f64).init(gpa, rng, model_dim, width, heads);
Attention
/// Scaled dot-product attention: every position reads every position it is allowed to see.
///
/// WHETHER IT CAN SEE THE FUTURE IS AN ARGUMENT, NOT A SETTING
///
/// The same lesson as `BatchNorm.Use`, and the consequence is worse. A language model trained
/// without a causal mask **can see the answer it is predicting**: the loss drops to near zero,
/// the training curve looks superb, and the model generates nonsense the moment it has to predict
/// something it has not been shown. Nothing fails, nothing warns, and the metric that would tell
/// you is the one that looks best.
///
/// So `look` is required at every call:
///
///     try attention.attach(&graph, x, .everywhere);     // encoder: all positions
///     try attention.attach(&graph, x, .backward_only);  // decoder: no peeking ahead
///
/// THE SCALE IS DERIVED, NOT PASSED
///
/// Dot products grow with the width they are summed over, so without `1/sqrt(width)` the softmax
/// saturates and the gradient vanishes - and passing the wrong dimension is a quiet, common
/// mistake. Here it comes from the projection's own shape, so it cannot disagree with the tensors
/// it scales.
///
/// SINGLE HEAD. Multiple heads need the projection split and the results concatenated, and this
/// tape has neither a slice nor a concat with a backward - the same limit that stopped `LstmCell`
/// fusing its gates. Stated rather than half-built.
pub fn Attention(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        /// What each position is looking for.
        query: Tensor(T),
        /// What each position offers as a label.
        key: Tensor(T),
        /// What each position hands over when chosen.
        value: Tensor(T),
        /// How many independent attention patterns share the projection width.
        heads: usize,

        const Self = @This();

        /// Which positions a query may attend to. **Required** - see the note above.
        pub const Look = enum {
            /// Every position, in both directions. What an encoder wants.
            everywhere,
            /// Only positions at or before this one. What a decoder MUST have.
            backward_only,
        };

        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            query: Var,
            key: Var,
            value: Var,
        };

        /// `heads` independent attention patterns sharing one projection of `width` columns.
        ///
        /// `heads = 1` is ordinary attention. More heads do not widen anything - they DIVIDE the
        /// same width, so each head attends in a narrower subspace and the cost is unchanged.
        /// That is the part the name hides: multi-head is not more capacity, it is the same
        /// capacity spent on several patterns instead of one.
        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            rng: Rng,
            model_dim: usize,
            width: usize,
            heads: usize,
        ) Error!Self {
            if (heads == 0 or width % heads != 0) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const query: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ model_dim, width });
            const key: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ model_dim, width });
            const value: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ model_dim, width });
            try initXavier(T, query, rng.split(0), model_dim, width);
            try initXavier(T, key, rng.split(1), model_dim, width);
            try initXavier(T, value, rng.split(2), model_dim, width);
            return .{ .query = query, .key = key, .value = value, .heads = heads };
        }

        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var, look: Look) Error!Attached {
            const x: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(input);
            if (x.rank != 2 or x.shape[1] != self.query.shape[0]) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const positions: usize = x.shape[0];
            const width: usize = self.query.shape[1];

            const wq: Var = try graph.parameter(self.query);
            const wk: Var = try graph.parameter(self.key);
            const wv: Var = try graph.parameter(self.value);
            const q: Var = try graph.matmul(input, wq);
            const k: Var = try graph.matmul(input, wk);
            const v: Var = try graph.matmul(input, wv);

            // RANK 2 THROUGHOUT, ONE HEAD AT A TIME
            //
            // znum splits heads by reshaping to rank 4 and permuting: `[rows, D]` becomes
            // `[B, S, H, d]` becomes `[B, H, S, d]`. That needs a differentiable reshape AND
            // permute, and it makes every line afterwards reason about four axes.
            //
            // With `graph.slice` and `graph.concat` the same thing is a loop: take each head's
            // columns, attend, and join the results. Every tensor stays rank 2 and each iteration
            // reads exactly like the single-head maths, because it IS the single-head maths.
            //
            // The cost: `heads` separate matmuls where a batched form would do one. For the head
            // counts a rank-2 tape sees that is a fair trade for not having two more tape ops and
            // four axes to track - and it is stated here rather than discovered.
            const head_width: usize = width / self.heads;
            const scale_by: T = 1.0 / @sqrt(@as(T, @floatFromInt(head_width)));
            var joined: ?Var = null;
            for (0..self.heads) |head| {
                const from: usize = head * head_width;
                const qh: Var = try graph.slice(q, 1, from, head_width);
                const kh: Var = try graph.slice(k, 1, from, head_width);
                const vh: Var = try graph.slice(v, 1, from, head_width);
                const raw_head: Var = try graph.matmul(qh, try graph.transpose(kh));
                const scaled_head: Var = try graph.scale(raw_head, scale_by);
                const allowed: Var = try applyLook(graph, scaled_head, look, positions);
                const attended: Var = try graph.matmul(try graph.softmaxRows(allowed), vh);
                joined = if (joined) |so_far|
                    try graph.concat(so_far, attended, 1)
                else
                    attended;
            }
            return .{ .out = joined.?, .query = wq, .key = wk, .value = wv };
        }

        /// The mask, or nothing, depending on `look`.
        fn applyLook(graph: *Graph(T), scores: Var, look: Look, positions: usize) Error!Var {
            if (look == .everywhere) {
                return scores;
            }
            // A LARGE NEGATIVE, NOT AN INFINITY. `-inf` would be exactly right in algebra and
            // produces `inf - inf = NaN` the moment a row is entirely masked, which the first row
            // of a causal mask never is but a padded batch would be. A number the exponential
            // flushes to zero says the same thing and survives.
            const mask: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(graph.gpa, &.{ positions, positions });
            for (0..positions) |row| {
                for (0..positions) |col| {
                    try mask.setAt(&.{ row, col }, if (col > row) -1.0e30 else 0);
                }
            }
            return graph.add(scores, try graph.constant(mask));
        }
    };
}

More heads do not widen anything — they divide the same width. Two heads over a width of 8 produce exactly the same output shape and the same parameter count as one head over 8; each simply attends in a narrower subspace. That is the part "multi-head" hides, and this is exact for it: same shape, different numbers.

The scale then comes from the head's width, not the projection's. Splitting 8 into two heads means each sums over 4, so the scale is 1/sqrt(4). Getting that wrong is invisible in the shapes and quiet in the output — it just saturates the softmax a little more than it should. The test pins it by checking that one head over 4 and two heads over 8 share a scale, where a projection-wide scale would have made them differ.

A width that does not divide by the head count is refused rather than silently dropping a column.

Rank 2 throughout, one head at a time

znum splits heads by reshaping to rank 4 and permuting: [rows, D] becomes [B, S, H, d] becomes [B, H, S, d]. That needs a differentiable reshape and permute, and every line afterwards reasons about four axes.

With graph.slice and graph.concat the same thing is a loop: take each head's columns, attend, join the results. Every tensor stays rank 2, and each iteration reads exactly like the single-head maths — because it is the single-head maths.

The cost is heads separate matmuls where a batched form would do one. For the head counts a rank-2 tape sees, that is a fair trade for not carrying two more tape ops and four axes, and it is stated here rather than discovered.

11.16 TransformerBlock, and why pre-norm#

h = x + attention(norm(x))
y = h + feedForward(norm(h))

The block normalises before each sublayer and adds the result to the untouched input. The original transformer did it the other way — sublayer, add, then normalise — and that version cannot be trained without a learning-rate warmup, because the normalisation sits on the residual path and rescales the gradient at every block.

Pre-norm leaves the path clear: the gradient reaches the input carrying a component of exactly one from each residual, whatever the depth. znum is pre-norm too; the reason usually is not written down, so it is here — and the consequence rather than the arrangement, by measuring that no element of the input gradient has collapsed.

A fresh block is close to the identity. The sublayers start small against the residual, so depth you have not trained yet does nothing rather than something arbitrary. That is what makes stacking blocks safe before any training has happened, and there is a bound on how far the output can move from its input.

Causality survives composition, and that is worth checking separately

The mask lives inside Attention. But a residual carries the input forward around the attention, so composition is exactly where a leak would appear — the block could route the future past its own mask. The same perturbation test therefore runs again at this level: disturb the last position, and every earlier output must be bit-identical.

The seven parameters are collected the same way Chain does it, for the same reason: one missing from that list would silently never train.

The feed-forward is chosen at init, because the gate changes a shape

try zn.TransformerBlock(f64).init(gpa, rng, model_dim, hidden, heads, eps, .swiglu);
TransformerBlock
/// One transformer block: attention and a feed-forward pair, each behind a residual.
///
/// PRE-NORM, AND THE RESIDUAL IS WHY
///
/// The block normalises BEFORE each sublayer and adds the result to the untouched input:
///
///     h = x + attention(norm(x))
///     y = h + feedForward(norm(h))
///
/// The original transformer did it the other way - sublayer, add, then normalise - and that
/// version **cannot be trained without a learning-rate warmup**, because the normalisation sits
/// on the residual path and rescales the gradient every block. Pre-norm leaves the path clear:
/// the gradient reaches the input with a component of exactly one from each residual, whatever
/// the depth. znum is pre-norm too; this note exists because the reason usually is not written
/// down, and the test below asserts the consequence rather than the arrangement.
///
/// The sublayers begin small relative to the residual, so a fresh block is close to the identity.
/// That is the point: depth you have not trained yet should do nothing, not something arbitrary.
pub fn TransformerBlock(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        /// Reads across positions.
        attention: Attention(T),
        /// The first half of the position-wise pair, widening.
        widen: Dense(T),
        /// The second half, narrowing back so the residual still fits.
        narrow: Dense(T),
        /// Added under the square root in both norms.
        epsilon: T,
        /// Which feed-forward this block was built with - it decided `widen`'s shape.
        feed_forward: FeedForward,

        const Self = @This();

        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            /// Everything the block attached, ready for the optimiser - the same argument as
            /// `Chain`: a parameter missing from this list would silently never train.
            parameters: [7]Var,
        };

        /// Which feed-forward the block uses. **Chosen at `init`, not at `attach`.**
        ///
        /// THE GATE CHANGES A LAYER'S SHAPE, NOT JUST THE ACTIVATION BETWEEN TWO
        ///
        /// It looks like an activation swap and is not. SwiGLU consumes half its input as a gate,
        /// so to produce `hidden` columns the widening layer has to make **2 x hidden** - which
        /// makes it a different network, not the same one with a different nonlinearity.
        ///
        ///     relu     widen (model -> hidden),     relu,   narrow (hidden -> model)
        ///     swiglu   widen (model -> 2 x hidden), swiglu, narrow (hidden -> model)
        ///
        /// At `model_dim = 4, hidden = 16` that is 148 parameters against 228. A caller comparing
        /// the two is comparing capacities as well as gates, and the doc says so rather than
        /// letting them conclude the gate alone did it.
        pub const FeedForward = enum { relu, swiglu };

        /// `hidden` is the width of the feed-forward middle. Four times `model_dim` is the usual
        /// choice and is the caller's to make rather than a constant hidden here.
        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            rng: Rng,
            model_dim: usize,
            hidden: usize,
            heads: usize,
            epsilon: T,
            feed_forward: FeedForward,
        ) Error!Self {
            // SwiGLU splits the widened columns in half, so it needs twice as many to hand the
            // same width to the narrowing layer.
            const widened: usize = switch (feed_forward) {
                .relu => hidden,
                .swiglu => hidden * 2,
            };
            return .{
                .attention = try Attention(T).init(gpa, rng.split(0), model_dim, model_dim, heads),
                .widen = try Dense(T).init(gpa, rng.split(1), model_dim, widened, .xavier),
                .narrow = try Dense(T).init(gpa, rng.split(2), hidden, model_dim, .xavier),
                .epsilon = epsilon,
                .feed_forward = feed_forward,
            };
        }

        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var, look: Attention(T).Look) Error!Attached {
            // Attention sublayer. `input` goes into the add untouched - that is the residual.
            const before_attention: Var = try graph.layerNormRows(input, self.epsilon);
            const attended = try self.attention.attach(graph, before_attention, look);
            const mixed: Var = try graph.add(input, attended.out);

            // Feed-forward sublayer, position by position, with its own residual.
            const before_feedforward: Var = try graph.layerNormRows(mixed, self.epsilon);
            const wide = try self.widen.attach(graph, before_feedforward);
            const activated: Var = switch (self.feed_forward) {
                .relu => try graph.relu(wide.out),
                // Halves the width, which is why `init` doubled it.
                .swiglu => try graph.swiglu(wide.out),
            };
            const narrowed = try self.narrow.attach(graph, activated);

            return .{
                .out = try graph.add(mixed, narrowed.out),
                .parameters = .{
                    attended.query, attended.key, attended.value,
                    wide.weight,    wide.bias,    narrowed.weight,
                    narrowed.bias,
                },
            };
        }
    };
}

It looks like an activation swap and is not. SwiGLU consumes half its input as a gate, so to hand hidden columns to the narrowing layer, the widening layer has to produce 2 x hidden:

relu     widen (model -> hidden),     relu,   narrow (hidden -> model)
swiglu   widen (model -> 2 x hidden), swiglu, narrow (hidden -> model)

At model_dim = 4, hidden = 16 that is 148 parameters against 228. A caller comparing the two is comparing capacities as well as gates, which is worth knowing before concluding the gate alone moved a training curve. The test asserts both widths and that the narrowing layer is unchanged.

What does not change: the output shape, so a block is still a block and still stacks; and the parameter count, still seven — the gate changed a shape, not the number of things to train.

Causality is re-checked for the gated block too. The mask is in the attention, but a residual carries the input around it either way, so the perturbation test runs again rather than being assumed to transfer.

11.17 Gated units, and a backward nobody wrote#

const h = try graph.glu(x);      // slice * sigmoid(slice)
const h = try graph.swiglu(x);   // slice * silu(slice)

Half the columns carry the value and half the gate, so the output is half as wide. SwiGLU is the gate in every recent transformer feed-forward.

Neither has a backward. znum writes gatedForward and then a hand-derived gatedBackward — walking coordinates and applying the product rule by hand. Here a GLU is

slice(x, 0, half) * sigmoid(slice(x, half, half))

and every one of those is already a tape node, so the gradient is the tape's. There is no second derivation to keep in step with the first, and no chance of the pair disagreeing under maintenance.

This is the third thing graph.slice and graph.concat paid for, after multi-head attention and the batched cartpole twin. They were built because their absence had shaped two designs; they keep shaping the ones that come after.

The test still checks the gradient against central differences at every element — over the value half and the gate half, since a product rule applied by hand is exactly the kind of thing that is right on one branch and wrong on the other. And it asserts SwiGLU differs numerically from GLU, because a version ignoring the gate kind would pass the shape check and every gradient check above it.

11.18 Keeping a trained network#

try zn.saveParameters(f64, gpa, &bytes, &graph, run.parameters);
// ... later, in a fresh process ...
try zn.loadParameters(f64, gpa, bytes, &graph, run.parameters);
saveParameters
pub fn saveParameters(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    out: *std.ArrayList(u8),
    graph: *const Graph(T),
    parameters: []const Var,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const names: [][]const u8 = gpa.alloc([]const u8, parameters.len) catch
        return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer {
        for (names) |name| {
            gpa.free(name);
        }
        gpa.free(names);
    }
    const tensors: []Tensor(T) = gpa.alloc(Tensor(T), parameters.len) catch
        return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer gpa.free(tensors);
    for (parameters, 0..) |parameter, i| {
        names[i] = std.fmt.allocPrint(gpa, "p{d}", .{i}) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
        tensors[i] = graph.valueOf(parameter);
    }
    return saveTensors(T, gpa, out, names, tensors);
}
loadParameters
/// Read a network's parameters back into it, in place.
///
/// The tensors are written THROUGH the graph's parameter values, so a network built with the
/// same architecture picks up the saved weights and is ready to run. `loadTensors` refuses a
/// shape that disagrees, which catches the common mistake of loading into a different
/// architecture.
pub fn loadParameters(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    bytes: []const u8,
    graph: *const Graph(T),
    parameters: []const Var,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const names: [][]const u8 = gpa.alloc([]const u8, parameters.len) catch
        return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer {
        for (names) |name| {
            gpa.free(name);
        }
        gpa.free(names);
    }
    const tensors: []Tensor(T) = gpa.alloc(Tensor(T), parameters.len) catch
        return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer gpa.free(tensors);
    for (parameters, 0..) |parameter, i| {
        names[i] = std.fmt.allocPrint(gpa, "p{d}", .{i}) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
        tensors[i] = graph.valueOf(parameter);
    }
    return loadTensors(T, bytes, names, tensors);
}

saveTensors and loadTensors were always here, and they take a list of names and a list of tensors. What was missing is the bridge from a network to those two lists — so this library could train a cartpole agent and not keep it, and every run started from noise.

What this guarantees

Not that the bytes round-trip — the tensor tests already make that claim. The claim is that a network rebuilt from a different seed and then loaded produces the same output as the one that trained.

Three things make that meaningful rather than decorative:

  • The network is trained first, so the weights are no longer the initialisation. A round trip that silently restored the seed rather than the weights would otherwise pass.
  • The fresh network uses a different seed, so if the load does nothing the answers differ. The test asserts they differ before loading — a control, so the comparison after means something.
  • It compares every output, not just the loss. A loss is one number, and two different networks can share it by coincidence.

Named by position, which is a real constraint

The parameters are saved as p0, p1, ... in attach order, because a Var carries no name. The loading network must have the same architecture in the same order.

loadTensors checks the shapes, so a changed layer is caught — loading a checkpoint into a network whose hidden width grew and gets a ShapeMismatch. A same-shaped layer in a different role is not caught, and that is worth knowing rather than discovering.

11.19 Gradient clipping, and the direction it must keep#

const norm = try zn.clipGradNorm(f64, &graph, run.parameters, 1.0);
// `norm` is the value BEFORE clipping - log it
clipGradNorm
/// Scale every gradient down together if their combined norm exceeds `max_norm`.
///
/// Returns the norm BEFORE clipping, which is the number worth logging: a spike in it is how you
/// find out a batch was bad, and clipping silently would hide exactly that.
///
/// ONE NORM ACROSS ALL PARAMETERS, NOT ONE PER TENSOR
///
/// This is the part that is easy to get wrong and hard to notice. Clipping each tensor to its own
/// limit **changes the direction of the update**: a layer whose gradient was twice its neighbour's
/// comes out the same size, so the step now points somewhere the loss surface never suggested.
///
/// Scaling everything by one factor keeps the direction exactly and shortens the step, which is
/// what "clip the gradient" is supposed to mean. The test asserts the direction survives, because
/// a per-tensor version passes every test about magnitude.
///
/// WHY THIS MATTERS MORE THAN THE OPTIMISER YOU PICK
///
/// A transformer meets a bad batch, the loss spikes, and one enormous update moves the weights
/// somewhere the model never recovers from. Adam does not save you - it normalises by a RUNNING
/// average, so a single outlier is still an outlier. Clipping is the thing that does.
pub fn clipGradNorm(
    comptime T: type,
    graph: *const Graph(T),
    parameters: []const Var,
    max_norm: T,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (max_norm <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var squares: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    for (parameters) |parameter| {
        const slopes: Tensor(T) = try graph.gradOf(parameter);
        for (0..slopes.size()) |i| {
            const g: T = slopes.data[i];
            squares.add(g * g);
        }
    }
    const total: T = @sqrt(squares.value());
    if (total <= max_norm) {
        return total;
    }
    // A SINGLE FACTOR, APPLIED TO EVERYTHING - that is what keeps the direction.
    const factor: T = max_norm / total;
    for (parameters) |parameter| {
        const slopes: Tensor(T) = try graph.gradOf(parameter);
        for (0..slopes.size()) |i| {
            slopes.data[i] *= factor;
        }
    }
    return total;
}

A transformer meets a bad batch, the loss spikes, and one enormous update moves the weights somewhere the model never recovers from. Adam does not save you — it normalises by a running average, so a single outlier is still an outlier. Clipping is the thing that does.

The return value is the norm before clipping, which is the number worth logging: a spike in it is how you learn a batch was bad, and clipping silently would hide exactly that.

One norm across all parameters, not one per tensor

This is the part that is easy to get wrong and hard to notice. Say two parameters whose gradients have norms 5 and 1:

global   both scaled by 1/sqrt(26)   ->  norms 0.98 and 0.196, ratio still 5
per-tensor  each scaled to 1         ->  norms 1 and 1,        ratio now 1

Clipping each tensor to its own limit changes the direction of the update. The layer whose gradient was five times its neighbour's comes out the same size, and the step now points somewhere the loss surface never suggested.

Scaling everything by one factor keeps the direction exactly and shortens the step, which is what "clip the gradient" is supposed to mean.

The test asserts the ratio survives, not just the final magnitude — a per-tensor version passes every check about size. Verified by writing the per-tensor version and watching the test fail.

One asymmetry worth noting: a gradient already under the limit is left alone, not scaled up to meet it. That would be a different operation entirely, and a tempting symmetry to add.

12. Statistics#

const v  = try zn.variance(f64, t, .sample);
const sd = try zn.stdDev(f64, t, .population);
const r  = try zn.rootMeanSquare(f64, t);
const d  = try zn.meanAbsDev(f64, t);
const se = try zn.stdErr(f64, t);

try zn.zscore(f64, out, t);        // mean 0, sample deviation 1
try zn.minMaxScale(f64, out, t);   // into [0, 1]

const c    = try zn.covariance(f64, a, b, .sample);
const corr = try zn.correlation(f64, a, b);
variance
/// The variance of every element, with the divisor chosen by `ddof`.
///
/// TWO PASSES, FOR THE REASON `layerNormRows` GIVES
///
/// `E[x^2] - E[x]^2` is one pass and subtracts two large nearly-equal numbers, losing most of its
/// significant digits when the mean is large relative to the spread. Two passes cost one more
/// read of memory that is already in cache and are correct for every input.
///
/// Returns `DomainError` when the divisor is undefined - `n = 0`, or `n = 1` with
/// `.sample` - rather than a NaN that propagates into a result nobody examines. That is what
/// `Ddof.divisor` returning an optional is for.
pub fn variance(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), ddof: Ddof) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    const divisor: f64 = ddof.divisor(n) orelse return Error.DomainError;
    const mean: T = try meanAll(T, a);

    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const d: T = (try a.at(at)) - mean;
        const term: T = d * d;
        total.add(term);
    }
    const divide_by: T = @floatCast(divisor);
    return total.value() / divide_by;
}
stdDev
/// The standard deviation: the square root of `variance`.
pub fn stdDev(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), ddof: Ddof) Error!T {
    return @sqrt(try variance(T, a, ddof));
}
rootMeanSquare
/// The root mean square of every element.
pub fn rootMeanSquare(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = try a.at(at);
        const term: T = x * x;
        total.add(term);
    }
    return @sqrt(total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n)));
}
meanAbsDev
/// The mean absolute deviation from the mean.
pub fn meanAbsDev(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const mean: T = try meanAll(T, a);
    var total: T = 0;
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        total += @abs((try a.at(at)) - mean);
    }
    return total / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
}
stdErr
/// The standard error of the mean: `stdDev(.sample) / sqrt(n)`.
pub fn stdErr(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n < 2) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    return (try stdDev(T, a, .sample)) / @sqrt(@as(T, @floatFromInt(n)));
}
zscore
/// `out = (a - mean) / stdDev`, elementwise - the z-score of every element.
///
/// Uses the SAMPLE deviation, matching what a reader gets from R or scipy by default. The
/// population form is a different number and the difference is visible at small n, so the choice
/// is stated rather than left to whichever the implementer reached for.
pub fn zscore(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const mean: T = try meanAll(T, a);
    const sd: T = try stdDev(T, a, .sample);
    if (sd == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = va.data[va.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = (x - mean) / sd;
    }
}
minMaxScale
/// Normalise every element into `[0, 1]` by the tensor's own range.
pub fn minMaxScale(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), a: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const lo: T = try minAll(T, a);
    const hi: T = try maxAll(T, a);
    if (hi == lo) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (out.isAliased()) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
    const va: Tensor(T) = try a.broadcastTo(shape);
    var walk: Walk = .over(shape);
