    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = va.data[va.offsetOf(at)];
        out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = (x - lo) / (hi - lo);
    }
}
covariance
/// The covariance of two tensors of equal size, with the divisor chosen by `ddof`.
pub fn covariance(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T), ddof: Ddof) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n != b.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const divisor: f64 = ddof.divisor(n) orelse return Error.DomainError;
    const mean_a: T = try meanAll(T, a);
    const mean_b: T = try meanAll(T, b);

    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var pair: Walk2 = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank], b.shape[0..b.rank]);
    while (pair.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = (try a.at(at.first)) - mean_a;
        const y: T = (try b.at(at.second)) - mean_b;
        const term: T = x * y;
        total.add(term);
    }
    const divide_by: T = @floatCast(divisor);
    return total.value() / divide_by;
}
correlation
/// Pearson's correlation coefficient of two tensors of equal size.
///
/// NOT clamped to [-1, 1]. Rounding can put it a few ULP outside, and clamping would hide the
/// only signal that something upstream is wrong - a correlation of 1.0000003 says the inputs are
/// collinear AND that the arithmetic is at its limit; a clamped 1.0 says only the first.
pub fn correlation(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), b: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const sa: T = try stdDev(T, a, .sample);
    const sb: T = try stdDev(T, b, .sample);
    if (sa == 0 or sb == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    return (try covariance(T, a, b, .sample)) / (sa * sb);
}

variance is two-pass, for layerNormRows's reason. Both it and covariance take a Ddof and return DomainError where the divisor is undefined. zscore and stdErr use the sample deviation, matching R and scipy.

correlation is not clamped to [−1, 1]. Rounding can put it a few ULP outside, and a value slightly beyond the range is a signal about the inputs' conditioning that clamping would erase.

minMaxScale and zscore return DomainError for a constant tensor, which has no range and no deviation to divide by.

12.1 Quantiles, shape and the mean family#

const q = try zn.quantile(f64, a, 0.9, scratch);   // scratch is sorted in place
const mid = try zn.median(f64, a, scratch);
const lean = try zn.skew(f64, a);
const tails = try zn.kurtosis(f64, a);             // excess: normal reads as zero
const g = try zn.geoMean(f64, a);
const hm = try zn.harmMean(f64, a);
try zn.histogram(f64, &counts, a, low, high);
quantile
/// The value below which a fraction `q` of the data lies, by linear interpolation between the
/// two neighbouring order statistics.
///
/// `scratch` must be at least `a.size()` long and is overwritten with the sorted values, so a
/// caller wanting several quantiles sorts once and calls `quantileSorted` for each.
///
/// THE INTERPOLATION RULE IS A CHOICE, AND THIS IS THE COMMON ONE
///
/// `q` maps to position `q * (n - 1)` in the sorted array: q=0 is the first element, q=1 the
/// last, and anything between is a weighted blend of its two neighbours. Nine such rules are in
/// common use and they disagree on small samples; this is the one numpy calls "linear" and the
/// one znum uses, so a reader comparing against either gets the same number.
pub fn quantile(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), q: T, scratch: []T) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0 or scratch.len < n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (q < 0 or q > 1) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    for (0..n) |i| {
        scratch[i] = a.data[a.offsetOf(walker[0..a.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
    }
    std.mem.sort(T, scratch[0..n], {}, std.sort.asc(T));
    return quantileSorted(T, scratch[0..n], q);
}
median
/// The middle value: `quantile(0.5)`.
pub fn median(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), scratch: []T) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return quantile(T, a, 0.5, scratch);
}
skew
/// The third standardised moment: how far the distribution leans.
///
/// Zero for anything symmetric, positive when the long tail is to the right. This is the
/// POPULATION form, dividing by `n` and by the population deviation cubed - the same as znum's,
/// and the same as what most plotting libraries show. The sample-corrected form multiplies by
/// `sqrt(n(n-1))/(n-2)` and matters below about twenty points.
pub fn skew(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return standardisedMoment(T, a, 3);
}
kurtosis
/// The fourth standardised moment, minus three: how heavy the tails are.
///
/// Three is subtracted so that a normal distribution reads as ZERO rather than as three, which
/// is what "excess kurtosis" means and what every reader expects when a number is called
/// kurtosis. Positive is heavier-tailed than normal.
pub fn kurtosis(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return (try standardisedMoment(T, a, 4)) - 3;
}
geoMean
/// The geometric mean: `exp(mean(log x))`. Every value must be positive.
///
/// Written through logarithms rather than as the n-th root of the product, because the product
/// of a few hundred values well inside the float range still overflows it. The logarithm turns
/// the multiplication into an addition that cannot.
pub fn geoMean(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = try a.at(at);
        if (x <= 0) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        total.add(@log(x));
    }
    return @exp(total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n)));
}
harmMean
/// The harmonic mean: `n / sum(1/x)`. Every value must be positive.
///
/// The right average for rates: two legs of a journey at 30 and 60 give a harmonic mean of 40,
/// which is the speed that covers the same distance in the same time. The arithmetic mean of 45
/// answers a question nobody asked.
pub fn harmMean(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const x: T = try a.at(at);
        if (x <= 0) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        total.add(1 / x);
    }
    return @as(T, @floatFromInt(n)) / total.value();
}
histogram
/// Count values into `counts.len` equal-width bins spanning `[low, high)`, with the top edge
/// closed so the largest value lands in the last bin rather than outside every bin.
///
/// Values outside the range are not counted, and `Error.DomainError` if the range is empty.
pub fn histogram(
    comptime T: type,
    counts: []usize,
    a: Tensor(T),
    low: T,
    high: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (counts.len == 0 or !(high > low)) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    @memset(counts, 0);
    const bins: T = @floatFromInt(counts.len);
    const width: T = (high - low) / bins;
    var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
    const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
    var remaining: usize = a.size();
    while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
        const x: T = a.data[a.offsetOf(walker[0..a.rank])];
        advance(shape, walker[0..a.rank]);
        if (x < low or x > high) {
            continue;
        }
        // The top edge is closed: without this, `high` itself falls one past the last bin.
        const slot: usize = if (x == high)
            counts.len - 1
        else
            @intFromFloat(@floor((x - low) / width));
        counts[@min(slot, counts.len - 1)] += 1;
    }
}

Nine interpolation rules for quantiles are in common use and they disagree on small samples. This is the one numpy calls "linear": q maps to position q·(n−1), so q=0 is the first element, q=1 the last, and anything between is a weighted blend of its two neighbours. The test asserts a case that does not land on an element, because an all-integer sample would hide the rule entirely.

kurtosis subtracts three so a normal distribution reads as zero rather than as three — that is what "excess kurtosis" means and what a reader expects from a number with that name. The test pins it against a uniform distribution, whose excess kurtosis is exactly −6/5: a number that merely looked small would not distinguish a correct fourth moment from a slightly wrong one.

geoMean goes through logarithms rather than taking the n-th root of a product, because the product of a few hundred values well inside the float range still overflows it. Three hundred values of 1e5 have a product near 1e1500; the answer is still exactly 1e5.

The three means are tested against each other: harmonic ≤ geometric ≤ arithmetic holds for any positive sample, with equality only when every value is the same. That one inequality checks all three at once, where three separate expected numbers would check none of them against the others.

12.2 Robust statistics, ties, and scales that have no answer#

const spread   = try zn.medianAbsDev(f64, scratch, a);
const relative = try zn.coefficientOfVariation(f64, a, .sample);
const err      = try zn.standardError(f64, a, .sample);
const common   = try zn.mode(f64, scratch, a);
const n        = try zn.valueCounts(f64, &values, &counts, scratch, a);
try zn.correlationMatrix(f64, out, &.{ x, y, z });
medianAbsDev
/// The median of the absolute deviations from the median.
///
/// TWO FUNCTIONS, NOT ONE WITH A FLAG - BECAUSE "MAD" MEANS BOTH
///
/// `meanAbsDev` already sits beside this one. znum has a single `mad` with a `MadKind` selecting
/// between them, which means every call site reads `mad(x, .median)` and every reader has to
/// know the enum. Two names say it without one.
///
/// The median version is the robust one: half the data can be arbitrarily wrong and this barely
/// budges, where a single outlier can dominate a standard deviation. That is the whole reason it
/// exists, so it is the one whose name should be unambiguous.
pub fn medianAbsDev(comptime T: type, scratch: []T, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (scratch.len < a.size()) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const centre: T = try median(T, a, scratch[0..a.size()]);
    var index: usize = 0;
    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| : (index += 1) {
        scratch[index] = @abs((try a.at(at)) - centre);
    }
    std.mem.sort(T, scratch[0..a.size()], {}, std.sort.asc(T));
    return quantileSorted(T, scratch[0..a.size()], 0.5);
}
coefficientOfVariation
/// The standard deviation as a fraction of the mean.
///
/// Spelled out rather than `cv`, because two letters that stand for three words are a lookup.
/// It answers "is this spread large FOR THIS SCALE" - a deviation of 3 means nothing until you
/// know whether the mean is 5 or 5000.
///
/// A mean of zero has no scale to be a fraction of, and that is a `DomainError` rather than an
/// infinity: the question does not apply, which is different from the answer being large.
pub fn coefficientOfVariation(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), ddof: Ddof) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const centre: T = try meanAll(T, a);
    if (centre == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    return @sqrt(try variance(T, a, ddof)) / @abs(centre);
}
standardError
/// How far the MEAN of a sample is likely to be from the mean of the whole.
///
/// The standard deviation shrunk by the square root of the count: more data, steadier estimate.
/// Spelled out rather than `sem`, on the same grounds as `coefficientOfVariation`.
pub fn standardError(comptime T: type, a: Tensor(T), ddof: Ddof) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    return @sqrt(try variance(T, a, ddof)) / @sqrt(@as(T, @floatFromInt(n)));
}
mode
/// The most common value, ties going to the smallest.
///
/// TIES GO TO THE SMALLEST, AND THAT IS A CHOICE THE CALLER SHOULD NOT HAVE TO GUESS
///
/// Two values can be equally common, and "the mode" is then ambiguous. Returning whichever the
/// scan happened to see first makes the answer depend on element order, which is exactly the
/// kind of thing that is reproducible until the day it is not. The smallest is arbitrary too,
/// but it is arbitrary the SAME WAY every time.
pub fn mode(comptime T: type, scratch: []T, a: Tensor(T)) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (scratch.len < n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    try sort(T, scratch[0..n], a);
    var best: T = scratch[0];
    var best_run: usize = 0;
    var run: usize = 0;
    for (0..n) |i| {
        run = if (i > 0 and scratch[i] == scratch[i - 1]) run + 1 else 1;
        // Strictly greater: the sorted order means the first run of a given length starts at the
        // smallest value, so ties resolve downward without a second comparison.
        if (run > best_run) {
            best_run = run;
            best = scratch[i];
        }
    }
    return best;
}
valueCounts
/// The distinct values of `a` and how often each occurs, most common first.
///
/// Writes into `out_values` and `out_counts` and returns how many distinct values there were, so
/// the caller sizes the buffers for the worst case and reads back the truth. Ties in frequency
/// are broken by value, ascending, for the same reason `mode` does it.
pub fn valueCounts(
    comptime T: type,
    out_values: []T,
    out_counts: []usize,
    scratch: []T,
    a: Tensor(T),
) Error!usize {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (scratch.len < n or out_values.len < n or out_counts.len < n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    try sort(T, scratch[0..n], a);
    var distinct: usize = 0;
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < n) {
        var run: usize = 1;
        while (i + run < n and scratch[i + run] == scratch[i]) {
            run += 1;
        }
        out_values[distinct] = scratch[i];
        out_counts[distinct] = run;
        distinct += 1;
        i += run;
    }
    // Insertion sort by descending count, ascending value - stable and short, and the list is
    // the number of DISTINCT values, which is usually far smaller than the input.
    var head: usize = 1;
    while (head < distinct) : (head += 1) {
        const value: T = out_values[head];
        const times: usize = out_counts[head];
        var slot: usize = head;
        while (slot > 0 and (out_counts[slot - 1] < times or
            (out_counts[slot - 1] == times and out_values[slot - 1] > value))) : (slot -= 1)
        {
            out_values[slot] = out_values[slot - 1];
            out_counts[slot] = out_counts[slot - 1];
        }
        out_values[slot] = value;
        out_counts[slot] = times;
    }
    return distinct;
}
correlationMatrix
/// Every pairwise correlation between `columns`, written into a square `out`.
///
/// The diagonal is exactly 1 - not computed, ASSIGNED, because a column's correlation with
/// itself is one by definition and letting the arithmetic produce 0.9999999 would invite a
/// reader to wonder. The matrix is symmetric, so only one triangle is computed and mirrored.
pub fn correlationMatrix(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    columns: []const Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.rank != 2 or out.shape[0] != columns.len or out.shape[1] != columns.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..columns.len) |i| {
        try out.setAt(&.{ i, i }, 1);
        for (i + 1..columns.len) |j| {
            const r: T = try correlation(T, columns[i], columns[j]);
            try out.setAt(&.{ i, j }, r);
            try out.setAt(&.{ j, i }, r);
        }
    }
}

medianAbsDev and meanAbsDev are two functions, not one with a flag. "MAD" means both, and znum takes a MadKind to disambiguate — so every call site reads mad(x, .median) and every reader has to know the enum. Two names say it without one, and the median version is the robust one: replacing a single value 1000 and watches the standard deviation multiply by 282× while the median deviation does not move at all. That gap is the whole reason the function exists.

Ties in mode go to the smallest value, and that is a decision the caller should not have to guess. Two values can be equally common; returning whichever the scan met first makes the answer depend on element order — reproducible until the day it is not. The smallest is arbitrary too, but it is arbitrary the same way every time. valueCounts uses the same rule, so the first value it reports is the mode and exactly that rather than the two agreeing by luck.

coefficientOfVariation and standardError are spelled out where znum has cv and sem. Two letters standing for three words is a lookup, and these are read far more often than they are typed.

A mean of zero gives the spread nothing to be a fraction of, so coefficientOfVariation returns DomainError rather than an infinity — the question does not apply, which is different from the answer being large.

One number is worth reading. Four times the data should halve the standard error, and sqrt(96/24) is exactly 2 — but with .sample the measured ratio is 2.032. The gap is Bessel's correction: dividing by n - 1 inflates the smaller sample proportionally more. Asserting 2.0 to a tight bar would have been asserting that Bessel does nothing and 2.032 for .sample and exactly 2.0 for .population, which is the cleaner way to state the law.

correlation takes no ddof. It cancels between the numerator and both deviations; znum takes one anyway and its own doc admits it "genuinely doesn't matter". One fewer decision at the call site is one fewer thing to get wrong.

12.3 factorize, and two ways to number a set#

Suppose a column holds repeated values from a small set — material ids, enemy states, weapon types, the winner of each round. You want to replace each one with a small integer, and you want to be able to get the original back.

const levels = try zn.factorize(f64, &codes, &out_levels, column, .sorted);
// codes[i] is a small integer; out_levels[codes[i]] is the original value
factorize
/// Replace each value with a small integer code, and report the distinct values.
///
/// THE CALLER CHOOSES THE ORDER, BECAUSE BOTH ARE RIGHT AND THE WRONG ONE IS SILENT
///
/// znum assigns codes in order of first appearance, which is pandas' default. My first version
/// here sorted instead and the register row claimed sorting was better. **That was too strong**,
/// and understanding why pandas chose first-appearance is what corrected it:
///
///   - **First-appearance needs only equality.** pandas factorizes strings, tuples and objects,
///     many of which have no total order or an expensive locale-dependent one. Sorting needs a
///     comparison. That is a capability difference, not a preference - though it does not bind
///     here, where `T` is always numeric.
///   - **It is the cheaper pass**, and if you are compressing a column or grouping by it, the
///     code VALUES do not matter - only that equal values share one.
///   - **And pandas offers both**: `sort=True` gives the sorted order. It defaults to the cheaper
///     one that works on more types, which is a reasonable default and not a claim that order of
///     appearance is correct.
///
/// The failure I argued from - train on one shuffle, serve with another - needs the encoder to be
/// RE-DERIVED at serving time. A pipeline that persists the levels, which is what this function
/// returns them for, is reproducible under either order. That was the hole in the argument.
///
/// What survives: factorizing a train split and a test split separately is a real and common
/// mistake, and under `.first_seen` the two halves silently disagree about what code 0 means.
/// Under `.sorted` they agree whenever they contain the same values.
///
/// So neither is a default here. Six other places in this file take a named argument for exactly
/// this reason - `Use`, `Look`, `Push`, `FeedForward`, `Comparison`, `Ddof` - and this is the
/// seventh: both answers are defensible, and choosing wrong produces plausible numbers.
///
/// Returns how many distinct values there were. `out_levels[out_codes[i]]` recovers the input
/// under either order, which is the inverse the encoding exists to have.
pub fn factorize(
    comptime T: type,
    out_codes: []usize,
    out_levels: []T,
    a: Tensor(T),
    order: CodeOrder,
) Error!usize {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const n: usize = a.size();
    if (out_codes.len < n or out_levels.len < n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    switch (order) {
        .sorted => {
            // `unique` sorts into `out_levels` and returns how many are distinct - no scratch
            // needed, which is one buffer fewer than the caller would otherwise have to size.
            const distinct: usize = try unique(T, out_levels, a);
            var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
            var position: usize = 0;
            while (walk.next()) |at| : (position += 1) {
                const value: T = try a.at(at);
                // A binary search, which is what sorted levels buy over a linear scan.
                var low: usize = 0;
                var high: usize = distinct;
                while (low < high) {
                    const middle: usize = low + (high - low) / 2;
                    if (out_levels[middle] < value) {
                        low = middle + 1;
                    } else {
                        high = middle;
                    }
                }
                out_codes[position] = low;
            }
            return distinct;
        },
        .first_seen => {
            // A linear scan over the levels seen so far. A categorical column has few levels, so
            // this stays effectively linear in `n` - znum makes the same observation, and reaching
            // for a hash map would be solving a problem neither of us has.
            var distinct: usize = 0;
            var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
            var position: usize = 0;
            while (walk.next()) |at| : (position += 1) {
                const value: T = try a.at(at);
                var code: ?usize = null;
                for (out_levels[0..distinct], 0..) |level, k| {
                    if (level == value) {
                        code = k;
                        break;
                    }
                }
                if (code) |found| {
                    out_codes[position] = found;
                } else {
                    out_levels[distinct] = value;
                    out_codes[position] = distinct;
                    distinct += 1;
                }
            }
            return distinct;
        },
    }
}

codes gets one integer per input element. out_levels gets the distinct values, one per code, so out_levels[codes[i]] recovers column[i]. The return is how many distinct values there were.

That is useful for three ordinary reasons: the codes index an array directly, they are cheap to compare and store, and a neural network can only eat numbers — so a column of "which of six enemy types" becomes a column of 0 to 5.

The only forced rule, and the one real choice

Forced: equal values must share a code, and the inverse must recover the input. Both hold whatever else you decide.

Chosen: which integer each value gets. Two answers are in common use and neither is wrong:

.first_seen   the value appearing first gets 0
.sorted       the smallest value gets 0

On the same six values in two arrangements:

.sorted      30 10 20 10 30 30  ->  2 0 1 0 2 2      levels 10 20 30
             10 30 30 20 10 30  ->  0 2 2 1 0 2      levels 10 20 30

.first_seen  30 10 20 10 30 30  ->  0 1 2 1 0 0      levels 30 10 20
             10 30 30 20 10 30  ->  0 1 1 2 0 1      levels 10 30 20

Under .sorted the codes depend only on which values are present. Under .first_seen they depend on the order the rows happen to be in — rearrange the data and 30 goes from code 0 to code 1.

That matters if you ever number two batches separately and then compare them: under .first_seen the two can disagree about what 0 means, and nothing will tell you. It does not matter at all if you number once and keep the levels, which is what they are returned for.

.first_seen is the cheaper pass and the one that would work on values with no natural order (text, say). .sorted costs a sort and gives an answer that cannot move. Neither dominates, so the argument is required — this library asks the caller to choose whenever both answers are defensible and picking the wrong one produces plausible-looking numbers. Ddof, Use, Look and FeedForward are the same idea.

If you know pandas

This is pd.factorize. pandas defaults to first appearance and offers sort=True; here there is no default. Its reason for that default is worth knowing: first appearance needs only equality, so pandas can number strings and arbitrary objects, where sorting needs a comparison. That does not bind here, where the element type is always a number — which is why this library asks rather than defaults.

It works at i32 as well as f64 — which is what such a column usually is, and could not have worked before integer element types were widened, because unique refused non-floats.

12.4 Series, and why missing is not NaN#

A Series is one named column of numbers, plus an optional record of which entries are actually there.

const col = try zn.Series(f64).fromSlice(gpa, "price", &.{ 1, 2, 3 });
col.valid == null   // nothing missing, so no mask was allocated
Series
/// One named column, with an optional record of which entries are actually there.
///
/// FOLLOWING znum HERE, BECAUSE ITS LAYOUT IS RIGHT
///
/// `valid == null` means **every value is present**, so an ordinary complete column costs no
/// mask and no allocation. Only a column with a real gap pays for one. That is znum's design and
/// it is the thing to copy: the alternative - always allocating a mask - taxes the common case
/// to make the rare one uniform.
///
/// MISSING IS NOT NaN, AND FOR AN INTEGER COLUMN IT CANNOT BE
///
/// A NaN is a float value that arithmetic produces and propagates. Missing is an ABSENCE: nobody
/// measured it, and a reduction should skip it rather than poison itself with it.
///
/// For floats you can almost get away with conflating them. **For integers there is no NaN at
/// all**, and the value a gap would otherwise take is zero - which is a number someone might
/// believe. The integer widening earlier in this port is what made that concrete: `diff`'s first
/// hole is NaN in a float column and 0 in an integer one, and only one of those announces itself.
///
/// So the mask is the representation and NaN is a source you can convert FROM, which is what
/// `fromTensorMarkingNaN` is for.
pub fn Series(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// Borrowed - whoever made the column keeps the string alive.
        name: []const u8,
        /// Rank 1 and contiguous. The column owns it.
        values: Tensor(T),
        /// `null` means every value is present. Otherwise `valid[i]` says whether entry `i` is
        /// real. **Never allocated unless something is actually missing.**
        valid: ?[]bool,
        /// Who allocated `values.data` and `valid`, so `deinit` can hand both back.
        gpa: Allocator,

        /// A complete column from raw values: no gaps, no mask, one allocation.
        pub fn fromSlice(gpa: Allocator, name: []const u8, data: []const T) Error!Self {
            comptime requireNumeric(T);
            const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{data.len});
            @memcpy(values.data, data);
            return .{ .name = name, .values = values, .valid = null, .gpa = gpa };
        }

        /// A column from a tensor, treating every NaN in it as MISSING.
        ///
        /// The bridge from float data that uses NaN as a gap marker - what `diff` leaves in its
        /// first slot, or what a reader produces from an empty CSV field - into an honest mask
        /// that reductions skip instead of a NaN that would poison them.
        ///
        /// **Only allocates a mask if something is actually missing**, so a clean column stays
        /// in the cheap representation.
        pub fn fromTensorMarkingNaN(gpa: Allocator, name: []const u8, source: Tensor(T)) Error!Self {
            comptime requireFloat(T);
            if (source.rank != 1) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const n: usize = source.size();
            const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{n});
            var gaps: usize = 0;
            for (0..n) |i| {
                const x: T = try source.at(&.{i});
                values.data[i] = x;
                if (isNan(x)) {
                    gaps += 1;
                }
            }
            if (gaps == 0) {
                return .{ .name = name, .values = values, .valid = null, .gpa = gpa };
            }
            const mask: []bool = gpa.alloc(bool, n) catch {
                gpa.free(values.data);
                return Error.OutOfMemory;
            };
            for (0..n) |i| {
                mask[i] = !isNan(values.data[i]);
            }
            return .{ .name = name, .values = values, .valid = mask, .gpa = gpa };
        }

        pub fn deinit(self: Self) void {
            self.gpa.free(self.values.data);
            if (self.valid) |mask| {
                self.gpa.free(mask);
            }
        }

        /// How many entries there are, present or not.
        pub fn len(self: Self) usize {
            return self.values.size();
        }

        /// Whether entry `i` is present. A column with no mask says yes to everything, which is
        /// what `null` means.
        pub fn isValid(self: Self, i: usize) bool {
            const mask: []bool = self.valid orelse return true;
            return mask[i];
        }

        /// The sum of the present entries, and how many there were.
        ///
        /// SKIPPING IS THE WHOLE POINT OF THE MASK
        ///
        /// `sumAll` over the raw values would add whatever sits in the gaps - NaN in a float
        /// column, which poisons the total, or **zero in an integer one, which does not** and is
        /// therefore worse: the answer looks fine and is wrong by however many gaps there were.
        ///
        /// Returns the count too, because a mean needs it and recomputing it means walking the
        /// mask twice. A column with nothing present sums to zero over zero, and the caller
        /// decides what that means rather than getting a NaN from here.
        pub fn sumValid(self: Self) struct { total: T, count: usize } {
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            var seen: usize = 0;
            for (0..self.len()) |i| {
                if (self.isValid(i)) {
                    total.add(self.values.at1(i));
                    seen += 1;
                }
            }
            return .{ .total = total.value(), .count = seen };
        }

        /// The mean of the present entries, or null if none are.
        ///
        /// **Null rather than NaN**: an empty mean has no answer, and saying so in the type is
        /// what stops the absence propagating silently into whatever comes next.
        pub fn meanValid(self: Self) ?T {
            const summed = self.sumValid();
            if (summed.count == 0) {
                return null;
            }
            return switch (@typeInfo(T)) {
                .int => @divTrunc(summed.total, @as(T, @intCast(summed.count))),
                else => summed.total / @as(T, @floatFromInt(summed.count)),
            };
        }

        /// Drop the mask if nothing is missing any more.
        ///
        /// After a fill, a column may be complete again - and a complete column should be back
        /// in the cheap representation, where `valid == null` means "everything is here" and no
        /// reduction pays to consult a mask. znum does this and it is worth copying: **a
        /// representation you can only enter and never leave is a one-way ratchet.**
        fn dropMaskIfComplete(self: *Self) void {
            const mask: []bool = self.valid orelse return;
            for (mask) |present| {
                if (!present) {
                    return;
                }
            }
            self.gpa.free(mask);
            self.valid = null;
        }

        /// Fill every gap with a constant, in place. pandas calls this `fillna(value)`.
        ///
        /// The column is complete afterwards by construction, so the mask is freed rather than
        /// left behind saying everything is true.
        pub fn fillMissing(self: *Self, value: T) void {
            const mask: []bool = self.valid orelse return;
            for (0..self.len()) |i| {
                if (!mask[i]) {
                    self.values.setAt1(i, value);
                }
            }
            self.gpa.free(mask);
            self.valid = null;
        }

        /// Each gap takes the last real value before it. pandas calls this `ffill`.
        ///
        /// **A LEADING GAP STAYS MISSING**, because there is nothing earlier to borrow. That is
        /// not an edge case to paper over: inventing a value for it - zero, or the first real
        /// value found later - would be fabricating data at exactly the point where the series
        /// has none, and the column would stop being able to say so.
        ///
        /// THE VALUE UNDER A MISSING SLOT IS UNSPECIFIED. A fill writes where it fills and
        /// nowhere else, so an unfilled slot keeps whatever it held - the NaN it was built from,
        /// or for an integer column whatever the tensor contained. **Only the mask is
        /// authoritative**; read `isValid` before reading a value, and every reduction here
        /// does.
        pub fn fillForward(self: *Self) void {
            if (self.valid == null) {
                return;
            }
            var carried: ?T = null;
            // `at1`/`setAt1`, not `data[i]` - a `Series` may sit over a STRIDED view, and
            // reading the buffer directly walks the wrong elements. Measured: a column taken
            // from a matrix summed to 21 where the answer was 7.
            for (0..self.len()) |i| {
                if (self.valid.?[i]) {
                    carried = self.values.at1(i);
                } else if (carried) |value| {
                    self.values.setAt1(i, value);
                    self.valid.?[i] = true;
                }
            }
            self.dropMaskIfComplete();
        }

        /// Each gap takes the next real value after it. pandas calls this `bfill`.
        ///
        /// The mirror of `fillForward`, and a TRAILING gap stays missing for the same reason.
        pub fn fillBackward(self: *Self) void {
            if (self.valid == null) {
                return;
            }
            var carried: ?T = null;
            var i: usize = self.values.data.len;
            while (i > 0) {
                i -= 1;
                if (self.valid.?[i]) {
                    carried = self.values.at1(i);
                } else if (carried) |value| {
                    self.values.setAt1(i, value);
                    self.valid.?[i] = true;
                }
            }
            self.dropMaskIfComplete();
        }

        /// A NEW column holding only the present entries, in order.
        ///
        /// Not in place, because dropping rows changes the LENGTH - and a column that shrinks
        /// under its neighbours is how a `Frame` becomes ragged. The caller gets a separate
        /// column and decides what to do with it; `Frame.addColumn` will refuse to put it back
        /// beside columns of the original height, which is the intended outcome.
        pub fn dropMissing(self: Self, gpa: Allocator, new_name: []const u8) Error!Self {
            const kept: usize = self.countValid();
            const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{kept});
            var at: usize = 0;
            for (0..self.len()) |i| {
                if (self.isValid(i)) {
                    values.data[at] = self.values.at1(i);
                    at += 1;
                }
            }
            return .{ .name = new_name, .values = values, .valid = null, .gpa = gpa };
        }

        /// How many entries are actually present.
        pub fn countValid(self: Self) usize {
            const mask: []bool = self.valid orelse return self.len();
            var total: usize = 0;
            for (mask) |present| {
                if (present) {
                    total += 1;
                }
            }
            return total;
        }
    };
}

valid == null means every value is present. An ordinary complete column costs no mask and no extra allocation; only a column with a real gap pays for one. That is znum's design and it is the right one — always allocating a mask would tax the common case to make the rare one uniform.

Two different things that look alike

A NaN is a floating-point value. Arithmetic produces it and propagates it. Missing is an absence: nobody measured that entry, and a sum should skip it rather than inherit it.

For floats you can almost get away with conflating them, because a NaN at least makes the total loudly wrong:

Say a sensor logged five readings and two of them failed:

readings  1, NaN, 3, NaN, 5
sumAll      -> NaN        poisoned, but you can SEE it
sumValid    -> 9 over 3   skipped, so the mean is 3 and not 1.8

For an integer column there is no NaN at all. Whatever sits in a gap is an ordinary number — zero, if nobody filled it in — and summing it is silent:

Now the same shape with a counter rather than a sensor — daily visits, where the logger was down on two days:

visits        10, 0, 30, 0, 50      the two zeros were never measured
raw mean      90 / 5 = 18           plausible, and wrong
meanValid     90 / 3 = 30           correct

An 18 and a 30 both look like reasonable daily averages. Nothing about the 18 announces itself — and "the logger was down" and "nobody visited" are the two readings of a zero that the mask exists to tell apart.

No NaN, no error, nothing to notice. That is the case that makes the mask a requirement rather than a convenience, and it only became expressible here once integer element types were widened — before that a column like this could not exist.

One smaller decision follows from the same thinking: meanValid returns null, not NaN, when nothing is present. An empty mean has no answer, and saying so in the type is what stops the absence travelling onward looking like a number.

12.5 Frame: columns of different types in one table#

var table = zn.Frame.init(gpa);
defer table.deinit();
try table.addColumn(.{ .f64_values = try zn.Series(f64).fromSlice(gpa, "price", &.{...}) });
try table.addColumn(.{ .i32_values = try zn.Series(i32).fromSlice(gpa, "visits", &.{...}) });
Frame
/// A table: named columns of possibly different types, all the same height.
///
/// A RAGGED TABLE IS A BUG, SO `addColumn` REFUSES ONE
///
/// Every column must be the same height. Accepting a short one and letting the disagreement
/// surface later - as a bounds failure in some reduction three functions away - is the kind of
/// thing that costs an afternoon. znum refuses it at the door and so does this.
pub const Frame = struct {
    gpa: Allocator,
    columns: std.ArrayList(Column),

    pub fn init(gpa: Allocator) Frame {
        return .{ .gpa = gpa, .columns = .empty };
    }

    pub fn deinit(self: *Frame) void {
        for (self.columns.items) |held| {
            held.deinit();
        }
        self.columns.deinit(self.gpa);
    }

    /// How many columns.
    pub fn width(self: *const Frame) usize {
        return self.columns.items.len;
    }

    /// How many rows. Every column is the same height, so the first one answers for all.
    pub fn height(self: *const Frame) usize {
        if (self.columns.items.len == 0) {
            return 0;
        }
        return self.columns.items[0].len();
    }

    /// Take ownership of a column and append it.
    ///
    /// Refuses a height that disagrees with what is already there, and refuses a duplicate name -
    /// **two columns called "price" make every lookup ambiguous**, and a lookup that silently
    /// returns the first of two is worse than one that fails.
    pub fn addColumn(self: *Frame, taken: Column) Error!void {
        if (self.columns.items.len > 0 and taken.len() != self.height()) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        if (self.find(taken.name()) != null) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        self.columns.append(self.gpa, taken) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    }

    /// The index of the column with this name, or null.
    pub fn find(self: *const Frame, wanted: []const u8) ?usize {
        for (self.columns.items, 0..) |held, i| {
            if (std.mem.eql(u8, held.name(), wanted)) {
                return i;
            }
        }
        return null;
    }

    /// Give a column a new name.
    ///
    /// Refuses a name another column already has, for the reason `addColumn` does: two columns
    /// called "price" make every lookup ambiguous, and renaming into a collision is the same
    /// mistake arriving by a different door.
    pub fn rename(self: *Frame, from: []const u8, to: []const u8) Error!void {
        const at: usize = self.find(from) orelse return Error.DomainError;
        if (self.find(to)) |taken| {
            // Renaming a column to what it already is should not be an error - it is a no-op,
            // and refusing it would make an idempotent script fail on its second run.
            if (taken != at) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            return;
        }
        switch (self.columns.items[at]) {
            inline else => |*held| held.name = to,
        }
    }

    /// Remove a column and free it.
    ///
    /// The remaining columns keep their order, which matters because a caller who found a column
    /// at index 3 before the drop expects index 3 to still mean something after it. Removing from
    /// the middle shifts the later ones down by one - stated because the alternative, swapping
    /// the last into the hole, is faster and reorders the table silently.
    pub fn dropColumn(self: *Frame, name: []const u8) Error!void {
        // `if (... == null) return` rather than `orelse return`, and not for style: the
        // `useless-error-return` rule reads a bare `return Error.X` STATEMENT as proof the body
        // can error, and does not see one buried in an `orelse`. Its body says so - the check is
        // an approximation and offers `lint:off` for what it misses.
        //
        // Restructuring beats suppressing. The plain form is also what `rename` above uses, so
        // the two now read alike.
        const found: ?usize = self.find(name);
        if (found == null) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        const at: usize = found.?;
        self.columns.items[at].deinit();
        _ = self.columns.orderedRemove(at);
    }

    /// The column with this name, or null.
    pub fn columnNamed(self: *const Frame, wanted: []const u8) ?Column {
        const at: usize = self.find(wanted) orelse return null;
        return self.columns.items[at];
    }
};
Series
/// One named column, with an optional record of which entries are actually there.
///
/// FOLLOWING znum HERE, BECAUSE ITS LAYOUT IS RIGHT
///
/// `valid == null` means **every value is present**, so an ordinary complete column costs no
/// mask and no allocation. Only a column with a real gap pays for one. That is znum's design and
/// it is the thing to copy: the alternative - always allocating a mask - taxes the common case
/// to make the rare one uniform.
///
/// MISSING IS NOT NaN, AND FOR AN INTEGER COLUMN IT CANNOT BE
///
/// A NaN is a float value that arithmetic produces and propagates. Missing is an ABSENCE: nobody
/// measured it, and a reduction should skip it rather than poison itself with it.
///
/// For floats you can almost get away with conflating them. **For integers there is no NaN at
/// all**, and the value a gap would otherwise take is zero - which is a number someone might
/// believe. The integer widening earlier in this port is what made that concrete: `diff`'s first
/// hole is NaN in a float column and 0 in an integer one, and only one of those announces itself.
///
/// So the mask is the representation and NaN is a source you can convert FROM, which is what
/// `fromTensorMarkingNaN` is for.
pub fn Series(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// Borrowed - whoever made the column keeps the string alive.
        name: []const u8,
        /// Rank 1 and contiguous. The column owns it.
        values: Tensor(T),
        /// `null` means every value is present. Otherwise `valid[i]` says whether entry `i` is
        /// real. **Never allocated unless something is actually missing.**
        valid: ?[]bool,
        /// Who allocated `values.data` and `valid`, so `deinit` can hand both back.
        gpa: Allocator,

        /// A complete column from raw values: no gaps, no mask, one allocation.
        pub fn fromSlice(gpa: Allocator, name: []const u8, data: []const T) Error!Self {
            comptime requireNumeric(T);
            const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{data.len});
            @memcpy(values.data, data);
            return .{ .name = name, .values = values, .valid = null, .gpa = gpa };
        }

        /// A column from a tensor, treating every NaN in it as MISSING.
        ///
        /// The bridge from float data that uses NaN as a gap marker - what `diff` leaves in its
        /// first slot, or what a reader produces from an empty CSV field - into an honest mask
        /// that reductions skip instead of a NaN that would poison them.
        ///
        /// **Only allocates a mask if something is actually missing**, so a clean column stays
        /// in the cheap representation.
        pub fn fromTensorMarkingNaN(gpa: Allocator, name: []const u8, source: Tensor(T)) Error!Self {
            comptime requireFloat(T);
            if (source.rank != 1) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const n: usize = source.size();
            const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{n});
            var gaps: usize = 0;
            for (0..n) |i| {
                const x: T = try source.at(&.{i});
                values.data[i] = x;
                if (isNan(x)) {
                    gaps += 1;
                }
            }
            if (gaps == 0) {
                return .{ .name = name, .values = values, .valid = null, .gpa = gpa };
            }
            const mask: []bool = gpa.alloc(bool, n) catch {
                gpa.free(values.data);
                return Error.OutOfMemory;
            };
            for (0..n) |i| {
                mask[i] = !isNan(values.data[i]);
            }
            return .{ .name = name, .values = values, .valid = mask, .gpa = gpa };
        }

        pub fn deinit(self: Self) void {
            self.gpa.free(self.values.data);
            if (self.valid) |mask| {
                self.gpa.free(mask);
            }
        }

        /// How many entries there are, present or not.
        pub fn len(self: Self) usize {
            return self.values.size();
        }

        /// Whether entry `i` is present. A column with no mask says yes to everything, which is
        /// what `null` means.
        pub fn isValid(self: Self, i: usize) bool {
            const mask: []bool = self.valid orelse return true;
            return mask[i];
        }

        /// The sum of the present entries, and how many there were.
        ///
        /// SKIPPING IS THE WHOLE POINT OF THE MASK
        ///
        /// `sumAll` over the raw values would add whatever sits in the gaps - NaN in a float
        /// column, which poisons the total, or **zero in an integer one, which does not** and is
        /// therefore worse: the answer looks fine and is wrong by however many gaps there were.
        ///
        /// Returns the count too, because a mean needs it and recomputing it means walking the
        /// mask twice. A column with nothing present sums to zero over zero, and the caller
        /// decides what that means rather than getting a NaN from here.
        pub fn sumValid(self: Self) struct { total: T, count: usize } {
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            var seen: usize = 0;
            for (0..self.len()) |i| {
                if (self.isValid(i)) {
                    total.add(self.values.at1(i));
                    seen += 1;
                }
            }
            return .{ .total = total.value(), .count = seen };
        }

        /// The mean of the present entries, or null if none are.
        ///
        /// **Null rather than NaN**: an empty mean has no answer, and saying so in the type is
        /// what stops the absence propagating silently into whatever comes next.
        pub fn meanValid(self: Self) ?T {
            const summed = self.sumValid();
            if (summed.count == 0) {
                return null;
            }
            return switch (@typeInfo(T)) {
                .int => @divTrunc(summed.total, @as(T, @intCast(summed.count))),
                else => summed.total / @as(T, @floatFromInt(summed.count)),
            };
        }

        /// Drop the mask if nothing is missing any more.
        ///
        /// After a fill, a column may be complete again - and a complete column should be back
        /// in the cheap representation, where `valid == null` means "everything is here" and no
        /// reduction pays to consult a mask. znum does this and it is worth copying: **a
        /// representation you can only enter and never leave is a one-way ratchet.**
        fn dropMaskIfComplete(self: *Self) void {
            const mask: []bool = self.valid orelse return;
            for (mask) |present| {
                if (!present) {
                    return;
                }
            }
            self.gpa.free(mask);
            self.valid = null;
        }

        /// Fill every gap with a constant, in place. pandas calls this `fillna(value)`.
        ///
        /// The column is complete afterwards by construction, so the mask is freed rather than
        /// left behind saying everything is true.
        pub fn fillMissing(self: *Self, value: T) void {
            const mask: []bool = self.valid orelse return;
            for (0..self.len()) |i| {
                if (!mask[i]) {
                    self.values.setAt1(i, value);
                }
            }
            self.gpa.free(mask);
            self.valid = null;
        }

        /// Each gap takes the last real value before it. pandas calls this `ffill`.
        ///
        /// **A LEADING GAP STAYS MISSING**, because there is nothing earlier to borrow. That is
        /// not an edge case to paper over: inventing a value for it - zero, or the first real
        /// value found later - would be fabricating data at exactly the point where the series
        /// has none, and the column would stop being able to say so.
        ///
        /// THE VALUE UNDER A MISSING SLOT IS UNSPECIFIED. A fill writes where it fills and
        /// nowhere else, so an unfilled slot keeps whatever it held - the NaN it was built from,
        /// or for an integer column whatever the tensor contained. **Only the mask is
        /// authoritative**; read `isValid` before reading a value, and every reduction here
        /// does.
        pub fn fillForward(self: *Self) void {
            if (self.valid == null) {
                return;
            }
            var carried: ?T = null;
            // `at1`/`setAt1`, not `data[i]` - a `Series` may sit over a STRIDED view, and
            // reading the buffer directly walks the wrong elements. Measured: a column taken
            // from a matrix summed to 21 where the answer was 7.
            for (0..self.len()) |i| {
                if (self.valid.?[i]) {
                    carried = self.values.at1(i);
                } else if (carried) |value| {
                    self.values.setAt1(i, value);
                    self.valid.?[i] = true;
                }
            }
            self.dropMaskIfComplete();
        }

        /// Each gap takes the next real value after it. pandas calls this `bfill`.
        ///
        /// The mirror of `fillForward`, and a TRAILING gap stays missing for the same reason.
        pub fn fillBackward(self: *Self) void {
            if (self.valid == null) {
                return;
            }
            var carried: ?T = null;
            var i: usize = self.values.data.len;
            while (i > 0) {
                i -= 1;
                if (self.valid.?[i]) {
                    carried = self.values.at1(i);
                } else if (carried) |value| {
                    self.values.setAt1(i, value);
                    self.valid.?[i] = true;
                }
            }
            self.dropMaskIfComplete();
        }

        /// A NEW column holding only the present entries, in order.
        ///
        /// Not in place, because dropping rows changes the LENGTH - and a column that shrinks
        /// under its neighbours is how a `Frame` becomes ragged. The caller gets a separate
        /// column and decides what to do with it; `Frame.addColumn` will refuse to put it back
        /// beside columns of the original height, which is the intended outcome.
        pub fn dropMissing(self: Self, gpa: Allocator, new_name: []const u8) Error!Self {
            const kept: usize = self.countValid();
            const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{kept});
            var at: usize = 0;
            for (0..self.len()) |i| {
                if (self.isValid(i)) {
                    values.data[at] = self.values.at1(i);
                    at += 1;
                }
            }
            return .{ .name = new_name, .values = values, .valid = null, .gpa = gpa };
        }

        /// How many entries are actually present.
        pub fn countValid(self: Self) usize {
            const mask: []bool = self.valid orelse return self.len();
            var total: usize = 0;
            for (mask) |present| {
                if (present) {
                    total += 1;
                }
            }
            return total;
        }
    };
}

A table is named columns, all the same height, each holding one element type. Zig has no dynamic dispatch, so holding several types in one list means a tagged union — Column.

inline else, so it is not six copies of everything

The usual cost of a union is a switch per operation with one identical-looking arm per variant. inline else removes that:

pub fn len(self: Column) usize {
    return switch (self) {
        inline else => |held| held.len(),
    };
}

That stamps out one arm per variant at compile time, with held bound to the concrete Series. It compiles into "if it is an f64 column call Series(f64).len, if i32 call Series(i32).len" while the source stays one line. znum reaches for the same construct for the same reason.

Two things it refuses

A height that disagrees. A ragged table is a bug, and accepting a short column means the disagreement surfaces later as a bounds failure in some reduction three functions away — the kind of thing that costs an afternoon.

A duplicate name. Two columns called "price" make every lookup ambiguous, and a lookup that silently returns the first of two is worse than one that fails.

Both leave the table untouched, so the rejected column is still yours to free.

What is deliberately not here

znum's Column has a text variant for string columns. This one does not: a string column is not a tensor, its values cannot be summed or scaled, and every numeric operation would need an arm that returns an error. factorize turns categories into integer codes, which is a representation the rest of the library can actually use.

A column with gaps sits beside complete ones and only it carries a mask — one gappy column does not tax the rest.

12.6 Filling gaps, and the one that cannot be filled#

col.fillMissing(0);   // a constant into every gap
col.fillForward();    // each gap takes the last real value before it
col.fillBackward();   // ... or the next one after it
const tight = try col.dropMissing(gpa, "name");  // a NEW, shorter column

A leading gap has nothing to borrow

A sensor that reports every hour but dropped three samples:

_, 2, _, 4, _     fillForward   ->   _, 2, 2, 4, 4     carry the last reading
                  fillBackward  ->   2, 2, 4, 4, _     or the next one

Forward fill is what you want for a sensor: hold the last known value until a new one arrives. Backward fill is for the opposite case — a value that becomes known later and applies retroactively, like a price correction.

The first slot stays missing under a forward fill, and the last stays missing under a backward one. That is the point, not an edge case to paper over. Inventing a value there — zero, or the first real value found later — would be fabricating data at exactly the place the column has none, and it would lose the ability to say so.

Run both and nothing is missing, so the column returns to the cheap representation: the mask is freed rather than left behind saying everything is true. A representation you can enter and never leave is a one-way ratchet.

The value under a missing slot is unspecified

A fill writes where it fills and nowhere else, so an unfilled slot keeps whatever it held — the NaN it was built from, or for an integer column whatever the tensor contained. Only the mask is authoritative.

Worth stating because it is easy to assume otherwise, and to expect the leading slot to read 2.0 after a forward fill, and it reads NaN. Read isValid before reading a value — every reduction here does.

Dropping is not in place

Removing rows changes the length, and a column that shrinks under its neighbours is how a table becomes ragged. So dropMissing returns a separate column, and Frame.addColumn will refuse to put it back beside columns of the original height — which is the intended outcome rather than an inconvenience.

None of this depends on NaN existing. An integer column fills exactly the same way, because the mask is the representation.

12.7 groupBy, and a sentinel worth copying#

Grouping means: given a column of keys, which rows share each value.

const g = try zn.groupBy(i32, gpa, team_column);
for (0..g.groupCount()) |k| {
    const rows = g.rows(k);   // row indices in group k
}
groupBy
/// Gather a column's rows by value.
///
/// Built on `factorize(.sorted)` rather than a hash: the codes are already the group numbers, and
/// sorted order means **the groups do not depend on how the rows arrived**. Two runs over the
/// same data in different orders produce the same grouping, which is what makes their outputs
/// comparable.
///
/// A missing key is its own group - `null` is a value people group by, and dropping those rows
/// silently would lose them. The caller gets `null_group` to find it, or no group at all when
/// every key is present.
pub fn groupBy(comptime K: type, gpa: Allocator, key: Series(K)) Error!GroupBy(K) {
    comptime requireNumeric(K);
    const n: usize = key.len();
    const present: usize = key.countValid();
    const gaps: usize = n - present;

    // FACTORIZE ONLY THE PRESENT VALUES
    //
    // The value sitting under a missing slot is unspecified - that is the rule `fillForward`
    // documents, and it bites here. Handing the raw column to `factorize` counts those leftovers
    // as real keys: a column of `{30, _, 30, _, 10}` whose gaps happen to hold 0 comes back with
    // THREE distinct keys instead of two, and then a fourth group for the gaps themselves.
    //
    // **The test caught this**, which is what asserting a group count is for. Compacting first
    // costs one pass and removes the whole class.
    const compact: Tensor(K) = try Tensor(K).alloc(gpa, &.{present});
    defer gpa.free(compact.data);
    var at: usize = 0;
    for (0..n) |row| {
        if (key.isValid(row)) {
            compact.data[at] = key.values.at1(row);
            at += 1;
        }
    }
    const codes: []usize = gpa.alloc(usize, if (present == 0) 1 else present) catch
        return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer gpa.free(codes);
    const levels: []K = gpa.alloc(K, if (present == 0) 1 else present) catch
        return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer gpa.free(levels);
    const distinct: usize = try factorize(K, codes, levels, compact, .sorted);
    const groups: usize = distinct + @intFromBool(gaps > 0);

    const starts: []usize = gpa.alloc(usize, groups + 1) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    @memset(starts, 0);
    // Count each group, then turn the counts into offsets - the standard two-pass bucket sort,
    // which is stable and needs no comparison beyond the one `factorize` already did.
    var seen: usize = 0;
    for (0..n) |row| {
        const g: usize = if (key.isValid(row)) blk: {
            defer seen += 1;
            break :blk codes[seen];
        } else distinct;
        starts[g + 1] += 1;
    }
    for (1..groups + 1) |g| {
        starts[g] += starts[g - 1];
    }
    const order: []usize = gpa.alloc(usize, n) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    const cursor: []usize = gpa.alloc(usize, groups) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer gpa.free(cursor);
    @memcpy(cursor, starts[0..groups]);
    var placed: usize = 0;
    for (0..n) |row| {
        const g: usize = if (key.isValid(row)) blk: {
            defer placed += 1;
            break :blk codes[placed];
        } else distinct;
        order[cursor[g]] = row;
        cursor[g] += 1;
    }
    const kept: []K = gpa.alloc(K, groups) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    @memcpy(kept[0..distinct], levels[0..distinct]);
    if (gaps > 0) {
        // The missing group has no key value; zero is a placeholder and `rows` is what matters.
        kept[distinct] = 0;
    }
    return .{ .gpa = gpa, .order = order, .starts = starts, .keys = kept };
}

The layout is two arrays. order holds row indices with each group's rows contiguous; starts says where each group begins. Group g is order[starts[g]..starts[g+1]].

Say five play sessions, each on some level:

row     0     1     2     3     4
level   3     1     3     2     1
score  900   250   750   400   300

Grouping by level gives:

order   1  4  3  0  2      row indices, gathered by level
starts  0  2  3  5
keys    1     2     3

group 0 (level 1)  order[0..2] = rows 1, 4    scores 250, 300
group 1 (level 2)  order[2..3] = row 3        score 400
group 2 (level 3)  order[3..5] = rows 0, 2    scores 900, 750

The trailing sentinel

starts has one more entry than there are groups, and the last equals the row count. That makes groupCount() simply starts.len - 1, and it means the last group needs no special case.

Without it, every walk over the groups ends in if (g == last) total else starts[g+1] — the kind of branch that is right nine times and wrong the tenth. znum does this and it is worth copying exactly.

Sorted keys, so a shuffle changes nothing

Groups come out ordered by key, inherited from factorize(.sorted). Two runs over the same data in different row orders produce the same groups in the same order — which is what makes their outputs comparable at all. Within a group the rows keep their original order, because the two-pass bucket sort is stable.

A missing key is its own group

null is a value people group by, and dropping those rows silently would lose them — the row count would stop adding up and nothing would say why. The gap group sorts last, after the real keys.

This is where a bug lived. The first version handed the whole column to factorize, which counted the values sitting under the missing slots as real keys — and those values are unspecified, as the fill section explains. A column with two real keys and two gaps came back with four groups instead of three. The test asserted a group count, the count was wrong, and one compaction pass removed the whole class.

12.8 aggregate, and a type that survives#

Carrying on with the five sessions from the last section:

const best = try zn.aggregate(i32, .maximum, gpa, grouped, score, "best");

level     1     2     3
best    300   400   900      one row per group, in key order
aggregate
/// Reduce every group to one value.
///
/// THE TYPE SURVIVES WHERE IT CAN, WHICH znum'S DOES NOT
///
/// znum's `agg` returns an f64 column for every aggregate. Six of the ten do not need one: a sum
/// of `i32` is an `i32`, and so are the minimum, maximum, first and last. **A count least of
/// all** - it is a count, and past 2^53 an f64 stops being able to hold one exactly, which is the
/// ceiling register row 31 already documents for index tensors.
///
/// `how` is COMPTIME, so asking for a mean of an integer column is a **compile error** naming the
/// problem rather than a silent widening to f64 or a truncating divide. That is the whole benefit
/// of knowing the aggregate at comptime, and it costs the caller nothing: `how` is a literal at
/// every call site anyone would write.
///
/// Missing values are skipped, not counted and not treated as zero - the same rule `sumValid`
/// follows, for the same reason.
pub fn aggregate(
    comptime T: type,
    comptime how: Aggregate,
    gpa: Allocator,
    grouped: anytype,
    values: Series(T),
    name: []const u8,
) Error!Series(T) {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    comptime {
        if (how.needsFloat() and @typeInfo(T) != .float) {
            @compileError("aggregate: ." ++ @tagName(how) ++
                " has no answer in an integer type - use an f64 column, or .sum with your own divide");
        }
    }
    const groups: usize = grouped.groupCount();
    const out: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{groups});
    // A group with nothing present has no answer, so it is MISSING rather than zero - which is
    // the distinction `Series` exists to keep, and an aggregate is the first place it would be
    // tempting to lose it.
    var mask: ?[]bool = null;
    for (0..groups) |g| {
        const rows: []const usize = grouped.rows(g);
        var seen: usize = 0;
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        var low: T = zm.highest(T);
        var high: T = zm.lowest(T);
        var first_seen: T = 0;
        var last_seen: T = 0;
        for (rows) |row| {
            if (!values.isValid(row)) {
                continue;
            }
            const x: T = values.values.at1(row);
            if (seen == 0) {
                first_seen = x;
            }
            last_seen = x;
            total.add(x);
            low = @min(low, x);
            high = @max(high, x);
            seen += 1;
        }
        if (seen == 0) {
            if (mask == null) {
                mask = gpa.alloc(bool, groups) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
                @memset(mask.?, true);
            }
            mask.?[g] = false;
            out.data[g] = 0;
            continue;
        }
        out.data[g] = switch (how) {
            .count => @intCast(seen),
            .sum => total.value(),
            .minimum => low,
            .maximum => high,
            .first => first_seen,
            .last => last_seen,
            .mean => total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(seen)),
        };
    }
    return .{ .name = name, .values = out, .valid = mask, .gpa = gpa };
}

best is a Series(i32), not a Series(f64) — which is the point of this section.

Six of ten aggregates do not need a float

A sum of i32 is an i32. So are the minimum, maximum, first and last. And a count least of all — it is a count, and past 253 an f64 cannot hold one exactly, which is the same ceiling the index-tensor work already ran into.

znum returns an f64 column for every aggregate. Keeping the type means no cast at the call site and no silent widening in the middle.

A mean of an integer column is a compile error

aggregate: .mean has no answer in an integer type
           - use an f64 column, or .sum with your own divide

how is a comptime parameter precisely so the compiler can say this. The alternatives are a truncating divide (wrong, silently) or a widen to f64 (right, but now every aggregate returns a float to serve one case). It costs the caller nothing — how is a literal at every call site anyone would write.

An empty group has no answer

Missing values are skipped, not counted and not treated as zero. If a group has nothing present, the result is missing rather than zero — a zero there is indistinguishable from a real total of zero, and an aggregate is the first place it would be tempting to lose that distinction.

So the aggregate is itself a Series with a mask, and a reduction over it skips the empty groups in turn. The absence carries.

What is deliberately absent

No variance, stdDev or median. Each needs more than one pass — a second once the mean is known, or a sort. An enum variant that always returns an error is worse than an absent one: it looks available, and the caller finds out at runtime. grouped.rows(g) gives the row indices, so those are three lines at the call site using functions that already exist.

12.9 join, and the null that matches nothing#

const pairs = try zn.joinRows(i32, gpa, left_ids, right_ids, .inner);
// each pair is { .left = row, .right = row }, or RowPair.no_row on the unmatched side
joinRows
/// The row pairs of a join, without building the result table.
///
/// WHY THE PAIRING IS SEPARATE FROM THE GATHER
///
/// znum's `join` returns a whole `Frame`: it matches the keys, then materialises every column
/// from both sides. That is the useful end product and it bundles two different jobs.
///
/// The matching is where the semantics live - which rows pair, what `outer` means, where the
/// unmatched go. The gather is a mechanical copy. **Splitting them means the interesting half
/// can be tested directly**, and a caller who wants three columns out of forty does not pay to
/// materialise the other thirty-seven.
///
/// Rows come out in left order, then the right-only rows - which is znum's order and pandas',
/// so a reader can compare an answer against either.
///
/// A MISSING KEY NEVER MATCHES, not even another missing key. Two rows that both failed to
/// record a value are not thereby the same row, and SQL agrees: `NULL = NULL` is not true. They
/// still appear as unmatched rows under `left`, `right` and `outer`.
pub fn joinRows(
    comptime K: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    left_key: Series(K),
    right_key: Series(K),
    how: JoinHow,
) Error![]RowPair {
    comptime requireNumeric(K);
    var pairs: std.ArrayList(RowPair) = .empty;
    errdefer pairs.deinit(gpa);

    // Group the right side once, so each left row costs a lookup rather than a scan. That turns
    // a quadratic join into `n log n` for the grouping plus a binary search per row.
    const right_groups: GroupBy(K) = try groupBy(K, gpa, right_key);
    defer right_groups.deinit();
    const gaps_on_right: bool = right_key.countValid() < right_key.len();
    const real_groups: usize = right_groups.groupCount() - @intFromBool(gaps_on_right);

    const matched: []bool = gpa.alloc(bool, right_key.len()) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer gpa.free(matched);
    @memset(matched, false);

    for (0..left_key.len()) |row| {
        var found: bool = false;
        if (left_key.isValid(row)) {
            const wanted: K = left_key.values.at1(row);
            // The groups are sorted by key, so this is a binary search.
            var low: usize = 0;
            var high: usize = real_groups;
            while (low < high) {
                const middle: usize = low + (high - low) / 2;
                if (right_groups.keys[middle] < wanted) {
                    low = middle + 1;
                } else {
                    high = middle;
                }
            }
            if (low < real_groups and right_groups.keys[low] == wanted) {
                for (right_groups.rows(low)) |other| {
                    pairs.append(gpa, .{ .left = row, .right = other }) catch
                        return Error.OutOfMemory;
                    matched[other] = true;
                    found = true;
                }
            }
        }
        if (!found and (how == .left or how == .outer)) {
            pairs.append(gpa, .{ .left = row, .right = RowPair.no_row }) catch
                return Error.OutOfMemory;
        }
    }

    if (how == .right or how == .outer) {
        for (0..right_key.len()) |other| {
            if (!matched[other]) {
                pairs.append(gpa, .{ .left = RowPair.no_row, .right = other }) catch
                    return Error.OutOfMemory;
            }
        }
    }
    return pairs.toOwnedSlice(gpa) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
}

The pairing is separate from the gather

znum's join returns a whole Frame: it matches the keys, then materialises every column from both sides. That is the useful end product, and it bundles two different jobs.

The matching is where the semantics live — which rows pair, what outer means, where the unmatched go. The gather is a mechanical copy. Splitting them means the interesting half can be tested directly, and a caller who wants three columns out of forty does not pay to materialise the other thirty-seven.

A duplicate key multiplies rows

A table of players and a table of their purchases:

players           purchases
row  id           row  id   item
 0   10            0   20   sword
 1   20            1   30   shield
 2   30            2   30   potion      <- player 30 bought twice
                   3   40   elixir      <- a purchase with no player row
inner  (1,0) (2,1) (2,2)      three rows from three-plus-four

Player 20 pairs once. Player 30 pairs twice, because they bought two things. Player 10 bought nothing and purchase 40 has no player, so neither appears in an inner join.

Left row 2 appears twice, because the key 30 is on the right twice. This is the property that surprises people and the reason a join can return more rows than either input had. Rows come out in left order, then the right-only rows — znum's order and pandas'.

A missing key matches nothing, not even another missing key

Two rows that both failed to record a value are not thereby the same row. SQL agrees: NULL = NULL is not true.

The trap is that the raw values under two missing slots may well be equal — both zero, say — and a join that compared values rather than consulting the mask would happily pair them. The test uses exactly that case: two columns with a missing first entry whose underlying value is 0 in both. They do not match, and under outer they appear as two separate unmatched rows.

Assembling the result

The pairs feed takeRows, which is the one gather engine: hand it a list of row indices and it produces a column. Filtering is a shorter list, sorting is a reordered one, sampling is a random one, and a join's assembly is the pair list — four operations, one loop. znum makes the same observation, and it is what keeps a dataframe library from growing five copies of the same code, four of which eventually differ.

const pairs = try zn.joinRows(i32, gpa, left, right, .left);
// ... split the pairs into two index lists ...
const joined_left  = try zn.takeRows(i32, gpa, left, left_side);
const joined_right = try zn.takeRows(i32, gpa, right, right_side);
joinRows
/// The row pairs of a join, without building the result table.
///
/// WHY THE PAIRING IS SEPARATE FROM THE GATHER
///
/// znum's `join` returns a whole `Frame`: it matches the keys, then materialises every column
/// from both sides. That is the useful end product and it bundles two different jobs.
///
/// The matching is where the semantics live - which rows pair, what `outer` means, where the
/// unmatched go. The gather is a mechanical copy. **Splitting them means the interesting half
/// can be tested directly**, and a caller who wants three columns out of forty does not pay to
/// materialise the other thirty-seven.
///
/// Rows come out in left order, then the right-only rows - which is znum's order and pandas',
/// so a reader can compare an answer against either.
///
/// A MISSING KEY NEVER MATCHES, not even another missing key. Two rows that both failed to
/// record a value are not thereby the same row, and SQL agrees: `NULL = NULL` is not true. They
/// still appear as unmatched rows under `left`, `right` and `outer`.
pub fn joinRows(
    comptime K: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    left_key: Series(K),
    right_key: Series(K),
    how: JoinHow,
) Error![]RowPair {
    comptime requireNumeric(K);
    var pairs: std.ArrayList(RowPair) = .empty;
    errdefer pairs.deinit(gpa);

    // Group the right side once, so each left row costs a lookup rather than a scan. That turns
    // a quadratic join into `n log n` for the grouping plus a binary search per row.
    const right_groups: GroupBy(K) = try groupBy(K, gpa, right_key);
    defer right_groups.deinit();
    const gaps_on_right: bool = right_key.countValid() < right_key.len();
    const real_groups: usize = right_groups.groupCount() - @intFromBool(gaps_on_right);

    const matched: []bool = gpa.alloc(bool, right_key.len()) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    defer gpa.free(matched);
    @memset(matched, false);

    for (0..left_key.len()) |row| {
        var found: bool = false;
        if (left_key.isValid(row)) {
            const wanted: K = left_key.values.at1(row);
            // The groups are sorted by key, so this is a binary search.
            var low: usize = 0;
            var high: usize = real_groups;
            while (low < high) {
                const middle: usize = low + (high - low) / 2;
                if (right_groups.keys[middle] < wanted) {
                    low = middle + 1;
                } else {
                    high = middle;
                }
            }
            if (low < real_groups and right_groups.keys[low] == wanted) {
                for (right_groups.rows(low)) |other| {
                    pairs.append(gpa, .{ .left = row, .right = other }) catch
                        return Error.OutOfMemory;
                    matched[other] = true;
                    found = true;
                }
            }
        }
        if (!found and (how == .left or how == .outer)) {
            pairs.append(gpa, .{ .left = row, .right = RowPair.no_row }) catch
                return Error.OutOfMemory;
        }
    }

    if (how == .right or how == .outer) {
        for (0..right_key.len()) |other| {
            if (!matched[other]) {
                pairs.append(gpa, .{ .left = RowPair.no_row, .right = other }) catch
                    return Error.OutOfMemory;
            }
        }
    }
