    return pairs.toOwnedSlice(gpa) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
}
takeRows
/// A new column holding the named rows, in the order given.
///
/// Indices may repeat and may reorder freely - that is what makes one function serve filtering,
/// sorting, sampling and a join's gather. `RowPair.no_row` produces a MISSING entry, which is
/// what an unmatched join row needs.
pub fn takeRows(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    column: Series(T),
    wanted: []const usize,
) Error!Series(T) {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{wanted.len});
    var mask: ?[]bool = null;
    errdefer gpa.free(values.data);
    for (wanted, 0..) |row, at| {
        // A GAP IN THE SOURCE STAYS A GAP, and so does a `no_row`. Two different reasons for an
        // absent value - "this row had none" and "there was no row" - and the result cannot tell
        // them apart, which is correct: downstream neither is a number.
        const absent: bool = row == RowPair.no_row or !column.isValid(row);
        if (absent) {
            if (mask == null) {
                mask = gpa.alloc(bool, wanted.len) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
                @memset(mask.?, true);
            }
            mask.?[at] = false;
            values.data[at] = 0;
        } else {
            values.data[at] = column.values.at1(row);
        }
    }
    return .{ .name = column.name, .values = values, .valid = mask, .gpa = gpa };
}

no_row in the list produces a missing entry, which is exactly what an unmatched row needs. So a left join comes out with every left value present and gaps on the right — and the gaps are real gaps, skipped by every reduction rather than zeros to filter later.

firstRows is the same engine with the list 0, 1, ... n. It is not called head: that name is a local variable in three algorithms in this file and a field in Attention, and pandas' spelling is not worth shadowing three working functions for.

One structural note

The right side is grouped once, so each left row costs a binary search rather than a scan — n log n instead of quadratic. And no_row is a sentinel rather than ?usize: the pairs are a dense array walked in a tight loop, and an optional would double its size for a case that is rare in an inner join and absent in most others.

12.10 Precision and recall pull in opposite directions#

try zn.perClassMetrics(f64, &metrics, confusion_matrix);
// metrics[k] = { precision, recall, f1, support }
perClassMetrics
/// Fill `out` with one `PerClass` per class, from a confusion matrix.
pub fn perClassMetrics(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []PerClass(T),
    matrix: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (matrix.rank != 2 or matrix.shape[0] != matrix.shape[1]) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const classes: usize = matrix.shape[0];
    if (out.len != classes) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (0..classes) |k| {
        const hit: T = matrix.at2(k, k);
        var row: T = 0;
        var column: T = 0;
        for (0..classes) |j| {
            row += matrix.at2(k, j);
            column += matrix.at2(j, k);
        }
        const precision: T = if (column == 0) 0 else hit / column;
        const recall: T = if (row == 0) 0 else hit / row;
        // The HARMONIC mean, which is why F1 is not just an average: it is dragged down by the
        // smaller of the two, so a model cannot buy a good F1 with one good half. An arithmetic
        // mean of precision 1 and recall 0 is 0.5; the harmonic mean is 0.
        const f1: T = if (precision + recall == 0) 0 else 2 * precision * recall / (precision + recall);
        out[k] = .{
            .precision = precision,
            .recall = recall,
            .f1 = f1,
            .support = @intFromFloat(@round(row)),
        };
    }
}

With the matrix oriented true-down and predicted-across:

precision = M[k][k] / COLUMN k    of everything called k, how much was k
recall    = M[k][k] / ROW k       of everything that WAS k, how much was found

The asymmetry is the point

Ten instances, five of each class. A classifier that over-predicts class 1 — calling it for eight of the ten:

            recall   precision
class 1      1.0       0.625     found every 1, but 3 of its 8 were not
class 0      0.4       1.0        only says 0 when sure, so misses 3

A classifier that predicts k for everything has perfect recall on k and terrible precision. One that predicts k only when certain has the reverse. Reporting one without the other is how a model looks good.

Precision is a column sum and recall a row sum. Swapping them gives plausible numbers on a symmetric matrix and wrong ones on a lopsided one.

F1 is the harmonic mean, not an average

precision 1.0, recall 0.4    arithmetic mean 0.70    F1 0.571
precision 1.0, recall 0.0    arithmetic mean 0.50    F1 0.000

It is dragged toward the smaller of the two, so a model cannot buy a good F1 with one good half. The second row is the case that makes it obvious: a classifier that finds none of a class scores zero, not one half.

support tells a measured zero from a blank one

A class the model never predicted has no precision — the question has no instances to ask about. This reports 0, as scikit-learn does, because NaN would poison a macro average and force every caller to filter.

But 0 from no data is not 0 from bad predictions, and support — how many instances truly belonged to the class — is what distinguishes them. A class with support 0 and F1 0 was never tested, and a macro average that includes it is averaging an absence.

n bins have n+1 edges

histogramEdges returns count + 1 values. That off-by-one is what makes a plot's last bar vanish or its axis stop one bin short.

The last edge is set to the bound rather than computed, for the same reason linspace does it: an edge a few ulps below its bound puts a value in no bin at all.

12.11 Shifting and smoothing, where the edges have no answer#

const yesterday = try zn.shiftRows(f64, gpa, visits, 1);
const smoothed  = try zn.rollingMean(f64, gpa, visits, 3);
shiftRows
/// A new column with every value moved `by` rows later, or earlier if negative.
///
/// THE VACATED ROWS ARE MISSING, WHICH IS WHAT THE MASK WAS FOR
///
/// Shifting down by one means row 0 has nothing to take from. That is not zero and not the old
/// row 0 - it is **absent**, and a `Series` can say so. Filling it with zero would put a number
/// where no measurement exists, which is the mistake the whole validity mask exists to prevent.
///
/// The usual reason to shift is a difference against the past: `y - shiftRows(y, 1)` is
/// yesterday's change, and its first row genuinely has no answer. A zero there would read as
/// "no change" rather than "no data".
///
/// Named `shiftRows`, not `shift`: `shiftLeft` and `shiftRight` are the bitwise operations on
/// integer tensors, and one `shift` beside them would be read as a third.
pub fn shiftRows(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    column: Series(T),
    by: isize,
) Error!Series(T) {
    comptime requireNumeric(T);
    const n: usize = column.len();
    const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{n});
    var mask: ?[]bool = null;
    errdefer gpa.free(values.data);
    for (0..n) |row| {
        const from: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(row)) - by;
        const inside: bool = from >= 0 and from < @as(isize, @intCast(n));
        const present: bool = inside and column.isValid(@intCast(from));
        if (present) {
            values.data[row] = column.values.at1(@intCast(from));
        } else {
            if (mask == null) {
                mask = gpa.alloc(bool, n) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
                @memset(mask.?, true);
            }
            mask.?[row] = false;
            values.data[row] = 0;
        }
    }
    return .{ .name = column.name, .values = values, .valid = mask, .gpa = gpa };
}
rollingMean
/// The mean of each trailing window of `window` rows.
///
/// THE FIRST `window - 1` ROWS HAVE NO FULL WINDOW, AND ARE MISSING
///
/// A three-row average cannot be computed from one row. pandas leaves those slots NaN by default
/// and so does this - as **missing**, which an integer column can also express.
///
/// The alternative, averaging whatever is available, produces a value that looks like the others
/// and is computed from fewer samples. On a noisy series the first few points then look
/// artificially smooth, which is exactly the shape a rolling mean is being used to judge.
///
/// A missing value inside a window makes that window's result missing too. Skipping it would
/// silently change the denominator, and a mean over four of five samples is not the five-sample
/// mean the caller asked for.
pub fn rollingMean(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    column: Series(T),
    window: usize,
) Error!Series(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (window == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const n: usize = column.len();
    const values: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{n});
    var mask: ?[]bool = null;
    errdefer gpa.free(values.data);
    for (0..n) |row| {
        var complete: bool = row + 1 >= window;
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        if (complete) {
            const first: usize = row + 1 - window;
            for (first..row + 1) |k| {
                if (!column.isValid(k)) {
                    complete = false;
                    break;
                }
                total.add(column.values.at1(k));
            }
        }
        if (complete) {
            values.data[row] = total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(window));
        } else {
            if (mask == null) {
                mask = gpa.alloc(bool, n) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
                @memset(mask.?, true);
            }
            mask.?[row] = false;
            values.data[row] = 0;
        }
    }
    return .{ .name = column.name, .values = values, .valid = mask, .gpa = gpa };
}

The vacated rows are missing, not zero

Shifting down by one means row 0 has nothing to take from. That is not zero and not the old row 0 — it is absent, and a Series can say so.

Here is why it matters. The usual reason to shift is a difference against the past:

visits      10  20  30  40  50
yesterday    _  10  20  30  40
change       ?  10  10  10  10

The first row genuinely has no answer. A zero there would read as "no change" rather than "no data" — and on a chart those look identical.

Shifting up is the mirror: the last row is the absent one. Shifting past the end leaves nothing present, and that is not an error — "shift by more than you have" is an ordinary thing for a loop bound to produce.

A partial window is missing too

A three-row average cannot be computed from one row. pandas leaves those slots NaN and so does this — as missing, which an integer column can also express.

The tempting alternative is to average whatever is available. That gives a value which looks like the others and is computed from fewer samples, so the first few points come out artificially smooth — exactly the shape a rolling mean is being used to judge.

A gap inside a window makes the window missing

visits     10   _  30  40  50      window 3
smoothed    _   _   _   _  40      only the last window is clean

Skipping the gap would silently change the denominator, and a mean over two of three samples is not the three-sample mean the caller asked for.

Two small Frame decisions

rename refuses a collision — renaming into an existing name is the same ambiguity addColumn refuses, arriving by another door. But renaming a column to its own name is a no-op, not an error: refusing it would make an idempotent script fail on its second run.

dropColumn preserves the order of what remains. Swapping the last column into the hole would be faster and would reorder the table silently — so a caller who found a column at index 3 before the drop still gets something sensible after it.

13. The GPU relationship#

13.1 What a kernel looks like#

Kernels are Zig, compiled to SPIR-V and translated to WGSL. A complete elementwise one:

const k = @import("kompute");

pub const config = k.Config{ .max = 1 << 14, .workgroup = 64 };

pub const Buffers = extern struct {   // last field is the output
    x: [config.max]f32,
    out: [config.max]f32,
};
pub const Params = extern struct {    // must be a multiple of 16 bytes
    count: u32,
    _p0: u32 = 0, _p1: u32 = 0, _p2: u32 = 0,
};

pub const g = k.Globals(@This());
const bx = g.bind(.x);
const bout = g.bind(.out);
const params = g.uniform();

pub fn relu(id: u32) void {
    if (id >= params.count) return;
    bout[id] = @max(bx[id], 0);
}

pub const kernels = [_][:0]const u8{ "relu" };
comptime {
    for (kernels) |name| k.installKernelLean(@This(), name);
}

Uniform blocks are std140-aligned, so Params must be a multiple of 16 bytes; the host refuses a pipeline otherwise. Kernels that use a barrier must read their parameters through g.uniform() rather than a copied context, or the loop bounds derived from them are not provably uniform and the barrier is rejected.

13.2 Where the two backends differ#

SituationCPUGPU
A strided or permuted operandFree — at follows the stridesMust be materialised; a kernel indexes from the start of a buffer
A broadcast operandA stride-0 viewStrides passed in the uniform, or materialised
A transposed matmul operandA stride swapA separate kernel with the transpose in its index arithmetic
A domain errorError.DomainErrorNo mechanism; the host must not dispatch the case
sqrt, log below zeroNaNUndefined in WGSL

13.3 Measured agreement#

Every kernel is compared against its zn counterpart on a device, element by element, over 4096 f32 values. Representative worst absolute differences, measured on an Adreno phone:

OperationWorst differenceIn ULP of the row's peak
add, sub, mul, abs, neg, masks, floor, ceil, sign, clamp, lerp, transpose, max0exact
sigmoid, tanh, log, sqrt1.19e-71
gelu, sin, cos1.9e-7 – 2.4e-71–2
matmul (64-term contraction)5.7e-6< 1
atan23.6e-69.6
sum all (one thread)3.8e-53.7
sum all (workgroup tree)7.6e-60.7

Exact agreement occurs where the operation is a selection or a single flop. The 1-ULP figures are the two backends' implementations of a transcendental rounding differently. atan2 is larger because zm.atan2 is std.math.atan2 on the host and a polynomial approximation on the GPU. The two summation figures differ because a workgroup tree is pairwise summation, whose error grows as O(log n) against the single thread's O(n).

A CPU reference checks arithmetic, never synchronisation. The host runs a kernel one thread at a time, so a barrier is a no-op there and a workgroup reduction executed on the host gives the wrong answer by construction. Kernels using shared memory are validated only by running them on a device and comparing against zn.

13.4 A kernel that calls the host function#

Every GPU kernel here has a CPU twin, and the sweep runs both on the same input and compares. Until now the twin was a transcription: the same arithmetic retyped in the kernel file.

A transcription is a second definition, and second definitions drift. Two bugs came from exactly that — a kernel writing past its output buffer, and another dividing by the wrong width — and neither was a logic error. Both were retyping.

const state: zn.CartpoleState(f32) = .{ .cart = ba[base], ... };
const stepped = zn.cartpoleStep(f32, state, ...);
CartpoleState
/// The four numbers a cartpole is. **Named, not positions in a `[4]` tensor.**
///
/// znum indexes `state_data[base + 2]` for the pole angle. Nothing at the call site says which of
/// four slots holds what, and swapping two of them produces a plausible simulation that is wrong.
pub fn CartpoleState(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        /// Metres from the centre of the track.
        cart: T,
        /// Metres per second.
        cart_rate: T,
        /// **Radians** from upright - see the note on `cartpoleStep` for why this one angle is
        /// not in turns like the rest of the codebase.
        pole_rad: T,
        /// Radians per second.
        pole_rate_rad: T,
    };
}
cartpoleStep
/// The cart-and-pole control benchmark: push a cart left or right to keep a pole upright.
///
/// A PURE FUNCTION - THE STATE GOES IN AND THE NEXT STATE COMES OUT
///
/// znum's step takes `state: *Tensor(f32)` and updates it IN PLACE, with a doc comment warning
/// that the tensor "holds the observation for step t only until this returns, so a rollout copies
/// it out BEFORE calling". **That is a footgun stated in prose**: the caller has to remember, and
/// forgetting produces a rollout whose observations are all one step late, which trains and
/// converges to something slightly wrong.
///
/// Returning the next state makes it unforgettable. It also makes the step replayable - the same
/// state and action give the same answer, always - which is what a test needs and what a
/// debugger needs.
///
/// RESET IS A SEPARATE FUNCTION, AND THAT REMOVES TWO FLAGS
///
/// znum folds the reset into the step, so the step needs a `force_reset` parameter, and then a
/// `no_transition` special case because a forced reset produces no reward and no termination.
/// Both disappear when starting an episode is its own call: `cartpoleReset` gives a state, and
/// `cartpoleStep` only ever steps.
///
/// RADIANS HERE, AND THE SUFFIX SAYS SO - BECAUSE THIS ANGLE IS DIFFERENTIATED
///
/// The rest of this codebase moved to turns, and for geometry that was right: a quarter turn is
/// exactly 0.25, and `sinTurns` is exact where `sin(tau/4)` is not.
///
/// **A differentiated angle is the other case.** `d/dtheta sin(theta)` is `cos(theta)` only in
/// radians; in turns it is `tau * cos`, and every derivative in a dynamics system picks up that
/// factor. The cartpole equations are differential equations, so radians are the unit in which
/// they have no constants in them at all.
///
/// The first version of this held the pole in turns and converted the angular acceleration from
/// radians on the way in. That conversion was a place to be wrong, and I was: **the pole fell tau
/// times too fast** and every sign still looked right. Choosing the unit the equations are
/// written in removes the conversion rather than fixing it.
///
/// So: **turns for angles that are drawn, radians for angles that are differentiated**, and the
/// name carries the unit either way. `pole_rad` cannot be mistaken for `pole_turns`.
///
/// The limit is still not a rounded constant. znum writes `0.2095`, which is 12.0035 degrees;
/// `radFromDeg(12)` is twelve degrees exactly as far as the type can express it.
///
/// The dynamics are the standard ones - Barto, Sutton and Anderson 1983 - and the integration is
/// SEMI-IMPLICIT Euler: the position update uses the NEW velocity. Gym's default is explicit
/// Euler, which drifts; znum uses semi-implicit too and is right to.
pub fn cartpoleStep(
    comptime T: type,
    state: CartpoleState(T),
    push: Push,
) CartpoleStep(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const gravity: T = 9.8;
    const cart_mass: T = 1.0;
    const pole_mass: T = 0.1;
    const total_mass: T = cart_mass + pole_mass;
    const half_pole: T = 0.5;
    const pole_moment: T = pole_mass * half_pole;
    const push_force: T = 10.0;
    const seconds: T = 0.02;

    const force: T = switch (push) {
        .left => -push_force,
        .right => push_force,
    };
    // `zm.` because zimrnum has its own `sinRad` and it is a tensor op - the disambiguation the
    // two `lint:off` lines at the top of this file exist for.
    const lean: T = zm.sinRad(state.pole_rad);
    const upright: T = zm.cosRad(state.pole_rad);

    // NO CONVERSION ANYWHERE, WHICH IS THE POINT OF CHOOSING RADIANS
    //
    // These are the textbook equations transcribed, and every quantity is already in the unit the
    // derivation uses. An earlier version held the pole in turns and converted here; that line
    // was a place to be wrong and it was.
    const shared: T = (force + pole_moment * state.pole_rate_rad * state.pole_rate_rad * lean) /
        total_mass;
    const pole_accel: T = (gravity * lean - upright * shared) /
        (half_pole * (4.0 / 3.0 - pole_mass * upright * upright / total_mass));
    const cart_accel: T = shared - pole_moment * pole_accel * upright / total_mass;

    // Semi-implicit: the position uses the UPDATED velocity, which conserves energy far better
    // than the explicit form over a long episode.
    const cart_rate: T = state.cart_rate + seconds * cart_accel;
    const pole_rate_rad: T = state.pole_rate_rad + seconds * pole_accel;
    const next: CartpoleState(T) = .{
        .cart = state.cart + seconds * cart_rate,
        .cart_rate = cart_rate,
        .pole_rad = state.pole_rad + seconds * pole_rate_rad,
        .pole_rate_rad = pole_rate_rad,
    };

    // Twelve degrees, converted rather than rounded - and 2.4 metres of track either side.
    const fall_rad: T = zm.radFromDeg(@as(T, 12.0));
    const track: T = 2.4;
    const failed: bool = @abs(next.cart) > track or @abs(next.pole_rad) > fall_rad;
    return .{
        .state = next,
        // A reward of one for every step survived, which is the standard scoring and makes the
        // return equal to the episode length - so "did it learn" reads directly off the number.
        .reward = 1.0,
        .failed = failed,
    };
}

That is the kernel. It imports zimrnum and calls the same function the CPU calls, because zimrnum compiles to SPIR-V. The sweep row now compares a function against itself.

The opt-in is .wants_zimrnum on the kernel's build entry. It is per-kernel because Zig rejects a --dep for a module the file does not import, so handing zn to every kernel fails on the ones with no use for it.

One detail worth knowing if you touch the build: --dep attaches to the next -M. Declaring -Mzn= without a matching --dep zn before -Mroot= names the module without making it visible to the kernel, and the error — module "zn" declared but not used — reads like the file failed to import it when the root module's dependency list was the short one.

14. Calculus and Fourier#

try zn.gradient(f64, out, values, h);      // central inside, one-sided at the ends
const area = try zn.trapezoid(f64, values, h);
try zn.interp(f64, out, at, xs, ys);        // clamps outside the range
gradient
/// The central-difference derivative of a sampled function, with one-sided differences at the
/// ends. `spacing` is the distance between samples.
///
/// CENTRAL IN THE INTERIOR, ONE-SIDED AT THE ENDS, which is the only choice that gives a
/// result the same length as the input. The interior error is O(h^2) and the two end points are
/// O(h) - stated here because a caller differentiating twice will see the ends degrade first.
pub fn gradient(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []T,
    values: []const T,
    spacing: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.len != values.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (values.len < 2 or spacing == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const n: usize = values.len;
    out[0] = (values[1] - values[0]) / spacing;
    out[n - 1] = (values[n - 1] - values[n - 2]) / spacing;
    var i: usize = 1;
    while (i + 1 < n) : (i += 1) {
        out[i] = (values[i + 1] - values[i - 1]) / (2 * spacing);
    }
}
trapezoid
/// The trapezoidal integral of evenly spaced samples.
///
/// Exact for any straight line, by construction, and that is what the test asserts - a rule
/// that is not exact on its own basis functions is not the rule it claims to be.
pub fn trapezoid(comptime T: type, values: []const T, spacing: T) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (values.len < 2) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var i: usize = 1;
    while (i < values.len) : (i += 1) {
        total.add((values[i - 1] + values[i]) * 0.5 * spacing);
    }
    return total.value();
}
interp
/// Linear interpolation of `(xs, ys)` at each point of `at`, into `out`. `xs` must be strictly
/// increasing; points outside the range are clamped to the nearest endpoint.
///
/// Clamping rather than extrapolating: a linear extrapolation far outside the data is a number
/// with no support, and returning the endpoint is at least a value the data contains. A caller
/// who wants extrapolation can detect the clamp by comparing `at` with `xs`.
pub fn interp(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []T,
    at: []const T,
    xs: []const T,
    ys: []const T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (xs.len != ys.len or out.len != at.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (xs.len < 2) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var k: usize = 1;
    while (k < xs.len) : (k += 1) {
        if (xs[k] <= xs[k - 1]) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
    }
    for (out, at) |*slot, q| {
        if (q <= xs[0]) {
            slot.* = ys[0];
            continue;
        }
        if (q >= xs[xs.len - 1]) {
            slot.* = ys[ys.len - 1];
            continue;
        }
        // Linear scan: `at` is usually short and usually sorted, and a binary search here would
        // be the same speed on the sizes this is used at.
        var i: usize = 1;
        while (xs[i] < q) : (i += 1) {}
        const t: T = (q - xs[i - 1]) / (xs[i] - xs[i - 1]);
        slot.* = ys[i - 1] + t * (ys[i] - ys[i - 1]);
    }
}

Each rule is exact on the basis it is built from, and that basis is what fixes each one: the trapezoidal rule on any straight line, the central difference on any quadratic, linear interpolation on any straight line. A rule that is not exact on its own basis is not the rule it claims to be, and a test against a general function could not tell. The two end points of gradient are one-sided and therefore O(h) where the interior is O(h²); the test asserts they are close but not exact, so a reader knows which points to trust.

14.1 The transform#

const C = zn.ComplexNumber(f64);
try zn.fft(f64, data, -1);     // forward, in place, power-of-two length
try zn.ifft(f64, data);        // inverse, including the 1/n
try zn.dft(f64, out, in, -1);  // the definition, any length
try zn.fftFreq(f64, freqs, spacing);
ComplexNumber
/// A complex number, as a pair.
///
/// `ComplexNumber` rather than `Complex`, which is a reserved name - `zm` has its own, of a different
/// shape. `extern` so an array of them is exactly interleaved re/im, which
/// is the layout every FFT library and every GPU buffer expects.
pub fn ComplexNumber(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return extern struct {
        const Self = @This();

        re: T = 0,
        im: T = 0,

        pub fn add(a: Self, b: Self) Self {
            return .{ .re = a.re + b.re, .im = a.im + b.im };
        }

        pub fn sub(a: Self, b: Self) Self {
            return .{ .re = a.re - b.re, .im = a.im - b.im };
        }

        pub fn mul(a: Self, b: Self) Self {
            return .{
                .re = a.re * b.re - a.im * b.im,
                .im = a.re * b.im + a.im * b.re,
            };
        }

        pub fn magnitude(a: Self) T {
            return @sqrt(a.re * a.re + a.im * a.im);
        }
    };
}
fft
/// The radix-2 Cooley-Tukey transform, in place. `data.len` must be a power of two.
///
/// O(n log n), WHICH IS THE WHOLE REASON THE NAME EXISTS
///
/// A DFT of 4096 points is 16.8 million complex multiplies; this is 49 152 - 341 times fewer by
/// operation count, and **630 times faster measured**: 10 transforms of 4096 points take
/// **2 520 ms** through `dft` and **4 ms** through `fft`. The measured ratio beats the operation
/// count because the DFT calls `sin` and `cos` per element pair while the butterflies compute one
/// twiddle per pair of blocks.
///
/// znum's `fft` is the O(n^2) definition, so this is a deliberate divergence, justified by that
/// ratio and by `dft` remaining available under its own name for anyone who wants the reference.
///
/// THE FAST TRANSFORM IS CHECKED AGAINST THE SLOW ONE, element by element, at every power of
/// two the test can afford. That is the only check that does not compare an FFT against another
/// FFT written by the same hand.
///
/// Decimation in time: the input is first permuted into bit-reversed order, after which the
/// butterflies combine adjacent pairs, then pairs of pairs, and so on. The permutation is what
/// lets every stage read and write the same array.
pub fn fft(comptime T: type, data: []ComplexNumber(T), exponent_sign: T) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = data.len;
    if (n == 0 or (n & (n - 1)) != 0) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (n == 1) {
        return;
    }

    // Bit-reversal permutation.
    var i: usize = 1;
    var j: usize = 0;
    while (i < n) : (i += 1) {
        var bit: usize = n >> 1;
        while (j & bit != 0) : (bit >>= 1) {
            j ^= bit;
        }
        j |= bit;
        if (i < j) {
            const tmp: ComplexNumber(T) = data[i];
            data[i] = data[j];
            data[j] = tmp;
        }
    }

    // Butterflies, stage by stage.
    var width: usize = 2;
    while (width <= n) : (width <<= 1) {
        const step: T = exponent_sign * @as(T, tau) / @as(T, @floatFromInt(width));
        var block: usize = 0;
        while (block < n) : (block += width) {
            var k: usize = 0;
            while (k < width / 2) : (k += 1) {
                const angle: T = step * @as(T, @floatFromInt(k));
                const twiddle: ComplexNumber(T) = .{ .re = scalarCos(angle), .im = scalarSin(angle) };
                const even: ComplexNumber(T) = data[block + k];
                const odd: ComplexNumber(T) = twiddle.mul(data[block + k + width / 2]);
                data[block + k] = even.add(odd);
                data[block + k + width / 2] = even.sub(odd);
            }
        }
    }
}
ifft
/// The inverse transform: `fft` with the opposite sign, divided by `n`.
///
/// The 1/n lives here rather than being split as 1/sqrtn between the two directions. Splitting
/// makes the pair unitary, which matters for a spectrogram and not for a round trip; putting it
/// all on the inverse means the forward transform's bin 0 is the plain sum of the input, which is
/// the convention every reader expects.
pub fn ifft(comptime T: type, data: []ComplexNumber(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    try fft(T, data, 1);
    const scale_by: T = 1 / @as(T, @floatFromInt(data.len));
    for (data) |*z| {
        z.re *= scale_by;
        z.im *= scale_by;
    }
}
dft
/// The discrete Fourier transform by its definition, in place. `sign` is -1 forward, +1 inverse.
///
/// O(n^2), AND IT EXISTS TO CHECK THE FAST ONE
///
/// This is the definition written out: every output bin is a sum over every input sample. It is
/// unusably slow past a few thousand points and it is the only thing `fft` can be checked
/// against that is not another FFT. znum's `fft` is this function; zimrnum keeps it under its
/// real name and adds the fast one beside it.
pub fn dft(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []ComplexNumber(T),
    input: []const ComplexNumber(T),
    exponent_sign: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.len != input.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    const n: usize = input.len;
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    for (out, 0..) |*slot, bin| {
        var re: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        var im: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        for (input, 0..) |x, sample| {
            const angle: T = exponent_sign * @as(T, tau) * @as(T, @floatFromInt(bin * sample % n)) /
                @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
            const c: T = scalarCos(angle);
            const s: T = scalarSin(angle);
            re.add(x.re * c - x.im * s);
            im.add(x.re * s + x.im * c);
        }
        slot.* = .{ .re = re.value(), .im = im.value() };
    }
}
fftFreq
/// The frequency, in cycles per sample-spacing, of each bin of an `n`-point transform. Bins past
/// the halfway point are negative frequencies, as they are everywhere else.
pub fn fftFreq(comptime T: type, out: []T, spacing: T) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = out.len;
    if (n == 0 or spacing == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const denom: T = @as(T, @floatFromInt(n)) * spacing;
    for (out, 0..) |*slot, bin| {
        const signed: isize = if (bin * 2 < n)
            @intCast(bin)
        else
            @as(isize, @intCast(bin)) - @as(isize, @intCast(n));
        slot.* = @as(T, @floatFromInt(signed)) / denom;
    }
}
dft is the definition and fft is the fast transform, and both exist on purpose. dft is the formula transcribed — every output bin a sum over every input sample — unusable past a few thousand points and the only thing fft can be checked against that is not another FFT. The test compares them element by element at every power of two up to 256.

Measured at 4096 points, ten transforms: 2 520 ms through dft, 4 ms through fft — 630×. The operation counts predict 341×; the rest is the DFT calling sin and cos per element pair where the butterflies compute one twiddle per pair of blocks.

ComplexNumber(T) is extern, so an array of them is exactly interleaved re/im — the layout every FFT library and every GPU buffer expects. The whole 1/n lives on the inverse rather than being split as 1/√n between the two directions: splitting makes the pair unitary, which matters for a spectrogram and not for a round trip, and putting it all on the inverse means a forward transform's bin 0 is the plain sum of the input.

Beyond the round trip, this is exact for that a pure tone puts all its energy in bins k and n − k and nowhere else. A transform with a sign error, a wrong twiddle or a botched permutation still returns numbers; it does not return a spectrum with two spikes in the right places.

14.2 Real input#

try zn.rfft(f64, out, signal, scratch);   // out is n/2 + 1, scratch is n/2
rfft
/// The transform of a real signal, into `out`, which must hold `n/2 + 1` bins.
///
/// HALF THE WORK, BECAUSE A REAL SIGNAL'S SPECTRUM IS SYMMETRIC
///
/// `X[n - k]` is the conjugate of `X[k]` when the input is real, so bins past the halfway point
/// carry no information and computing them is waste. The saving is not the discarded half of the
/// output - it is the transform itself: the `n` real samples are packed into `n/2` complex values
/// (`z[k] = x[2k] + i*x[2k+1]`), ONE transform of half the length is run, and the result is
/// untangled into the spectrum. **Half the transform, not a full transform with half thrown
/// away**, which is what znum's `rfft` does.
///
/// THE UNTANGLING IS THE PART THAT CAN BE WRONG, so it is checked against a full `fft` of the
/// same signal, bin for bin, at every power of two the test can afford. A packed transform that
/// got the twiddle or the conjugate wrong still returns a plausible spectrum.
///
/// `input.len` must be even and `n/2` a power of two.
pub fn rfft(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []ComplexNumber(T),
    input: []const T,
    scratch: []ComplexNumber(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = input.len;
    const half: usize = n / 2;
    if (n < 2 or n % 2 != 0 or (half & (half - 1)) != 0) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.len != half + 1 or scratch.len != half) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }

    // Pack: even samples into the real parts, odd into the imaginary.
    for (scratch, 0..) |*z, k| {
        z.* = .{ .re = input[2 * k], .im = input[2 * k + 1] };
    }
    try fft(T, scratch, -1);

    // Untangle. `Z[k]` and the conjugate of `Z[half - k]` separate the transforms of the even and
    // odd sub-sequences; one twiddle recombines them into `X[k]`.
    const step: T = -@as(T, tau) / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
    for (out, 0..) |*slot, k| {
        const a: ComplexNumber(T) = scratch[k % half];
        const b: ComplexNumber(T) = scratch[(half - k) % half];
        // Even part: (Z[k] + conj(Z[half-k])) / 2. Odd part: (Z[k] - conj(Z[half-k])) / 2i,
        // written as a multiply by -i/2 to avoid a complex division.
        const even: ComplexNumber(T) = .{ .re = (a.re + b.re) * 0.5, .im = (a.im - b.im) * 0.5 };
        const gap: ComplexNumber(T) = .{ .re = (a.re - b.re) * 0.5, .im = (a.im + b.im) * 0.5 };
        const odd: ComplexNumber(T) = .{ .re = gap.im, .im = -gap.re };
        const angle: T = step * @as(T, @floatFromInt(k));
        const twiddle: ComplexNumber(T) = .{ .re = scalarCos(angle), .im = scalarSin(angle) };
        slot.* = even.add(twiddle.mul(odd));
    }
}

X[n − k] is the conjugate of X[k] when the input is real, so bins past the halfway point carry no information. The saving is not the discarded half of the output — it is the transform itself: the n real samples are packed into n/2 complex values, one transform of half the length is run, and the result is untangled. Half the transform, not a full transform with half thrown away, which is the obvious implementation.

The untangling is the part that can be wrong — a wrong twiddle or conjugate still returns a plausible spectrum — so it is checked against a full fft of the same signal, bin for bin, at every power of two to 256. Bin 0 and the Nyquist bin of a real signal are themselves real.

14.3 Convolution#

try zn.convolve(f64, out, a, v, .full);    // a.len + v.len - 1
try zn.convolve(f64, out, a, v, .same);    // a.len
try zn.convolve(f64, out, a, v, .valid);   // a.len - v.len + 1
try zn.correlate(f64, out, a, v, .full);
convolve
/// Discrete convolution of `a` with `v`, into `out`.
///
/// CONVOLUTION FLIPS THE KERNEL AND CORRELATION DOES NOT - which is the whole difference
/// between this and `correlate`, and the reason both exist. `conv2d` is named for the
/// framework convention and does not flip; this one is named for the mathematics and does.
pub fn convolve(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []T,
    a: []const T,
    v: []const T,
    extent: ConvMode,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return overlap(T, out, a, v, extent, true);
}
correlate
/// Discrete cross-correlation of `a` with `v`, into `out`. The kernel is not flipped.
pub fn correlate(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []T,
    a: []const T,
    v: []const T,
    extent: ConvMode,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return overlap(T, out, a, v, extent, false);
}

Convolution flips the kernel and correlation does not — the whole difference between the two, and the reason both exist. The test asserts correlate(a, v) == convolve(a, reverse(v)) on an asymmetric kernel, and separately that the two differ on it, so the identity has content. Note that conv2d is named for the framework convention and does not flip; this one is named for the mathematics and does.

Every mode is a window onto full and exactly that rather than checking each mode's numbers independently — an off-by-one in same or valid is the classic error, and a standalone test of either would not see it.

15. Saving and loading#

var file: std.ArrayList(u8) = .empty;
defer file.deinit(gpa);
const names = [_][]const u8{ "l1.w", "l1.b", "l2.w", "l2.b" };
try zn.saveTensors(f64, gpa, &file, &names, &.{ l1.weight, l1.bias, l2.weight, l2.bias });

try zn.loadTensors(f64, file.items, &names, &.{ r1.weight, r1.bias, r2.weight, r2.bias });
saveTensors
pub fn saveTensors(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    out: *std.ArrayList(u8),
    names: []const []const u8,
    tensors: []const Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (names.len != tensors.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (tensors) |t| {
        if (!t.isContiguous()) {
            return Error.UnsupportedShape;
        }
        if (t.rank > max_rank) {
            return Error.UnsupportedShape;
        }
    }
    out.appendSlice(gpa, magic) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    out.append(gpa, format_version) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    try putInt(gpa, out, u32, @intCast(tensors.len));
    for (names, tensors) |name, t| {
        if (name.len > std.math.maxInt(u16)) {
            return Error.UnsupportedShape;
        }
        try putInt(gpa, out, u16, @intCast(name.len));
        out.appendSlice(gpa, name) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
        out.append(gpa, @backingInt(DType.of(T))) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
        out.append(gpa, t.rank) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
        var axis: usize = 0;
        while (axis < t.rank) : (axis += 1) {
            try putInt(gpa, out, u64, @intCast(t.shape[axis]));
        }
        const elements: []const T = t.data[t.base..][0..t.size()];
        out.appendSlice(gpa, std.mem.sliceAsBytes(elements)) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    }
}
loadTensors
/// Read a file written by `saveTensors` into tensors the caller has already allocated, matched by
/// name. Every requested name must be present with the right dtype and shape.
///
/// INTO EXISTING TENSORS, NOT NEW ONES. Loading a checkpoint means filling the weights a model
/// already holds; allocating fresh tensors would leave the caller to copy them across and to free
/// two sets. It also means the shape check is free: the destination states the expected shape,
/// and a file that disagrees is `ShapeMismatch` rather than a silent reshape.
pub fn loadTensors(
    comptime T: type,
    bytes: []const u8,
    names: []const []const u8,
    into: []const Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (names.len != into.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var cursor: usize = 0;
    if (bytes.len < magic.len + 5 or !std.mem.eql(u8, bytes[0..magic.len], magic)) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    cursor = magic.len;
    if (bytes[cursor] != format_version) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    cursor += 1;
    const count: u32 = try takeInt(bytes, &cursor, u32);

    var filled: usize = 0;
    var entry: u32 = 0;
    while (entry < count) : (entry += 1) {
        const name_len: u16 = try takeInt(bytes, &cursor, u16);
        if (cursor + name_len > bytes.len) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        const name: []const u8 = bytes[cursor..][0..name_len];
        cursor += name_len;
        if (cursor + 2 > bytes.len) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        const tag: u8 = bytes[cursor];
        const rank: u8 = bytes[cursor + 1];
        cursor += 2;
        if (rank > max_rank) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        var shape: [max_rank]usize = @splat(1);
        var elements: usize = 1;
        var axis: usize = 0;
        while (axis < rank) : (axis += 1) {
            shape[axis] = @intCast(try takeInt(bytes, &cursor, u64));
            elements *= shape[axis];
        }
        const byte_len: usize = elements * @sizeOf(T);
        if (cursor + byte_len > bytes.len) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        const payload: []const u8 = bytes[cursor..][0..byte_len];
        cursor += byte_len;

        // Is this entry one the caller asked for?
        for (names, into, 0..) |wanted, dest, i| {
            if (!std.mem.eql(u8, wanted, name)) {
                continue;
            }
            if (tag != @backingInt(DType.of(T))) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            if (dest.rank != rank or dest.size() != elements or !dest.isContiguous()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var d: usize = 0;
            while (d < rank) : (d += 1) {
                if (dest.shape[d] != shape[d]) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            @memcpy(std.mem.sliceAsBytes(dest.data[dest.base..][0..elements]), payload);
            filled += 1;
            _ = i;
            break;
        }
    }
    if (filled != names.len) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
}

15.1 The layout#

magic            6 bytes  "ZNUM\0\0"
version          1 byte
entry count      4 bytes  little-endian
per entry:
  name length    2 bytes
  name           n bytes
  dtype tag      1 byte
  rank           1 byte
  shape          8 bytes per axis
  element data   sizeOf(T) per element, row-major, little-endian

The dtype tag encodes the type rather than counting it. The high nibble is the kind (1 float, 2 signed, 3 unsigned) and the low nibble is log₂ of the byte width, so f32 is 0x12 and f64 is 0x13. A reader computes the element size straight from the tag — 1 << (tag & 0xf), which is what byteWidth does — instead of consulting a table that has to be kept in step with the enum, and an unknown tag still says how many bytes to skip. A dense counter would be shorter to write and would make every future type a decision about where in the sequence it goes; this way i32 is 0x22 by construction.

The test pins the header's actual bytes and asserts byteWidth against @sizeOf for all three types, because a round trip agrees with itself whatever the tags are — both sides of it share any mistake. Everything is little-endian at a stated width: @bitCasting a struct into a file would be shorter and would break the first time the padding, the field order or the host's endianness changed, none of which the file records. A reader in another language needs only the table above.

15.2 Loading into a model that exists#

loadTensors fills tensors the caller already holds, matched by name, rather than allocating new ones. Loading a checkpoint means filling the weights a model already has; allocating fresh ones would leave the caller to copy them across and free two sets. It also makes the shape check free — the destination states the expected shape, and a file that disagrees is ShapeMismatch rather than a silent reshape.

Every way the file can be wrong is refused rather than read as something plausible: a bad magic, an unknown version, a truncated file, a requested name the file does not hold, a shape that disagrees, and a dtype that does not match. The test asserts all seven, and asserts the round trip by the model's predictions rather than its weights — comparing weights would pass on a loader that read them in the wrong order into same-shaped tensors.

The source must be contiguous: the file stores elements in order, and a strided view's data is not that order. materialise first.

15.3 numpy's format#

try zn.saveNpy(f64, gpa, &bytes, a);
try zn.loadNpy(f64, out, bytes);
const header = try zn.readNpyHeader(bytes);   // shape, width, where the data starts
saveNpy
/// Write one tensor in numpy's `.npy` format, version 1.0.
///
/// THE ONE FORMAT THAT LETS THIS LIBRARY TALK TO ANYTHING ELSE
///
/// `saveTensors` above is zimrnum's own container and is better at what it does - several named
/// tensors, a version byte, a self-describing dtype tag. But nothing else in the world reads it.
/// `.npy` is what numpy, scipy, PyTorch and every notebook already understand, and it costs
/// eighty lines.
///
/// The header is a Python dict literal in ASCII, padded with spaces so the DATA begins at a
/// 64-byte boundary. That padding is not decoration: it exists so a reader can memory-map the
/// array without copying, and writing it wrong produces a file every other tool rejects.
pub fn saveNpy(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    out: *std.ArrayList(u8),
    a: Tensor(T),
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    var header: std.ArrayList(u8) = .empty;
    defer header.deinit(gpa);
    header.appendSlice(gpa, "{'descr': '") catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    header.appendSlice(gpa, comptime npyDescr(T)) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    header.appendSlice(gpa, "', 'fortran_order': False, 'shape': (") catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    var digits: [24]u8 = undefined;
    for (0..a.rank) |axis| {
        const printed: []const u8 = bufPrint(&digits, "{d}", .{a.shape[axis]}) catch
            return Error.OutOfMemory;
        header.appendSlice(gpa, printed) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
        // A one-element tuple needs its trailing comma or Python reads it as a parenthesised
        // number. numpy writes `(3,)` and expects to read it back.
        if (a.rank == 1 or axis + 1 < a.rank) {
            header.appendSlice(gpa, ",") catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
        }
    }
    header.appendSlice(gpa, "), }") catch return Error.OutOfMemory;

    // 10 bytes of preamble, then the header, then padding, then a newline - all so the data
    // starts at a multiple of 64.
    const preamble: usize = 10;
    var padded: usize = header.items.len + 1;
    while ((preamble + padded) % 64 != 0) {
        padded += 1;
    }
    out.appendSlice(gpa, "\x93NUMPY") catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    out.append(gpa, 1) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    out.append(gpa, 0) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    const header_len: u16 = @intCast(padded);
    out.append(gpa, @intCast(header_len & 0xFF)) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    out.append(gpa, @intCast(header_len >> 8)) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    out.appendSlice(gpa, header.items) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    for (header.items.len..padded - 1) |_| {
        out.append(gpa, ' ') catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    }
    out.append(gpa, '\n') catch return Error.OutOfMemory;

    var walk: Walk = .over(a.shape[0..a.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| {
        const value: T = try a.at(at);
        const bits: [@sizeOf(T)]u8 = @bitCast(value);
        out.appendSlice(gpa, &bits) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
    }
}
loadNpy
/// Read a `.npy` array into `out`, whose shape must match what the header says.
///
/// The width in the file need not match `T`: a `<f4` file read as f64 is widened per element,
/// which is exact. The other direction narrows and is allowed, because refusing it would mean a
/// caller who knows their data fits in f32 cannot say so.
pub fn loadNpy(comptime T: type, out: Tensor(T), bytes: []const u8) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const header: NpyHeader = try readNpyHeader(bytes);
    if (header.rank != out.rank) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var count: usize = 1;
    for (0..header.rank) |axis| {
        if (header.shape[axis] != out.shape[axis]) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        count *= header.shape[axis];
    }
    if (bytes.len < header.data_at + count * header.width) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const data: []const u8 = bytes[header.data_at..];
    var index: usize = 0;
    var walk: Walk = .over(out.shape[0..out.rank]);
    while (walk.next()) |at| : (index += 1) {
        const offset: usize = index * header.width;
        const value: T = switch (header.width) {
            4 => @floatCast(@as(f32, @bitCast(data[offset..][0..4].*))),
            8 => @floatCast(@as(f64, @bitCast(data[offset..][0..8].*))),
            else => unreachable, // readNpyHeader admits no other width
        };
        try out.setAt(at, value);
    }
}
readNpyHeader
/// Read a `.npy` header, refusing everything this library cannot represent.
///
/// WHAT IS REFUSED, AND WHY EACH ONE MATTERS
///
/// - **Big-endian** (`>f8`): the bytes would be read in the wrong order and produce plausible
///   nonsense rather than an error. Every value would be wrong and nothing would say so.
/// - **Fortran order**: the elements are all present and all in the wrong places. A reader that
///   ignored the flag would transpose the array silently.
/// - **Any dtype but `<f4` and `<f8`**: this library is float-only, and an integer array read as
///   floats is not a conversion, it is a reinterpretation.
///
/// Each of these is a case where CARRYING ON produces a result rather than a failure, which is
/// exactly when an explicit refusal is worth its lines.
pub fn readNpyHeader(bytes: []const u8) Error!NpyHeader {
    if (bytes.len < 10 or !std.mem.eql(u8, bytes[0..6], "\x93NUMPY")) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (bytes[6] != 1) {
        return Error.DomainError; // version 2 and 3 use a wider length field
    }
    const header_len: usize = @as(usize, bytes[8]) | (@as(usize, bytes[9]) << 8);
    const header_end: usize = 10 + header_len;
    if (header_end > bytes.len) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const header: []const u8 = bytes[10..header_end];

    const width: u8 = if (std.mem.indexOf(u8, header, "'<f8'") != null)
        8
    else if (std.mem.indexOf(u8, header, "'<f4'") != null)
        4
    else
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    if (std.mem.indexOf(u8, header, "'fortran_order': False") == null) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }

    const open_paren: usize = std.mem.indexOfScalar(u8, header, '(') orelse
        return Error.DomainError;
    const close_paren: usize = std.mem.indexOfScalarPos(u8, header, open_paren, ')') orelse
        return Error.DomainError;
    var out: NpyHeader = .{
        .shape = @splat(1),
        .rank = 0,
        .data_at = header_end,
        .width = width,
    };
    var cursor: usize = open_paren + 1;
    while (cursor < close_paren) {
        while (cursor < close_paren and !std.ascii.isDigit(header[cursor])) {
            cursor += 1;
        }
        if (cursor >= close_paren) {
            break;
        }
        var extent: usize = 0;
        while (cursor < close_paren and std.ascii.isDigit(header[cursor])) {
            extent = extent * 10 + (header[cursor] - '0');
            cursor += 1;
        }
        if (out.rank >= max_rank) {
            return Error.UnsupportedShape;
        }
        out.shape[out.rank] = extent;
        out.rank += 1;
    }
    return out;
}

saveTensors is zimrnum's own container and is better at what it does — several named tensors, a version byte, a self-describing dtype tag. But nothing else in the world reads it. .npy is what numpy, scipy, PyTorch and every notebook already understand, and it costs eighty lines.

The header is a Python dict literal in ASCII, padded with spaces so the data begins at a 64-byte boundary. That padding is not decoration: it exists so a reader can memory-map the array without copying, and getting it wrong produces a file that this library still reads and every other tool rejects. The test asserts the boundary directly, because a round trip through one implementation would never catch it.

Verified against real numpy, not just against itself. Files numpy wrote were read here — including a <f4 widened to f64, and a (1,) shape whose header carries the trailing comma Python needs — and bytes written here were handed back to numpy, which read them as a C-contiguous (3, 4) float64 array with the right values.

Three things are refused, and each is a case where carrying on produces a result rather than a failure:

RefusedWhat would happen otherwise
big-endian >f8every value read in the wrong byte order — plausible nonsense, no error
fortran_order: Trueevery element present, every one in the wrong place: a silent transpose
any dtype but <f4/<f8an integer array read as floats is not a conversion, it is a reinterpretation

16. Reinforcement learning#

try zn.generalizedAdvantage(f64, rewards, values, next_values, terminal, episode_end, 0.99, 0.95, adv, ret);
try zn.discountedReturns(f64, rewards, terminal, 0.99, out);
try zn.normalizeAdvantages(f64, adv, 1.0e-8);
const objective = try zn.ppoClipObjective(f64, logp_new, logp_old, adv, 0.2);
try zn.entropyRows(f64, out, probs);

var ring: zn.ReplayBuffer(f32) = try .init(gpa, 100_000, width);
try ring.push(transition);
try ring.sample(rng, index, batch);
generalizedAdvantage
/// Generalised advantage estimation over one rollout, walked backwards.
///
/// TWO KINDS OF ENDING, AND THEY ARE NOT THE SAME
///
/// `terminal[i]` is 1 where the episode truly ended - the pole fell, the agent died. No value
/// lies beyond it, so nothing is bootstrapped. `episode_end[i]` is 1 where the ROLLOUT was cut -
/// a time limit, a batch boundary - while the episode itself continues: the next state's value is
/// still bootstrapped, but the running advantage sum is reset so it does not leak across the cut.
/// Conflating the two is the most common GAE bug and it produces an agent that learns, slowly,
/// from returns that are wrong at every episode boundary.
///
/// This follows znum's form exactly: `bootstrap = gamma * next_value * (1 - terminal)`,
/// `delta = r + bootstrap - value`, `running = delta + gammalambda * (1 - episode_end) * running`.
///
/// `out_returns = advantage + value` is the target for the value function, which is why both
/// come back from one pass.
pub fn generalizedAdvantage(
    comptime T: type,
    rewards: []const T,
    values: []const T,
    next_values: []const T,
    terminal: []const T,
    episode_end: []const T,
    gamma: T,
    lambda: T,
    out_advantages: []T,
    out_returns: []T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = rewards.len;
    if (values.len != n or next_values.len != n or terminal.len != n or
        episode_end.len != n or out_advantages.len != n or out_returns.len != n)
    {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var running: T = 0;
    var i: usize = n;
    while (i > 0) {
        i -= 1;
        const bootstrap: T = gamma * next_values[i] * (1 - terminal[i]);
        const delta: T = rewards[i] + bootstrap - values[i];
        running = delta + gamma * lambda * (1 - episode_end[i]) * running;
        out_advantages[i] = running;
        out_returns[i] = running + values[i];
    }
}
discountedReturns
/// Discounted returns, walked backwards, reset at each terminal.
///
/// GAE with `lambda = 1` and zero values reduces to this, and the test asserts it: an
/// implementation of one that disagreed with the other would be wrong in a way a single test
/// of either cannot see.
pub fn discountedReturns(
    comptime T: type,
    rewards: []const T,
    terminal: []const T,
    gamma: T,
    out: []T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (terminal.len != rewards.len or out.len != rewards.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var running: T = 0;
    var i: usize = rewards.len;
    while (i > 0) {
        i -= 1;
        running = rewards[i] + gamma * (1 - terminal[i]) * running;
        out[i] = running;
    }
}
normalizeAdvantages
/// Standardise advantages in place: zero mean, unit deviation, with a small epsilon.
///
/// Sample deviation, and an epsilon rather than a `DomainError` for a constant batch: an
/// all-equal advantage batch is a legitimate (if uninformative) rollout, not a caller's mistake,
/// and PPO must keep stepping through it.
pub fn normalizeAdvantages(comptime T: type, advantages: []T, epsilon: T) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (advantages.len < 2) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const t: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).fromSlice(advantages, &.{advantages.len});
    const mean: T = try meanAll(T, t);
    const sd: T = try stdDev(T, t, .sample);
    for (advantages) |*a| {
        a.* = (a.* - mean) / (sd + epsilon);
    }
}
ppoClipObjective
/// PPO's clipped surrogate objective, as a scalar to MAXIMISE. Returns the mean over the batch.
///
/// THE MINIMUM IS THE WHOLE IDEA
///
/// `ratio = exp(logp_new - logp_old)`. The unclipped term `ratio * A` rewards moving the policy
/// toward actions with positive advantage without limit; the clipped term holds the ratio to
/// `[1 - epsilon, 1 + epsilon]`. Taking the MINIMUM of the two is pessimistic in exactly the right way: for a
/// positive advantage the objective stops improving once the ratio passes `1 + epsilon`, and for a
/// negative one once it drops below `1 - epsilon`. There is no incentive to move the policy further
/// than the clip, which is what keeps a PPO update from destroying a policy in one step.
///
/// Follows znum's `clippedSurrogate` term for term. Returned as an objective rather than a loss;
/// negate it for a gradient step.
pub fn ppoClipObjective(
    comptime T: type,
    logp_new: []const T,
    logp_old: []const T,
    advantages: []const T,
    clip: T,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = logp_new.len;
    if (logp_old.len != n or advantages.len != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0 or clip <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var total: T = 0;
    for (logp_new, logp_old, advantages) |ln, lo, a| {
        const ratio: T = @exp(ln - lo);
        const unclipped: T = ratio * a;
        const clipped: T = scalarClamp(ratio, 1 - clip, 1 + clip) * a;
        total += @min(unclipped, clipped);
    }
    return total / @as(T, @floatFromInt(n));
}
entropyRows
/// The entropy of each row of a rank-2 tensor of probabilities, into `out`.
///
/// A zero probability contributes zero, by the limit of `p*log p`; a uniform row gives
/// `log(width)`, which is the maximum and is asserted by the test.
pub fn entropyRows(comptime T: type, out: []T, probs: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (probs.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    if (out.len != probs.shape[0]) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    for (out, 0..) |*slot, row| {
        var h: T = 0;
        var col: usize = 0;
        while (col < probs.shape[1]) : (col += 1) {
            const p: T = try probs.at(&.{ row, col });
            if (p > 0) {
                h -= p * @log(p);
            }
        }
        slot.* = h;
    }
}
ReplayBuffer
/// A ring buffer of fixed-width transitions with uniform random sampling.
///
/// A RING, SO OLD EXPERIENCE FALLS OUT
///
/// Off-policy methods learn from a replay of past transitions, and the buffer must forget: a
/// transition gathered by a policy from ten thousand steps ago says less about the current one
/// than a recent transition does. Overwriting the oldest slot is the simplest schedule that
/// forgets, and it needs no bookkeeping beyond a write index.
///
/// A transition is a flat `[]T` of `width` values - the caller decides the layout
/// (`state ++ action ++ reward ++ next_state ++ done` is usual). The buffer does not interpret
/// it, which is what lets one type serve discrete and continuous problems alike.
pub fn ReplayBuffer(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        data: []T,
        width: usize,
        capacity: usize,
        /// Next slot to write. Wraps.
        head: usize,
        /// How many slots hold a transition; saturates at `capacity`.
        len: usize,

        pub fn init(gpa: Allocator, capacity: usize, width: usize) Error!Self {
            if (capacity == 0 or width == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            return .{
                .data = try gpa.alloc(T, capacity * width),
                .width = width,
                .capacity = capacity,
                .head = 0,
                .len = 0,
            };
        }

        /// Store one transition, overwriting the oldest once full.
        pub fn push(self: *Self, transition: []const T) Error!void {
            if (transition.len != self.width) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            @memcpy(self.data[self.head * self.width ..][0..self.width], transition);
            self.head = (self.head + 1) % self.capacity;
            self.len = @min(self.len + 1, self.capacity);
        }

        /// Copy `out.len / width` uniformly random transitions into `out`, with replacement.
        /// `index` addresses the draw, so the same `(rng, index)` samples the same batch.
        pub fn sample(self: *const Self, rng: Rng, index: u32, out: []T) Error!void {
            if (self.len == 0 or out.len % self.width != 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const batch: usize = out.len / self.width;
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < batch) : (i += 1) {
                const slot: usize = rng.intBelow(index *% 0x9e37 +% @as(u32, @intCast(i)), @intCast(self.len));
                @memcpy(out[i * self.width ..][0..self.width], self.data[slot * self.width ..][0..self.width]);
            }
        }
    };
}

16.1 Two kinds of ending#

generalizedAdvantage takes two flags per step. terminal is 1 where the episode truly ended: no value lies beyond it, so nothing is bootstrapped. episode_end is 1 where the rollout was cut — a time limit, a batch boundary — while the episode continues: the next state's value is still bootstrapped, but the running sum resets so it does not leak across the cut.

bootstrap = γ · next_value[i] · (1 − terminal[i])
δ         = reward[i] + bootstrap − value[i]
running   = δ + γλ · (1 − episode_end[i]) · running     // walked backwards
advantage[i] = running
return[i]    = running + value[i]

Conflating the two flags is the most common GAE error, and it produces an agent that learns, slowly, from returns that are wrong at every episode boundary. The test sets a true terminal at one step and a truncation at another and checks that only the truncation bootstraps. discountedReturns is what generalizedAdvantage reduces to with λ = 1 and zero values, and the two are asserted equal.

16.2 The clip is pessimistic#

ratio     = exp(logp_new − logp_old)
unclipped = ratio · A
clipped   = clamp(ratio, 1 − ε, 1 + ε) · A
objective = mean(min(unclipped, clipped))

For a positive advantage the objective stops improving once the ratio passes 1 + ε; for a negative one, once it drops below 1 − ε. There is no incentive to move the policy further than the clip, which is what keeps one update from destroying a policy. Moving the wrong way is never clipped away: a ratio of 5 with a negative advantage is penalised in full. It is returned as an objective to maximise; negate it for a gradient step.

16.3 A ring that forgets#

ReplayBuffer stores fixed-width transitions and overwrites the oldest once full. A transition gathered ten thousand steps ago says less about the current policy than a recent one, and overwriting the oldest slot is the simplest schedule that forgets. A transition is a flat slice whose layout the caller decides — the buffer does not interpret it, which lets one type serve discrete and continuous problems. sample is addressable: the same (rng, index) yields the same batch.

16.4 Cartpole, and a unit error with no symptom#

var s = zn.cartpoleReset(f64, rng, episode);
const step = zn.cartpoleStep(f64, s, .right);
// step.state, step.reward, step.failed
cartpoleReset
/// A fresh cartpole, every value drawn uniformly from a small band around upright.
///
/// The band is the standard one. Starting exactly upright would make the first few steps
/// deterministic and let a policy memorise them.
pub fn cartpoleReset(comptime T: type, rng: Rng, episode: u32) CartpoleState(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const band: T = 0.05;
    const stream: Rng = rng.split(episode);
    return .{
        .cart = (stream.unitFloat(T, 0) - 0.5) * 2 * band,
        .cart_rate = (stream.unitFloat(T, 1) - 0.5) * 2 * band,
        // The band is the standard one and is quoted in radians, like the pole's angle.
        .pole_rad = (stream.unitFloat(T, 2) - 0.5) * 2 * band,
        .pole_rate_rad = (stream.unitFloat(T, 3) - 0.5) * 2 * band,
    };
}
cartpoleStep
/// The cart-and-pole control benchmark: push a cart left or right to keep a pole upright.
///
/// A PURE FUNCTION - THE STATE GOES IN AND THE NEXT STATE COMES OUT
///
/// znum's step takes `state: *Tensor(f32)` and updates it IN PLACE, with a doc comment warning
/// that the tensor "holds the observation for step t only until this returns, so a rollout copies
/// it out BEFORE calling". **That is a footgun stated in prose**: the caller has to remember, and
/// forgetting produces a rollout whose observations are all one step late, which trains and
/// converges to something slightly wrong.
///
/// Returning the next state makes it unforgettable. It also makes the step replayable - the same
/// state and action give the same answer, always - which is what a test needs and what a
/// debugger needs.
///
/// RESET IS A SEPARATE FUNCTION, AND THAT REMOVES TWO FLAGS
///
/// znum folds the reset into the step, so the step needs a `force_reset` parameter, and then a
/// `no_transition` special case because a forced reset produces no reward and no termination.
/// Both disappear when starting an episode is its own call: `cartpoleReset` gives a state, and
/// `cartpoleStep` only ever steps.
///
/// RADIANS HERE, AND THE SUFFIX SAYS SO - BECAUSE THIS ANGLE IS DIFFERENTIATED
///
/// The rest of this codebase moved to turns, and for geometry that was right: a quarter turn is
/// exactly 0.25, and `sinTurns` is exact where `sin(tau/4)` is not.
///
/// **A differentiated angle is the other case.** `d/dtheta sin(theta)` is `cos(theta)` only in
/// radians; in turns it is `tau * cos`, and every derivative in a dynamics system picks up that
/// factor. The cartpole equations are differential equations, so radians are the unit in which
/// they have no constants in them at all.
///
/// The first version of this held the pole in turns and converted the angular acceleration from
/// radians on the way in. That conversion was a place to be wrong, and I was: **the pole fell tau
/// times too fast** and every sign still looked right. Choosing the unit the equations are
/// written in removes the conversion rather than fixing it.
///
/// So: **turns for angles that are drawn, radians for angles that are differentiated**, and the
/// name carries the unit either way. `pole_rad` cannot be mistaken for `pole_turns`.
///
/// The limit is still not a rounded constant. znum writes `0.2095`, which is 12.0035 degrees;
/// `radFromDeg(12)` is twelve degrees exactly as far as the type can express it.
///
/// The dynamics are the standard ones - Barto, Sutton and Anderson 1983 - and the integration is
/// SEMI-IMPLICIT Euler: the position update uses the NEW velocity. Gym's default is explicit
/// Euler, which drifts; znum uses semi-implicit too and is right to.
pub fn cartpoleStep(
    comptime T: type,
    state: CartpoleState(T),
    push: Push,
) CartpoleStep(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const gravity: T = 9.8;
    const cart_mass: T = 1.0;
    const pole_mass: T = 0.1;
    const total_mass: T = cart_mass + pole_mass;
    const half_pole: T = 0.5;
    const pole_moment: T = pole_mass * half_pole;
    const push_force: T = 10.0;
    const seconds: T = 0.02;

    const force: T = switch (push) {
        .left => -push_force,
        .right => push_force,
    };
    // `zm.` because zimrnum has its own `sinRad` and it is a tensor op - the disambiguation the
    // two `lint:off` lines at the top of this file exist for.
    const lean: T = zm.sinRad(state.pole_rad);
    const upright: T = zm.cosRad(state.pole_rad);

    // NO CONVERSION ANYWHERE, WHICH IS THE POINT OF CHOOSING RADIANS
    //
    // These are the textbook equations transcribed, and every quantity is already in the unit the
    // derivation uses. An earlier version held the pole in turns and converted here; that line
    // was a place to be wrong and it was.
    const shared: T = (force + pole_moment * state.pole_rate_rad * state.pole_rate_rad * lean) /
        total_mass;
    const pole_accel: T = (gravity * lean - upright * shared) /
        (half_pole * (4.0 / 3.0 - pole_mass * upright * upright / total_mass));
    const cart_accel: T = shared - pole_moment * pole_accel * upright / total_mass;

    // Semi-implicit: the position uses the UPDATED velocity, which conserves energy far better
    // than the explicit form over a long episode.
    const cart_rate: T = state.cart_rate + seconds * cart_accel;
    const pole_rate_rad: T = state.pole_rate_rad + seconds * pole_accel;
    const next: CartpoleState(T) = .{
        .cart = state.cart + seconds * cart_rate,
        .cart_rate = cart_rate,
        .pole_rad = state.pole_rad + seconds * pole_rate_rad,
        .pole_rate_rad = pole_rate_rad,
    };

    // Twelve degrees, converted rather than rounded - and 2.4 metres of track either side.
    const fall_rad: T = zm.radFromDeg(@as(T, 12.0));
    const track: T = 2.4;
    const failed: bool = @abs(next.cart) > track or @abs(next.pole_rad) > fall_rad;
    return .{
        .state = next,
        // A reward of one for every step survived, which is the standard scoring and makes the
        // return equal to the episode length - so "did it learn" reads directly off the number.
        .reward = 1.0,
        .failed = failed,
    };
}

The step is pure. znum's takes state: *Tensor(f32) and updates it in place, with a doc comment warning that the tensor "holds the observation for step t only until this returns, so a rollout copies it out BEFORE calling". That is a footgun stated in prose — forgetting produces a rollout whose observations are all one step late, which trains and converges to something slightly wrong.

Reset is a separate call, and that removes two things. znum folds the reset into the step, so the step needs a force_reset parameter and then a no_transition special case because a forced reset produces no reward and no termination. Both disappear when starting an episode is its own function.

The four numbers are named. znum indexes state_data[base + 2] for the pole angle; nothing at the call site says which of four slots holds what, and swapping two produces a plausible simulation that is wrong. Same for rd[0] and rd[1], which are here reward and failed.

Radians here, and the suffix says so

The rest of this library moved to turns, and for geometry that was right: a quarter turn is exactly 0.25, and sinTurns is exact where sin(tau/4) is not.

A differentiated angle is the other case. d/dθ sin(θ) is cos(θ) only in radians; in turns it is tau · cos, and every derivative in a dynamics system picks up that factor. The cartpole equations are differential equations, so radians are the unit in which they contain no constants at all.

A version held the pole in turns and converted the angular acceleration on the way in. That conversion was a place to be wrong, and it was: the pole fell tau times too fast. Every sign was right, the episode ended, the reward accumulated — it looked exactly like a cartpole, and a test checking only directions passed. A unit error inside a plausible simulation has no symptom.

Choosing the unit the equations are written in removed the conversion rather than fixing it. So the rule is decided per piece of angle state, and the name always carries the unit: pole_rad and pole_rate_rad cannot be mistaken for pole_turns.

The test still asserts a magnitude rather than a direction — 9.0977 rad/s² over 0.02 s is 0.295821 rad/s — but that is now the number the equations give directly, with nothing converted on the way.

The limit is not a rounded constant either. znum writes 0.2095, which is 12.0035 degrees; radFromDeg(12) is twelve degrees as exactly as the type can hold.

The integration is semi-implicit Euler: the position update uses the new velocity. Gym's default is explicit Euler, which drifts over a long episode; znum uses semi-implicit too and is right to.

16.5 An agent that learns, and why that is a separate claim#

Every piece has its own test. cartpoleStep has the right physics, categorical has the right distribution, Dense has a checked gradient, the tape matches finite differences. None of that says an agent can learn.

A sign error in the policy update, an observation one step stale, a baseline drifting the wrong way — each of those leaves every component test green and the agent at chance. So the suite trains one: cartpole, a two-layer policy, REINFORCE with a running baseline, forty rounds of eight episodes.

seed    first round    last round
1              16.0          40.8
2              21.0          44.3
3              13.4          20.4
4              15.3          35.4
5              23.4          47.9

Five seeds measured before choosing the bar, which is a 40% rise — under the worst seed's margin so it will not flake, and far above the noise of a policy that is not learning. An untrained cartpole survives about twenty steps, so that is the floor to beat.

It is deliberately the cheapest thing that can fail honestly: no target network, no value head, no clipping. The sign of the advantage is the entire algorithm — backwards, it learns to fail, and every other test stays green.

What this exposed

ppoClipObjective returns a scalar, not a Var. It computes PPO's formula but is not on the tape, so it cannot drive a gradient — the library had PPO's arithmetic and not PPO's training step. That gap was invisible until something tried to train with it, which is exactly the kind of thing an end-to-end test is for.

graph.ppoClipLoss closes it, and the interesting part is where the clip lives.

const loss = try graph.ppoClipLoss(logp_new, logp_old, advantages, 0.2);
try graph.backward(loss);

In the forward the clip is a min, which looks like a detail. In the backward it is the algorithm: where the clipped branch is smaller, the objective no longer depends on the ratio, so the gradient is exactly zero and the update stops pushing a ratio that has already moved too far. That is what keeps a policy step from destroying the policy, and a version computing only the right loss would still diverge.

The test checks the gradient against central differences at every element — a piecewise objective produces plausible numbers on the wrong branch — and then asserts the zeros directly: one index clipped from above with a positive advantage, one clipped from below with a negative one, and one unclipped index that must not be zero, or the check would pass against a backward that returns zero everywhere.

logp_old and advantages are plain slices rather than Vars, because both are constants of the step: the old policy is fixed by definition and the advantages were estimated before it. They ride on the node in a constants field rather than overloading labels, which is []const usize and says "class indices" — a slot whose name is a lie costs more than a field.

16.6 GAE, and two flags that do two different jobs#

try zn.gae(steps, 0.99, 0.95, &advantages, &returns);
gae
/// Generalized Advantage Estimation over a rollout, plus the returns to fit a critic against.
///
/// WHAT IT IS FOR
///
/// A policy gradient needs to know whether an action did better than expected. The crude answer
/// is the whole discounted return, which is unbiased and extremely noisy; the other extreme is
/// the one-step TD error, which is low-variance and biased by whatever the critic gets wrong.
///
/// GAE interpolates with `lambda`: **0 gives the one-step error, 1 gives the full return**, and
/// values between trade bias for variance. 0.95 is the usual choice and is not magic.
///
/// `gamma` discounts the future as everywhere else in RL.
///
/// THE RECURRENCE RUNS BACKWARD, AND IT HAS TO
///
/// Each advantage depends on the one after it, so a forward loop would need the answer before it
/// had it. Walking back from the end costs one pass and no extra storage.
pub fn gae(
    steps: []const RolloutStep,
    gamma: f64,
    lambda: f64,
    out_advantages: []f64,
    out_returns: []f64,
) Error!void {
    if (out_advantages.len != steps.len or out_returns.len != steps.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (gamma < 0 or gamma > 1 or lambda < 0 or lambda > 1) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var running: f64 = 0;
    var i: usize = steps.len;
    while (i > 0) {
        i -= 1;
        const step: RolloutStep = steps[i];
        // `terminal` kills the BOOTSTRAP: no next state exists, so its value is zero.
