        const bootstrap: f64 = if (step.terminal) 0 else gamma * step.next_value;
        const delta: f64 = step.reward + bootstrap - step.value;
        // `episode_end` kills the TRACE: the next step's advantage belongs to another trajectory.
        running = if (step.episode_end) delta else delta + gamma * lambda * running;
        out_advantages[i] = running;
        // The return the critic should have predicted, which is the advantage plus what it did
        // predict. Fitting the critic against this rather than the raw discounted return is what
        // makes the two halves consistent.
        out_returns[i] = running + step.value;
    }
}

A policy gradient needs to know whether an action did better than expected. The crude answer is the whole discounted return: unbiased and extremely noisy. The other extreme is the one-step TD error: low variance, biased by whatever the critic gets wrong.

GAE interpolates with lambda:

lambda 0     the one-step TD error
lambda 0.5   between
lambda 1     the full discounted return

0.95 is the usual choice and is not magic. gamma discounts the future as everywhere else.

terminal kills the bootstrap; episode_end kills the trace

These are two different things and treating them as one flag is the classic mistake.

terminal means the episode genuinely ended — the pole fell. There is no future to bootstrap from, so the next state's value is zero by definition, not whatever the critic happens to predict about a state that does not exist.

episode_end means the rollout stopped here, terminal or not. What stops is the trace: an advantage must not carry across a seam where the following reward belongs to a different trajectory.

reward 1, value 1, next_value 99

terminal = true    ->  advantage 0     the 99 is ignored
terminal = false   ->  advantage 99    the 99 is used

The case one flag cannot express is a truncation: the step limit was reached while the episode was still running. The next state exists and its predicted value is real, so the bootstrap stands — but the trace must still stop. Using terminal for that throws away a legitimate estimate and biases the critic low; using episode_end alone for a real termination bootstraps off a state the agent never reached and biases it high.

The recurrence runs backward

Each advantage depends on the one after it, so a forward loop would need the answer before it had it. Walking back from the end costs one pass and no extra storage.

returns comes out as the advantage plus the critic's own prediction. Fitting the critic against that rather than the raw discounted return is what keeps the two halves consistent.

16.7 Distributions as types#

const policy = zn.Categorical(f64){ .logits = scores };
const action = try policy.sample(rng, row, stream);
const lp     = try policy.logProb(gpa, row, action);
const bonus  = try policy.entropy(gpa, row);
Categorical
/// Write a network's parameters, named by position.
///
/// THE GAP THIS CLOSES: A TRAINED MODEL YOU CANNOT SAVE IS A DEMO
///
/// `saveTensors` and `loadTensors` have been here all along, and they take names and tensors.
/// What was missing is the bridge from a network to those two lists - so a library that could
/// train a cartpole agent could not keep it, and every run started from noise.
///
/// NAMED BY POSITION, AND THAT IS A REAL CONSTRAINT
///
/// The names are `p0`, `p1`, ... in `attach` order. A `Var` carries no name, so there is nothing
/// better available - and the consequence has to be stated rather than discovered: **the loading
/// network must have the same architecture in the same order.** `loadTensors` checks the SHAPES,
/// so a mismatched layer is caught; a same-shaped layer in a different role is not.
///
/// znum's `StateDict` carries owned names, which is better for a network assembled dynamically.
/// This is the honest version for a `Chain`, whose layers are a comptime tuple and therefore
/// always in the same order.
/// A distribution over discrete actions, parameterised by logits.
///
/// WHY THIS IS A TYPE AND NOT THREE FUNCTIONS
///
/// A policy gradient needs `log p(action)` for the action it actually took, and an exploration
/// bonus needs the entropy. Both have to be computed from **the same normalisation `sample`
/// used** - otherwise the gradient points somewhere the sampler never went.
///
/// Bundling them means the three cannot disagree. They are separate functions in znum and the
/// caller is responsible for pairing them correctly.
///
/// LOGITS, NOT PROBABILITIES
///
/// The input is unnormalised scores. A network's last layer produces exactly that, and asking a
/// caller for probabilities means asking them to normalise - which is the step that overflows
/// when a logit is large, and which `logSoftmaxRows` exists to do stably.
pub fn Categorical(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// Unnormalised scores, one row per batch element.
        logits: Tensor(T),

        /// Draw one action for row `row`.
        ///
        /// Delegates to `categorical`, which subtracts the row maximum before exponentiating and
        /// walks a cumulative sum. `index` selects an independent stream so two draws in one
        /// step do not correlate.
        pub fn sample(self: Self, rng: Rng, row: usize, index: u32) Error!usize {
            if (self.logits.rank != 2 or row >= self.logits.shape[0]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            const slice: Tensor(T) = try (try self.logits.slice(0, row, 1)).reshape(
                &.{self.logits.shape[1]},
            );
            return categorical(T, slice, rng, index);
        }

        /// `log p(action)` for one row.
        ///
        /// This is `logSoftmax(logits)[action]`, computed by the stable route rather than as
        /// `log(softmax(x))`. The naive form loses the value entirely once a probability falls
        /// below the smallest representable float - and for a confident policy that is the
        /// common case, not the edge case.
        pub fn logProb(self: Self, gpa: Allocator, row: usize, action: usize) Error!T {
            const classes: usize = self.logits.shape[1];
            if (self.logits.rank != 2 or row >= self.logits.shape[0] or action >= classes) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            const normalised: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, classes });
            defer gpa.free(normalised.data);
            const one_row: Tensor(T) = try self.logits.slice(0, row, 1);
            try logSoftmaxRows(T, normalised, one_row);
            return normalised.at2(0, action);
        }

        /// The Shannon entropy of one row, in nats.
        ///
        /// High entropy means the policy is still exploring; zero means it always picks the same
        /// action. Added to a loss with a small coefficient, it is what stops a policy collapsing
        /// onto one action before it has tried the others.
        pub fn entropy(self: Self, gpa: Allocator, row: usize) Error!T {
            const classes: usize = self.logits.shape[1];
            if (self.logits.rank != 2 or row >= self.logits.shape[0]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            const normalised: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, classes });
            defer gpa.free(normalised.data);
            try logSoftmaxRows(T, normalised, try self.logits.slice(0, row, 1));
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            for (0..classes) |k| {
                const log_p: T = normalised.at2(0, k);
                // `p * log p` from the LOG, so a vanishing probability contributes a vanishing
                // term rather than `0 * -inf`, which is NaN.
                total.add(@exp(log_p) * log_p);
            }
            return -total.value();
        }
    };
}

Why a type rather than three functions

A policy gradient needs log p(action) for the action actually taken, and an exploration bonus needs the entropy. Both must be computed from the same normalisation sample used — otherwise the gradient points somewhere the sampler never went.

Bundling them means the three cannot disagree.

Logits, not probabilities

The input is unnormalised scores, which is what a network's last layer produces. Asking for probabilities means asking the caller to normalise, and that is the step that loses the value:

logits 0 and 800, asking for log p of the first

log(softmax(x))   ->  -inf      softmax gave exactly 0
logSoftmax(x)     ->  -800      correct

For a confident policy that is the common case, not the edge case — and a policy gradient that reads -inf has nothing to learn from.

Entropy is an exploration bonus

It is highest when the policy is uniform: over three actions, exactly log 3. Added to a loss with a small coefficient, it rewards a policy for not yet having made up its mind, which is what stops a collapse onto one action before the others have been tried.

The term p log p is computed from the log, not the probability. From the probability a vanishing action would give 0 × -inf, which is NaN; from the log it vanishes.

DiagGaussian takes log_std, not std

A network outputs an unconstrained real, and a standard deviation must be positive. Taking exp guarantees that for free. Parameterising the deviation directly needs a clamp, and a clamp that ever binds gives a zero gradient on the parameter it clamped — so the network cannot learn its way out.

It also spaces the parameter sensibly: an equal step in log_std is an equal ratio change in the spread, which is how scale behaves.

"Diagonal" records that the dimensions are independent, so the joint log density is the sum of the per-dimension ones. A full covariance would need the off-diagonal terms and a matrix inverse.

One surprise worth naming

A Gaussian's entropy does not depend on its mean. Shifting a distribution moves it without changing its shape, and entropy measures shape. Only the spread matters: 0.5 log(2πe) per dimension, plus log_std.

16.8 The squash correction, and where the naive form dies#

var pre: [dim]f64 = undefined;
var action: [dim]f64 = undefined;
try policy.sample(rng, row, &pre, &action);   // keep BOTH
const lp = try policy.logProb(row, &pre);     // scored from `pre`, not `action`

Why squash

A continuous action usually has bounds — a motor torque, a steering angle. An unbounded Gaussian will sometimes propose a value outside them, and clipping afterwards is not free: the clipped action has a different density from the one the policy scored, so the gradient no longer matches what happened. Squashing puts the bound inside the distribution.

The correction, and the form that loses it

Transforming a random variable changes its density by the derivative of the transform:

log p(a) = log p(u) - log(1 - tanh(u)^2)      with a = tanh(u)

Written that way it is useless in floating point:

u = 20   ->   tanh(u) == 1.0 exactly
              1 - 1 = 0
              log(0) = -inf

And a trained agent lives exactly there, saturated against its action bound. The stable identity instead:

-log(1 - tanh(u)^2)  =  -2 log2 + 2u + 2 softplus(-2u)

Every term stays finite for any u, because softplus is built not to form the 1. This is the same trick logSumExp and log(softmax) need, in a third place.

logProb takes the pre-squash value

Not the action. Recovering u from a would need atanh, which is unbounded as the action approaches its limits and loses all precision in f32 exactly where a saturated policy sits.

Storing u alongside the action costs one float per dimension and removes the problem, so sample returns both and the caller keeps both.

One clamp, used by both halves

log_std is clamped to [-20, 2] — SAC's usual values — and the clamp is applied inside both sample and logProb from the same two constants.

That is the whole reason they are public constants on the type. A policy that samples with one spread and scores with another is off-policy against itself, and the importance ratio it computes means nothing. The clamp matters in practice because an unclamped head emits a log_std of −60 early in training, whose exp underflows to a zero spread and whose log-density is then infinite.

16.9 The PPO objective, and the two numbers that say it works#

const stats = try zn.ppoObjective(
    advantages,        // modified in place if normalising
    log_prob_old,
    log_prob_new,
    entropies,
    .{},               // PpoConfig defaults
);
ppoObjective
/// The clipped surrogate objective over a batch, with the diagnostics that go with it.
///
/// Returns the loss to MINIMISE: the negated objective, so it drops into a gradient step like
/// any other loss. `advantages` is modified in place when the config asks for normalisation.
///
/// THE KL ESTIMATOR IS `exp(r) - 1 - r`, NOT `old - new`
///
/// The cheap estimator every reference implementation started with is `mean(log_old - log_new)`,
/// which is unbiased and **can come out negative** - and a negative divergence is not a small
/// number, it is a wrong one. A caller thresholding on it either never triggers or triggers on
/// noise.
///
/// The k3 estimator `exp(r) - 1 - r` with `r = log_new - log_old` is **non-negative for every
/// r**, because `exp(r) >= 1 + r` everywhere. znum uses the same one.
pub fn ppoObjective(
    advantages: []f64,
    log_prob_old: []const f64,
    log_prob_new: []const f64,
    entropies: []const f64,
    config: PpoConfig,
) Error!PpoStats {
    const n: usize = advantages.len;
    if (log_prob_old.len != n or log_prob_new.len != n or entropies.len != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (config.clip <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }

    if (config.normalize_advantage) {
        var total: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
        for (advantages) |a| {
            total.add(a);
        }
        const mean: f64 = total.value() / float64(n);
        var spread: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
        for (advantages) |a| {
            spread.add((a - mean) * (a - mean));
        }
        // POPULATION deviation, not sample: this is the whole batch, not a sample from it, and
        // the distinction is visible for a small minibatch.
        const deviation: f64 = @sqrt(spread.value() / float64(n));
        for (advantages) |*a| {
            a.* = (a.* - mean) / (deviation + config.advantage_epsilon);
        }
    }

    var objective: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
    var kl: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
    var entropy_total: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
    var clipped: usize = 0;
    for (0..n) |i| {
        const r: f64 = log_prob_new[i] - log_prob_old[i];
        const ratio: f64 = @exp(r);
        const unclipped: f64 = ratio * advantages[i];
        const bounded: f64 = zm.clamp(ratio, 1 - config.clip, 1 + config.clip) * advantages[i];
        // THE MINIMUM, which is what makes it pessimistic: the objective takes the worse of the
        // two branches, so a ratio that moved favourably stops paying once it leaves the trust
        // region while an unfavourable one keeps counting.
        objective.add(@min(unclipped, bounded));
        if (ratio < 1 - config.clip or ratio > 1 + config.clip) {
            clipped += 1;
        }
        kl.add(@exp(r) - 1 - r);
        entropy_total.add(entropies[i]);
    }
    const count: f64 = @floatFromInt(n);
    return .{
        // NEGATED: the objective is maximised, a loss is minimised.
        .policy_loss = -objective.value() / count,
        .clip_fraction = float64(clipped) / count,
        .approx_kl = kl.value() / count,
        .entropy = entropy_total.value() / count,
    };
}

The minimum is pessimistic, and asymmetrically so

The objective takes the worse of the clipped and unclipped branches. What that buys:

ratio 2.0, advantage +1   ->  min(2.0, 1.2) = 1.2    improvement CAPPED
ratio 2.0, advantage -1   ->  min(-2.0, -1.2) = -2.0  penalty NOT capped

A step that moved favourably stops paying once it leaves the trust region; an unfavourable one keeps counting in full. That asymmetry is what makes PPO safe — it will not let a good-looking update run away, but it will always punish a bad one.

Two numbers, because the loss alone cannot tell you

A broken PPO run and a healthy one look the same from the loss, because the clipped objective is supposed to stop improving once the policy has moved far enough.

clip_fractionWhat it means
near 0the policy barely moved — the epochs after the first did nothing
near 1slamming the clip on every sample — the step is too large and the clip is all that holds it together

approx_kl rising sharply is the usual trigger for PPO's early exit, and watching it is cheaper than discovering a collapsed policy several thousand steps later.

The KL estimator cannot go negative

The cheap one is mean(log_old - log_new). It is unbiased and it can come out negative — and a negative divergence is not a small number, it is a wrong one. A threshold on it either never fires or fires on noise.

r = log_new - log_old

cheap:  mean(-r)          can be negative
k3:     exp(r) - 1 - r    non-negative for every r

exp(r) ≥ 1 + r holds everywhere, so the k3 estimator is non-negative by construction rather than by luck. It is positive whether the policy moved up or down, which the cheap one is not.

Advantage normalisation is on by default

Raw advantages carry the scale of the reward. An environment paying 1 per step and one paying 1000 produce gradients a thousand times apart from the same policy error, so one learning rate cannot serve both.

Subtracting the mean and dividing by the deviation makes the step size a property of the algorithm rather than of the reward units. The deviation is the population one, not the sample one — this is the whole batch, not a sample from it, and the difference is visible for a small minibatch.

16.10 The DQN target, and a bias you can measure#

const y = try zn.dqnTarget(f64, transition, q_next_target, q_next_online, 0.99, .double);
dqnTarget
/// The regression target for one transition: `r + gamma * (1 - terminal) * Q(s', a')`.
///
/// WHY `double` EXISTS, AND WHAT IT FIXES
///
/// Vanilla DQN takes `max_a' Q_target(s', a')`. The maximum of noisy estimates is **biased
/// upward** - if every Q is right on average but wrong individually, the largest one is likely
/// to be one that happened to be over-estimated. Bootstrapping off it feeds that error back in,
/// and the values climb away from anything the environment pays.
///
/// Double DQN splits the two jobs: the ONLINE network picks which action looks best, the TARGET
/// network says what it is worth. The two networks have independent errors, so the selection
/// no longer systematically picks the target's over-estimates.
///
/// It is the same fix in one line, and it is why `double` is the sensible default for new code
/// even though `vanilla` is what the original paper did.
///
/// `q_next_online` is ignored for `vanilla` - pass it anyway so a caller switching between the
/// two changes one argument rather than restructuring.
pub fn dqnTarget(
    comptime T: type,
    transition: Transition,
    q_next_target: []const T,
    q_next_online: []const T,
    gamma: T,
    kind: DqnKind,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (q_next_target.len == 0 or q_next_online.len != q_next_target.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (gamma < 0 or gamma > 1) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    // A TERMINAL TRANSITION HAS NO FUTURE, so the target is the reward alone. Not
    // `reward + gamma * something_small` - there is no next state, and its value is zero by
    // definition rather than by estimate.
    if (transition.terminal) {
        return transition.reward;
    }
    const bootstrap: T = switch (kind) {
        .vanilla => blk: {
            var best: T = q_next_target[0];
            for (q_next_target[1..]) |q| {
                best = @max(best, q);
            }
            break :blk best;
        },
        .double => blk: {
            // The ONLINE net chooses...
            var chosen: usize = 0;
            var best: T = q_next_online[0];
            for (q_next_online[1..], 1..) |q, i| {
                if (q > best) {
                    best = q;
                    chosen = i;
                }
            }
            // ...and the TARGET net values that choice.
            break :blk q_next_target[chosen];
        },
    };
    return transition.reward + gamma * bootstrap;
}

vanilla:  y = r + gamma*(1-terminal) * max_a' Q_target(s', a')
double:   a* = argmax_a' Q_online(s', a')
          y = r + gamma*(1-terminal) * Q_target(s', a*)

Why double exists

Vanilla DQN takes the maximum of the target network's Q values. The maximum of noisy estimates is biased upward: if every Q is right on average but wrong individually, the largest one is likely to be one that happened to be over-estimated. Bootstrapping off it feeds that error back in, and the values climb away from anything the environment pays.

Double DQN splits the two jobs. The online network picks which action looks best; the target network says what it is worth. Their errors are independent, so the selection no longer systematically picks the target's over-estimates.

The bias is measurable, not just arguable

Take a state where the true value of every action is zero and give two networks independent noise around it. Over 400 trials with eight actions:

vanilla   mean target  >  1.0     the max of eight standard normals averages about 1.4
double    mean target  <  0.3     close to the truth

When the two networks agree the estimators give the same answer, so switching is safe on a converged agent and differs only while the estimates are still noisy.

A terminal transition has no future

The target is the reward alone — not reward + gamma * something_small. The next state does not exist, and its value is zero by definition rather than by estimate. A target Q of 9 sitting in the array is ignored entirely, which is what a missing terminal flag would let through.

explainedVariance, because a value loss does not tell you enough

A falling value loss says the critic is fitting something. It does not say the something is the returns.

critic predicts the mean return for every state
  value loss      small and shrinking
  explained var   exactly 0        it has learned no relationship at all

Negative means worse than that constant baseline — a real and common state early in training, and invisible in the loss curve. Constant returns give zero rather than a division by zero: there was nothing to explain.

16.11 SAC, and the one knob that covers DroQ, REDQ and CrossQ#

const y = try zn.sacTarget(f64, transition, next_estimates, next_log_prob,
                           gamma, alpha, .minimum);
sacTarget
/// The SAC regression target for one transition.
///
/// WHAT MAKES IT SAC RATHER THAN TD3
///
/// The entropy term. TD3's target is `r + gamma * min_i Q_i(s', a')`; SAC subtracts
/// `alpha * log p(a'|s')` from the bootstrap, so an action the policy was CONFIDENT about is
/// worth less than an equally-good action it was uncertain about.
///
/// That single term is what makes the objective "maximise return **and** keep exploring", and it
/// is why SAC does not need an exploration schedule bolted on: the incentive is in the value
/// function.
///
/// `next_estimates` is a slice for the reason `CriticAggregate` documents - two networks, M
/// dropout passes, or a random subset of an ensemble all arrive the same way.
pub fn sacTarget(
    comptime T: type,
    transition: Transition,
    next_estimates: []const T,
    next_log_prob: T,
    gamma: T,
    alpha: T,
    how: CriticAggregate,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (gamma < 0 or gamma > 1 or alpha < 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (transition.terminal) {
        // No next state, so neither its value NOR its entropy enters. Keeping the entropy term
        // here would pay a bonus for the uncertainty of an action that was never taken.
        return transition.reward;
    }
    const q: T = try aggregateCritics(T, next_estimates, how);
    return transition.reward + gamma * (q - alpha * next_log_prob);
}

Four algorithms, one call with a different list

Every modern off-policy method attacks the same problem — Q values drifting upward, which caps how many gradient steps you can take per environment step. They differ only in where the diversity of estimates comes from:

MethodEstimates come fromCombined by
TD3, SACtwo separate networksminimum
DroQM dropout passes of one netminimum
REDQa random M of an ensemble of Nminimum
CrossQone LayerNorm net, no target networksingle value

next_estimates is a slice, not a pair, so all four are the same call. A single estimate passes through under either rule, which is what makes CrossQ a one-element list rather than a special case.

The minimum is biased downward on purpose

A minimum of unbiased estimates under-estimates. Measured over noise around a true value of zero, with two critics:

minimum   mean target  <  -0.3     the min of two standard normals averages -0.56
mean      mean target  ~   0        unbiased

The asymmetry is deliberate: an over-estimate gets bootstrapped into the next target and compounds, while an under-estimate merely slows learning. .mean is there for critics whose regularisation is elsewhere — a dropout or LayerNorm critic is already conservative and does not need a second pessimism on top.

The entropy term is what makes it SAC

TD3:  y = r + gamma * min_i Q_i(s', a')
SAC:  y = r + gamma * (min_i Q_i(s', a') - alpha * log p(a'|s'))

An action the policy was confident about is worth less than an equally-good action it was uncertain about. That single term is why SAC needs no exploration schedule bolted on: the incentive lives in the value function. Setting alpha to zero gives exactly TD3's target, so the two methods differ by one coefficient.

A terminal transition drops the entropy term as well as the value — keeping it would pay a bonus for the uncertainty of an action that was never taken.

REDQ re-draws its subset

Using all N critics would be maximally pessimistic and slow to learn. A fixed M would let the same critics' shared error dominate every target. Re-drawing each step is what stops the pessimism being systematic — M = 2 of N = 10 is the paper's setting.

The temperature target is negative, which catches people out

alpha is learned rather than set, because a fixed value trades return against exploration at a rate that has to suit the reward scale, the action dimension and the stage of training — and it suits at most one of those at a time.

The gradient is alpha * (entropy - target_entropy), and the usual target is -action_dim. So with target_entropy = -2:

log_prob -5   entropy  5   above target  ->  gradient +, alpha falls
log_prob -1   entropy  1   still above   ->  gradient +, alpha falls less steeply
log_prob +3   entropy -3   below target  ->  gradient -, alpha rises
log_prob +2   entropy -2   AT the target ->  gradient 0

The fixed point is log_prob = +2, not −2. A continuous density can exceed 1, so a positive log-probability is an ordinary state for a concentrated policy and is exactly what -action_dim asks for.

log_alpha is the parameter, not alpha, for the same reason DiagGaussian takes log_std: exp keeps it positive for free, where a clamp would give a zero gradient exactly when it binds.

17. Autograd#

A Graph records operations as it performs them and walks the record backwards to produce gradients. The same XOR network as the previous section, differentiated automatically:

var graph: zn.Graph(f64) = .init(scope.allocator());

const gx  = try graph.constant(x);
const gt  = try graph.constant(target);
const gw1 = try graph.parameter(w1);
const gb1 = try graph.parameter(b1);
const gw2 = try graph.parameter(w2);
const gb2 = try graph.parameter(b2);

const pre1 = try graph.add(try graph.matmul(gx, gw1), gb1);
const h    = try graph.tanh(pre1);
const y    = try graph.add(try graph.matmul(h, gw2), gb2);
const loss = try graph.mseLoss(y, gt);

try graph.backward(loss);
const dw1 = try graph.gradOf(gw1);

17.1 Values are indices#

A Var is a u32 index into the graph, not a pointer. Keying gradients off the tensor would not work: a view shares data.ptr with its parent, so w and w.transpose(0,1) would collide, and a Tensor is copied by value throughout the library so it has no stable address at all. The graph owns the correspondence between a value, its tensor and its gradient, and Tensor needs no change to support it.

parameter allocates a gradient buffer; constant does not. gradOf on a constant is DomainError rather than a buffer of zeros that looks like an answer.

17.2 Eager forward, recorded backward#

Each operation computes its result immediately and appends a node describing how to push a gradient back through it. Computing eagerly means every shape is known when it is needed, so outputs are allocated as the graph is built and there is no separate shape-inference pass to disagree with the forward pass.

Nodes are appended in execution order, and a value can only depend on values recorded before it. Walking the list backwards therefore visits every consumer of a value before the value itself — a topological order by construction, with no sort, no visited set and no cycle check.

backward takes a value of a single element and seeds its gradient with 1. Differentiating a tensor with respect to parameters gives a Jacobian rather than a gradient; reverse mode answers how one number changes, so the entry point requires one. Anything else is UnsupportedShape. The pass zeroes every gradient before accumulating, so calling it twice gives the same answer rather than doubling.

17.3 Broadcasting is a sum on the way back#

A bias of shape (1, n) added to (m, n) is used m times in the forward pass, so its gradient is the sum of m contributions. Accumulation reduces along any axis that was broadcast:

if (dest.size() == contribution.size()) {
    return zimrnum.add(T, dest, dest, contribution);
}
// Broadcast along the leading axis: sum the contribution down to the bias's width.

Adding the raw gradient instead would be low by a factor of m, and the network would still train — with a bias moving m times too slowly. The test asserts the bias gradients specifically for that reason.

17.4 What is differentiable#

OperationBackward
addBoth inputs receive the gradient; a broadcast input receives its sum.
subThe left input receives the gradient, the right its negation.
mulEach side receives the other side's value times the incoming gradient.
matmuldA = g @ Bᵀ, dB = Aᵀ @ g
tanhtanhGrad(out, g), using the forward output
sigmoidsigmoidGrad(out, g), using the forward output
relureluGrad(in, g), using the forward input
mseLoss2(a − t)/n, scaled by the incoming gradient
crossEntropysoftmax − onehot, over the row count
softmaxRowsy · (g − ∑ g·y) per row; the Jacobian applied without being formed
layerNormRows(1/σ) · (g − mean(g) − y · mean(g·y)) per row
scaleThe gradient times the factor
dropoutThe gradient times the same mask as the forward pass
transposeThe transpose of the gradient. Materialised, because a value of the graph must own its storage.

The layer norm rule is checked against a finite difference on rows given very different scales and offsets, where a rule that dropped the variance term would be visibly wrong. Measured disagreement per operation, each alone: layer norm 9.7e-12, softmax 1.1e-11, scale 1.1e-9, dropout 7.1e-9.

dropout draws its mask once and stores it as a value of the graph. A dropout that redrew on every recompute would be a different function each time, and checkGradient would compare a derivative against a finite difference of something else. resampleDropout draws fresh masks, which a training loop calls once per step. The scaling is inverted — kept elements are multiplied by 1/(1 − rate) — so removing the node at inference is exactly equivalent to rate = 0.

17.5 Replay and gradient checking#

try graph.recompute();
const worst = try graph.checkGradient(loss, gw1, 1.0e-5);

recompute replays the recorded operations forward, writing new results into the buffers already allocated. Leaves are skipped, so writing a new batch into an input tensor and calling recompute evaluates the same model on new data. Without it a graph would be single-use and a training loop would rebuild the whole tape every step.

checkGradient perturbs each element of a parameter, replays, and compares a central finite difference of the loss against the tape's gradient, returning the largest disagreement. It is the one check that does not repeat the derivation: a hand-written backward pass and a tape rule can be wrong in the same way, because the same person derived both, but a finite difference does not know the derivative at all.

Central rather than forward: (f(x+h) − f(x−h)) / 2h has error O(h²) where (f(x+h) − f(x)) / h has O(h). At h = 1e-5 in f64 that is the difference between agreeing to ten digits and to three, which decides whether a real error is visible above the noise. Every parameter of a graph exercising all eight operations agrees to better than 1e-8.

The routine restores every value and recomputes before returning, so checking a model leaves it exactly as it was found.

Convolution and embedding on the tape#

const l1: zn.Conv2d(f64) = try .init(a, rng, .{ 3, 3 }, .{ 1, 1 }, .{ 1, 1 }, .he);
const h1 = try l1.attach(&graph, input);       // .out, .kernel, .bias

const table: zn.Embedding(f64) = try .init(a, rng, vocabulary, width);
const e = try table.attach(&graph, &indices);   // .out, .table
Conv2d
/// A 2-D convolution layer: a learned kernel and a scalar bias, on `Dense`'s template.
///
/// SAME SHAPE AS `Dense`: own the parameters, `attach` to a graph, hand back the handles the
/// optimiser needs. That is the whole contract of a layer here, and it is why there is no
/// `Sequential` - a struct holding two of these IS the composition, and `attach` chains.
///
/// Single channel, rank 2. The kernel is initialised by fan-in, counting the window: a 3x3
/// kernel sees nine inputs per output, so `He` over 9 is the right scale and `He` over the whole
/// image is not.
pub fn Conv2d(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        kernel: Tensor(T),
        bias: Tensor(T),
        stride: [2]usize,
        pad: [2]usize,

        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            kernel: Var,
            bias: Var,
        };

        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            rng: Rng,
            size: [2]usize,
            stride: [2]usize,
            pad: [2]usize,
            scheme: InitScheme,
        ) Error!Self {
            const kernel: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &size);
            const bias: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, 1 });
            const fan_in: usize = size[0] * size[1];
            switch (scheme) {
                .xavier => try initXavier(T, kernel, rng, fan_in, fan_in),
                .he => try initHe(T, kernel, rng, fan_in),
            }
            bias.fill(0);
            return .{ .kernel = kernel, .bias = bias, .stride = stride, .pad = pad };
        }

        /// Convolve, then add the bias - which broadcasts from `(1, 1)` over the whole output.
        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var) Error!Attached {
            const k: Var = try graph.parameter(self.kernel);
            const b: Var = try graph.parameter(self.bias);
            const convolved: Var = try graph.conv2d(input, k, self.stride, self.pad);
            return .{ .out = try graph.add(convolved, b), .kernel = k, .bias = b };
        }
    };
}
Embedding
/// A lookup table of learned row vectors, indexed by whole numbers.
pub fn Embedding(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        table: Tensor(T),

        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            table: Var,
        };

        /// Rows are drawn from the unit normal rather than by fan-in: an embedding row is not
        /// a projection of anything, so there is no fan to scale by. The usual choice.
        pub fn init(gpa: Allocator, rng: Rng, count: usize, width: usize) Error!Self {
            const table: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ count, width });
            rng.fillNormal(T, table.data);
            return .{ .table = table };
        }

        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), rows: []const usize) Error!Attached {
            const t: Var = try graph.parameter(self.table);
            return .{ .out = try graph.embedding(t, rows), .table = t };
        }
    };
}

The backward pass is the forward loop with the accumulation reversed. Forward, each output reads a window: y[i][j] += x[i·s+a−p][j·s+b−p] · k[a][b]. Both gradients fall out of that one line by moving what is read and what is written:

dx[i·s+a−p][j·s+b−p] += g[i][j] · k[a][b]
dk[a][b]                     += g[i][j] · x[i·s+a−p][j·s+b−p]

So it is written as one pass producing both, rather than as the "full correlation with a flipped kernel" the textbooks give. That form is correct and is a second thing to get right; this one cannot disagree with the forward pass about which taps are in range, because it asks the identical question.

An embedding's gradient is a scatter-add, and the += is the whole point: a token appearing three times in a batch must accumulate three contributions into one row. A scatter that assigned would train on the last occurrence and silently discard the rest. The test indexes row 2 three times and lets the finite difference — which perturbs the row and sees all three uses move — catch it.

17.6 Attention is not a primitive#

const q = try graph.matmul(gx, gq);
const k = try graph.matmul(gx, gk);
const v = try graph.matmul(gx, gv);
const scores = try graph.scale(
    try graph.matmul(q, try graph.transpose(k)),
    1.0 / @sqrt(@as(f64, @floatFromInt(width))),
);
const weights = try graph.softmaxRows(scores);
const attended = try graph.matmul(weights, v);

softmax(Q Kᵀ / √d) V is four operations the tape already has. Composing them is what shows the tape composes: a gradient error in any one, or in how gradients accumulate through a value used more than once, appears here. gx feeds all three projections, so its gradient is the sum of three paths — the accumulation a single-use test never exercises. All four parameters agree with a finite difference to better than 1e-8, and no backward rule was written for attention itself.

17.7 Initialisation and layers#

try zn.initXavier(f64, w, rng, fan_in, fan_out);   // uniform ±√(6/(in+out))
try zn.initHe(f64, w, rng, fan_in);                 // normal, deviation √(2/in)

const layer: zn.Dense(f64) = try .init(a, rng, 2, 16, .he);
const attached = try layer.attach(&graph, input);
// attached.out, attached.weight, attached.bias
initXavier
/// Fill `w` with Glorot/Xavier uniform values: `+/-sqrt(6 / (fan_in + fan_out))`.
///
/// WHY THE SCALE DEPENDS ON THE SHAPE
///
/// A layer's output variance is roughly `fan_in` times its weight variance. Fill every layer from
/// the same distribution and that factor compounds: after ten layers the activations are either
/// saturated or vanishing, and the network cannot train regardless of the optimiser. Scaling by
/// the fan sizes holds the variance constant from layer to layer.
///
/// Xavier balances the FORWARD and BACKWARD passes, which is why both fans appear - the
/// forward pass wants `1/fan_in` and the backward wants `1/fan_out`, and `2/(fan_in + fan_out)`
/// is their harmonic compromise. It suits `tanh` and `sigmoid`, whose useful range is symmetric
/// about zero.
pub fn initXavier(
    comptime T: type,
    w: Tensor(T),
    rng: Rng,
    fan_in: usize,
    fan_out: usize,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (fan_in == 0 or fan_out == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const total: T = @floatFromInt(fan_in + fan_out);
    const limit: T = @sqrt(@as(T, 6.0) / total);
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < w.size()) : (i += 1) {
        w.data[w.base + i] = rng.uniform(T, @intCast(i), -limit, limit);
    }
}
initHe
/// Fill `w` with He normal values: standard deviation `sqrt(2 / fan_in)`.
///
/// THE 2 IS THERE BECAUSE `relu` DISCARDS HALF ITS INPUT. A rectifier zeroes the negative
/// half, halving the variance that reaches the next layer; doubling the weight variance restores
/// it. Using Xavier with `relu` loses a factor of two per layer, which is a factor of a thousand
/// over ten layers.
pub fn initHe(comptime T: type, w: Tensor(T), rng: Rng, fan_in: usize) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (fan_in == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const deviation: T = @sqrt(@as(T, 2.0) / @as(T, @floatFromInt(fan_in)));
    var i: usize = 0;
    while (i < w.size()) : (i += 1) {
        w.data[w.base + i] = rng.normal(T, @intCast(i)) * deviation;
    }
}
Dense
pub fn Dense(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        weight: Tensor(T),
        bias: Tensor(T),

        /// Allocate and initialise. `He` for a layer feeding a rectifier, `Xavier` otherwise.
        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            rng: Rng,
            fan_in: usize,
            fan_out: usize,
            scheme: InitScheme,
        ) Error!Self {
            const weight: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ fan_in, fan_out });
            const bias: Tensor(T) = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, fan_out });
            switch (scheme) {
                .xavier => try initXavier(T, weight, rng, fan_in, fan_out),
                .he => try initHe(T, weight, rng, fan_in),
            }
            // Biases start at zero. A random bias adds nothing a random weight does not already
            // provide, and a non-zero one shifts every unit's activation before any data is seen.
            bias.fill(0);
            return .{ .weight = weight, .bias = bias };
        }

        /// What `attach` hands back: the output, and handles for the two parameters.
        ///
        /// RETURNING ONLY THE OUTPUT IS NOT ENOUGH, which a first attempt at a training loop
        /// showed immediately: the loop needs the parameter handles to read their gradients, and
        /// with the layer holding no `Var` there is nowhere else to get them. Returning all three
        /// keeps the layer graph-agnostic AND gives the caller what a step requires.
        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            weight: Var,
            bias: Var,
        };

        /// Record `input @ weight + bias` onto `graph`.
        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var) Error!Attached {
            const w: Var = try graph.parameter(self.weight);
            const b: Var = try graph.parameter(self.bias);
            return .{
                .out = try graph.add(try graph.matmul(input, w), b),
                .weight = w,
                .bias = b,
            };
        }
    };
}

A layer's output variance is roughly fan_in times its weight variance. Filling every layer from the same distribution compounds that factor once per layer, and after ten layers the activations are saturated or vanishing regardless of the optimiser. Both schemes scale by the fan sizes to hold the variance constant.

Xavier balances the forward and backward passes — the forward wants 1/fan_in, the backward 1/fan_out, and 2/(fan_in + fan_out) is their compromise. It suits tanh and sigmoid, whose useful range is symmetric about zero.

He doubles that variance because relu discards its negative half, halving what reaches the next layer. Using Xavier with a rectifier loses a factor of two per layer — a factor of a thousand over ten.

Dense owns its weights as ordinary tensors and holds no Var. A Var is meaningful to one graph, so storing one would tie a layer to the first graph it met; keeping the layer graph-agnostic is what lets the same weights back two paths. attach therefore returns the parameter handles alongside the output, because a training loop needs them to read gradients and there is nowhere else to get them. Biases start at zero: a random bias adds nothing a random weight does not, and shifts every unit before any data is seen.

17.8 Optimiser#

var opt: zn.Optimizer(f64) = try .init(
    a, &graph,
    &.{ a1.weight, a1.bias, a2.weight, a2.bias },
    .{ .adam = .{ .rate = 0.02 } },      // or .{ .sgd = .{ .rate = 0.1 } }
);

while (step < 1000) : (step += 1) {
    try graph.recompute();
    try graph.backward(loss);
    try opt.step(&graph);
}
Optimizer
/// An optimiser bound to a set of a graph's parameters, owning whatever state the algorithm
/// needs per parameter.
///
/// THE STATE LIVES HERE, NOT WITH THE CALLER
///
/// `sgdMomentum` needs a velocity per parameter and `adamStep` needs two moments; without this,
/// a caller training four parameters with Adam allocates and threads eight extra tensors by
/// hand, and a mistake - one moment buffer shared between two parameters - trains silently
/// wrong. Binding the state to the parameter list at construction makes that mistake
/// unrepresentable.
///
/// ONE TYPE, THREE ALGORITHMS. The choice is a field, not a type parameter, so a training loop
/// can be written once and the algorithm changed at one call site. The state buffers for an
/// algorithm that does not use them are never allocated.
///
/// `step` reads gradients through the graph and writes updates into the graph's parameter
/// tensors, so the next `recompute` sees them with no further bookkeeping.
pub fn Optimizer(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        pub const Kind = union(enum) {
            sgd: struct { rate: T },
            momentum: struct { rate: T, momentum: T },
            adam: Adam,
        };

        kind: Kind,
        params: []const Var,
        /// Per parameter: one buffer for momentum, two for Adam, none for SGD.
        state: []Tensor(T),
        /// Adam's 1-based step counter.
        steps: usize,

        pub fn init(
            gpa: Allocator,
            graph: *const Graph(T),
            params: []const Var,
            kind: Kind,
        ) Error!Self {
            const per: usize = switch (kind) {
                .sgd => 0,
                .momentum => 1,
                .adam => 2,
            };
            // THE PARAMETER LIST IS COPIED, NOT RETAINED
            //
            // The first version stored the caller's slice. Every test passed, because a test
            // writes `&.{ a, b, c }` in the same frame that calls `step`. The GPU training example
            // built its optimiser in `init` and stepped it from `update`, and the array literal
            // was gone - an out-of-bounds read inside `valueOf`, on the first frame. A type that
            // outlives the call that made it owns what it needs.
            const owned: []Var = try gpa.alloc(Var, params.len);
            @memcpy(owned, params);
            const state: []Tensor(T) = try gpa.alloc(Tensor(T), params.len * per);
            for (params, 0..) |v, i| {
                const shape: []const usize = graph.valueOf(v).shape[0..graph.valueOf(v).rank];
                var k: usize = 0;
                while (k < per) : (k += 1) {
                    state[i * per + k] = try Tensor(T).alloc(gpa, shape);
                    state[i * per + k].fill(0);
                }
            }
            return .{ .kind = kind, .params = owned, .state = state, .steps = 0 };
        }

        /// Apply one update to every bound parameter, from the gradients `backward` left.
        pub fn step(self: *Self, graph: *const Graph(T)) Error!void {
            self.steps += 1;
            for (self.params, 0..) |v, i| {
                const weight: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(v);
                const grad: Tensor(T) = try graph.gradOf(v);
                switch (self.kind) {
                    .sgd => |h| try sgdStep(T, weight, weight, grad, h.rate),
                    .momentum => |h| try sgdMomentum(
                        T,
                        weight,
                        weight,
                        grad,
                        self.state[i],
                        h.rate,
                        h.momentum,
                    ),
                    .adam => |h| try adamStep(
                        T,
                        weight,
                        weight,
                        grad,
                        self.state[i * 2],
                        self.state[i * 2 + 1],
                        h,
                        self.steps,
                    ),
                }
            }
        }
    };
}

An Optimizer is bound to a list of a graph's parameters at construction and owns whatever state the algorithm needs for each: nothing for SGD, a velocity for momentum, two moments for Adam. Without it a caller training four parameters with Adam threads eight extra tensors by hand, and a mistake — one moment buffer shared between two parameters — trains silently wrong. Binding the state to the parameter list makes that unrepresentable.

The algorithm is a field rather than a type parameter, so a loop is written once and the algorithm changed at one site. State for an algorithm that does not use it is never allocated. step reads gradients through the graph and writes updates into the graph's own parameter tensors, so the next recompute sees them.

SGD through the optimiser is asserted equal to sgdStep written by hand — the wrapper adds no arithmetic, so a difference would be a bookkeeping error in which parameter received which gradient. The three algorithms on the classifier of the previous section, from identical weights:

StepsSGDMomentumAdam
500.7690.5800.539
2000.6280.1770.043
10000.3540.00890.0012

The ordering holds at 200 steps. Adam's two buffers per parameter are a cost; the table is what they buy.

17.9 A training loop#

The graph is built once, outside the loop. Each step replays it, differentiates it, and steps the parameters in place:

while (step < 3000) : (step += 1) {
    try graph.recompute();
    try graph.backward(loss);
    try zn.sgdStep(f64, w1, w1, try graph.gradOf(gw1), 0.2);
    try zn.sgdStep(f64, b1, b1, try graph.gradOf(gb1), 0.2);
    try zn.sgdStep(f64, w2, w2, try graph.gradOf(gw2), 0.2);
    try zn.sgdStep(f64, b2, b2, try graph.gradOf(gb2), 0.2);
}

Nothing is allocated after the first iteration. sgdStep writes into the parameter tensors the graph already holds, so the next recompute sees the updated weights without any bookkeeping.

A three-class classifier on 32 points, labelled by quadrant sign, trained this way:

StepLossAccuracy
01.2820.22
2000.1260.97
30000.0211.00

The gradient check runs before and after: 3.8e-11 at the start and 9.7e-12 after training, at a point far from where it began. A model that trains is not by itself evidence that its gradients are right — a wrong but correlated gradient still reduces a loss — so the two are checked separately.

17.10 slice and concat, and why they were missing#

const top    = try graph.slice(x, 0, 0, 2);   // axis, start, len
const bottom = try graph.slice(x, 0, 2, 3);
const back   = try graph.concat(top, bottom, 0);

Tensor.slice has always given a strided view for free. What it could not do is carry a gradient — so anything slicing a computed value was out of reach. That absence shaped two designs in this file: LstmCell could not fuse its four gates into one matmul, and Attention could not split a projection into heads. Both said "stated rather than half-built" and moved on. znum has neither op either.

The backward of a slice is a scatter

The gradient lands in the rows the slice read, and every other row gets exactly zero — a row nobody read influenced nothing. concat's backward is the same thing read the other way: each input takes back the part of the gradient that came from its own rows.

A scatter that writes to the wrong offset still runs, still produces numbers of the right shape, and trains something adjacent to what you meant. So the comparison is against a central difference at every element, and separately asserts the zeros: slice the middle two rows of five, and rows 0, 3 and 4 must have no gradient — not small, zero.

It also checks that slicing and rejoining is the identity on values. That matters because it means a wrong offset in either backward shows up in the gradient while the forward pass still looks perfect.

17.11 Activations on the tape, and one that cannot be#

const h = try graph.silu(x);   // x * sigmoid(x)
const h = try graph.gelu(x);   // the tanh form

silu, gelu, softplus and elu all existed at tensor level with no tape node at all. A network using any of them could be evaluated and not trained — the forward works, the loss falls for the layers that do have nodes, and the activation simply never learns. Nothing reports it.

That is a worse failure than a missing gradient function, because it looks like training.

Two of the four are compositions of nodes that already exist, so they cost nothing and their gradients are the tape's.

exp, log and softplus — and why the third is not a composition

exp and log are on the tape now. They unlock compositions it could not express — mish is x * tanh(softplus(x)), and a ratio of exponentials becomes ordinary arithmetic.

But having the primitives does not mean composing them is safe. Written as log(1 + exp(x)), softplus overflows: measured at f32, exp(89) is infinity, so the composition returns inf from x = 89 upward where the true answer is 89 — softplus is very nearly the identity for large x.

So softplus is its own node. The identity behind its form:

log(1 + e^x) = max(x, 0) + log(1 + e^-|x|)

  for x > 0:  log(1 + e^x) = log(e^x (e^-x + 1)) = x + log(1 + e^-x)
  for x ≤ 0:  max is 0 and |x| = -x, leaving the original

The exponential is therefore only ever taken of a negative number and cannot overflow.

But overflow is not the only failure. At x = −50 the answer is 1.93e−22, and any form computing log(1 + e^-|x|) returns exactly zero — 1 + 1.9e-22 rounds to 1 in f64 and its logarithm is 0. znum's softplus does this; so did ours, with a threshold at 20 that also left a 1e−10 discontinuity just above it.

log1p takes the small argument directly and never forms the 1:

x        log1p form        log(1 + ...) form
-50      1.929e-22         0
-700     9.860e-305        0
-745     4.941e-324        0

The tail survives to the smallest subnormal. It is the same trick logSumExp uses one screen away and the one log(softmax(x)) needed — three places in this library where forming the 1 loses everything.

The backward is what makes a dedicated node cheap: d/dx softplus(x) is exactly sigmoid(x) — no branch, nothing to derive.

The test probes all four gradients separately, because the three backwards read three different things: exp uses its own output, log needs the input, and softplus uses sigmoid of the input. Picking the wrong one is easy and gives plausible numbers. It also checks log(exp(x)) == x, which would fail if either node were wrong in a way the separate probes happened to miss.

ELU, and a probe that would have passed anyway

elu is a real node, not a composition: it switches on the sign, and there is no elementwise select to build that from. Its backward is 1 above zero and alpha * exp(x) below, read from the input — the output would give the same numbers, but only the input says which side of the branch a value was on.

The shared gradient probe above uses all-positive values, because log needs them. On positive input ELU is the identity, so that probe exercises one side of the branch and reports success. It gets its own spread straddling zero — {-3, -1, -0.1, 0, 0.1, 1, 3} — which is the only place the branch shows.

Two properties pin it beyond the derivative: above zero the output equals the input exactly, and below zero it saturates toward -alpha without reaching it.

Every activation in the library now has a tape node — relu, sigmoid, tanh, silu, gelu, softplus, mish, elu. Before this pair of turns, four of those could be evaluated and never trained.

GELU is written x * sigmoid(2u) rather than 0.5x * (1 + tanh(u)). They are the same function:

(1 + tanh u)/2 = (1 + (e^u - e^-u)/(e^u + e^-u))/2
               = (2e^u / (e^u + e^-u))/2
               =  1 / (1 + e^-2u)
               =  sigmoid(2u)

The first form needs a tensor of ones to add — allocated on every call, and on a graph rebuilt each training step that is a per-step allocation for a constant. The second needs none and is two nodes shorter.

GELU is the tanh form, not the erf definition. The exact version needs erf; the approximation is what PyTorch, JAX and every published transformer actually run, so matching it matters more than matching the definition — weights were trained against it.

Two things worth stating

One might expect the tensor gelu was the erf definition and expected the two to differ by about 1e−3. They agree to 8e−13 — zimrnum's tensor version is the same approximation. The bar is set from that measurement rather than from an assumption about the code.

And the finite-difference probe mutates its input, so the input must be allocated. Every other test here builds tensors with @constCast(&[_]f64{...}), which is safe because none of them writes — a literal lives in read-only memory and @constCast only silences the type system. Writing through it aborts.

18. A network that learns#

Every piece above has its own test. None of those catches a mistake in how the pieces fit — a gradient with the wrong sign, a transpose on the wrong operand, a bias summed along the wrong axis. Each of those leaves every individual function correct and the network unable to learn.

The following is a test in src/zimrnum.zig. It trains a two-layer network on XOR, which is the smallest problem a linear model cannot solve: without a working hidden layer the loss stalls near 0.25.

// forward
try zn.matmul(f64, pre1, x, w1);
try zn.add(f64, pre1, pre1, try b1.broadcastTo(&.{ samples, hidden }));
try zn.tanh(f64, h, pre1);
try zn.matmul(f64, y, h, w2);
try zn.add(f64, y, y, try b2.broadcastTo(&.{ samples, 1 }));
const loss = try zn.mseLoss(f64, y, target);

// backward: d(mse)/dy is 2(y - t)/n
try zn.sub(f64, dy, y, target);
try zn.scale(f64, dy, dy, 2.0 / @as(f64, @floatFromInt(samples)));
try zn.matmul(f64, dw2, try h.transpose(0, 1), dy);
try zn.sumAxis(f64, db2, dy, 0);
try zn.matmul(f64, dh, dy, try w2.transpose(0, 1));
try zn.tanhGrad(f64, dpre, h, dh);
try zn.matmul(f64, dw1, try x.transpose(0, 1), dpre);
try zn.sumAxis(f64, db1, dpre, 0);

// update
try zn.sgdStep(f64, w1, w1, dw1, rate);
try zn.sgdStep(f64, b1, b1, db1, rate);
try zn.sgdStep(f64, w2, w2, dw2, rate);
try zn.sgdStep(f64, b2, b2, db2, rate);

Weights and intermediates come from an arena, so nothing in the loop is freed individually and the whole allocation goes when the scope closes. The transposes are stride swaps — no copy.

StepLoss
00.541
1006.2e-6
5004e-8
10000

Final outputs are −0.000000, 1.000000, 1.000000, −0.000000 against targets of 0, 1, 1, 0, and the weight gradients have fallen to about 2e-11.

One detail worth noting: tanhGrad takes h, the forward output, not pre1. Passing the input compiles, runs, and produces a network that learns far too slowly — the kind of error only an end-to-end check finds.

19.5 Running on the GPU#

zimrnum compiles to SPIR-V, so a kernel can call the same function the CPU does rather than restating it. The pattern is three lines in build.zig and one in the kernel:

// build.zig: mark the kernel module as wanting zimrnum
.wants_zimrnum = true,

// the kernel: call the host function directly
pub fn cartpole_step(c: k.Ctx(@This())) void {
    if (c.id >= c.params.count) return;
    state[c.id] = zn.cartpoleStep(state[c.id], action[c.id], dt);
}
cartpoleStep
/// The cart-and-pole control benchmark: push a cart left or right to keep a pole upright.
///
/// A PURE FUNCTION - THE STATE GOES IN AND THE NEXT STATE COMES OUT
///
/// znum's step takes `state: *Tensor(f32)` and updates it IN PLACE, with a doc comment warning
/// that the tensor "holds the observation for step t only until this returns, so a rollout copies
/// it out BEFORE calling". **That is a footgun stated in prose**: the caller has to remember, and
/// forgetting produces a rollout whose observations are all one step late, which trains and
/// converges to something slightly wrong.
///
/// Returning the next state makes it unforgettable. It also makes the step replayable - the same
/// state and action give the same answer, always - which is what a test needs and what a
/// debugger needs.
///
/// RESET IS A SEPARATE FUNCTION, AND THAT REMOVES TWO FLAGS
///
/// znum folds the reset into the step, so the step needs a `force_reset` parameter, and then a
/// `no_transition` special case because a forced reset produces no reward and no termination.
/// Both disappear when starting an episode is its own call: `cartpoleReset` gives a state, and
/// `cartpoleStep` only ever steps.
///
/// RADIANS HERE, AND THE SUFFIX SAYS SO - BECAUSE THIS ANGLE IS DIFFERENTIATED
///
/// The rest of this codebase moved to turns, and for geometry that was right: a quarter turn is
/// exactly 0.25, and `sinTurns` is exact where `sin(tau/4)` is not.
///
/// **A differentiated angle is the other case.** `d/dtheta sin(theta)` is `cos(theta)` only in
/// radians; in turns it is `tau * cos`, and every derivative in a dynamics system picks up that
/// factor. The cartpole equations are differential equations, so radians are the unit in which
/// they have no constants in them at all.
///
/// The first version of this held the pole in turns and converted the angular acceleration from
/// radians on the way in. That conversion was a place to be wrong, and I was: **the pole fell tau
/// times too fast** and every sign still looked right. Choosing the unit the equations are
/// written in removes the conversion rather than fixing it.
///
/// So: **turns for angles that are drawn, radians for angles that are differentiated**, and the
/// name carries the unit either way. `pole_rad` cannot be mistaken for `pole_turns`.
///
/// The limit is still not a rounded constant. znum writes `0.2095`, which is 12.0035 degrees;
/// `radFromDeg(12)` is twelve degrees exactly as far as the type can express it.
///
/// The dynamics are the standard ones - Barto, Sutton and Anderson 1983 - and the integration is
/// SEMI-IMPLICIT Euler: the position update uses the NEW velocity. Gym's default is explicit
/// Euler, which drifts; znum uses semi-implicit too and is right to.
pub fn cartpoleStep(
    comptime T: type,
    state: CartpoleState(T),
    push: Push,
) CartpoleStep(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const gravity: T = 9.8;
    const cart_mass: T = 1.0;
    const pole_mass: T = 0.1;
    const total_mass: T = cart_mass + pole_mass;
    const half_pole: T = 0.5;
    const pole_moment: T = pole_mass * half_pole;
    const push_force: T = 10.0;
    const seconds: T = 0.02;

    const force: T = switch (push) {
        .left => -push_force,
        .right => push_force,
    };
    // `zm.` because zimrnum has its own `sinRad` and it is a tensor op - the disambiguation the
    // two `lint:off` lines at the top of this file exist for.
    const lean: T = zm.sinRad(state.pole_rad);
    const upright: T = zm.cosRad(state.pole_rad);

    // NO CONVERSION ANYWHERE, WHICH IS THE POINT OF CHOOSING RADIANS
    //
    // These are the textbook equations transcribed, and every quantity is already in the unit the
    // derivation uses. An earlier version held the pole in turns and converted here; that line
    // was a place to be wrong and it was.
    const shared: T = (force + pole_moment * state.pole_rate_rad * state.pole_rate_rad * lean) /
        total_mass;
    const pole_accel: T = (gravity * lean - upright * shared) /
        (half_pole * (4.0 / 3.0 - pole_mass * upright * upright / total_mass));
    const cart_accel: T = shared - pole_moment * pole_accel * upright / total_mass;

    // Semi-implicit: the position uses the UPDATED velocity, which conserves energy far better
    // than the explicit form over a long episode.
    const cart_rate: T = state.cart_rate + seconds * cart_accel;
    const pole_rate_rad: T = state.pole_rate_rad + seconds * pole_accel;
    const next: CartpoleState(T) = .{
        .cart = state.cart + seconds * cart_rate,
        .cart_rate = cart_rate,
        .pole_rad = state.pole_rad + seconds * pole_rate_rad,
        .pole_rate_rad = pole_rate_rad,
    };

    // Twelve degrees, converted rather than rounded - and 2.4 metres of track either side.
    const fall_rad: T = zm.radFromDeg(@as(T, 12.0));
    const track: T = 2.4;
    const failed: bool = @abs(next.cart) > track or @abs(next.pole_rad) > fall_rad;
    return .{
        .state = next,
        // A reward of one for every step survived, which is the standard scoring and makes the
        // return equal to the episode length - so "did it learn" reads directly off the number.
        .reward = 1.0,
        .failed = failed,
    };
}

This works when the host function has a pure scalar form — one input struct to one output struct. A whole-tensor operation expressed over shapes and strides is a different algorithm from a kernel indexed by thread id, and sharing code between them gains nothing.

Every operation with a kernel is checked against its CPU twin on the same input, with a per-operation tolerance. Operations whose arithmetic cannot drift carry a bar of exactly zero — 9.10.

20. Gotchas#

Every entry here is something that produced a wrong answer without producing an error, during the writing of this library. They are collected because a trap you meet at three in the morning has to be findable, not discoverable by reading the section it happens to live in.

Each links to where it is explained.

20.1 Missing data#

  • The value under a missing slot is unspecified. A fill writes where it fills and nowhere else, so an unfilled slot keeps whatever it held. Read isValid before reading a value — 19.1 / 12.6
  • A fill can free the mask. Completing a column triggers the return to the cheap representation, so a defer gpa.free(col.valid.?) written before the fill unwraps a null — 12.6
  • An integer gap is a silent zero. A float gap is a loud NaN; an integer column has no NaN, so a mean over five slots where two were never measured is plausible and wrong — 12.4
  • A missing key matches nothing, not even another missing key. And the raw values under two gaps may well be equal — 12.9
  • A Series can be a VIEW. A column taken out of a matrix is strided, and reading its buffer directly walks a different column. Such a read gives 21 where the answer is 7 — 12.4

20.2 Edges and empties#

  • all of an empty tensor is TRUE and any is false. Forced by composition, not chosen — and getting it backwards passes every non-empty test — 9.8
  • arange excludes its stop; linspace asks. Same count, different spacing — 4.6
  • A trapezoid's endpoints count half. Five samples span four intervals, so a constant 1 integrates to 4 — 9.11
  • One sample integrates to zero, not to its own value — 9.11
  • The shifted-in rows and a partial window are missing, not zero. A zero reads as "no change" rather than "no data" — 12.11
  • n bins have n+1 edges, and the last is set to the bound rather than computed — 12.10

20.3 Numerics that lose the value#

  • Forming the 1 loses everything. log(1 + small) is zero once small falls under the epsilon of 1. It has cost three separate values here — log(softmax), softplus, and what logSumExp avoided by design. log1p and the max-subtraction are the fixes — 17.11
  • exp(-x) reaches zero near x = 103 in f32, so a value 110 below a row maximum softmaxes to exactly zero and its logarithm is -inf — 8.8
  • A product cannot have a zero tolerance. Multiplication is associative in real arithmetic and not in floating point — 9.10
  • Constant folding is a different machine. If a claim is about float or integer arithmetic, the operands must come from memory — a const is folded at full precision and proves nothing about the type you named
  • @constCast on a literal gives read-only memory. Fine to read from, aborts on a write — and a finite-difference probe writes — 17.11
  • Out of range in astype is an error, not a wrap. numpy wraps; Zig's @intFromFloat is undefined out of range, so the choice was forced — 4.7

20.4 Shapes and names#

  • stack grows the rank; concat grows an axis. Both take a list and an axis and the shapes look alike — 7.11
  • swapaxes returns a VIEW. Writing through it writes through to the original — 7.11
  • Precision is a COLUMN sum, recall a ROW sum. Swapping them gives plausible numbers on a symmetric matrix — 12.10
  • A duplicate join key multiplies rows, so a join can return more rows than either input — 12.9
  • Turns for angles that are drawn, radians for angles that are differentiated. d/dθ sin θ is cos θ only in radians, and the name carries the unit either way

21. Reference#

The complete public surface, in source order, generated by tools/zimrnum_ref.zig. A test in src/zimrnum.zig compares this table against the source and fails the build if either gains an entry the other lacks. Names appearing twice — init, transpose — belong to different types; the signature column distinguishes them.

The GPU column names the kernel or kernels compared against that function on a device, derived from the sweep in examples/zimrnum_field: each of its rows names a kernel and calls one zn function. host means the function runs on the CPU only. For factorisations, statistics, views and the autograd machinery that is by design; for an elementwise or row-wise operation it means a kernel has not been written yet.

gluglu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Varmethodslice * sigmoid(slice). No backward: znum hand-derives one.
swigluswiglu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!VarmethodThe same, gated by SiLU. Every recent transformer uses it.
